I
David had told him where they were hiding; and now he was
back in a dank black cavern on the Orontes, going with grim
stealth on the palms of his hands and on his knees, his lower
legs drawn up lest his feet strike off an echo; scenting along like a
huge ferret, and smelling or hearing or feeling the abominable
lust which he sought, the evil incestuous God-shaming lust of a
son and a daughter; of his own Peta, the frail pale sly wicked one,
who had abandoned the true church and joined the Paternians,
that body of Satan-lovers for whom the lusts and lecheries of hell
had become holy sacraments; and his daughter Piamon, slender,
long-legged, with laughing eyes and a constant smile of innocence,
behind which shone and danced and mocked, like a weasel's eyes
in a rabbit-warren, the eternal evil that was woman.
“Why," he had asked David, "has this wickedness come upon
my house and head?"
David had shrugged. "This wickedness," he had said, "is with
many."
Ah God, with how many indeed! How many of the Christians,
the new race, the new chosen of the Almighty, had set up house
with the Devil? Hareb had seen their wickedness. At the agapae
or love-feasts he had seen such drunkenness and debauchery as
shamed the pagans. He knew that some of the clergy chose among
the loveliest of the virgins and took them into their house, there
pretending to live in holy chastity while sharing the same bed;
that professing to live in celibacy they kept mistresses whom they
passed off as their sisters; that clerical sycophants surrounded
themselves with rich widows. Had not Tertullian said that many
in his time affected chastity, yet indulged in secret vices? Only
9three years ago, at the Council of Elvira, had not leaders said that
all who ministered at the altar must abstain from the wives or for-
feit their position? Was it not a rule of the holy church of God
that no ecclesiastic should permit any woman to dwell with him
save his sister or daughter, and then only if she had consecrated
herself to the life of a virgin in Christ? But the evils continued to
multiply; the Devil counted his disciples in many Christian homes.
Moving along in the darkness he was recalling that Clement and
Cyprian in their time, and other elders after them, had inveighed
against the luxurious living of the clergy-against their costly
raiment and rich banquets, their utensils of gold, their gilded
litters and chariots, their multitude of slaves, their concubines
and whores. In the very temples of the pagans, Tertullian had
said, adulteries were consummated and pimping was practiced at
the very altars, as though the gods were brokers in sin; yet the
Christian good life had been made plain in the Gospel according
to the Egyptians, where virginity was a condition of the coming
of the Kingdom, and Jesus said, "I am come to suppress the acts
of woman." The Acts of Thomas had made separation of husband
and wife the price of eternal life; and the great Tatian had called
marriage a corruption and a fornication. . . .
He crept forward, smelling the evil, feeling in memory the odor
that was woman hugging her loins. Out in the desert was a plant
the smell of which was that of a woman in heat; and in a recent ser-
mon a bishop had said that this plant was the Devil's own symbol.
There were many plants that smelled of woman. This open cav-
ern, with its hot damp odors and its evil caressing warmth, was
an accursed symbol of the womb, out of which a man came into
an accursed world. Little wonder that Peta and Piamon, his
wicked son and daughter, had crawled back into this place; for
caves, like the desert, were a favorite haunt of demons. Hareb
could not only smell the evil, he could feel it, so palpable it was,
so saturated with all the abominations of time and earth.
He paused, breathing hard, to listen; and after a moment he
heard the gurgles of delights and raptures, the indrawn breath
gasping and sucking across wet teeth and tongue; the low mur-
murs and cries; and then what he took to be the soft and volup-
tuous movement of the thing in its sheath. He crawled forward,
choked and outraged by the sense of a thousand evil presences; of
demon eyes staring with moist yellow spite out of the darkness; of
the warm embracing limbs of woman. He was now reaching out,
10seeking the outline of rock or earth walls, feeling for a door or a
passage; and again pausing to listen. He could hear low tender
music. He could smell the moist open womb of Piamon. Then,
suddenly, he sat up, thrilled and frightened, with all his nerves
tingling, for he had felt all around him the presence of evil so
monstrous and menacing that he was challenged, and he erected
his arms in the form of the cross, his bearded lips murmuring,
"Come out, Satan!-for Christ is in me and I am in Christ, and
not all the demons in hell can overthrow me!" In a low whisper
like a prayer he said, "I will go down into Egypt. . . ."
And then his senses were overwhelmed and he was in the dark.
When he again found himself he moved to the spot where they
had lain, entwined like two serpents: a palm told him that the
earth was still warm, his twitching nostrils that the atmosphere
was heavy with the odor of woman. He recalled now having heard
soft disappearing footfalls and he knew that they had torn them-
selves free of the embrace and fled. Over and over the earth he
rubbed a palm while his nostrils sniffed, until that moment when,
with horror and shame, he realized that his own organ had come
erect. This proof of Satan's presence filled him with murderous
fury and he staggered to his feet, clenching a violent hand upon
his penis while every muscle in his body turned rigid; and then
trembling through and through as he turned cold. With one
desperate gesture he tried to tear the thing from him, and with
another gesture to scatter the demons as he might have scattered
raindrops from his hair. Then he listened. He felt outward with
all his nerves tingling. In his confused and frightened mind he
was trying to formulate a holy phrase, in the sound of which all
evil would be abashed; and there came at last from his tortured
lips these words: "For thus saith the Lord, Behold, I will extend
peace to her like a river, and the glory of the gentiles like a
flowing stream; then shall ye suck, ye shall be borne upon her
sides, and be dandled upon her knees."
An hour later, haggard and filled with horrors, he entered the
shack south of Antioch where he lived with his wife. She was
patching an old ragged garment and did not look up, this gaunt
woman with the sad face and the infinitely sad and lonely eyes,
who had known forty years of hunger and sorrow. She did not
look up, even when it became known to her numbed mind that
Hareb was standing above her and looking down; looking down
as she had seen him look so many times, with loathing in his eyes;
11looking down as a man, created in the image of God, looked on
woman who, by bringing sin into the world, had robbed him of his
godliness.
"I must leave you now," he said, his voice harsh and unpitying.
"Yes, my lord," said Takuda.
"Shall I ever enter the Kingdom?—I who gave my virginity to
a woman? Is there no hope for me? Yes! The Acts of Thomas
tell us that if a man will put away his wife and leave her, he
may
yet inherit eternal life.”
"My Lord, where is Soulai? Have they burned her?”
"Do you listen when I speak?”
"Yes, my lord."
"Then listen. What did Tertullian say?" he asked, standing
above her, menacing, outraged, a man who had been a virgin and
godlike but was godlike no more. "Woman, he said, you ought to
go about clad in mourning and rags, your eyes filled with tears,
to make us forget that you have been mankind's destruction.
Woman, he said, you art the gate to hell. And what did Clement
say? that every woman ought to be filled with shame at the
thought that she is a woman. Do you not know that you are an
Eve? He said, God's verdict on your sex still holds, and your
guilt will hold also; for you are the Devil's gateway, and for your
deceit the very Son of God had to perish. The very Son of God,"
he cried with anguish, "had to perish because of you women! Did
you know that?”
"Yes, my lord. You have told me many times.”
"Did not Paul command you to keep your mouth shut?”
"Yes, my lord."
"Then be silent while I tell you what our Church Fathers have
said. Married people ought to blush at the state in which they are
living. Why have I not left you long ago? Because I am a wicked
man. Because I have lived in corruption and fornication. Ah, my
God, how long it has taken me to see the light! I have been wicked
because you, this temptation, this seductive calamity, this deadly
fascination has clung to me, as it clung to Adam! You have de-
stroyed my godhead, do you hear?”
"Yes, my lord."
"Do our leaders err? No, for God speaks through them. He
spoke through Cyprian when Cyprian said, Woman is the instru-
ment which the Evil One employs to possess our souls. He spoke
through Tertullian when he said, Celibacy must be chosen even
12though the human race shall perish. He spoke through Ambrose
when he said, Let the race die rather than propagate it with the
sin of sexual intercourse. He spoke through Justin when he said,
Sexual activity is unnecessary to life. He spoke through our own
bishop the other day when he said, Virginity is as high above mar-
riage as the heavens are above the earth."
"My lord, you have told me all these things.'
"But you doubt them, being woman. What does the holy book
of Deuteronomy say? When a man has taken a wife and married
her, and if it come to pass that she find no favor in his eyes, be-
cause he has found some uncleanness in her, then let him send her
out of his house. This means—”
"My lord-"
"Silence! Did not the blessed Paul say, Let woman keep silence
and obedience, and if they will learn anything, let them ask their
husbands at home, for it is a shame for a woman to speak in a
church.”
"My lord, I never have."
"But when I say I must leave you to save my soul what do you
say? You say you will go with me to care for me and pray for me.
How can a man save his soul if there abides with him the servant
of the Evil One?"
"I will not see you or speak to you. I will only be near you.”
"No," he said. "You have almost destroyed me. By you I have
sired two devils out of hell, two monstrous abominable imps, two
hellspirits in incestuous lust, two shapes of darkness who even
while I speak-'
"My lord, do not say it."
"But I will say it!" he cried, bending over her, clenching his
hands and shaking above her. "I will say this, you gateway to hell,
that I'd like to crush your boneskull like eggshells! I'd like to pop
your eyes out of their caves the way a bull's stones pop out when
the inner sheath is cut! For what am I but a doomed man?"
Takuda began to weep in her big silent tragic way, without
tears or sounds of grief; her large gaunt frame shaking with its
deep buried woes and its horrors of memory; and Hareb then fell
back and sank muttering to a stool.
"Jesus opened the kingdom of heaven to virgins," he mumbled
to himself, his fierce black eyes scowling down at the earth floor,
his hair hanging shaggy and uncombed down his shoulders. “But
I, God have mercy on me, am no virgin. I have lain in sin and
13begotten monsters by a daughter of Satan. I, created in the image
of the Almighty, am no longer godlike but am an Adam slinking
from the garden. All men were Adams in the innocence of Eden
until the wicked creature made from our rib conspired with Satan
to overthrow us. Now the whole world is peopled with her off-
spring, born from the lust of her thighs, forced out of that deep
trench of a woman's hindend, so called in the Holy Book. Man
that is born of woman is of few days and full of trouble. Job said
it. Man born of woman.
""
He stood up, shaken by bitterness, haggard with loathing of the
sin he had lived in, hating women, the sisters of evil, the whores
of darkness. Matrimony, one of the Church Fathers had said, was
in every case without exception a vice. If it had not been for Eve
God would have found a clean and innocent way to propagate
life; but the bitch of Eden, the slut of Gomorrah, naked and shame-
less and burning in her secret parts with hell's own fires had
seduced the innocent and godlike Adam. And now all flesh was
corrupt. It was all corrupt, and in this corruption of flesh was
housed the soul that struggled to free itself and return to God. . . .
There was only one way to free it, his bishop had told him: to
abhor women, deny all sensual pleasures, and abuse and mortify
and at last destroy the body with any means a man could find.
You could you should-leave it in its own filth-for was it not
a sin to be clean? You could refuse to cleanse it of its stink and
waste; for were not those who had been washed in the blood of
Christ in no need of washing again? You could weep and pray,
pray and weep, and above all you could weep: for did not tears
approach the Throne and obtain what they sought? You could
shave your head like a priest of Serapis and Isis and go into the
wilderness and live naked with beasts, to eat grass and meditate,
while the corrupt body suffered and died. You could endure
torments and agonies, for in suffering was the most unfailing sign
of devotion to the Christ. In all forms of abuse of the body a man
brought himself nearer to the disembodied purity of God.
Yes, you could weep; and now Hareb began to weep, not in the
deep silent way of his wife but with great belching sounds of woe;
for he was filled up, filled up mind and soul, with a sense of his
sinfulness and his ghastly doom. What a pity it was, he thought,
rocking with sobs and groans, that man, created pure, had been
brought to this shameful degradation, in which the soul lived
like a virgin in a foul dungeon! And all-absolutely and utterly.
14all- because of a woman and the lusting in her thighs! He stag-
gered up, gulping with sin and heartbreak; and Takuda, who
could no longer endure his sorrowing, tried to comfort him. Yet
how could a man be comforted by the very one who had sent him
downward on his way to hell!
"No!" he cried, shaking his big shaggy head and flinging the
tears from his eyes. "Woman, don't touch me!" He looked at her
through raindrops and fetched the desperate words up his throat:
"I will go to the desert! I will hang stones from my private parts
or burn them off with fire and I will die of thirst while I live on
salt! I will destroy this abominable house of flesh-"
"My lord," she said, yearning toward him, "I will go with you."
"Oh no, never!"
At this moment a figure darkened the doorway. It was David,
their younger son; a handsome young man but rather pale, and
by habit solemn, anxious and impassioned. He also felt himself
doomed, yet was in love and his beloved had vanished.
"It's David," Takuda said.
Hareb turned fierce wet eyes on his son. "Are the assassins
coming?"
"I know only that they have taken Helene away."
Hareb stood scowling at his son and thinking about him. One
son was an incestuous monster and this one before him was little
better: for months, ignoring his father's exhortations, he had been
wildly infatuated with this Helene, the wickedly beautiful and
dazzling Helene, to lie with whom half the men in Antioch would
have given their souls. David, who might live as a virgin in the
flowing light of his godhead, seemed determined to be an utter and
abominable sinner, walking one by one in the footprints of his
evil father.
"Why in the name of the Almighty do you care where this
wench is?"
"Because I love her."
"There is only one love. Did the Christ our Lord go lusting
after women? What does Paul say-
""
"Oh, in God's name, I know what he says! You have told me a
thousand times." He looked over at his mother. "Is Soulai-?”
"Let us thank God and pray. My Son David, what have they
done with Helene?"
With such an effort that it drained his face David said, "I
think they've put her in a brothel.”
15"Dear son, do not act with desperation. In times like these-"
"But I am desperate!" he cried. "With God's help I will free
her!"
"Leave her there," said Hareb, still scowling at him. "In a
whorehouse? That's where she belongs. Haven't I told you—”
"Yes, father, a thousand times."
"Beautiful women, what are they but the Devil's own agents?
If you must commit the sin of marriage, find a homely wench.
Then, if you come home unexpectedly, you'll not find her lying
with a priest of Marcus.”
"Helene is a good woman," David said, looking with steady
contempt at his father.
"There are no good women. They're evil in proportion as they
are beautiful. Have you so soon forgotten what our church leaders
have said? Tertullian-”
"I know, I know!" cried David with passion. "I know what he
said and what Clement said, what Justin said, what Paul said,
what Cyprian said! I know it all, I deny it all!”
"Then you are no Christian but a wicked hellhound. You will
disguise yourself as a libertine," said Hareb, sneering, “and go into
this whorehouse; and she will put on your clothes and escape; and
do you know, stupid son, what they will do with you? And this
whore, now lying under the pimps, where will she be after you've
been thrown to the lions? She will be the whore of Antioch. The
very men who throw you to the beasts will then sleep with her; and
she will caress them and sprinkle them with sweet scents and kiss
the figcake and hold it to their lips. This woman. . . .”
Takuda had been furtively watching her son and when she saw
his hands clench and his face run white she spoke up quickly,
saying: "David, your father is going away.”
"Where?" asked David hoarsely.
"To the desert."
Hareb advanced a step; and David looked deep into the black
fierce brooding eyes until his own passions rose to choke him,
when, with a shrug of pity, he broke the fixed and friendless stare
that held them and dropped his gaze to the floor. Hareb swung
and left the shack, and David looked after him until the broad
back passed out of sight. He then sat on a stool near his mother
and said: "You mean he's going to the desert to live with the
solitaries?"
"That's what he says."
16"There to live like beasts?-because it says in a psalm that man
that is in dishonor and understands not is like the beasts that
perish!"
"I do not understand the holy books," his mother said quietly,
mending the garment.
"Do you think I sin because I love Helene?"
"Our church leaders say that all man-women love is from Satan."
"Then all tenderness is in Satan. Is that what they tell us?"
With her gaze fixed on her needle Takuda said: "I do not under-
stand these things. God gave understanding only to men." She
looked up and for a moment met his gaze, her own eyes full of a
mother's pleading. "If you go into the brothel to free Helene you
know what they will do to you."
"Throw me to the beasts, I suppose, or blind and cripple me and
send me to the mines. But am I to leave her to a life of whoredom?"
So softly that he barely heard she said, "God does not speak
through women. I cannot tell you what to do."
He went to the doorway and stood there, looking out. To the
north he could see the walls and towers and trees of the city. The
streets a few days ago had been littered with the bloody fragments
of flesh torn from the bodies of Christians; and far away in Rome,
surrounded by sycophants in one of his sumptuous villas, was the
wicked Galerius who had goaded Diocletian to a resolve to bring
all Christians to their death. Five years ago there had been a
dreadful persecution, then a lull, and now violence and horror
still more dreadful. Hareb's father had perished and his father's
father and his father's mother. For years David had heard of the
awful atrocities of Christians tied up in sacks with venomous
serpents and thrown into the sea; of naked women hung down-
ward by one leg from trees; of men tied to the horns or tails of
beasts; of men tortured with claws, scrapers and pincers or broken
on the wheel or slowly roasted in iron chairs; of men with redhot
plates of brass pressed hard upon their genitals; of men stretched
out on their bellies while boiling lead was poured along their
spine or into their rectum; of men blinded with redhot slivers of
metal thrust into their eyeballs. .He had heard it all, all. In
recent months he had seen it all.
•
He had seen his own bishop tortured and to the day of his
death he would never forget this scene-the wild entreaties of
friends, beseeching him to have pity on his children, his wife,
his loved ones; and the cool toneless voice of the inquisitor:
•
17'Doesn't your conscience tell you to consider the welfare of your
wife and children?" "God has a higher claim on my conscience."
"When you die you think you will go straight to heaven to re-
ceive some great reward?" "I will close my eyes on the world of
men and open them on the kingdom of my Father."
They had tortured him. When he did not yield they brought
forth the instruments, stained with human blood and hair: the
hobby horse, the claws, the rack, the heated irons, the boiling oil.
They forced him to look at them and they asked him to say,
"Great are the gods." But he said, "I will say, Great are the gods
in destroying the souls of them who believe in them." "My good
man," they said, "it is little we ask of you. It isn't necessary to
offer sacrifices. We only ask you to take a pinch of incense, a drop
of wine, an olive branch and say, Most sovereign Zeus." "I am
a Christian," he said. "But you will do so little to save your wife
and children, to quiet the frenzies in your old parents? Come!"
And he replied: “Our Lord Jesus said that he that loves father or
mother more than me is not worthy of the Kingdom.'
Recalling the scene David wondered if he ought to purchase a
libellus. The libellus was a certificate which said that the one
who carried it had been faithful in his worship of the gods; sup-
ported by witnesses it said that he had offered a slaughtered beast
in sacrifice, and a drink offering, and had himself consumed a
portion of both. It said that he had offered homage to the Emp-
eror, the Invincible Sun, whose birthday was December 25th.
Quite a few Christians carried the libellus, but Hareb said that
those who wore it were doomed to eternal torments in hellfires a
thousand times worse than woodflame and boiling lead.
He turned to his mother. "Should I get a libellus?"
Without looking up she said, quietly, tonelessly, like one who
had seen all things and endured all things: "Why don't you go
to the desert with your father?"
"God forbid! Would you have me like Simeon stand a whole
year on one leg, with ropes bound round me till my flesh rots
and worms swarm in the flesh?-until I'm only a thing of hair
and filth, a skeleton that stinks from Thebes to Antioch, a creature
worse than the beasts? Mother, I cannot believe that God wills it.
I will not believe that the tenderness I feel for Helene is the work
of Satan and his hellspirits. If tenderness is not a part of God then
what is God?"
"Do not ask me. A woman does not know."
18"Marcus Aurelius said, Be like the promontory against which
the waves break. Are these Christians out in the desert promon-
tories?-squatting there in the loathsome mess of their sores and
weeping and praying!"
In a low voice she said, "I do not know."
"They say all forms of abuse of the body bring a man nearer to
God. Is that true? Am I nearer to God if I castrate myself, as
Origen did, or when I hold Helene and want her to be the mother
of my children?"
"David, are you no longer a Christian?"
David shrugged. Moving a stool close to his mother he sat and
said: "What is a Christian? There are a hundred different sects
who call themselves Christians, yet they hate one another even
more than they hate those who persecute them. Yes, much more.
Is the man a Christian who calls himself an Abstinent and curses
marriage as a sin and covers his body with sores and rot? Is the
disciple of Valentinius or Prodicus or Marcus a Christian, who
debauches himself with every form of vice? I know a Christian
who follows Carpocrates and he says we must experience every
kind of sin to be saved. Is my brother a Christian? Is the follower
of Basilides a Christian, who says that all passions, even the evil,
come from God? Is the Ophite a Christian, who worships Cain?
A Christian! Shall I follow Marcion or Manes, Sibellius or
Montanus?—or my father? And what kind is he? Mother, who is
a Christian nowadays?"
"One who lives in the Christ."
"But all of them pretend to do that. They can't all be Chris-
tians, can they? If one church among so many is the true one,
which one is that?"
"You should talk to your father about these things."
"Haven't I listened to him all my life? He doesn't know. Father,
some say Jesus was born December 25th but that is the birthday
of the sun, isn't it? He sends me to the bishop and the bishop
doesn't know. Father, some say the holy supper should be on the
Jewish 14th of Nisan but in Rome they say it should be the Sun-
day morn of the resurrection at the time of the first full moon after
the equinox. Father sends me to the bishop. The bishop doesn't
know. Father, some say the Son is as old as God, some that he is
not; some say he is a second god, some that he is only the son of
the one God. Father sends me to the bishop. The bishop—”
"My son, please do not utter wicked words.'
19David's laugh was bitter and contemptuous. "Who knows what
is wicked and what is not!" He went to the door and after a few
moments turned to say: “Mother, I am no prophet but this I fore-
see, that if all tenderness of man-woman love is in Satan, the time
will come when people will pray to Satan and give him their souls.
I may make a pact with him myself."
“David!” she cried, horrified. "What demon is in you?”
"The demon that makes a man love a woman, even if such love
is wicked. The demon that sees the glories of the heavens in
Helene's eyes. The demon that tells me I shall give my life if
necessary to set her free."
"David," she said, her eyes on her mending, "go see if you can
find out what they've done to Soulai."
20II
The cell was made of stone and was small and gloomy and dark,
with nothing in it but the girl who sat on the floor and prayed to
Christ her Lord. The guard who stood at the cell door peering
through was a huge man with hair growing low on his forehead
and with jawbones so wide that they looked abnormal. The girl
knew that he was there trying to see her and she had been thinking
about him and at last she arose and went to the door. They could
not see one another clearly and for a few moments he peered in
at her and she looked at his big eager face, wondering what sort
of man he was. When the new persecution broke over Antioch
she had prepared for this hour, had tried to imagine and foresee
all the details of what she would do and how she would die.
Standing close to the iron bars and looking through at his face
she saw that his eyes were small and green and shining like green
jewels. With the daring of youth she offered him a common
Christian greeting, saying, "What is the news of the world? Do
the waters of the river come as usual and do the birds still sing?"
He looked disconcerted. His two big hands were gently clasp-
ing a bar and his big face was pressed against the bars while his
green eyes peered at her. Could she move him?-not with pity, no,
but with an appeal to his lust? For where in all of Antioch was
there a lovelier girl than Soulai, the Daughter of Hareb? She
moved a little closer, still looking at his face, at his bearded mouth
or into his green eyes, studying the thickwitted lout and wonder-
ing desperately what words she could use to have her way with him.
"Tonight," she said, her voice soft and plaintive, "I will stay
here; and tomorrow they will throw me to the beasts or drown me
or burn me alive." She shuddered at the horror of it. "Tomorrow!
21Yet I am young, and beautiful, don't you think? But I have never
known love. No man has ever kissed my lips-" She was silent a
moment, her inner voice praying to her Lord; and she did not
observe that he was trying to make out the form of her body and
breasts. When he spoke at last his voice came to her as a shock,
thick, suspicious, almost astonished.
"Are you a virgin?”
"Yes," she said, looking into his eyes. "I-I am only seventeen."
She opened her eyes wide to show him their full loveliness. "Men
have called me beautiful but no man has ever lain with me.
Would you want me to die, never having known even one moment
of love?" She waited, and whispered: "Would you?”
He was pressing his face against the bars and was staring at her
with intent interest. "You Christians have love-feasts," he said.
"Yes," she said, looking very helpless and pathetic and lonely.
“But I never went to them. My father is a very stern man.
What is your name?”
"Markos," he said.
...
“Oh, I like that name!" And inside herself she said, God give
me strength! "I knew a man named Markos," she said, looking
into his eyes, trying to read his dull mind. “He was very big and
strong, like you. Markos-?" She waited.
"What?" he said.

23wall and tried to think. Markos might come. She had seen lust

How many had died for their Lord? How many Christian women,
to escape the lust of their persecutors, had leapt into rivers or old
wells or had poisoned themselves or had jumped off high places?
How many Christians under torture had bitten off their tongues
to keep from denying their Lord? And with what miracles had
the living God made known his own children! There were those
martyrs who had been devoured by wild beasts, and upon the
beasts being strangled and their bellies opened, the martyrs were
found alive and hale in their stomach, after having been fully
digested. Bishop Eusebius said it was so. God set these marvels
on the earth as a sign to the wicked. He could turn the flames cold
and a Christian could stand in them and not be harmed, while
in consternation the wicked looked on and marveled.
What miracles had astounded the evil men who killed the
blessed Polycarp! After he had finished his prayer and said amen,
the flames blazed up around him and at once shaped themselves
in the form of an arch, like the sail of a ship filled with wind,
and encompassed Polycarp's body with a circling shield of fire.
He stood within the fire and seemed not like flesh which is burnt
but like bread that is baked, or like gold glowing in a furnace.
Then there came from the flames a sweet odor, as of frankincense
or of precious spices smoking. And when the wicked men per-
ceived that the fire would not consume him they sent a man to
pierce him with a long dagger; but when the dagger entered Poly-
carp's body there came forth a dove of unearthly whiteness, and
blood spouting so high that it extinguished the flames. Such was
the wonderful story in a letter written by the church at Smyrna.
Polycarp dreamed a dream in which his pillow was afire; and
when he awoke he exclaimed, "I am to be burned alive!" When
he entered the stadium there came to him a voice from heaven,
saying, "Be strong, and show yourself a man, O Polycarp!" Soulai
was resolved to be strong, and from nothing did strength come
so much as from holy and marvelous tales of the way other martyrs
had died. She had heard her father tell how his own father had
died. They had stood him with his back to a wooden pillar and had
bound his arms behind the pillar; and they had piled round him
24a small mountain of fagots soaked with oil; and the flames from
the pile had enveloped him with a whitehot envelope. But he
had not cried out or moved. Indeed, he had smiled through the
flames at his friends and smiling still had given up his soul to his
God.
One by one Soulai recalled all the wonderful stories she had

When David left his mother he went to Peter's tavern to find
Mayda. He chose a table back in the shadows and ordered wine,
knowing that if he was convincingly to act the part of a libertine
he would have to be half-drunk and have on his tongue some of
the bawdy verses with which gay young fops in pursuit of pleasure
entertained the women of their world. Having learned, unlike
most young men, to read and having read the poets he knew
some of the bawdy verses. There was Horace's-

When he entered the brothel, arrayed in the rich and colorful

lewd jest, he would address the hard old whore, the madam, saying
gaily, "I come seeking the wide-lipped wanton kisses of Psyche.
Or he would say, “When Julia the daughter of Augustus was asked
how so notorious an adulteress could have children that looked
28only like her husband, did she not say, Numquam enim nisi naui
plena tollo vectorem?" Or swaggering like an exquisite and per-

wanton mistress! Her lewd inventions must be beyond com-
pare! And when, exhausted, I ask for something boyish she must
give it. . . .
""
But he was not at all sure that he could carry the thing off, even
when made bold by wine. He had heard of Christian men, and had
known one, who disguised themselves and slipped into brothels,
to liberate Christian girls who had been sold to whoremongers.
Sometimes they afterward effected their escape; but usually they
were found out and sentenced to slave-labor in the mines or
hauled off in chains to the slave-market. David knew that he was
not a good actor: he was too morbid, too much the hostage of his
clamoring passions, too choked with contempt for mankind. Still,
the thing had to be done, at the cost of all his savings, at the cost
it might be of his life. He called for more wine.
After a while Mayda passed his table and paused as though to
consult his wishes in drink or entertainment; and when in a low
voice David asked, "Did you send it?" and in a whisper she replied,
"Yes," David knew that his favorite sister, Soulai the beautiful
one who had refused to deny her Lord, was dead. How many,
O Lord, how many would die under the wicked and accursed
hands of their persecutors, before God in his own way and in his
own time made his plan known! Soulai, the lovely and tender
one, was dead; his father would flee to the desert; and Helene was
at this moment in a brothel, lying under some foul pimp who
reeked of frankincense or rose water. His bitter mind turned to
the words of Seneca, "There I see silken clothes, which protect
neither a woman's body nor her modesty-so that our women may
show as much of themselves to the world at large as they show to
their lovers in the bedroom." What had Juvenal written?

Helene, the good and beautiful one, who also had refused to

Come, David, paint your lips and your face and darken your eyes
and drench your hair with scents; and put on the red and yellow

Two hours later, in the chambers of one who catered to liber-
tines, he was ready for the ordeal. The brothel to which Helene
had been sent was one of the most fashionable in Antioch. It stood
on a hill above the river, in a beautiful yard of trees, flowers and
fountains, without walls or enclosures; and David imagined that
once he was out of Helene's room he would be able to escape. At
the gate he paused to take stock of himself. He was pretty drunk,
he thought, drunker than he had ever been; his vision was misted
over and his tongue seemed numb where it was attached to his
throat. He tried to formulate the lewd and witty things he would
say, to declare his reckless nature and his mission; but in this
moment he could recall only the bitter words of Petronius: "Some
women are kindled to love by the dirt. Their passion is never
roused unless they see a slave or groom in short garments. Others
burn for a man from the arena, a mule-driver thick with dust, or
an actor from the filthy exhibitions." Was it true that women were
of a baser nature than men? Was Helene enjoying herself, clasped
in arms that had clasped a thousand women? He hated himself
for such thoughts but they would not down.
Walking slowly and striking round him with the staff, as he

There is still a little wine for you-"
After one last quick look of yearning and farewell David drew
the bar and opened the door. No one was there. Once more,
with fear and hunger overriding their wills, they folded one
another close and tight, but only for a moment; in the next mom-
ent she was gone. Frantically, David looked round him for a
weapon. He wrenched the leg off a stool. He tried to listen but
could hear only his heart beating like a trapped beast in his
throat. He stepped outside and stood, breathless, but could hear
no sounds below. He thought she must have left the building
by this time. Softly he returned to the room, closing the door
behind him, and again looked round for a weapon but could see
only the legs of a stool like the leg he had in his grasp. He sat on
the bed. Then at once he raced to the window but found that it
was barred and recalled that he had known this from the moment
of entering. Curiously he looked at Helene's garments, which he
was wearing; he sniffed at them, trying to detect the odor of men
who had been here. He was tormenting himself with visions of
the many intimacies in this room when there was a knock on the
door, and at once the door was opened and a man stepped in.
David turned. The man before him was as dissolute a wretch as
he had ever seen, a sotted creature, now in middle age, ravaged by
his debaucheries, his skin wrinkled and yellow and his eyes sunken,
yet resplendent in costly raiment and saturated with scents. The
fop closed the door behind him and looked at David.
"Where is Helene?"
David did not dare to speak at once. He was trying to subdue
the murderous fury that was storming his heart and mind. Slowly
like one in his drink yet conscious of danger, the man looked all
around him, and again at David.
"What are you doing here?"
"Helene?" said David, sneering. "How many times have you
been here?"
"That's none of your business," the man said. "I've paid for
her. Where is she?'
"You have paid for her?" asked David, ominously quiet. He
now advanced, looking into the man's eyes, his right hand grasping
the club; and the man turned and seized the door to open it. But
with one leap David was against the door. "I want your clothes,"
he said. "You may have these."
36"Ah, by the gods, then that is it! You're one of these abomin-
able Christians-"
"Keep your voice down or your eyes will fall down your cheeks.
Off with your garments!"
"Oh no you don't!"
The man moved to open the door and David closed with him
but before he could get his hands on the shrunken throat the man
was screaming for help. David seized him and flung him down and
silenced him with one blow under his chin. He was up then and
hurled the door open, just as two men gained the head of the
stairway. David turned and picked up the club and faced his more
formidable foes, two huge guards armed with cudgels. Tensed
and ready to spring David watched them and in turn they looked
at him and at the man who was visible in the room beyond. In
the next moment the man on the floor came to his senses and began
to shriek. He was shouting that Helene, the Christian whore, had
escaped, and that a man wearing her garments was the fault of it.
The two guards moved, and David rushed upon them and struck
with all the power he had. What happened next he was never to
know. He was never to know whether he was choked into sub-
mission or knocked senseless with a blow on his skull. There was
an inrush of darkness and a sound of music and bells and the
muffled terror of a building falling; and his last memory was of
something soft that he was crushing in one of his hands.
37IV
Helene's parents had perished in the first horrors under Dio-
cletian five years ago. She had no brothers, no sisters, and the only
uncle known to her was in Smyrna and Smyrna was far away. The
first night after her escape, while lying in a hillside cave where a
number of Christians had hidden, she tried to think of her prob-
lem and of what she could do; but for hours she could only sob,
after all tears were spent and nearly all pain was past. Toward
morning she had a dream or a vision, in which Jesus came to her,
smiling and showing his wounds and bending over to kiss her
forehead. "I will watch over David," he had said. "Wait, for he
will come."
But if David lived and escaped would he ever come to her, a
woman who had sinned with all the sins of a whore? Not for her
now or ever could there be the words of Paul, I have fought the
good fight, I have finished the course, I have kept the faith. Not
for her the words of Isaiah, When you pass through the waters
I will be with you; and through the rivers, they shall not over-
flow you; when you walk through the fire you shall not be burned.
Nor for her the words, her own father's favorite, I the Lord have
called you in righteousness and will hold your hand. Still, Jesus
had forgiven the harlot and she, Helene, was no harlot. . . .
In desperation she took her problem to Hareb, because he was
David's father; and she told him what she had done, sparing him
the more indelicate things, and sat a suppliant before him who
looked at her as though she were the Devil himself.
Takuda said: "God will have mercy on you."
All her life Helene had heard that women knew nothing about
these things, or about much of anything at all. She wanted
38Hareb's judgment. She said: “Jesus forgave the halot, who was
a harlot by her own will. Surely he will forgive me, who was
forced into sin."
With his shaggy brows scowling on her Hareb said in his deep
gloomy voice that the soul that sinned was an imp in hell.
"But my soul didn't sin!" she cried. "It was only my body."
"Does a man smell who lives in a stinking house?" He seemed
to be sniffing.
"But you have sinned, you say," said Takuda gently. "You hope
for forgiveness."
"I have sinned, dog that I am! But I'll not sit like a weakling
and beg forgiveness. I'll go to the desert and wrestle with the
demons and destroy this vile body that has known woman."
"Can't a woman do that?" asked Helene.
"Woman sinned the great sin," he said, scowling furiously.
"There is no hope for woman.”
O God, how many times had she heard it! Patiently, gently she
said: "Many women have gone to the desert. Why should they?—
if there's no hope for women! Candida went." Yes, Candida went,
a patrician lady of great wealth who, after giving all her riches to
the poor, went with nothing but a coarse garment and the cheapest
sandals on her feet. Was there no mercy in God for her?
There was no hope for woman, said Hareb, shaking his big
gloomy head and sniffing. She was the Devil's gateway and for
her the very son of God had to perish. Man was created pure—
why, he asked, raising his brows, did she forget these things?—in
the image of God. Woman deceived and betrayed him. Did she
think there could be atonement under heaven for that?
"Agathon," Takuda reminded him gently, "says there is."
"Agathon!" Hareb snorted, and wiped his nose across an arm.
"This man who smiles all the time? Doesn't it say in our holy
books that laughter and gayety come not from God but from
Satan?"
"In a vision," said Helene, bracing herself for an explosion,
"Jesus came to me. He was smiling."
Hareb turned on her black eyes filled with horror. He shuffled
his stool close to her and breathed into her face: "It was a demon!”
"Would a demon tell me to wait for David?" When he gave
no reply but only stared at her she said: "For if I wait I'll be pure,
yet the demon would want me to lie with men.”
39Takuda glanced at her mate. “Perhaps it was Jesus,” she said,
speaking softly as always, lest her dreadful mate explode in her face.
Scowling into Helene's eyes Hareb said, "You will see David
no more, the wicked man! Did he despise the advice of his father
and go lusting after a woman?—and a woman in a whorehouse at
that!"
"I was no whore!" Helene cried, her whole face as well as her
eyes on fire. "Is a woman a whore because these sons of bitches
called men overpower her and force her under them? Are we
to be degraded by man's lusts, yet there shall be no forgiveness
for us? I don't believe it!" she said, rising. "I won't believe it!
If I were to believe that, then I'd give my soul to Satan and I'd pray
to Satan and I would plot with Satan for the overthrow of God!"
"Silence!" Hareb roared, appalled.
"No!" she said, almost screaming, and tossing her golden brown
hair back to show her contempt. Breathing at him as he had

but you men will never understand that! Nowhere does it say
that he forgave the man who covered her! I love David and we will
have children and to the bottom of hell with what the Fathers say!
Do you hear me?" she asked, hoarse and hysterical.
"Go!" he said, pointing.
"Yes, I will go. I will go to the desert and my tears, a woman's
tears, will rise to the throne of God as a man's tears never can.
But first, I will know where David is." She turned and left him,
horrified and appalled, his eyes wide and angry and astonished.
She was resolved to find news of David; and to disguise herself
from her persecutors, as well as to enter the baser taverns, where
she would be more likely to hear about him, she arrayed herself
as a common whore, putting on cheap colorful garments and
painting her face and lips. For many days she haunted the baths
and taverns of the city. It was at the baths that the hellhounds took
their ease, lolling half-naked and languorous after spending their
passion on the brothels. She hoped to overhear a remark that
would tell her where David was but the only words that came to
her ears were obscenities so coarse that she wanted to spit on the
scented fops that uttered them.
One evening at the baths a man stretched out on a couch
accosted her. "Open your garment and let me see you... .Here,"
he said, and tossed a small coin.
She was on the point of outrage when she remembered that
40she was disguised as a common harlot. "You don't appeal to me,"
she said.
"Why not?"
"Your breath stinks.”
Ah, base Satan, that was one thing at least that she had learned
about men, after lying under a hundred of them, with their
breath blowing hot and foul into her face. Yes, and she had
learned that they were creatures of the most prodigious vanity,
each fancying himself in the image of God!
Day after day she sat in taverns. She had little money but it
was enough to buy food and an occasional goblet of cheap wine.
She sat in taverns, sipping wine, looking like any painted harlot,
and listening. The brothel in which she had left David was so
popular with the city's wastrels that she thought if David had es-
caped or had got into a fight some word of it would reach her.
And would none of them wonder about Helene, the Christian
virgin who had been a favorite?
She was alone one evening when a man detached himself from
a group of revelers and came over to her. On looking up, she
saw that it was Peta, David's incestuous brother.
"So it's you!" he said, with that nervous stuttering insincere
laugh that invariably followed on his words. "How did you get
out of the whorehouse?"
"Be still," she said, glancing round her.
Peta laughed again, the laugh of one who for years had been
the constant companion of evil. He fetched a stool over and sat.
He was a slender, pale and rather frail young man, with strange
restless eyes that never for more than a moment fixed on an ob-
ject. Helene had always loathed him and felt unclean in his
presence.
"Well," he said, his gaze sweeping her face, going away, return-
ing and going away. "The deeper the sin the more certain the
salvation. Having been a whore your chances are good." Then
he was trying to make out the form of her breasts; he was glancing
down over her body, and again at her face. "Still, from your
point of view you must feel abandoned to the Devil. What did
Cyprian says? How indignant Christ must be when he sees a
virgin, dedicated to himself, lying with a man. She who has been
guilty of this crime is an adulteress against Christ." He laughed.
He thought of himself as a wit and a gay thing, and above all as a
Christian who had seen the light.
41"But look," he said, his gaze darting over her, his eyes dancing
and mocking. "The filthier the penitent the more beautiful, say
the Christians you belong to. Yet I smell no stink. Have you
bathed? How can you be a real penitent if you wash yourself?”
He snickered in his hand and looked round him. "Will you have
some wine?"
"I have some.”
"Where is David?"
"I don't know."
Whispering, Peta said: "Is he the one who let you out? But of
course he is, the self-righteous bastard. Have they taken him
away?"
"Will you help me find him?”
"Oh, why should I? I detest him, you know, almost as much as
I detest my father. If he is my father. When can a man be sure?
As for David, I suppose they've taken him to the mines."
Weak from grief, sick with despair, Helene studied his eyes,
his mouth, his laugh; thinking of his odious iniquities; wonder-
ing if she could enlist his aid, for he knew all the fops in Antioch.
"You knew that Soulai is dead?"
"What of it? She's now with Christ and that's what she wanted."
"You haven't heard anyone mention David?”
"No."
"Nor seen-"
"No." His mind was quick and intuitive, it anticipated those
whose wits were slower. "Are you going to wait for him?"
"Of course."
"You'll wait a long time. Even if he escapes he'll have one eye
gouged out and be hamstrung in one leg."
"Do you like to torture me?"
"You torture yourself by grieving over what you can't change.
They'll probably castrate him.”
"I love the spirit, not the flesh."
He snorted and choked. "Excuse me," he said, and with a
scented cloth wiped his nose.
"And listen," she said, her voice sibilant. "Stop looking at me
that way. I was there, yes, but against my will and I was no whore."
He was nonplused. "Well, why aren't you? What did Tertull-

42Anyway you've lost your mark and I was fool enough to be looking

"You dog!" she breathed at him. "You hideous thing!"

but all men have been since Eve seduced Adam. Is that our fault?
My soul is as pure as any soul on earth. Do you know," he asked,

"You loathe me because I copulate with my sister. But I say with

"Listen a moment. You want to save your soul but you don't

We can try to destroy the body and free the soul. If some ways of

Without the promise of pleasure we could not be lured into evil;

of Eden. He knows that my soul obeys his laws, while my vile

with my own mother-"
"You horrible thing!"
"You stupid Eve. How many Roman emperors did it to their
sisters or mothers?"
"They weren't Christians."
"Well, how many Christian men do it?"
"They aren't Christian."
"Like hell they aren't. Listen, would you be a bride of Christ?”
"In the spirit I am."
He sneered in her face. "In the spirit! Sure, you would lie with
43your Lord—in the spirit! What contemptible cowards you women
are! In what we do with the flesh we cannot sin, because Eve made
the flesh abominable. But in the spirit?" he said, touching her

"Please don't tell me."
"By God I will tell you. Look," he said, poking at her with the

For a moment he looked into her eyes. "Listen further. A few of
these God damned fools who go to the desert do see the light at

themselves into the brothels with such violent passion that they

"Oh, you are wicked! Please—”
Clutching her arm he said, "Listen! You were created pure too,
like man. You were put in a beautiful garden with all good things.
But woman was a creature of lust and evil who from the moment

44destroy it with the very vices which she forced upon Adam in
Eden."
Helene had turned pale. She stared at him with fascinated

“I shouldn't. No Christian should. But I'm fastidious. We
should do nothing to prolong the life of the evil house we live in.
Who is once washed in the blood of Christ need wash no more.
The soul, I mean. Cleanliness of the body pollutes the soul. For

"Will you help me find David?' '
"To hell with David! Listen. I know a Christian who washes

"Take my advice and don't try to find him. Why should I help

"I don't believe it."
He lifted his brows at her. "You don't believe your own
leaders?"
"I know it is good for a woman to want a child. I know it is
good for a woman to want the love of a husband. I know that
God does not find such things wicked."
"But what have the leaders said?"
She wanted to say that she did not give a damn what they had

"Isn't my own father going to leave his wife? Hasn't he said

"Read our holy books."
"I won't believe it!"
45"I'll tell you what they say."
"I won't listen!"

"I don't want to know."

46"Beloved One, who suffered and died for us, have mercy on me,

47V
It was true, as David had said, that there was no one Christian
faith but a hundred faiths; no one church to which all Christians
turned but a hundred churches, disputing among themselves and
hating one another with a bitterness that amazed the non-Chris-
tian world. What was a man to believe, whom follow, where lay his
devotion and soul among so many who claimed to have the sole
dispensation? David had almost lost his mind trying to determine
which sect was the true one. He learned that a hundred and
fifty years before he was born a bishop of Rome had threatened
to put all the churches of Asia "outside the catholic union" be-
cause they did not agree with Rome on the date of the Passion.
In the lifetime of David's father a bishop of Antioch, Paul of
Samosata, a protege of the learned Zenobia, Queen of Palmyra,
had been denounced and deposed because he had subordinated
Jesus to God. David was amazed to hear that everywhere the many
sects were revising the numerous gospels and other holy writings
to make them conform to the tenets of their faith; and some, a
bishop proclaimed with horror, were trying to make them conform
to the works of Aristotle, Euclid and Galen! The members of any
Sect called the members of all other sects heretics, because sect
and heretic were the same word in Greek. Was any sect catholic
enough, David had asked his father, to make room for the fana-
ticism of an Ignatius, the fierce asceticism of a Tatian, the breadth
of view of a Clement, the bold imagination of an Origen, and the
imperious ecclesiasticism of a Cyprian? His father had said,
"Ask our bishop" and the bishop had said, “I do not know."
David had learned that the sect in Rome, which called itself
the catholic church, abhorred, above all others, those sects with a
48gnostic basis, of which only God knew how many there were scat-
tered from Egypt to Persia. These all believed that matter was
evil, including the human body; upon all other points they were
in acrimonious dispute. Was Saturninus right in saying that the
earth and life on the earth had been created by seven angels, of
whom Yahweh, the Jewish God, was one? Was he right when he
said that marriage came from Satan? David asked his father,
"Did marriage come from Satan?" and his father had said, “Cyp-
rian answered you. He told us that woman is Satan's instrument.'
But how could that be, when he felt such tenderness for Helene!
To show their contempt for matter and for all earthly princes,
Christians who followed such leaders as Saturninus debauched
themselves with every vice they could think of, for it was the duty
of the true gnostic to have experienced all, from the most shocking
perversions to the purest spiritual contemplations. The Car-
pocratians met in secret to debauch themselves-and what tales
were told about them!—and were known to one another by
secret marks on their bodies, including a brand on the back of the
right ear which they received at baptism. Peta and Piamon were
gnostics but David had never learned to which sect they belonged.
Were they followers of Basilides?—who had said that the chief
of the rebel angels was Yahweh, God of the Jews, who had brought
upon that dispersed people the hatred of all nations. Basilides had
written a score of books about the gospels but David trying to
read one of them had failed: he was soon lost in the refinements
and subleties of this man's extraordinary system, with its Great
Archon, the Ogdoad, the Arrhetus, the Hebdomad and all the
other figures in his ecclesiastical pantheon. Lord, what a fellow
Basilides must have been! Or Valentinus, whose system had a
Bythos as the source of all, with a female consort called Silence:
from these two all acts of the godhead descended. From Bythos
proceeded Mind or Nous, in which was the beginning of all things:
Nous was equal to and like the Father, Bythos, and these two gave
birth to Logos or the Word, and Zoe or Life, who in turn pro-
duced Anthropos or Man, and Ecclesia or Church. David had
found no one who could expound for him these complex and
baffling systems. Where, he asked his father, and later his bishop,
did Jesus, the humble Galilean Jew, fit into these vast systems
which the gnostic Christians were evolving?
And there was the large sect founded by Marcion, a man dedi-
cated to the task of wiping from Christianity every mark, however
49small, of Jewish origin or influence. This Christian leader hated
Jews as no man had ever hated them. "If God is good," he asked,
"why did he allow man, his own image and likeness, nay, his own
substance, to be tricked by the Devil?" For if good, if knowing
what would happen and with power to prevent it, he let it happen,
then he was not God but Satan. "Why did he let it happen?"
David asked his father; and Hareb said: "It was settled in the
beginning that woman would destroy man.” That, David thought,
was no answer at all: why would God create man, only to allow him
to be destroyed?
Many Christian Fathers, including Justin, Tatian and Irenaus,
had written copiously in their efforts to answer that question.
Marcion had rejected, completely and utterly, all the holy writings
of the Jews, saying that the Jewish God even lied to his own
prophets, as when he said in Ezekiel, "If the prophet be deceived
when he hath spoken a thing, I have deceived that prophet." Did
not Jeremiah ask, Wilt thou be altogether unto me as a liar? Did
the Jewish God not say in Numbers, Ye shall know my breach of
promise? Did Isaiah not say, We have made lies our refuge, and
under falsehood have we hid ourselves? With scornful glee and
withering contempt Marcion had cited scores of passages in the
Jewish holy books to show that their God approved deception,
cheating, theft, war, slavery, obscenity, intemperance, injustice to
women, cruelty to beasts, tyranny, intolerance and persecution.
He also cast Paul out, for Paul had said, "Being crafty, I caught
you with guile." Paul had said, "Unto the Jews I became as a
Jew, that I might gain the Jews." Paul had said, “For if truth of
God hath more abounded through my lie unto his glory, why yet
am I also judged as a sinner?" David had warmed to Marcion's
sect because of the position it gave women; but how could you be
a Christian and a Jew-hater? If every trace of Judaism were to be
obliterated then what became of Jesus?
"Was Jesus a Jew?" he asked his father; and his father had
said, “Ask our bishop."
"Was Jesus a Jew?" he asked the bishop; and the bishop had said,
"I don't know."
It looked to David as if the Chrisians would lose Jesus, with no
trace of him left, before they were done with it.
Then one day he learned that the Christians who followed
Marcion were enjoined to eat no meat, drink no wine, and live
without sexual intercourse. That could lead only to the extinc-
50tion of the race. Horrified, David turned from them to the
followers of Montanus, who rebelled against the luxury and
worldliness of the catholic church in Rome and called for a return
to the purity and simplicity of the early Christians. But these
thousands of Christians, though they believed in kindliness to
beast as well as to man, and placed women higher than most Chris-
tians were willing to, also called for celibacy. What a powerful
movement for extinction of the race there was in most Christians!
Weary, he turned next to the followers of Manes, and found that
these Christians, who comprised one of the largest sects, also
condemned meat and marriage and knelt in reverence to virginity.
They also taught that man's body was the work of Satan, his soul
of the essence of God. He turned to the Monarchians, who said
that Jesus had been only mortal man; to the Sabellians—the Mod-
alists the Theodotians-the Encratites-searching among the
scores of sects for the one that was true. All that he found was
astonishment and pain. There were Christians who with vice con-
verted their bodies to loathsome stink and pus; and Christians so
immolated in fasting and tears that they died on their hands and
knees in attitudes of prayer; Christians who lived in rags and on
garbage in back alleys, and Christians who vied with princes in
luxury and pomp. What, non-Christians asked, did this religion
mean anyway? What did it mean by faith? To be ignorant of
everything outside the rule of faith, a Christian Father had said,
was to possess all knowledge. Why, yes, said the non-Christian,
Celsus: Christians looked on knowledge as an evil: prove not,
only believe, your faith will save you! What kind of nonsense
was this?
And what, David had been wondering, of the new and power-
ful sects just rising or risen? Good Lord, in his own lifetime there
had come Novatian, denouncing all churches that professed the
right to forgive sins. His followers were increasing in number.
And over in Alexandria was rising one that would be immensely
powerful at its summit, the doctrine of Arianism. And what of
the countless followers of Mithra, between whom and Christians
the feeling was intensely bitter? There were so many similarities
between the old faith and the new that Christian leaders, driven
to the end of their wits, could only cry out that Mithraism was
the work of Satan, contrived to deceive the foolish. With hoots
of derision non-Christians said that Christians were forgers, pla-
giarists and liars. "Before they are done," one of them wrote,
51"they will steal our Mithra's birthday of December 25th for their
Jesus. We baptize with total immersion, our virgins are vowed
to continence, we address one another as brother and sister, and
we call our priests Father. All these and many more they have
stolen from us.
22
From Constantinople to Alexandria, from Damascus to Carthage
and Rome, the marketplaces and shops and parks, even the streets,
were filled with Christians from different sects, all setting them-
selves up as experts in their doctrine, all hotly eager to dispute
or denounce or convert anyone who would listen. If a man was
asked to change a coin he would insist on explaining wherein the
Father differed from the Son. If the price were asked of a loaf of
bread there would follow a discourse on the difference between
homoousios and homoiousios: on the presence or absence of a
single vowel, now, as it was to be years later at Nicea, hung the
fate of the world.
Men fought like savage beasts and killed one another over a vow-
el or a preposition-over ek and en, for instance, which determined
whether Jesus was of or in two natures. Husbands denounced
wives or fathers or sons; sons fled from fathers; or married couples
sought divorce over the question whether Jesus had been of mortal
flesh or only an emanation; over whether Mark or Matthew was
right; over whether Luke or John was to be believed; over the pre-
cise day of the holy meal-over these and a thousand other dispu-
ted points which no man had settled. Non-Christians looked on and
marveled, or turned away in disgust or said it would be a good
thing to obliterate from the face of the earth the disciples of a
Jewish rab in the land of Israel. When David was eighteen years
old two bishops in Syria had fought so furiously over a point of
doctrine that one had killed the other.
Many a time David had stood by and listened. It had seemed
to him that no question aroused them to such unholy and murder-
ous rage as that of the nature of the Father, Son and Spirit. One
day a non-Christian, a merchant of Antioch, put the question:
"Do you have three gods or one? Are you people polytheists?"
A Christian: "The Father, Son and Holy Spirit are the same."
"You fathead, how could that be?"
Second Christian: "We worship one god in trinity, and trinity
in unity. The Father is God, the Son is God, the Holy Ghost is
God, and yet they are not three gods but one God. The catholic
church forbids us to say there are three gods-"
52"Why, you worshiper of mutton, you're as ignorant as a Jew!"
"Wait a minute! The Father is neither created nor begotten.
The Holy Ghost is of the Father and Son, neither made nor
created nor begotten but preceding. In this trinity none is before
or after the other, none greater or less—"
Another Christian: "You are mad! What did Paul say in his
letter to the Corinthians? For God has put all things under his
feet, but when he says all things are put under him it is plain that
he is excepted. When all things shall be subdued unto him, the Son
also shall be subdued, that God may be all in all."
"No, you blockhead! Jesus is the visible of the Father, and the
Father is the invisible of the son. Irenaeus said so."
"And who in hell was Irenaeus? How can the Son be equal to
the Father when he himself said, My father is greater than I? He
who does the will of another, as our Lord did, is inferior to him
whose will he serves. How could the Son be equal, for there was
a time when the Son did not exist. He only became the Son of
God when he became man."
"Why, you ignorant pig, he never was a man! The son is the
wisdom of God; he has always been, will always be. If the Son said
he was inferior, was he not expressing grace and humility? Did
he not say, Glorify me with the glory which I had with you before
the world was; and the Father replied, I have glorified you and will
glorify you again."
"No, I tell you it wasn't that way. The human nature of our
Lord was infused with the Logos, and the Logos took to itself the
weakness of mankind, so that there became one personality, uni-
ting God in man and man in God. When our Lord was hanged as
a curse on a tree he took on himself the sins of humanity, so that
we may all be saved.”
"May you die in a whorehouse, you imbecile! Jesus was not a
man. He was a divine phantom from which shone forth the prim-
ordial light of God. This—”
"No, no," said a man who had not spoken. "Jesus was not a god,
but mortal man, yet much greater and nobler than any man be-
fore him. He did not teach this metaphysical nonsense which you
people are spouting, but love and kindliness, fellowship, peace,
goodwill. You idiots have dunked him in Plato-"
"Wait a minute!" cried another who had not spoken. "You are
all wrong. It was simply this way: God revealed himself in the
form of man, who shone with his attributes and glory; so that man,
53created in his image and then degraded, could again see what he
had once been like. Jesus was Adam before the fall."
"Well, that makes as much sense as any of it," said the merchant.
"Certain great men do have the attributes of God, that we may
imitate them. But why have you chosen only four gospels as holy,
of the fifty that were written?"
The catholic answered: "Because, as Irenaeus told us, there
could not be more than four, nor fewer. Since there are four
zones of the world, and four catholic spirits, which are the four
winds, while the only true church is scattered, it is fitting that
she should have four pillars, which are the gospels under four
aspects."
One of the Christians vented an unholy snort. "Rome speak-
ing!" he cried scornfully, looking round him. "There's only one
gospel, that according to John.”
"You lie!” said another, and moved to choke him. "The only
true gospel is the first two-thirds of Luke, the last being a forgery.
This "
"Come, now!" said the merchant.
"But he lies!" one Christian said, trembling before another.
"Very well. What is your Christian god like?”
"Our Father. As man is, God once was; as God is,
come. He is like us, only glorified and perfect.”
man may be-
"Lord!" said another, his voice thick with disgust. "God is the
ultimate Monad. He is mind, the source and spring of knowledge,
the intellectualis natura simplex. Since mind takes up no room
he has no shape or size-"
"You dullwitted follower of Marcion! God is Logos, the Word.”
"No, God is love."
"God is mind, auto-theos, absolute, eternal; the Son is merely
theanthropos, a godlike man."
"These agents of Satan are all wrong! They would confuse and
deceive, saying, Lo, the truth is not there but here-not here but
there-"
But where? David wondered, at last turning wearily away. Had
there been a Jesus in the land of the Jews? If so, what had he
been, what had he taught, where was he now?
54VI
Hareb fled. Because his wife was bent on going with him to be
near him and to care for him he sneaked away in the night, taking
nothing but the rags on his back, skulking along under the stars,
a man set on one purpose and one thought, the salvation of his
soul. He cared little enough for the theological disputes, the
creeds and formulas of worship, the rituals and sacraments and
all that. He had never really belonged to any of the sects, having
been too extreme an individualist to surrender his will to a system
of dogmas. But in his way he was a very devout Christian, who
had his own notions, long in coming from the seed but now
ripened, of what a man must do if he would save his soul from
the dreadful fires of hell.
He was a strange figure, this man in his middle years, slinking
through the night like a hunted thief, with one hand now and then
on his groin. His hair, wildly shaggy and for years uncombed, was
black with patches of gray. His eyes, deepset and fierce and brood-
ing, were overhung and halfconcealed by eyebrows as coarse and
shaggy as his hair; and these too were streaked with gray. His
beard was short and thick and as tough as thorn; and from his
upper lip there fell down over his mouth a mustache that was so
heavy and stiff that it set off the brooding ferocity of his face. He
was a tall man and quite broad, but flat and spare, with muscles
as tough as leather; and to his natural stamina was now added the
desperate strength of a man who felt himself doomed.
What was he thinking as he fled through the night? He was
thinking of women and of his wife. A woman, Hareb would have
told you, had a way of looking at a man when her house of life
was empty. When her womb was filled she was indifferent to men,
55and sat placid and inert, her belly swelling and her eyes growing
soft, while she made garments for the life to come. But when she
was empty she looked at a man with sad and reproachful or
accusing eyes; or, if sitting, she wiggled obscenely on her but-
tocks; or if standing, she would suddenly envelop a man with
little erotic gestures; in all these things indicating in her shame-
less way that she wanted to be kissed and covered and filled. If
she got her way with you she would abandon herself to passionate
moaning and gasping and cries, pulling a man down upon her,
almost pulling a man into her, her thighs spread and her body
hungry and odorous and absorbing, with no shame at all for her
wickedness, no sense at all of herself as the incarnation of evil,
no regret for what she did in Eden.
His first night out Hareb sought a lone tree and curled under it
to sleep; and he dreamed a dream. He entered the belly of God
and knelt as a child there to build a temple, using the
stones that lay around him like squares of light; and when he
arose to his feet the temple was an enormous edifice and he wand-
ered away from it and went down the canal of the colon and en-
tered complete darkness, where he was caressed by spiced warmth
as tender as a virgin's nipple; and the whole darkness around him
was like rich tapestries. He walked in this darkness with his tongue
thrust out, for he was now in a woman; and enveloping his tongue
was the sensation of warm moist flesh. Erotic sensations were mak-
ing him gentle and tearful with tenderness when suddenly he was
ejected in a kind of vast illsmelling belch and seemed to be lying
stark naked and wet in the shack where he had lived, with Takuda
murmuring over him, while her hands clothed him with cool
white linen. . . .
When he awoke he was very frightened and felt that he had been
struggling all night with demons in the form of serpents. He
stood up and looked round him and became aware that his nos-
trils were twitching and that small spasms as though made by tiny
hands were tickling his cheeks. He pinched a cheek and pinched
his nostrils together, and sneezed; and then he smote himself
across the nose and shook a clenched fist at the morning. Let the
evil one send his demons; let the son of a dog try the soul of
Hareb, of what stuff it was made! "Back to hell with you!" he
cried, scowling at the presence which he could feel but could not
see. During the day he sat to rest and dozed off and dreamed
again. He was somewhere on the earth in a reclining position
56and close to him but on a plane a little above his own was a naked
woman, lying on her back. He looked up over her, at the curve
of her thighs, across her belly and to her breasts, and saw that she
was remarkably beautiful. She was looking down at him, her face
warm and seductive. Then she spread her thighs and he looked
at the female of her and was astonished, for there was no hair.
There was nothing at all that he could see save a very alluring
dimple that was like a baby girl's. He awoke with a start and
finding his organ erect he smote it and staggered filled with shame
to his feet. For more years then he liked to remember it had
been a habit with him to have an erect organ on waking. Once
when he was slowly coming out of sleep Takuda had softly drawn
the blanket back and gently touched her lips to his penis and he
had leapt up and cursed her. . . .
He went out to the road and walked, a shaggy man scowling
round him and smelling something that he did not like. He had
nothing to eat but he did not care about that. One way a man
could conquer evil was to starve his body to death but Hareb
had the notion that he could walk from Antioch to the Thebaid
without taking a drop of water or a morsel of food. He was mak-
ing his retreat to the school of discipline, to the place for reflection,
to the home of solitude-to the desert, not in Syria, not in the land
of Israel, but to Egypt, to which Christians had fled by the thous-
ands ever since the wicked Decius had burned many of them, in-
cluding Hareb's father, at the stake. He should have gone long
ago. By the suffering Lord, he should have gone when he was a
pure young man, untouched by woman!
During the afternoon of this day he was joined by a stranger,
a man who overtook him on the road and accosted him, smiling
and friendly and full of talk. The man said his name was Mark.
Two years ago he had been a prosperous Roman merchant but
his wife had made vows to continence, as so many Christian wives
were doing, and Mark had found himself without a wife. She
had refused not only to sleep with him but to speak to him. Then
one day he learned that she was living with a priest as his sister,
and when he challenged her on this she admitted, not that she
was intimate in the flesh but in the spirit. He had never paid
much attention to the Christian leaders in Rome. Now, taking
time off from his labors, he had a good look at things, including
the priest with whom his wife had gone to live; and what he dis-
covered so shocked him that he fled to Egypt. He learned that
57most of the agapetai, or female companions of the clergy, were
nothing but harlots; yet, only the year before, at the Council of
Elvira, it had been decreed that those who ministered at the altar
must have no intercourse with women. It had been the will of
God, speaking through his holy bishops, that no member of the
clergy should permit a woman to dwell with him, save as sister
or daughter; and then only if the woman was vowed to virginity.
Well, but what had he found! He had found that girls who were
hardly more than children were living with priests or bishops as
their sisters or daughters and sleeping with them every night.
Mark told his tale without rancor or bitterness, while trudging
at Hareb's side. "I was told that they were all virgins but I ob-
served that some of their bellies were swelling." Not once had
Hareb spoken since this man joined him. Now and then he turned
to look at Mark and found always on the man's face the same
friendly smile, the wide-open blue eyes, full of mirth and wonder.
Mark said he had learned a lot of things. So many Christians,
in Rome anyway, believed that the end of the world was near.
They had given to the church all their property, freely and gladly
with the result that the clergy had enormously enriched itself.
Now these destitute people, waiting for the end, lived in the most
wretched poverty, while certain bishops, adding steadily to their
earthly treasures, encouraged the belief, so common among the
innocent, that the end of the world was imminent and that Chris-
tians should stand forth and surrender to the church everything
they had. "But I didn't," Mark said. "I had more than most and
still have."
These things, he said, and many more which he could tell, had
filled him with such disgust and loathing for the church that he
had decided that it was not a church of God at all but a church of
hell. Then he had fled. His wife had told him that one day when
she was praying at the altar she had heard the words, "Sanctus—
sanctus sanctus" like a sweet heavenly chorus all around her;
and the son of God had come down to her and, giving her soul
a soft sweet kiss, had said, "In this sanctus, which the angels sing
for me, receive with my kiss all the divinity of my humanity, and
let it prepare you for the communion table." That, Mark had
decided, was a most abominable lie and he had not seen his wife
again. What a vile woman! She had told him that in visions the
Son came to her and held her in strong embrace, and at the mom-
58ent when passion rose from her belly to suffocate her the Lord
murmured, "My love! My love!"
"Women," said Hareb, turning his scowling face on Mark,
"are the gateway to hell."
Mark said he had spent two years in the desert with Christians.
A lot of non nuptae were there, the nuns, the women who had
not married; and thousands of hermits, as some of them called
themselves, from eremos, meaning the desert; or cenobites, as
others called themselves, from koinos bios, meaning life in com-
mon. Hareb turned again to peer at Mark, dourly, gloomily,
wondering why in the name of the living God this man smiled
constantly, as though life were a thing of joy!
"I know you are on your way to the desert," Mark said. “I
sensed that you have fled from your wife. I knew it when I walked
behind you and saw you now and then smite your organ. Well,
Paul warned us, did he not? How, he asked, can a man be clean
that is born of woman?" Mark had been talking in Greek. Now
in a strange language Hareb heard him mutter: "Post coitiem
omne animal triste est! Is it not true?" Mark asked, and again
Hareb turned to peer at him.
They spent the night together under a tree. Mark was carrying
a knapsack well-filled with bread, salted fish, dates and figs and
a skin of water. He offered food to Hareb but Hareb would not
eat. He talked far into the night, smiling, cheerful, his blue eyes
fixed steadily on Hareb's gloomy countenance. Did Hareb know
that the Devil could impregnate a woman? How, then, was a man
to know if his sons and daughters were his own?
"He never knows," Hareb growled, thinking of Peta.
Did Hareb know how the Devil did this? He first assumed the
shape of a woman and lay with a man. When the seed was in him
he changed to the form of a man and lay with a woman. Anthony
said that was the way of it. Had he heard of Anthony? Yes, Hareb
muttered, he had heard of Anthony. Who had not? "You will
meet him," Mark said. Anthony, he said, was the greatest of all
