the saints and knew all things. Anthony said man was a creature
of light and woman was a creature of darkness. Indeed, he said that
the kingdom of darkness was female. "Did you know that?" he
asked, peering round at Hareb's dark and brooding face. He said
that light shone like a sun in Anthony. But oh, by the blessed
Lord, he was a filthy thing! He had not touched water to himself
59for thirty years. You could smell him halfway across the desert.
But his soul shone like the star of Bethlehem.
"Have you met Pachomius?"
Well, Pachomius was a very young man but some day he would
be a very great saint. He now lived on nothing but a little bread
and water and ashes. He sprinkled the ashes on his bread. "And
that reminds me," Mark said jovially, "that we should eat."
"I'm fasting," Hareb growled.
Well, it was good for a man to fast, but when he had to walk
many miles he had to eat, or he might not walk again. Mark opened
his sack and drew forth a piece of bread. It was a flat piece, about
an inch thick, dark brown, and almost as hard as stone. Mark took
a small stone cup and poured water into it and broke off a morsel
of bread and soaked it in the cup, stirring it around with a finger.
Did Hareb know that Abel had no children? Mark had searched
the holy books from end to end but there was no mention of
children by Abel. What could this mean but that they were all
the children of Cain? He plopped the morsel into his mouth and
Hareb could hear his teeth crunching it. With a piece of stone he
broke off another morsel and put it into the water.
"What did Paul say?" asked Mark. "He said I would that all
men were as I—and he was a virgin. How many children do you
have?"
"Four that I have called mine."
"More than two," said Mark slyly, "are a sign of lasciviousness."
Had he read Aristotle? Aristotle said that man was completed by
the Creator but that woman was an unfinished product. Well, you
could tell that by looking at her. She didn't look finished, did she?
There was something missing in her but Mark confessed that after
long study of the female form he could not be sure what it was.
Perhaps she lacked a soul.
While Mark's teeth were crunching bread and figs and his hands
were preparing other morsels his bland blue eyes met the fierce
black opaqueness of Hareb's gaze. Great marvels and wonders,
Mark said, were happening to the saints in the desert. Had Hareb
heard about them? When a saint died they buried him of course,
for if they did not the jackals would eat him. The blessed Anthony
heard one day that a saint had died out in the desert and went to
bury him, but forgot to take a spade along; and while he stood be-
fore the body, wondering how to dig a grave, two big lions came
up to him; and when they purred and rubbed against his legs he
60knew that they wished to be blessed, and he blessed them. Then
they dug a deep grave, using their claws, and Anthony buried the
body and rejoiced. Even lions, he said, were more obedient to
the will of God than women.
"I've heard of greater wonders," Hareb muttered.
Mark was astonished. While chewing a fig he searched his mind
for wonders and said at last: "Have you heard of the saint on the
Jordan river?" It was a very hot day and the saint found a cave
and crawled into it to rest. A lion was there and the lion began
to roar and the saint said to him, "Good fellow, what is the trouble?
There is room enough here for both of us but if you don't like me
for a companion, get up and go." And the lion said, "I can't
stand your stink," and he got up and left.
Hareb had been looking into Mark's eyes. Now he clasped his
hands to his belly and made a dreadful sound, like that of deep
infernal laughter. Mark stared at him, amazed. Then the in-
fernal sound came out of Hareb and he sat clutching his belly and
rumbling like a man in terrible pain. Continuing to stare at him,
Mark plopped a date into his mouth.
"You torture me," said Hareb, groaning. "These wonders are of
no account."
Very well, Mark said cheerfully, he would tell of greater won-
ders. Out in the desert was a man who said that the holy bread did
not become the body of the Lord, but only seemed so to foolish
men. So Anthony prayed for this man and argued with him,
saying, "We were created from the dust of earth in the image of
God, and bread comes from the same dust; so why do you doubt?”
And still the man would not believe. Then Anthony asked God
to convince the man with a wonder, and it happened this way.
When the holy bread was placed on a table a child appeared, and
as Anthony stretched out his hand to the bread an angel came
down and slew the child, and caught its blood in a cup; and when
the doubting man drew near, a piece of living flesh dripping with
blood was given to him. The man fell on his face, crying, “O
Lord, forgive me, for now I believe!" And at once the bloody
flesh in his hands became bread.
"Did you see this wonder?" asked Hareb, impressed.
Well, no, Mark admitted that he had not seen it. But he could
tell of greater wonders than that. He told of many marvels-of
how a saint, freezing in a cold night, was saved by a lion that came
and brooded over him; of how another lion came to a crippled
61saint unable to walk and became his beast of burden; of how a
brigand was chasing a wounded stag when the beast turned to con-
front him and showed a shining cross of white light on its fore-
head. He told of a saint who had three constant companions, a
cock, a mouse and a fly. The cock announced the hour for de-
votions, the mouse gently nibbled at the sleeping saint's ear to
awaken him, and the fly buzzed over his face and tickled him when
his thoughts turned to evil. He told of a wolf that had become
a Christian and of a jackal that had learned to utter prayers, its
favorite being the 23rd psalm. . . .Suddenly Mark realized that
Hareb, though still sitting up, was sound asleep.
He'll never make a good saint, Mark thought, if he pampers
his body with such luxuries. Did he dare arouse him? He was a
little afraid of this dark brooding man whose eyes shone with such
baleful fires behind overhanging brows. "Hareb, my brother,"
said Mark, as though speaking to a corpse, "wake up. You'll get
no closer to heaven than a fornicating bishop if you sleep instead
of pray." He peered at Hareb's eyelids but could see no fluttering.
"Out in the desert, my brother, are saints immured in cells so
small that they can neither lie nor sit, but have to stand year after
year until they die. Have you heard of the blessed saint called
Stephen? He stood on his head in a cell for two months. When
he was taken out his body was dead down to his belly, only his
head and chest were alive. Hareb, my brother, your snores offend
God. Wake up. . . .
""
Then slumber overcame Mark and, sitting up with his head
sunk, he dozed. It was daybreak when he was aroused by a sense
of fright and became aware that Hareb's fierce eyes were studying
him.
"You've been sleeping," Hareb growled. "A hell of a saint you
are!"
"But you, Brother Hareb, were also sleeping."
"I did not sleep. It was you. I did not eat. It was you. You
might as well still be in Rome."
"I'm a wicked man," said Mark, his eyes sly and twinkling.
"I'll never be an Anthony or an Agios."
Hareb said: "I'll be the greatest saint in the desert."
"God will bless you if you are. But you'll have to be greater
than Anthony or Agios—and which of them is greater no man can
say. Anthony is gabby and that is bad in a saint. Agios is silent.
Once in a while he says, Inspexit et despexit and then won't even
62grunt for days on end. Or he says, Inter faeces et urinas nascimur.
That shuts him up. That ought to silence any man.”
"What does it mean?" asked Hareb.
Mark moved over and put his mouth to Hareb's ear and whis-
pered: "It says we are born between-well, in the Roman tongue
it is faeces et urina. And it's true, isn't it? I'd never thought of
it before I heard Agios. He makes an awful face now and then
and says those words and then won't speak for weeks.”
Staring at Mark, Hareb said: "There's something obscene
about a woman's hindend."
"That is true," Mark said agreeably.
"I'll be the greatest of the saints," Hareb proclaimed. “If
Anthony eats six ounces of bread daily I'll eat four. Docs he
drink two ounces of water? I will live off the dew."
"I'll never be a great saint," said Mark, sadly wagging his head,
though he was not sad at all. "For one thing, I talk too damned
much. For another, I carry with me hidden in the hems of my
garments many gold coins. I am a rich man. But I see it this way,
my brother: not all of us can be the greatest, or there would be
nobody with whom to compare the great. There are many orders
of angels. I'm content to let you and Anthony and Agios stand far
above me."
"I'll stand above them all," said Hareb.
"Then you must face all temptations. In Alexandria I slept
with the virgins."
"Great God!" said Hareb, appalled.
"You don't understand. You must lie with them. How can you
know your strength if you don't tempt it?”
Hareb reflected gloomily and admitted, “It is true. Show me the
virgins and I will show my strength.'
"I arose pure," Mark said, but his eyes were sly. “Can you re-
sist any temptation the Devil presents?"
"Do I look like a feeble sinner?” asked Hareb angrily. “Have I
eaten in forty-eight hours? Did I sleep?"
"Then, come," said Mark, rising. "Over on the Jordan are
Christians who worship the Devil. Wrestle with them and we shall
see what kind of saint you will be."
63VII
It was true, as Mark said, that on the Jordan river, on sites former-
ly occupied by the Essenes, was a Christian sect, who, believing the
body vile, punished it with the most extreme license. In their
sexual practices they were promiscuous; in their love-feasts, held
under the tent of darkness, they inflamed their passions with wine
and then yielded to every impulse that passion prompted, copulat-
ing with every conceivable posture, including that of beasts. Mark
had seen them on his way north, while traversing the deserts of
Israel and communing with famous monks there. He wanted to
see if Hareb could resist the temptations at a love-feast but Hareb
would not go. They could also, Mark said, trying to offer induce-
ments, see some of the greater saints; but Hareb said he was not
interested in saints, for would he not make them all hang their
heads before God?
But he must see Hilarion, who was at Gaza, right in their path.
Hilarion was a very young saint, who had been converted by
Anthony, and who was now holed up in a tiny stone cell, living
in solitude on bread and water, weeping and praying. They could
also see Alexandra, who had had herself immured in a tomb and
now received a little bread and water through a small opening,
but steadfastly refused to see or speak to anyone. Hareb should
see for himself how these saints were wrestling with demons—
"Demons!" Hareb snorted. "Do I not wrestle with them day
and night?"
Yes, it was true; Hareb was struggling mightily against his evil
passions. Though he had yielded to Mark's gentle urging and had
taken bread and water he strove not to sleep and when in spite of
his will he did sleep he had dreams and visions which came from
64Satan. He dreamed that he was wildly in love with his sister while
both were in their mother's womb, and that they copulated there,
with the result that a monster was born to them. The previous
evening Mark had been telling him about Osiris and Isis, who
loved in their mother's womb and mated, so that two gods were
born into the world as brother and sister twins and as man and
wife also. In Hareb's dream he and his sister were not born but
were strangled in the womb by the monster whom they had be-
gotten; and the terror of it had been so dreadful that he came out
of sleep striking round him and gurgling. Mark had never known
a man so constantly beset and tormented by devils. Even while
they trudged along the road Hareb would now and then utter a
sudden mad cry and bend over clutching his belly and groan.
Mark had no doubt that he would make a great saint.
One morning his behavior was so strange that Mark became
frightened. Hareb had eaten a little stale bread and drunk a little
water when, with no warning at all, he dropped to hands and
knees and rushed round and round like a beast, making the un-
human sounds of woe that sometimes burst from his lips while he
slept. He began to crawl away, uttering sounds of torture and
terror; and Mark, running after him, thought that a demon in
the form of a beast had entered Hareb. "Make the sign of the
cross!" he begged him, himself making it; but Hareb only
floundered along on hands and knees, tormented and desperate,
like a creature goaded by the lash. Mark was beside himself when
on looking round him he saw that two women had come down the
road and were looking at Hareb. They were Takuda and Helene.
"What is the trouble?" asked Takuda.
"There's a demon in him. For days he has suffered like this."
"For years," she said. "He's my husband and I know."
"Your husband?" Mark ran to Hareb and seized him, crying,
"Brother, your wife has come! . . ."
Hareb had known that. He had seen the two women far down
the road and had recognized them. When he understood that this
sinful thing, this Eve, who had already fetched him to the gates of
hell, was indeed bent on pursuing him, as she had promised to do,
and would now trail him all the way to the deserts of Egypt, his
mind blacked out and he had tried to lose his identity in the form
of a beast. Takuda's voice, most familiar of all the evil sounds on
earth, now called him to his feet and he stood erect, scowling at
her. Mark meanwhile was looking at Helene, his blue eyes frankly
65searching her form and bosom, his mind reflecting that here was
one lovely enough to tempt a saint to his last flash of quivering
will.
"Send her away!" Hareb groaned. "She seduced me into sin!
Now she follows to torment and taunt me—this gateway to hell,
this instrument of Satan!" He advanced on her, his face distorted
with loathing, his hands raised to strike. "Why do you follow
me?" he hissed at her. "Isn't it enough that you have brought me
to the door of hell? Go back!"
"Women can also be saints," Helene said.
"And why did you come?"
"To wait for David."
Hareb was feeling as he had always felt in his later years on
completing the sexual embrace-feeling degraded, feeling a loath-
some uncleanness, feeling a loss of his manliness and godhead,
feeling spent, feeling dehumanized and doomed. While working
out his salvation was he to be dogged day and night by this woman,
this gentle evil insistent sheltering presence!
"Why not let her go?" Mark asked. "If you're going to be the
greatest saint you must challenge all the legions of hell to come
against you. Anthony says it is greater to meet temptation and
overthrow it, than to hole up, as many have done, where tempta-
tion cannot enter. Would you be immured and safe?—or would
you seek the Devil in his own arena?"
"I'll be the greatest," Hareb muttered.
"Then let her come-and this one. What is your name?"
"Helene."
"Look at this girl! Think of what a Devil's agent she will be out
there! Would we had a thousand as beautiful to tempt us every
day! Then the weak would fall, and the strong would stand taller
for being so few."
"You're right," Hareb said, staring under heavy brows at
Helene. "Let Satan tempt me as he tempted Job. I will overthrow
him. There will be no saint as great as I."
Turning to Takuda Mark said: "He will be a great saint. Only
Anthony and Agios will be greater."
"None," said Hareb, still staring at Helene. "None will be
greater.'
“I,” said Mark, smiling and twinkling, "will be one of the lesser
saints, if one at all. Still, I lay with the virgins in Alexandria and
arose pure. So will Hareb." He looked now at Helene and saw
66her brows rise a little, saw the amusement or was it
in her eyes.
contempt?—
Takuda turned her sad bony face, now gray with dust and fa-
tigue, on her husband, and regarded him, it seemed to Mark, with
a sly concealed mirth that had become weary with the years.
Though he accepted without question the Christian belief that
women were the root of evil and had corrupted godlike man, Mark
could not find it in his heart to be unkind to them. He liked them.
He liked Takuda but best of all he liked what he saw in Helene's
eyes.
Hareb now turned down the road with Mark at his side, with
the women trailing. It was a hot road deep with dust but there
was a cooling breeze from the sea. Now and then they had to leave
the road to let a caravan pass, a camel- or donkey-train, laden with
provisions going north or south; or they left the road to sit and
rest. When night came they sought a shelter of tree or bushes or
an old wall and Mark regaled them with the wonders with which
God confused and dismayed the Devil. Or he described as well as
he could the principal areas of the monastic life.
There was Mount Nitria, sometimes known as the Nitre Valley
or as Nitre Lake, which was a two-day journey from the Nile. Be-
tween it and Alexandria was Lake Mareotis: you crossed this lake
in a two-day journey and then walked to the valley if you could
not beg or hire a ride. The part of the valley beyond Scete was
wild and rugged and the nearest drinking water was twelve or
fifteen miles away; but Christians lived there nevertheless, licking
the dew off plants or trudging the long distance with earthen jars.
A part of this Nitrian valley was called the Cells, where lived those
who were hermits in the strictest sense; each monk occupied a tiny
cell at some distance from his neighbors, joining his fellows only
when on Saturday or Sunday he went to the church. Oxyrhyncus
was in the area called the Thebaid, twelve miles south of Cairo;
many monks were there and many nun-virgins.
"Where is this Agios?" Hareb asked.
"In the Thebaid."
"Where is Anthony?"
"Sometimes in one place and sometimes in another."
Some of the greatest saints, Mark said, were not in the deserts.
Hilarion had immured himself near Gaza, and Alexandra was
entombed just outside Alexandria. When they came to Gaza Mark
took them proudly to the small stone cell in which the youthful
67Hilarion had been immured, and now lived, like all Christians
who had themselves sealed in, in black solitude and in his own
excrement, accepting now and then the hard bread and stale water
which friends thrust in to him through the one small hole in the
prison. Hilarion had been here for two years.
Putting his mouth to the hole in the stone wall Mark shouted,
"This is Mark! Hilarion, my brother, how are you?"
Muffled and echoing inside was a voice. "Mark, what is the
news? Does the world flourish?"
"As always in wickedness. I have a friend here, one Hareb, who
will be one of the greatest saints!"
Muffled and echoing, as from awful depths, the words broken
and flung, came the reply: "The greatest is Anthony! May God
bless and keep him!"
"Do you need food or drink?"
"No."
Helene, who had never seen one of these cells, was walking
around it, astonishment and wonder filling her. About six feet
square, its walls and roof were of stone and mortar. There was no
door or window, nothing but the small black hole the size of a
man's arm. She thought the interior must be almost as black as
night. In such a place, Mark had said, some of the saints lived for
twenty or thirty years, never emerging-for how could they
emerge, unless the structure was torn down! There in darkness,
in their own filth, with nothing but a little bread and water and
their tears and prayers they lived until they died! Why? Because
they thought women an evil and a curse! No, she could not accept
it, not now or ever. She could never believe that God willed such
horrors as this....
Mark meanwhile was cheerfully saying, "Hilarion, tell my friend
the gospel of salvation—you know, the gospel Anthony told you.
He will put his ear close and you tell him, for he is a holy man."
Hareb put an ear to the hole and heard a voice saying: “Do you
hear me?"
"Yes."
"Remember, then: fasting is the subjugation of the body, which
is vile. Remember: prayer is converse with God. Ceaseless vigil
is war against Satan and his powers. Humility was the state of
Adam: we must return to it. Kneeling is inclining our body be-
fore our Judge. Tears are the remembrance of sin; so weep, my
brother, day and night. Nakedness is our captivity, caused by
68transgression. It is better that a man go naked, as I am now. Have
you heard me?"
"Yes."
"Eat only enough to keep the body alive for its torments: too
little and it will die and cheat you; too much and it will grow
energetic and overthrow you. Abjure women as you abjure hell.
Weep-fast-pray and praise God day and night. Then will you
rise to the highest glory ..
Helene had stepped close to listen. Becoming agitated she drew
a deep breath; and Mark, fascinated and delighted, observed that
her ripe bosom expanded to sudden voluptuous fullness. How
strange were the ways of Satan!—for even when a woman had no
thought of amorous delights her body and her ways were seductive.
She enticed a man even when her mind was unaware! He stared
at Helene, enraptured. Her breathing was slow, deep, and Mark
was gazing hypnotized at the wonder of her bosom when, suddenly,
she turned and met his eyes. She read the meaning there and
stepped back.
"God damn you!" she cried. "What sort of man are you?"
"Not as God made me," said Mark blandly, “but as I am because
of Eve."
"Eve!" she breathed, her face distorted by scorn and contempt.
"I don't believe all this! I don't believe any of it!"
From gloomy depths came a sharp question: "Have you a
woman there?"
Mark stepped to the hole and said: "Hareb's wife is here, and a
girl named Helene."
"Does the fool expect to become a saint if he journeys with his
wife?"
"He is strong to resist all temptations."
"Tell him to be entombed, as I am!"
"Hilarion, my brother in Christ, how long will you be in there?"
"Send those women away!"
Yielding to an impulse Helene stepped up to the hole and was
about to speak when the stench from inside smote her. She wav-
ered, closing her eyes. Then, putting her mouth to the hole:
"We'll tear this vile prison down and release you! . . . Do you
hear? This is not God's will!"
There was no sound. Mark said: "He won't talk to women.”
Helene had turned pale at the horror of it. She now withdrew
69a few paces and stared at the ugly stone prison, hating it, despising
the man inside it. "How old is he?" she asked bitterly.
"Hilarion, how old are you?”
"Twenty," said the voice.
Only twenty, only a boy, yet already immured, and resolved to
remain there for thirty years! Lord and God, the horror of it!
His own filth would rise round him and fill the cell; he would
crawl with vermin; he would become blind; while year after year
through all the ardent springs and burgeoning summers and glori-
ous autumns he would weep and pray because of the sin of Eve!
When they had left the cell and the women were walking be-
hind the men, Helene said to Takuda: “I won't believe it! God
can send me to hell and roast and boil and torture me till the end
of time but I won't believe it! It's not God doing this, but Satan.
Is David's love for me so wicked that he should entomb himself
alive in a black and stinking place?”
"Don't ask me,” said Takuda wearily. "I'm only a woman.”
Furious and frustrated, Helene looked at Hareb's broad back,
at Mark's, thinking, Here go two men to the desert to become
saints! She turned to gaze back at the solitary cell on the hill, in
the blazing sun, and wondered how a man, above all a young man,
could stand the infernal heat, the stink and the loneliness. What
was a man anyway? She looked again at Mark and Hareb. Yet
here I go also, she thought, to cleanse myself of sin, because of
what men did to me! They force us to become whores; they prac-
tice on us all their abominations and lusts; and then wall them-
selves in to stink and starve and die! What did it all mean she
asked the earth and the sky and there was no answer. What did
it mean that one man would corrupt a woman to the vilest prac-
tices, and another man would flee to the desert to perish of famine
and thirst?—that one would debauch himself day and night in
ways to amaze the beasts, and another would go naked on all fours,
eating grass?—that one would burn his own kind alive and rejoice
at their agonies and drench them with water to prolong their
agonies, and another would sit in his own dung for thirty years
weeping and praying to God? What was a man?
When they sat again to spend the night Helene looked in turn
at Hareb and Mark, trying to understand what a man was. What
a woman was she knew well enough. A woman wanted love and
peace, children and a home, neighbors, a garden, a sweet earth
and a friendly sun, and a divine Mother and Father to watch over
70her while she watched over her children. But what did a man
want? He did not want the blessings of a wife and children, a
garden and love. In God's name what did he want! What was the
deep unhappiness in him, the loneliness and horror, the frenzied
unrest, that drove him to war and torture and all the hideous fol-
lies which he had multiplied on the earth? Why did he blame Eve
for these things? Why did he blame women? God had given a
woman breasts to nurse her child, and because a man could not
keep his eyes off her breasts he cried, Woman, you are the gateway
to hell! God gave woman a womb to bring forth birth, and be-
cause she needed the man's seed, and submitted to his ardent em-
brace to receive it, he cried reproachfully, Have not the blessed
Fathers told us that copulation is wickedness? Then what would
they do, these Harebs and Marks and Hilarions and Anthonys and
Cyprians and Origens and Justins and Clements?-flee women
altogether and allow the race to perish? Did the fools think this
was God's will?
Mark now arose and came over and sat by the woman, and
Helene observed at once that his eyes fell to her breasts. His gaze
went downward to seek out, if it could, the line of her thighs,
though he was talking all the while. He was telling them that the
journey across the desert from Gaza to Egypt was long and danger-
ous, because of distance, drouth, bandits and the terrible sun.
He had money to hire passage by boat for all of them but Hareb
said he would cross the desert alone.
"He will," Takuda said. "There's no use to talk to him."
"He'll die of thirst or be murdered."
"No," said Takuda, shaking her head. "You don't know
Hareb."
The next morning Hareb strode off alone, taking only a little
bread and water with him, after telling Mark that he would meet
him at Alexandra's cell. The three stood together and watched
the lonely figure disappear, growing smaller and smaller in the
shimmering mists of dust and heat, until it appeared and vanished
and appeared, and then was gone. For several days they waited
here for a boat and then took ship for Alexandria, with Mark
paying the passage and staring daily at Helene's breasts. He told
them that Hareb was determined to be the greatest saint of all
and was eager to see a hermit named Agios, who in the practice
of the most dreadful austerities had found no rival. Not even the
71great and blessed Anthony, or Paul the Simple, or Kepha could
endure such torments as Agios daily endured.
"Hareb will," said Takuda. "What Agios does, Hareb will
double."
"Then he will be a great saint," said Mark, and grimaced at
the presence of his own weaknesses. When he saw Helene walk-
ing, as now, with her garment falling back between her thighs, he
was so entranced that he had to keep looking. "I'll never be much
of a saint," he said. "Well, but I'm not ambitious. A lesser glory
will do well enough for me." His enraptured gaze rose to Helene's
bosom.
He said: "You also will be tried, Sister Helene. Yonder, out
there, is a monk named Apollo, whom women find irresistible. He
slips around under cover of darkness to seduce the nuns.”
Helene looked a moment into distance and then at Mark. “How
dare they!" she cried. And then, murmuringly: "Poor dear help-
less Apollo!"
72VIII
When they arrived at Alexandra's cell Hareb was not there, and
Takuda reproached herself bitterly, saying that she ought to have
gone with him across the desert; but Mark said Hareb would be
all right. Possibly he would catch a ride with a camel-train, but
whatever he did he would come through, a man for whom the
rigors of the desert held no terrors. They would wait for him, and
meanwhile he would show them how women, as well as men,
humbled themselves before God, in solitude with fasting and
prayer.
Alexandra's cell was similar to Hilarion's, save that the hole
through which she was fed was a little larger and was higher on
the wall. Mark had never talked to this woman but now he
dragged a stone up, to stand on, so that he could speak into the
hole. Alexandra, he had told them, had been a wealthy highborn
lady in the city, who, on being converted to the Christian faith,
had given all her riches to the poor. She had then arrayed herself
in the cheapest cloth and she ate only black bread. She had been
only a girl then, hardly more than twenty; and when she was im-
plored to tell why she had immured herself she had given this re-
ply: "The love of God filled my soul and I offered to him my vir-
ginity as it had been born with me. A certain young man of noble
birth and high position had looked on me with ardor, and when I
refused his embrace he sought to destroy me; and so I chose to shut
myself up alive in this grave rather than cause a man made in the
image of God to sin." When asked how she spent her time she had
said: "From daylight until the ninth hour I weave linen and pray
and recite the psalms; and then I commemorate in my heart the
sayings of the holy Fathers, and recall the histories of the prophets
73and apostles and martyrs; and so I am comforted while waiting the
end of my life in good hope."
Standing on the stone with his mouth to the small window Mark
said, “Sister Alexandra, how goes it with you? This is Mark speak-
ing, a monk from the Cells." There was no response. "Sister, can
you hear me? Are you asleep?" He listened. He stepped down
and said in a low voice, "There is no sound within."
"Maybe she's dead," said Helene.
No, Mark said, it was not that. Drunken fops came out from
the city to taunt her and tempt her. For a whole month the Devil
himself had stood at this window. Mark went round the cell to see
if any stones had been removed. He returned and said, "She won't
speak, thinking we're from Satan. Let's go see Mother Sara.”
Mother Sara, he said, while they walked back to the city, was the
greatest of all the women saints. She had been married in her
young years, but the spirit of God coming upon her she had out-
witted her husband with one pretext or another, urging sickness
in her private parts or a fainting sickness in her stomach; and so
had preserved her virginity. Like Alexandra she was said to have
been of noble birth and great beauty, and had lived among men
every one of whom, as it was written in Jeremiah, neighed after
his neighbor's wife. They had tried to seduce her with every cun-
ning known to man; and, failing, had thought to break her with
fornication, and had abducted her and forced her into a brothel.
But she had outwitted the wicked men. She would say to them, "I
have a hidden sore, and the smell of its running is exceedingly
strong; and I am afraid that after you have embraced me you will
then loathe me, for the smell of me will be upon you." The men
were abashed and angry and cried, "What manner of woman is
this?" Then Sara would pray to God that she might be saved; and
there came at last a young Christian, dressed as a fop and seeming
to be in a great lust; and he gave five darics to the brothel-keeeper
and said, "Let me be with this virgin this night." When he was
permitted to be with her he dressed her in his own clothes, making
her to appear like a man. Whereon she escaped.
"What happened to him?” asked Helene breathlessly, thinking
of David.
When the fraud became known, Mark said, and the Devil was
thus put to shame by a Christian martyr, the young man was cast
to the beasts, and so now wore a crown of double martyrdom, hers
and his own.
74Sara lived in a cell overlooking the river and had now been
there, it was said, thirty years. When Hilarion came to Anthony,
asking what he must do to be saved, the blessed saint had replied,
"Be like Mother Sara, the virgin above the waters, and you will
know God." In all those years, said Mark, she had never once
looked through the window at the river below, lest the sight of the
Devil's world should tempt her.
Her cell was a little larger than Alexandra's but like it was
built of stone and had only one opening, the tiny window above
the waters. Mark said she was the wisest of all the women saints,
having spent the years memorizing much of the Jewish scriptures
and the sayings of the Fathers. "She will tell you," he said, "how
to save your souls." And cheerfully, almost gaily, like one proceed-
ing to a circus, he took them to the cell and spoke into the window.
"Mother Sara!"
"Yes?" said a strong voice that sounded masculine. "Who is it?"
"It is Mark, friend of the blessed Anthony. I have two women
here who are going to the desert to become nuns."
"I was busy praying. Why have you bothered me?”
"To let them hear from your lips what they must do to be
saved." Turning, Mark whispered, "Step up close, so that you can
hear." When Helene stepped up she touched him and he looked
for a moment at her bosom. Into the window he said, "How is a
monk or a nun known?"
“As Poemen has said, by the temptations they resist."
"If evil thoughts disturb the Christian what must he do?”
"Poemen has told us. Isaiah has told us. Shall the axe boast it-
self against him that uses it?-or the rod shake those who lift it?
Do not aid the axe or the rod and they will have no strength."
There was a moment's pause and then from back in the darkness
the strong voice said: "If you are a man live not with boys, for
they make a means for sin; and if you are a woman, live not with
women, for the Devil's lust can use woman, boy or beast."
"Mother, I have evil thoughts, for one of the women with me is
beautiful."
"When an evil thought fires you with its lust, say, Old man,
behold your fornication! The chief of all wickedness is the wan-
dering of our thoughts from the Way."
"But how can I keep them from wandering? Even now I am
thinking of this girl unclothed."
"Isaiah said it. Woe is me, for I am undone; because I am a
75man of unclean lips, and I dwell in the midst of a people of un-
clean lips. Do you know the story of the monk Iben?”
"No, Mother."
"He lived in a cell like this and his thoughts rose in lust to over-
come him, and the thoughts said, Stupid one, go find yourself a
woman! So he asked friends to give him some mud and of mud he
made the figure of a woman, saying, Lo, this is my wife and now I
must labor to feed her! Long and hard he labored in his cell. He
made another figure of clay and said, Lo, your wife has brought
forth a child! He vexed his body sorely working to support the
two. He made another child and a third, until his toil was so great
that he cried to God, saying, A wife is unnecessary, and so are these
children! When he put them away he put away his thoughts of
fornication and had peace."
Speaking into the gloom Mark said: “I'm afraid my case is not
so simple as that.”
"Well, there was a monk who was weak like you and he was
tormented day and night. The blessed Abraham heard about it
and said to him, If you go into your cell and find a woman on your
mat, and she is beautiful and ardent, would you be able to keep
from thinking that she was a woman? The monk said, No, but I
would struggle with the Devil and I would not touch her. Abraham
said, Then you have not slain your lust, it still abides in you,
though fettered. Brother Mark, are you listening?"
"Yes, Mother."
"You'll never be a saint until you have slain your lust, and the
Devil looking at you says, I would waste my time tempting this
man, for he is with God. You are like the man the blessed Poeman
tells about, who had a fire on his left hand and a tank of water on
his right. If you don't like the fire, extinguish it, for the water is
the pouring out of the soul before our Lord."
Helene, tiptoeing, was trying to look in for a view of this amaz-
ing woman. Mark said, "Mother, they wish to see your blessed
face. Will you show it?"
“No, for what is my face but something that will die? It is my
soul speaking to you. I will tell these women a parable. A certain
man came to his bishop and said, Father, tell me what I must do to
live. The bishop said, Go to the cemetery and curse the dead. The
man went and cursed the dead and stoned them, and returned,
saying, But they said nothing to me. The bishop said, Go, then,
and praise them and call them apostles and saints and righteous
76men. This he did, and said, I praised but they gave no answer.
The bishop said, You reviled them and they responded not, neither
to praise, so let it be with you."
Mark turned to the women, grinning. "It will take me a long
time to become a saint." To the interior he said, "Mother, I still
think of this woman. How can I humble myself and be as a child?”
"Why do you keep lusting after this woman?"
"Because she is so beautiful and I am so weak."
"Is she a virgin?"
"Yes," Mark said and saw Helene start.
“I can tell you are very weak,” Mother Sara said. “I despair for
you."
“I am so weak,” Mark said, “that I cannot weep. Can I be saved
without tears?"
"A certain brother went to an old saint and knelt before him,
saying, Father, what shall I do?-for Satan haunts me day and
night. Watch, said the saint, and weep continually. Brother Mark,
I see that you are a very wicked man. Have you given your prop-
erty to the poor?"
"No, Mother."
"Give it at once. Did you spend last night in prayer?"
"No, Mother."
"Were you lusting after this girl?"
"May God forgive me, yes.”
"Do you eat more than bread and water?"
"In Antioch I sat at a great feast and I got drunk."
"What did the blessed Anthony tell you?"
"He told me I'd never be much of a saint."
Her next words startled him. "It is I," she said, "who am a
man, and you are a woman. If I were to pray to God that all men
might be built up through me, I should be found expressing con-
trition at the door of each of them."
Mark turned wondering eyes on Helen. "What does she mean?"
"Don't ask me."
"Blessed," said Sara, "is he who looks at his sins continuously
day and night. You are a wicked man. Go away now, for I perceive
that the Devil is in you."
"Mother Sara-"
"Go away! Do you hear me?"
A little crestfallen Mark turned away, and the women with him;
77when, smiling, his eyes fixed on Helene with skyblue candor, he
said, "I am wicked. I'll never be a saint."
Helene stopped to look back. She wanted to gaze a little while
at the cell above the waters, and try to realize, if she possibly could,
that a woman, a human being, a creature like herself, had lived
there for thirty years without once looking out. Remembering,
then, that there had been no bad odor at this window, or at Alexan-
dra's, she turned to Mark, saying: “If she has been there so long
why doesn't her prison stink?”
Mark said she had an earthen pot which she handed out to
friends to clean.
"I thought all saints lived in their filth."
"Not all women. Some women won't do it. Women," he said,
looking at her strangely, "are cleaner than men, having need to
be."
"Need to be! Oh, I don't know about that. Women are just
cleaner than men, that's all."
"Some of the nuns are as filthy as any saint in the desert. You
will see."
"I expect to see many things," said Helene coldly. "Women are
different from men but it is not because they are more sinful. They
are less like the beasts." Looking at him steadily she said: "Do any
women go naked on their hands and knees and live on grass?"
"I have never heard of any."
"Do they mutilate their private parts?”
"I don't know. But there's a nun out there, named Zinara, who
does things no man ever does."
"What?"
"Well, the only water she will ever drink is water the other nuns
have washed their hands in. She will eat nothing but the scraps
which the other nuns drop. Sometimes—"
"Yes, what?"
"You must wait and see."
"Why does she do these things?"
"Because she has been so sinful."
"You mean a man was once on top of her?"
"Well," said Mark, trying to smile but looking embarrassed and
unhappy, “she used to live in Caesarea. She became pregnant and
accused one of the elders. When this became known to the bishop
he went to the elder and called him a vile and miserable and pol-
luted scoundrel. The elder said, I am not guilty, but if you wish
78me to confess to this I will confess it. But if I must be thought
guilty of it then let me marry this woman. The bishop said all
right and the man married the woman and placed her in a home
for sisters, where she would have the best of care.
"But this woman,” Mark said, watching Helene's face, "was very
wicked. When the time was there her child did not come forth,
nor after two days nor four nor ten; and she suffered the dreadful
suffering and could neither eat nor sleep but screamed constantly.
She said, Woe unto me, for I am dying, yet am a wicked wretch,
for I falsely accused a holy man. Hearing this, the sisters ran and
told the bishop that this woman had accused the wrong man; and
the bishop sent his deacons to the elder, telling them to pray for
his relief. But the elder had locked himself up and would not let
the deacons in. Then the bishop came to beg his forgiveness and
he and the bishop prayed together, and at once the woman gave
birth to her child and suffered no more. So you can see," said
Mark, "how wicked she was."
"Yes," Helene admitted, "she was wicked, but no more wicked
than the coward who let another man be punished. Where is he?"
"He was not a Christian."
"Then she is doubly wicked."
"See how women are!" said Mark, twitting her. "A moment ago
you were telling me that women are better than men. But when I
point out the guilt of a sister you are ready to tear her to pieces.
Women have no compassion on one another. Helene, do you know
how the nuns treat this girl? Why, God bless their small souls,
they spit on her and curse her and hate her and tell scandals against
her. And what does she do? Waits on them and drinks their hand-
water and weeps day and night.”
"Do you tell me men have compassion on men? Is that why they
wage war eternally and make slaves of one another?"
"So it is," Mark said and shrugged. "There just isn't much com-
passion in the world. But there was compassion in Jesus our Lord.
He forgave the wicked woman, yes, but her sisters cast her beneath
their feet."
"I'll be kind to Zinara. I'll despise those who hate her."
"You'll despise the sisters?" asked Mark, winking at Takuda.
"But our Lord told us to love all people."
"Do you?"
"Damn me, I try to," said Mark. “I'm so wicked myself and have
79so many evil thoughts that I could be nobody's judge. I hope God
will be merciful when he judges me.'
Helene's face softened. “Mark, just what is the terrible wicked-
ness in you? Is it that you lust after me?"
"You know I do," said Mark. "You can see it in my eyes when
I look at you."
"But if God made me beautiful, and men look on me to desire
me, is that their fault?"
"All love between men and women is the work of Satan."
"Oh, to hell with that! I love David and David loves me, and it
is good on earth and it will be good in heaven." They had come to
Alexandra's cell. "Do we go on or wait for Hareb?”
"You two go on," said Takuda. "I'll wait."
"I must lie with the virgins," said Mark.
Helene looked at him with eyes curious and pitying. "Why?"
Mark's smile was a little foolish. "To wrestle with the Devil and
overthrow him."
"Are these virgins more beautiful than I?"
"Oh no, heavens no. Some are not beautiful at all."
"Then if all you wish to do is to wrestle with the Devil you may
lie with me."
Mark looked full into her eyes. He said: "I might not be able
to resist you. If you arouse—”
"O Lord!" said Helene and made a face. "You poor foolish
man! You poor ridiculous image of God! I would resist you if
you were the angel of light himself. I would resist you if you were
anyone in the world but David. How silly of men to put them-
selves in the way of temptation and to groan and wrestle and make
the sign of the cross! Our Lord commanded none of these things.
So go, you absurd ass, and lie with the virgins, who are too homely
to be tempting anyway." She had been looking into his eyes and
she saw something there. "I think it's the whores you're going to.
Which is the way to the nuns?"
80IX
Helene and Takuda spent the night together outside the city.
Mark said he was going to the virgins but Helene doubted that
there was a house of sisters where men could wrestle, as they liked
to put it, with the Devil. She told Takuda he had slipped away to
a whorehouse. Takuda was too weary to care what he did; for her
the ways of men were beyond knowing.
The next morning Helene set off alone into the desert, in the
direction which Mark had pointed out. She had a parcel of food
and a pouch of water. Whether she would remain long in the
desert with the nuns she did not know; she had told her friends in
Antioch where David could find her and she had been eager to
purify herself of sin. Now she was not at all sure that the monks
and nuns in the wilderness had found the way to salvation or were
the special favorites of God.
She left a little after daybreak and about noon came to the first
of the cells. It stood alone in desolate waste, with nothing in sight
all around it but the yellow sands-a small structure of gray stone
with a tiny window on the north side. She looked at it a little
while, wondering if she dared approach it; and at last went softly
to the window and stood by it, listening. She could hear no sound
within. She walked round the cell to see if any stones had been
removed; for she had heard that when the solitaries died, friends.
came to breach a wall and take the body away. These walls were as
sound as the day they were laid. She returned to the window and
became aware of the wild racing of her heart and the suffocation
in her breast. She stood for a few moments, her eyes closed. At
last, putting her face to the window, she tried to look into the
gloom; and in a voice which she did not recognize as her own she
81called, "Is somebody there?" The echoes astonished and alarmed
her. She stepped back and looked around at the sands and up at
the sky, wondering if the man was dead.
Gathering her courage she returned to the window and looked
in and then recoiled and screamed. There, at the other end of this
window tunnel, had been a face, looking out at her. Was it a
human face? She had seen it only a moment but in that moment
she had seen its ghastly emaciation, the black staring eyes, the in-
flamed lower lids sunken and fallen away, the gray beard and the
long gray hair. She crouched down by the wall, trembling with
terror. Overcome by fear she stood up and ran but after a little
distance was so weak and shaken that she sank to the earth. Above
her was the awful furnace of the sun. Around her was the awful
silence. She thought she was losing her mind, for she learned now
that she was covered with sweat, yet felt chilled and numbed, as
though her limbs were dying. She gasped and closed her eyes, and
the whole earth seemed to be rocking like a ship. She wanted to
vomit but tried to choke down the sickness, the shuddering nausea
that filled her stomach and chest and was blacking out her mind.
Clasping her hands she tried to pray. Failing in this she tried to
open her eyes and look round her but her lids were stuck fast
against the terrors of what she might see. "God!" she whispered.
"God, be with me!" There was a strange humming in her ears, a
resonance in her whole being; she crumpled downward, trying to
shrink into herself, to peel off her surface as a glove was peeled off
a hand.
She supposed afterward that she must have fainted. She re-
turned very slowly out of a stupendous vault in which were the
sweet low harmonies of music and a gentle humming in great stone
walls. The whole world as she came back to it was vibrant with
these sounds, as from low sad choirs in heaven; and at first, hearing
only the music, she thought she had died and gone away. As
consciousness returned she reached out with a hand and felt the
warm earth; and then was still; and then made an effort to open
her eyes. But her lids seemed to be stuck fast and with a finger she
probed gently at the lids and felt moisture, and then there came to
her the thought that she had been weeping. She tried to feel in
what position she was by sensing the emotions in her body, and at
first she thought she was in a bed. She thought she was in her bed
in Antioch and birds were singing outside her window.
Abruptly she sat up and opened her eyes wide and felt tears fall
82from her lashes. At first she could see only golden shimmering
haze and she stared into it; and little by little there emerged, very
dimly at first, but slowly taking shape and size, a small thing stand-
ing on a golden plain. She looked at it, wondering what it could
be. Then the full impact of consciousness struck her, opening vast
and wide, to show the sun and the desert and the cell; and with a
cry of horror she struggled up and stood swaying. She rubbed at
her eyes and looked down at her feet and saw the sands. She looked
beyond and saw the cell and heard the faint music retreating across
the silences.
So unnerved that she could not stand she sank again, and placing
her arms in the form of the cross she prayed. She asked God to give
her strength. She asked her Lord to let his presence be with her
until she could feel strong enough to stand alone. Then she
thought of David—of her dear impetuous tender David, a slave
somewhere underground in the mines; and with her lips she
formulated the words: "What manner of woman am I, that he
should come to me, if I faint at the sight of a madman walled up?
David darling, forgive me . . ." She stood up, feeling stronger; she
gathered her hair and tossed it back across her shoulders; she
rubbed a palm over her cheeks to wipe away the tear stains and
felt the abrasive sand; and then laughed a low bitter mirthless
laugh. Great God! No wonder men believed demons were here,
for only madmen would live in such a place. She now saw that her
parcel of food and skin of water had fallen and she picked them up
and brushed the sand off. Then she began to walk, going westward
along the sun's path, and stopping after a few minutes to look back
at the cell.
Lord, what a coward she was! She was going out to learn how
Christians lived, yet faltered at the sight of the first one! Should
she return and look again into those mad eyes, look as a woman
calm and unafraid? Should she perhaps offer him her water and
food? Undecided she stood a little while and then turned back.
At the window she tried to look in and was sickened by the
stench. In a voice which she tried desperately to control she said,
"Greetings, in there! Is there anything I can do for you?" She
waited but heard no sound. "Brother in our Lord, is there any
way I can help you?" She waited again, and at last the face ap-
peared. As before she recoiled, for surely there had been no such
face as this in all the years since Adam. This man was old, very
old; his hair was white and long and filthy; his face was nothing
83but ghastly pallor upon bones; and his eyes were agleam with what
she had never seen in eyes before. Then he was hissing at her with
a kind of insane triumphant glee, hissing across toothless gums;
and she realized with shock that he was looking on her not as a
woman but as Satan in one of his many forms. He was babbling
some kind of unintelligible speech and trying to spit at her
through the hole but his mouth was dry. She could not understand
his jargon but she did understand his meaning: he was telling her
with gloating small-boyish glee that she could not tempt him, that
he had wrestled long and hard with Satan and had overthrown him,
that even if she were twice as lovely and twice as shrewd and crafty
in evil practice she could not lure him out of his holiness where
he now lived with God. Then he was slavering and making glee-
ful gestures at her with his shrunken hands and arms, his dreadful
eyes all the while fixed on her face. With an effort that took all
the strength she had Helene asked if he would accept her food
and water; and his whole face told her that he saw through that
ruse. By all the brothels, did the fool think to give him a love
potion? Did she think to drug his will and then somehow get
through the window and have her way with him, while Satan
stood by and rejoiced? He was babbling with his small shrill old-
man triumph, trying to tell her that all the demons in hell could
not overthrow the soul of this monk. Lord, how he gloried in
his strength, standing there in his filth and stink and challenging
Satan and all his imps to come forth and wrestle with him! He
was not telling her to go away. Oh, by a man's vanity, no: he was
daring her, Satan in infernal guise, to do her worst! In his little
old-man cackling way he was telling her of all the times he had
wrestled and triumphed, until now again he was in the image of
God, a pure man illumined with holiness, a saint, a little garden
walled around, chosen and made peculiar ground, a man of the
Third Race. All this he was telling her, babbling, grinning, slob-
bering, his burning eyes in their sunken basins never for a mo-
ment leaving her eyes....
Feeling at last numbed and senseless and meaningless she turned
away and then looked back at the cell, her eyes filled with wo-
manly compassion and pain. How long had he lived there? How
many thousands of days and nights had he been immured in that
appalling hot little oven, praying, reciting holy words, meditating,
weeping, and struggling with the demons of his lust? She looked
up at the sky, wondering what the Father thought of such a son.
84Did God feel pain too, and gentle compassion? Had this man
never known a woman's love, and a child's? O Lord, it was all
too utterly horrible to think of!
She looked away across the loneliness of the desert. How many
Christians had fled to this vast area of burning sun? Thousands
of them, she had heard, scattered over an area larger than Syria.
Over the horizon, no doubt, there were other cells, housing small
shriveled old men, cackling with triumph of the victory over their
passions and their flesh. And beyond and beyond, for miles and
miles, there were still other cells, each alone, or colonies of cells;
and thousands of nuns living here where nothing grew; and
thousands of monks competing with one anothers to see who would
be the holiest saint among them. Somewhere under this red
flaming sky was Anthony, trudging in deep sands from place to
place, calling on Christians to endure the torments and starve
their flesh until it shrank like the flesh on mummies; to weep and
pray, to weep and pray. . . .
She now did something that did not seem strange to her at the
time. She drew her garment back to bare her breasts and put a
palm under each to cup them. They were full firm breasts that
stood up without support and the nipples were sun-ripened ber-
ries. She liked the warm voluptuous pressure of them against her
palms. Were they a symbol for her of living and growing things?-
of the scent of flowers, of trees drooping with their fruits, of fields
of corn? Were these her answer to the lifeless wastes around her?
She went a few hundred yards beyond the cell and afraid that
she might lose the path she bent over, going back and forth look-
ing for footprints. But footprints in these deep sands were soon
effaced. She looked at the sun and walked again, still holding
her breasts, wishing that some person would come along, any man
or woman, bandit or madman, enslaver or whore, to share with
her this dread; for she was afraid that she would wander into the
desert and walk and walk and never come to the end of anything.
When her water was gone she would die. She would fall to the
sands and the winds then would bury her and David would never
know. She felt panic, thinking of the risks she had taken; she
tried to remember what Mark had said. Mark had said: “Go west
and a little south, guided by the sun. It is about twelve miles
and you cannot walk fast because your feet will sink into the
sands." He had said that she would not see the colony until it
was before her, because there were no hills, only the flat waste.
85How far had she come? She had no idea at all. She thought
she must be off the path for if this cell were on it Mark would
have mentioned it as a landmark. What time was it now? She
looked at the sun but she could not tell; she thought it was past
noon but the sun here was a strange and terrible thing, close to
the earth and spreading over the sky. She felt weak and dying of
thirst but she hoarded her water, because without water no per-
son lived here long. Now and then she thought she saw other
cells or ruins and towers but when she looked at them fixedly
they came out clear for a moment and vanished. Now and then
she looked back at the cell she had left. It was far away now. She
walked on and on, despairing, feeling herself doomed; when sud-
denly before her she saw ruins of a stone wall and was sure that
she heard a human voice; but in the desert you saw many things
and heard many things that were not of this world. She walked
steadily toward it, expecting it to vanish, but it stood there and
presently she saw other stones. She ran toward them and felt of
them but was not sure that they were real. She was standing in
thought, quiet, deeply troubled, wondering if she had lost her
mind because of thirst and heat, when she sensed that something
was walking toward her. She covered her breasts. It came toward
her and at a distance of twenty paces stopped and seemed to be
looking at her; and unable longer to endure the creeping terror
she cried out, "Speak! Are you a person?" The image was silent.
It advanced until it was only a few feet away and it looked at her;
and Helene stared at him, doubting her senses.
He was quite tall or seemed to be to her amazed and doubting
eyes; and he was completely naked save for a dirty rag that covered
his parts. Still, his whole body was clothed with coarse black hair,
or as much of the body as she could see. She thought that she
stood in a haze wherein everything was indistinct but now she
became aware that there was no haze, or very little, and that she
could see to a considerable distance; could see some sort of build-
ing beyond the man and two or three persons moving there; and
could see this man sharply and clearly. He looked at her and she
looked at him, at his stern face, stern even though almost hidden
by beard, and dark, very dark, and accusing. On his forehead he
had a cross tatooed; the base of the tree stood between his heavy
brows and reached up to his matted hair. The crosspiece was
about as wide as his mouth. His eyes were black and fierce and
terribly bright, and the right eye seemed to be higher in the face
86than the left eye; and the long nose was crooked to the left, toward
the eye which was lower. There were no lips showing. In either
cheek was a deep furrow, out of which stood the hair like furze.
It was a savage and sinister face and nowhere in it could she see
a hint of human kindliness. He was absolutely motionless from
head to feet, his long hairy arms hanging at his sides, his legs long
and straight, his feet unshod, his tangled hair falling low on his
shoulders.
"I'm Helene," she said at last, her voice quavering in the still-
ness. "I'm thirsty. Do you have water here?" There was no re-
sponse and no change in him. She looked round her. "Is this
where Mark has been living?.. .Do you know Mark?" She tried
to smile at him but felt that her face only grimaced, like the face
of a beast. She wondered again if there was actually a person be-
fore her. “Are there women here?" His eyes just looked at her,
unfriendly and accusing. “I'm a Christian," she said, trying
again. "I've come from Antioch."
Then he spoke. In a voice so toneless and emotionless that it
chilled her, like the voice of a man speaking from a tomb, he said:
"You whore."
"I tell you I'm Helene, a sister in Christ our Lord!"
"You're from Alexandria. You've been here before."
"I've never been here before."
"You've been here many times."
Then she knew what he meant! He meant that she was not a
woman at all but one of Satan's forms. He meant that Satan, dis-
guised in the form of a woman, had come here many times to
tempt the holy men. The fool! Why did Christian men think
that women had nothing to do but connive with Satan to corrupt
their precious souls!
She turned aside and went away from him and toward the
building, and he turned a little to allow his gaze to follow her.
A hundred yards away was an ugly ragged structure of some sort
that looked like the cells but was much larger. She had seen a
woman over there and she now went toward it; for Mark had
said that the nuns here lived in a building by themselves, apart
from the men, taking their meals together and spending their
hours in prayer and meditation or in making simple garments.
She went to what she took to be the main entrance and then
looked back: the strange hairy man was standing where she had
left him and was still watching her. This building, she saw, look-
87ing at a wall, was made of unhewn stones, roughly set on one
another in mortar. Standing at the entrance she peered in.
"Is anyone here?"
There came out of the gloom to face her a tall angular bony
woman, as lank and thin as a gangling man; completely flat in
her chest; her neck long and scrawny; her face large and un-
womanly in its high cheek bones and its strong and slightly hairy
chin. But it was her eyes that held Helene. They were very large
and round and greenish gray and unhumanly cold, like the eyes
of some large birds she had seen, like the eyes of a falcon maybe,
or of a vulture; so pale of color, so open and staring, like the eyes
of an owl in daytime; and so cold.
"Who are you?" asked the woman.
“I'm Helene, a sister from Antioch, and Mark is my friend."
"Mark." The word was not a question; it was a toneless sound,
as flat as the woman's face, with its small bent nose between the
hills of the cheeks. "What do you want?"
“A drink. I'm very thirsty."
The woman's eyes now left Helene's face and looked over her
body, moving coldly up and down, pausing at last at her breasts.
I begin to see, thought Helene, that the only women Christians
think evil are beautiful women. That was woman's curse, to be
born attractive! She felt an impulse to bare her breasts. Her
thoughts turned to lovely lines in Virgil which David had read
to her: of Dido, always alone, abandoned, companionless, forever
seeking her people in a land not her own. Then, because the
woman was still sizing her up with those pale hostile eyes, she
felt rebellious and angry.
"I'm a Christian. But tell me, what is a Christian? Do you
know?" She curled her lips and turned. "Is the one standing out
there a Christian saint? Is Anthony? Are you? And where is
Zinara?"
"Zinara." It was the same flat tone. "Did you come to see
Zinara?"
"Yes!" She was so exhausted that she felt she must weep. "She's
human, isn't she? She's a sinner like me, isn't she? Maybe she will
talk to me!"
"You have sinned?" asked this nun, whose name was Ida.
Why had she confessed it? "Am I beautiful? Men force beauti-
ful women into sin. I want to see Zinara, who drinks the water
88you wash your hands in, eats the crumbs you drop from the table!
Is she here? Are you going to give me a drink?”
"No."
"You would let me die of thirst? Are you a Christian?"
"Zinara is over there."
Helene turned to see what she meant by over there. She saw
a few cells scattered in the desert at some distance apart. She
could see no sign of human life. "Which one?" When there was
no response she turned and found herself alone.
She crossed the sands to the nearest cell. In this area she saw
around her what looked like ruined walls, and wondered if a
city had once stood here, or if these ugly fragments of stone were
the handiwork of monks. She recalled then the strange hairy man
and looked over to see if he was still there. Yes, he was there,
motionless, tall and straight, still watching her. Well, if a woman
as homely as sin had come from the city no doubt she would have
been given water, a blessing even; but if a beautiful woman came
she was looked on with hostile suspicion, the nuns turned fran-
tically back to their prayers, the monks stood fixed like statues
of stone! She tried to smile, but winds had driven the dust too
deep into her skin. She tried to laugh but there was no sound.
Approaching the nearest cell she went round it to the door,
which faced away from the building where the nuns lived. She
looked in. There, inside, bowed over her work, sat a woman.
Helene's footfalls in the sand had been so soft that the woman had
not heard and did not look up; and so for a few moments Helene
curiously observed her, this Zinara who lied about an elder. Her
head was covered with a kind of veil that fell over her face and
her hands were busy patching a garment. She seemed to be weep-
ing. Now and then her hands would be still a moment and her
chest would fill with a deep sigh and then her hands would be
busy again.
"Hello," said Helene softly.
The woman turned and looked up. Had she been weeping?
Mark had said that the monks and nuns drank so little water,
deliberately punishing themselves with thirst, that when they
wept they wept dry, as he put it. This woman's face certainly was
once beautiful but had been ravaged by loneliness and grief. Her
eyes were dark; her lips were parched and pallid and untidy.
"Are you Zinara?"
In a whisper she said yes.
89"I'm Helene. May I come in?”
"If the Mother says you may."
"Who is she? The tall one with the eyes of an owl in daylight?”
"Sister Ida is our Mother. If you talk to me you'll have to get
her permission.”
“I have it,” said Helene, stepping inside. “Mark has told me
about you."
Zinara moved as if to flee. "About my sin?" she whispered.
"Yes." The cell was small; it was only walls and a doorway,
with a roof of stone and a floor of sand. Zinara was sitting in the
sand in one corner. Helene now sat, facing her, and looked at
the piece of garment in her hands. "What are you making?"
"Something for Mother Ida."
"How long have you been here?"
"Years."
There was silence. Helene was trying to imagine all the years
this woman had been here, weeping and praying, living on scraps
from the floor, drinking water in which her holier sisters washed
their hands, and even, Mark had said, flinging herself down in
awful abasement to touch her tongue to their spittle. Where was
her child? Had she forgotten all her old friends? Would she
never see her people again, but die here, and be buried in the
sands, this pathetic thing so convinced of her wickedness that not
once had she met Helene's gaze! Was this what God would have
her do? Or would he have her remember the blue smoke of morn-
ing fires and the gentle shadows of hills in the cool evenings; the
birds singing at the gates of their nests; a babe's soft hand on
its mother's face? Would he have her comfort her soul with the
sweet lines from Virgil and Lucretius, or the awful words of Paul?
-this other Dido, astray in her wits and lost, sitting on sand in-
stead of leafdrift!
"Zinara?"
"What?" said Zinara, busy with the garment.
"I also have sinned, men tell me. In Antioch where I lived men
are again persecuting Christians; and the man I love has been
killed or sent away to the mines; and because I was cursed with
beauty they forced me into a brothel. The father of the man I
love said I must come here and purify my soul. So I am here.
Can we be friends?"
The low voice said: "Mother Ida does not want me to have
friends."
90"Does she make you live alone?"
"Yes."
"She won't let you live in the building with the other nuns?"
"They're not wicked."
"Oh, I'm not so sure. Do Christians know what wickedness is?"
"Yes," she said. "The holy Fathers knew. Mother Ida knows."
"Where do you sleep?"
"Here."
Month after month, then, year after year she had sat here in
the sands, laboring over those tasks which Ida set before her. It
was too terrible to think of, too terrible for a young healthy
woman like Helene, who loved life and love, who wanted a hus-
band, a baby, a garden of sweet things; the laughter of friends
and birds at her window at daylight. She arose, feeling choked
and furious, and went outside to look round her. Over there, a
hundred yards away, was another cell like this one; and beyond
it another; and a third beyond that. Over there, still rooted in
the sands, was the wretched blockhead with the cross on his fore-
head, still looking at her. Helene felt a mad impulse to rush over
to him and taunt him and tempt him; to twit him with her ripe
lips, mock him with her dark eyes, inflame him with her beautiful
body and drive him roaring and shrieking out of his mind. The
incredible thing! The incredible monster of a man, standing
there and thinking in his tight wizened shrunken little soul that
Satan had come here in her form to tempt him and make him
wrestle with his passions and show off his holines to the astonished
gaze of God!
She looked back in at Zinara. "May I be with you tonight?"
"If Mother Ida says you may."
"I have some food and a little water." There was no response.
"Zinara, let's go away from here! Let's leave this accursed place
at once!"
Zinara shook her head. "I've been wicked and I must find my
way back." She spoke without looking up or raising her face.
Her hands were busy.
Helene looked at the hot sun splashing its red fires over the
afternoon sky; at the cells; at the loneliness and desolation every-
where; and for the moment she could endure no more of it but
went behind the cell, where the monk could not see her, and
flung herself down in fear and grief. "Our Father," she breathed,
"bring David to me, bring David to me before I go mad. . . .”
91X
Not knowing if she would be given water when her own was gone
she drank a little and ate a little of the bread and sweetcakes which
Mark had given her; and she sat until darkness came and nobody
bothered her. When the wonderful desert night drew on, with its
stars and heavens so close to the earth, with the glory above sham-
ing the desolation below, she went to the cell door. She listened
but could not tell if Zinara was asleep. Then she slipped inside
and sank softly to the sands.
She felt great need of Mark and wondered when he would
come. He might be a rascal in his way; possibly he had gone to
harlots; possibly he was a hypocrite and a fraud but he was human
and he was tender. When he came he would tell her what to do.
Maybe, if he were wealthy, as he seemed to be, he would give her
money and she would then go over the world to find David. She
felt that she could not stay here long. What could a woman do
here but lose her beauty and at last her reason and become a ty-
rant like Ida or a pathetic half-dead thing like Zinara?
She was sitting, slumped down, with her back to a stone wall.
There was sand deep in her pores, in her ears, between her teeth,
under her nails and in her boots. She was very weary and sleepy
but she was afraid. Though she listened there was no sound any-
where-none of the night sounds to which she was accustomed;
none of the music of frogs and tree-toads and other night-singers,
nor the sound of waters nor of trees stretching their limbs nor of
anything. The silence was utter, absolute, such silence as she had
not known existed anywhere on earth. She recalled the words of
Anthony as Mark had told them: sit in your cell and your cell
will teach you all things, for a Christian out of his cell is a fish
92out of water. What, in God's name, could this cell teach any-
one?-except silence and loneliness and terror. What could these
stone walls and this floor of sand teach but death! Outside one
could see the glory of the firmament, the marvelous lights of God's
world; but here in the gloom one could see nothing and hear
nothing and know nothing, but the troubled beating of one's
heart and the strange sound of one's breathing.
She dozed and awakened and dozed again. When awake she
tried to recall the sound of the waters, the birdsong, the playing
of children, the warm human life in the taverns; and when she
slept she dreamed. She dreamed that she was in her bed in Anti-
och and David was lying at her side, an arm around her, his face
snuggled against her throat; and he was whispering, “Do not be
afraid. God is love and there is nothing to fear." He kissed her
with soft little kisses, and exquisite sensations tingled in her flesh
and ran like birdfeet down her thighs. He whispered to her
verses from the poets, from the evil pagan poets: "Helene, if I
might kiss your honey-sweet lips as I liked, I'd kiss them both five
hundred thousands of kisses, and have never enough, not even
if our kisses grew thicker than barley in Africa!" He whispered,
"So in her madness she dreamt Aeneas led her on and always left
her behind; she saw herself deserted on lonely roads, seeking her
friends in a wasteland. . . ." The horror of this aroused her. She
sat very still, thinking of the lonely Dido, and David, alone and
lonely; and after a while she slept and dreamed again. In this
dream some men stripped off all their clothing and poured honey
on a robe spread on the earth and sprinkled grain on the honey,
and then rolled over and over in the honey until thousands of
kernels stuck to the hair of their bodies; and then they took these
kernels and baked them into a loaf and gave it to a beautiful
woman to eat, so that she might sicken and die. The woman died
and then there were no more women on earth, and men rejoiced
and were triumphant, saying that they had recovered their image
of God and their godhood, and never more would be sinners but
would live forever in bliss. . . . Then she dreamed another dream
that awakened her. She was a small child playing with other
children, and a mischievous boy said to her and the other girls,
Do you know how you came to be? Your papa and mama put
their backends together and blew air into one another and then
your mama swelled up and you were born. She had been shocked
by the tale when a child; and shocked again she awakened, think-
93ing at first that she had just escaped from the brothel. Thinking
about the dream she remembered the debauched men, drunk and
goatish and full of lustful stupors, who had forced her to act as
the boy for them. She struggled up and went outside to sit by
the wall and look at the stars.
"The Lord is my shepherd," she whispered. "I shall not want.”
But if the Lord wished his people to lie down in green pastures
and walk by the waters, why did they come here? "Though I
walk through the valley of the shadow of death I will fear no evil."
Was this that valley? "Goodness and mercy, goodness and mercy
shall follow me. The Lord will hear you in your day of trouble
and the God of Jacob defend you." What beautiful words they
were, these that David had taught her! But Anthony said, sit in
your cell and your cell will teach you all things.
At daybreak she arose and became aware that Ida and her nuns
were already aroused and stirring. Mark had said that the monks.
and nuns slept only a little, for sleep was one of Satan's allure-
ments. Some of them tried not to sleep at all. Agios, Mark said,
had stood seven days and nights on one foot and had not slept.
Others never slept but stood day and night in unbroken prayer.
Mark had told of one named Paul,.who had three hundred peb-
bles; he uttered three hundrd prayers every day and night, dis-
carding a pebble with each prayer. Another monk labored cease-
lessly; by the light of day when it was day, and by a glorious light
that shone round him when it was night. There was one named
Abraham, bald and beardless, with flesh dried upon his bones like
a potter's sherd, with a ragged garment of many pieces patched
together and of the color of dirt. He had never been known to
smile and he rarely spoke; and he took only a few drops of water
daily and only a morsel of bread and a few herbs. Recalling what
Mark had said and looking round her she wondered if these were
the things the cells taught. Why did they eat and drink so little,
she had asked, and Mark had said it was to reduce and humble
their passions. "But the stronger their passions the more desper-
ate their struggle with Satan and the greater their triumph if they
win." Mark had given her a queer sly smile and changed the
subject.
She went round to the door and looked in; Zinara was weeping

ing that housed the nuns; and when she came close to it she was
94startled by what she saw. Hanging from the wall were three

one was used to flog monks who committed acts of sin and folly;
a second was used to chastise thieves; and the third was used to
punish strangers who came here and misbehaved.

What was it Mark had said about this man?-that he had about

Agios, he told her, as they walked out into the desert, had been

After Hareb came he would compete with Agios and the wonders

Agios, Mark said, usually remained about a half-mile out, com-

99torture he was determined to destroy his flesh and bones. He was

When they came in sight of Agios the man seemed to be stand-

IN HOC SIGNO VINCAS
She asked Mark what the words meant and he said: "In this sign

Yes, Agios was standing on one leg, with the other drawn up

Well, it was said that he had stood on one leg a week without

that Agios was one of Satan's most formidable opponents. An-

100cleansed his soul, and stood there in the godlike purity of the
