There was not much for Helene to learn about the habits of the
nuns. A score of them, most of whom, said Mark, were as homely
as virtue, lived in the ugly stone building under the stern and
vigilant supervision of Mother Ida. They ate together but cov-
ered their faces with their cowls, and under the rules were not
to speak or look around while they ate. They spent a part of
their time in prayer and in meditation on their sins, and a part
in weaving cloth and making garments, most of which were sold in
the city. They slept on mats on a stone floor and two or three
times during the night were aroused to say prayers; and they were
up at daybreak. All these nuns, Mark told her, were very holy;
Ida did not admit to her group any women who had been as sin-
ful as Zinara. Once a day Ida took to Zinara the scraps and
leavings of the holy nuns and a little of the water in which they
had washed their fingers. In some of the places the nuns did not
wash even their fingers but lived year after year without touching
water to anything but their lips and without discarding their
garments until, decayed with their own filth, they fell off.
As for the monks in this area, they were governed by certain
rules but in other respects did as they pleased. A monk might wear
a skull cap with a cross worked in purple on the front or he might
wear no cap at all; he might sleep in a sleeveless garment or stark
naked, he might eat and pray and meditate with his fellow monks
or live wholly by himself, as Agios did. In one colony which
Mark had visited the monks lived three in a cell and ate to-
gether, and slept sitting up but never lying down. By night they
wore a linen garment and by day a robe of sheepskin with a girdle
and hood. In most places a novice was supposed to undergo three
103years of probation, to determine, with austerities and hardships,
if he had the fortitude demanded of monks. In other places, as
over on the river, the monks based their way of life on their under-
standing of the last verse of Psalm 49, which said, Man that is
in honor and understandeth not is like the beasts that perish. In
an effort to recover their lost likeness to God these monks lived as
beasts, never rising from their hands and knees and living on grass
and insects. They were naked but for their long hair. After they
lost their teeth, Mark said, it was pathetic to see them trying to
eat.
While waiting for Hareb and Takuda, Mark and Helene would
sit in Zinara's cell and Mark would tell of the wonders with which
God marked his chosen ones. Above all, he liked the marvels that
had distinguished Anthony. One day the Lord spoke to this holy
man, saying, "Far away in the desert is a man who is more excel-
lent than you. With great joy you should go see him." So Anthony
had set out across the desert to find this one more holy than him-
self and he had walked a great distance in the fierce heat, yet saw
no one; but he kept telling himself, "God will not withdraw his
hand from me but will show me this servant, the holiest of men."
He walked again and kept walking until at last he saw a man who
possessed two natures, one half of him being man and the other
half a horse; and Anthony addressed him, saying, “I, a man of
God, wonder where you dwell around here." The creature an-
swered him in barbarous and impure words, with fear in his
mouth; and so Anthony went on his way, knowing that Satan had
taken this dreadful form to terrify him and force him to turn back.
Then after a while he saw another creature, standing on a rock;
and this one had horns on his forehead; and Anthony grasped the
helmet of faith and the shield of righteousness and said, "Who
are you?" and the creature replied, “I'm a mortal man but I'm a
satyr whom some worship as a god." And Anthony proceded on
his way, his tears flowing with joy and falling to the earth; and he
smote the earth with his staff and cried in a loud voice, "Woe to
Alexandria! Woe to the city of the heathen, wherein are all the
devils of creation!"
Then he went on his way, asking himself where he was to find
this holy man; and while he was meditating he observed on the
flat face of the desert the footprints of a beast that had passed that
day; and still he journeyed on. He then saw a huge hyena running
to gain a summit and he followed the creature and saw it enter
104a cave; and he looked into the cave and saw perfect love, for the
holy man Paul was there. Putting aside all fear Anthony looked
in again and saw that there was a great and brilliant light in the
cave; and he knocked at the entrance; but at once the old man
rolled a stone down and made shut the door. Anthony then be-
seeched him and the man asked, "Why have you come?" Anthony
said, “I am not worthy to see you, but since you receive beasts,
why abhor the children of men? I have sought and found you and
if you do not open the door I will die here." Then the holy man
opened the door and they kissed one another with holy kisses and
each told his name. Paul asked if there were still kings in the
world and if they lived in subjection to the Devil.
They looked out and saw a raven sitting on the branch of a
tree-
Abruptly, with a gasping cry, as though stricken, Helene stag-
gered up and rushed to the doorway. Alarmed, Mark went to
her, his eyes anxiously searching her face.
"I can't listen to any more!" she gasped. "Please!”
"About the saints?" he asked, puzzled and a little astonished.
"I was just going to tell you how a raven brought them a loaf of
bread."
"I don't want to hear it!"
Looking at her more sharply he thought that she seemed to be
in pain. She was breathing hard. Her eyes looked wild.
"I won't tell you more now," he said.
"Or ever! These igorant men!"
Ignorant? Mark wondered about that. Well yes, it was true
that Anthony, holiest of all the saints, could neither read nor write
and as a child refused to go to school at all. Still breathing hard,
Helene turned her stricken eyes to look at him, her own eyes ex-
pressing only incredulity and pity. And did he think that one
who could neither read nor write was the greatest of men?
Mark was a little nettled. She talked, he said, like a wicked
woman—for were not greater marvels told in the holy scriptures of
the Jews? Had she ever heard about Samson and Jonah and Dan-
iel and Elijah? If these tales were true, did she doubt that a raven
daily brought a loaf of bread to the two holy men, or that a vir-
gin with a demon in her-
"Mark, please! If you must believe that everything good and
sweet in life is from Satan-
“It is,” Mark said gravely. “With blandishments and entice-
105ments, sweet scents, wines, rich foods he seduces us into sin. The
Devil-"
"O God!" she cried, striking an arm across her forehead. "I
must not smell a rose, for that is Satan! I must not listen to the
song of birds, for that is Satan! I must not want to lie with David,
so that I may hold a child in my arms. That is Satan! If I am to
be a good woman I'll sit in hot sands and my own stink and die of
thirst! I will wall me around with stones! I will never bathe, so
that I may smell like a privy! I drink only stale water and eat
only bread six months old! I—”
"Helene? Listen-"
"Shut up! I'll flee from the orchard to the home of the jackal!
I'll flee from the nursery to watch an idiot stand on one leg! No,
no!"
"If we are wicked-"
"All right, I'm wicked!" She left the cell and walked away in
the sand, with Mark following her. Flinging her arms to the sky
she said, "Because I like sweet and lovely things I am wicked! Then
all women are wicked. And as for you men," she said, turning to
look at him, “you wouldn't like sweet and tender things, even if
you knew they were from heaven. See that Agios!" she said, point-
ing out. "While he was standing on one foot there was a blissful
look on his face, as if he were copulating! What is this? What is
it in you men?"
"We seek godliness,” Mark said.
She snorted at him. She hissed and sneered at him. She wanted to
pick up a handful of sand and fling it into his face. “All right,
Agios out there on one leg is the image of God. I'll take the rose
and the lily, the figtree in fruit, the rainbow in a mist, a mother
nursing her child." She looked out into the desert and then swung
and went swiftly to the cell. During all the time Mark had sat
there and talked Zinara had not once looked up. Now she looked
up and straight into Helene's eyes, and what Helene saw in
Zinara's eyes she had never seen in human eyes before. It was only
for a moment. Then Zinara's gaze fell and she was busy with the
needle.
That night when the two women were alone Helene said:
"Don't you ever talk to anyone? Where is your child?" There
was no response. "Why do you drink water that these other women
wash in? There's plenty in the well. . . .Will you tell me why?"
There was no response. "Zinara, look, let's be friends. I'm going
106mad out here . . . .or would you prefer that I do not come here
at all?" In her abrupt impetuous way Helene dropped to her
knees before the woman and shook her. "In God's name speak!
Have you no tongue?" Weak with rage she stared at Zinara a
few moments and left the cell.
The next afternoon she slipped away, she thought unobserved,
to go out into the sands to be alone and decide what she ought
to do or was able to do. If she had had the money to travel she
would have tried to find David, but Mark would give her no
money; she was welcome to his food, and if she wanted garments
he would buy them for her; but he told her she ought to remain
here awhile. She acted like a very wicked woman, he said. She
had laughed at that and mocked him. She had tried to feel wicked.
She had been forced into a brothel to share the shame and vice of
men but she did not feel sinful. She did not feel that she ought to
ask God for forgiveness. What was there to forgive? She was not
able to weep or supplicate, and when she prayed her prayers were
only a woman's things that she told God because she had nobody
else to tell them to.
When they were coming over on the boat Mark had given her a
piece of parchment on which words were written. She drew it
from inside her girdle and standing alone in the sands read the
words again: "Satyrs, priests of Bacchus, in the paintings and
statues, have the phallus erect, as the symbol of the divine per-
formance. It is also a form of disease, in which the patient has
erection of the genital organ, the appellation of satyriasis being
derived from its resemblance to the figure of the god. It is an un-
restrainable impulse to connection; but neither are they all re-
lieved by these embraces, nor is the tentigo soothed by many and
repeated acts of sexual intercourse. Spasms of all the nerves, and
tension of all the tendons, groins, and perineum, inflammation
and pain of the genital parts, redness of countenance, and a dewy
moisture. Wrapped up in silent sorrow, they are stupid, as if
grievously afflicted with their calamity. But if the affection over-
come the patient's sense of shame, he will lose all restraint of
tongue as regards obscenity, and likewise all restraint in regard to
the open performance of the act. Raving with his obscene imagi-
nation, he cannot contain himself; and the foam sits on his lips
as in a lascivious goat, and he has a smell like that animal."
These words, Mark had said, were from a famous medical book
by one named Aretaeus. He asked her to read them and study
107them. She had read them until she almost knew them by heart
but she understood their meaning only vaguely, if at all. Or in
any case she did not grasp the deeper meaning which Mark had
Isaid was there. Mark had told her that unbridled sexual inter-
course was the world's disease among all but Christians, and was
too common even there; and that the thousands of monks fleeing
to the deserts to wrestle with their satyriasis and the sin of Adam
were the only possible hope the world had. As the learned book
said, the impulse to copulate was becoming unrestrainable and
loosened tongues to the vilest obscenities and compelled embrace
in public places. But was it true that men were so completely in
the power of their lusts? Was their ardor becoming so uncontroll-
able that they were getting foam on their lips, as in a goat, most
ardent of all beasts, and taking on its odor?
Apollo, Mark had said, had the same odor, that of a goat's front
feet; and on his lips the same lascivious foam. He had a continu-
ous erection, which sometimes was visible under his cloak. He was
a satyr. Bemused, she was standing very still, thinking about these
things, when a low voice spoke at her ear. Stifling a scream she
swung and looked into the bland knowing eyes of Apollo.
"Well, my pretty one, here you are."
"I'm not your pretty one."
"But a pretty one, just the same, even in sands under the sun.
I saw you reading something as I approached. What was it?"
She put a palm over her nose and sniffed but she did not know
what the front feet of a goat smelled like. She met his eyes again,
her own mocking. "Do you have spasms?"
"What?" he said. "Helene, why have you been avoiding me?"
"For the simple reason that I don't like you."
"I had thought it might be that," he said, his low laugh soft and
casual and insolent.
"Do you have tension of the perineum?"
"Tension of what?"
"Do you have unrestrainable impulses?"
"Sometimes."
"A red countenance and dewy moisture?"
"Just what are you talking about?”
"Are you afflicted with a calamity?”
"Well yes, I am. I have had the calamity every man has had
since Eve revealed her beauty to Adam."
His words annoyed her. "You're an awful hypocrite, aren't you?"
108"In a way, I suppose."
"Why do you pretend to be a saint?"
"Only a very minor one."
"What were you before you came here?"
"A scholar and a gentleman."
She was still discreetly sniffing, "Why did you come here?"
"I have a sister here."
“Oh? I hadn't known. But do men usually follow their sisters?"
"Let's sit," he said, sinking to the sands. At a few feet from him
she sat and looking at him saw that his profile was good. The
breeze was moving across him and then to her and she thought
she would be able to make out the odor, if there was one. "I
am very devoted to my sister," he said at last, speaking out to the
sky. "She is a strange girl who sometimes has visions and is very
irresponsible. I want to be near her to see that she is all right.'
That sounded manly and decent but Helene did not believe a
word of it. "Is your sister with Mother Ida?"
"Yes."
"What is her name?”
"Julia."
""
"How old is she?" asked Helene, studying his face all the while,
trying to read his mind.
"Oh, about sixteen, I guess."
"Beautiful?"
"Quite beautiful-but not as beautiful as you."
"And you pretend to be a saint?”
"Yes, in my way. You see, Helene, there are many things that
women don't understand. If that is not so— -I mean," he said, with
a low laugh, "if women are not weak in their understanding, why
has God always given his revelations to men? It is because women
could never understand them. That's why Paul commanded
women to be silent in churches, for to speak is only to reveal their
ignorance."
"You have quite a way of wooing a woman, haven't you.”
He smiled, showing his strong well-scrubbed teeth. "Do you
deny that God endowed men with superior understanding?"
"Of men, you mean?"
He laughed softly. "You women like to think that you know
men better than men know men. You may know their weaknesses
better, not their strengths. Men understand things of the mind,
women only of the emotions."
109"You're horribly fascinating," she said.
He smiled again. "Which church do you belong to?"
"None. Which do you?"
"That is what I want to tell you. Only one can have the truth.
You admit that?"
"Not at all. I don't think God is so stingy with truth that he
gives it only to one!"
"Only one," said Apollo, gently, firmly. "We had a great pro-
phet two hundred years ago. God revealed to him certain truths.
For instance, only those who sink deep into sin can find salvation."
"I've heard all that. You're a Manichaean.”
"Yes. Our prophet also told how women can be saved. You
know of course that it is much harder for a woman to save her
soul than for a man. Her sin is greater."
"In that case we are already saved." With one hand he was
doodling in the sands. It was a strong hand, with long tapering
fingers.
"No," he said. "Woman's sin is the original sin. That is what
damns her. Man had sin forced on him and now must sink deep to
save himself. Why do you think Jesus descended into hell before
he rose to heaven? To symbolize for us what I am telling you."
"You bore me utterly," she said. "I suppose you have some
purpose behind all this."
He was silent a few moments, as though offended. Then,
smiling, he said: "There's a learned bishop in Alexandria. He
says if man is to attain to godhood, then God must become man.
If God were to become man, would man then dare to occupy his
present position? No, he must sink lower."
"I don't know," she said wearily, "what you're driving at.
Possibly you have a point somewhere."
"I have," he said, and for a moment looked shockingly lecherous.
"Do you believe that if a holy man lays hands on a sick person it
helps him?"
"Some Chrisitans believe that."
"All right. The touch of a holy man is good. If his touch is
good for a woman then sexual intercourse with her is better."
She could hardly believe her ears. She looked into his eyes
and saw there what she had expected to see. "I suppose you mean,"
she said, holding his gaze, "that you want to make me a pure
woman by seducing me."
"I would sanctify you," he said.
110O Lord! She didn't know whether to laugh in his face or rise
and leave him. She would have been aghast if she could have
looked far into the future and known that this belief, which she
found so odious and silly, would then be held by many priests.
"You would santify me?" she said, raising her brows at him. “You
men are so holy that your touch makes us well, and your embrace
glorifies us! Two things I have learned about men. One is your
colossal gall. The other-"
"You speak like an ignorant woman," he said blandly.
"And you like Satan himself."
He shifted his approach. He said that when two Romans
married many gods were present to help consummate the marriage,
to see that the groom was manly and able. Why was Priapus there?
"I'm not curious," she said and made a move to rise and go.
She looked toward the cells, wishing that Mark would come. But
Mark, the sly fellow, had probably hidden somewhere to watch
her. He seemed to have no doubt that this scoundrel named Apollo
would seduce her.
"Do you know," the scoundrel was saying, "that I am the living
image of Priapus? Do you know that many Christians believe that
the erect staff is a symbol of godhood? If that is true, then I am
godlike."
It took a few moments for his incredible meaning to sink into

glance and smiled.
"Shall I show you?"
"Are you insane?" She struggled to her feet.
"I will some day,” he said, speaking as one who usually had his
way with women. "You won't be able to resist me."
Looking down at him she felt a sense of unreality. She won-
dered if she was actually here with this man or in Zinara's cell,

"No woman has ever resisted me," he said.
She moved away from him, looking at him with mounting in-

"I must go now," she said. "I wanted to talk with someone--'
"You're afraid of me, aren't you?"
"Is there simply no limit to your love of yourself?"
"Helene, God created the earth, the heavens and the universe.
111You don't dispute that if you're a Christian. He created man in
his image. If you're a Christian you believe that also. Men per-
form great wonders. But have you ever heard of a miracle per-
formed by a woman? Not one. An inferior thing, to save itself

She was looking at him, trying to understand him, trying to

"You are afraid of me."
Sucking in a deep breath, her eyes flashing, she said: "I under-

“Oh, by the earth and the heavens I won't listen to that any

She moved to go and Apollo stepped forward to restrain her.

She was not sure that she understood him. She stopped and

"You will give your soul to Satan for me? Is that what you

"Yes."
"Then let me tell you something. Make your pact but you will
112never have me, never, not in all the years of God's world! Not
if you have the power of Satan and all of hell with you!"

of a woman's passion by the way she walks. Yes, and her weak-

113XII
Helene went to Zinara's cell and entered and sank by a wall, ter-
ribly agitated, with blood pounding in her ears. She was afraid
and she hated herself for being afraid. She had heard of men who
made pacts with Satan; she had heard that if a man pledged his
soul to the Devil, then the Prince of Hell assisted the man with all
the power he had. Could she, a mere woman, resist such power as
that? I will, she thought, I will, so help me God! And if God did
not help her, then she would make a pact with Satan and she would
destroy this monster. But how did one make such a pact? She
did not know. She had heard that one had to seek out a Jew.
She had heard that the Jews, having been abandoned, were little
better than the imps. But she had known Jews and she had
thought them better men and women than many Christians.
""
Bowing and weeping she tried to pray but choked. Then she
murmured, "David, please come! . . . For what could she do
against a man who would enlist all the dreadful powers and
stratagems of hell! Should she flee?-and if she did, would he
follow? Should she tell Mark? Should she tell Hareb when he
came? Or should she make this her own private and solitary
fight against wickedness? She did not doubt nor did any Christian
in the world doubt the powers of Satan or that men could make
pacts with him. But she would wait a little while. Apollo would
not act at once and she would wait for his next move.
Hareb and Takuda came the next day, Hareb striding far in
advance of her, his fierce burning eyes annoucing that he had come
to wrestle with the Devil; and Takuda meekly trailing him a
hundred yards, gaunt, dusty, weary and sad. The people here
and monks in a colony a few miles south had heard that a mighty
114one was coming, who in his prowess against evil and in his zeal
to be holy would concede to no man. The interest aroused was
comparable to that in a city on the appearance of a carnival or a
religious festival; and though some maintained the strict silence
of their orders, others gossiped with one another and speculated
on the wonders that Hareb might perform.
The power of God was in him, Mark had said, gleefully whet-
ting the interest. He would make Agios look like a clown with a
red nose. But would he perform such wonders as Anthony?
Would he see the prophets and apostles come down to earth?
Would the savage lion come up and lick his hand, the raven bring
him food, the lizard speak to him with a human voice? “We shall
see," Mark said.
A holy man second to none on earth was what Hareb resolved
to be. On long strides he came in from the wilderness, burned
and blackened, all his muscles like sinews, his mind stored with
prayers and holy words. He brought with him a flat stale loaf
of bread, an inch thick, a foot wide and two feet long, and a stone
cup out of which to drink. That was all. That was all except one
ragged garment on his back. He stopped by the ruins of the wall,
where Helene had first seen Agios, and looked around him, his
scowling countenance turning this way and that. Standing by
Zinara's cell Helene had seen him but did not dare to go meet
him; but when she saw Takuda coming she ran to embrace her.
"O Takuda!" she gasped, throwing arms around her and kissing
her. "Have you heard from David?"
Takuda licked her hot dusty lips. "No."
"Did Hareb have trouble in the desert?"
"He doesn't speak to me." She clutched a hand to her breast
and looked round her with tired inflamed eyes. "Is this where
they live?"
"Some of them."
Takuda managed a wan smile. "It looks God-for-saken enough."
"Come," said Helene, "come over here where you can rest. I'll
bring you water." She led the exhausted woman to Zinara's cell
and helped her sink by a wall. Then she took her own pouch
and went to the well. It was only a hole scooped out of the sands
and walled up with stones. Lizards fell into it and other small
beasts, and insects from earth and sky; its water was brackish and
had a bad odor; but the people here did not seem to mind. It
115was the kind of water, Mark said, that you would expect to find
in demonland.
She returned to Takuda with the water and the woman drank
and wiped her lips and drew a deep breath of weariness. She
put her head back to the stone and closed her eyes. Hareb mean-
while had stripped off his garment and stood naked; for had not
Saint Hilarion told him that it was best to go without clothes?
Mark had come over to tell him about Agios's phenomenal feats
but was silenced when his glance fell on Hareb's private parts.
He then looked at the man's nose, which was abnormally large,
and at his fierce burning eyes; and he reflected that here indeed
was a man who had great need to wrestle with Satan. With another
startled glance downward he told Hareb that Agios at this mo-
ment was standing, stooped over, with enough stones suspended
from his neck to fell an ox.
Hareb grunted. He thought such austerities childish and did
not intend to compete with any man in supporting burdens of
stone or by standing in mosquito bogs to be eaten alive. He
would never stand on one leg on top of a pole or on his head
in a basin of mud; he would not stir up wasps until they had
punctured him with a thousand needles; nor drag his tongue
out of his mouth and down across his chin, to fix it there with
a string around his neck and leave it exposed until it almost
rotted; nor sit with one foot over his neck until he was paralyzed;
nor put stinging nettles and thornpods in his armpits and between
his thighs and buttocks; nor stand for forty days and nights pray-
ing and weeping. In Hareb's opinion, if a man were to become
godlike he would have to attack the thing that was once pure
and now offensive.
Did a fool think to wrestle with a demon by standing on his
head? Would he overthrow the power of Satan by going around
like an idiot with his tongue out? You had to grant that the most
obvious force in the world was that of evil. You had to grant that
evil poured most mightily in those channels where human pas-
sions ran deepest. Granting these, you had to admit that the
attack on evil should be on that part of the body most susceptible
of blissful sensations. In himself that was where he would attack.
Did not the holy Father Origen attack it there and cut it off?--
and did not Jesus say that there were men who had made them-
selves eunuchs for the kingdom of heaven's sake?
These things he explained to Mark, who listened patiently, his
116glance now and then turning downward; and who then begged
Hareb to go with him to see the mighty Agios.
"This fool with the stones hung round his neck?"
"He knows that you are coming—”
"Does he think that by hanging stones round his neck he can
overthrow the seat of lust? Why not from his scrotum?"
"Hah!” cried Mark, delighted. "I will tell him that.”
Such men were fools, said Hareb. They were not illuminated.
What did they ever do that was holy and beloved of God? Had
not one of the Fathers said that woman was the gateway to hell?—
and what was it that entered the gate?
"I begin to understand you," said Mark, again looking down.
"Isn't it the eunuch who is most godlike?"
"Oh, but our Father is no eunuch!"
"What did Jesus say?” asked Hareb, as though speaking to a
multitude. "It's there in Matthew, the nineteenth chapter and
the twelfth verse. There are eunuchs who have made themselves
eunuchs for the kingdom of heaven's sake. He that is able to
receive it, let him receive it. He who is not able," said Hareb
contemptuously, "can hang stones from his neck." Now, with
the outside of his hard right hand, he struck a blow across his
staff but did not look down.
Mark supposed he had felt some pleasurable sensation there.
He smiled. He was immensely enjoying Hareb. But he was
startled when he heard a gasp behind him and turned to find
Helene covering her eyes. She had come up behind Mark to hear
what Hareb was saying, and without knowing that Hareb was
naked; and when she looked round Mark-
"Hilarion said the saints should go naked," Mark told her,
noting the crimson face and her confusion. "You'll get used to it."
Paying no attention to Helene, Hareb went on, fixing Mark with
his fierce eyes and speaking in the tones of a prophet. Mark, the
sly fellow, was teasing him and leading him on.
"Agios says we should mortify the flesh, the whole body, not just
one part."
"The man is a fool. If you castrate a bull what have you? You
still have his horns, his thick neck, his bulging eyes but you don't
have a bull."
"That's true," Mark said. "But the best way to reduce our
passions is to eat very little.”
"There are better ways. Go read Matthew."
117"But is it good not to bathe and to wallow in mud and cover
the body with dust and filth?”
The body was vile, Hareb said, and it was all right to destroy
it; but when the seat of passion was destroyed, when the pouch
was empty and the stones gone, then the flesh would shrink on the
bones and waste away. Did the salted field bear its increase? Did
the girdled tree bear its fruit?
"But the castrated bull grows fat. Hareb, tell me this: can a
man make a pact with Satan and seduce a woman against her will?”’
To lie with a woman, Hareb said, a man did not usually have
to make a pact with Satan. All man-woman love was under his
control. The pact was made in Eden.
"But if a man can't win the woman and then pledges his soul
to Satan, will he then have power over her?"
Yes, indeed, if more power was needed, but when since Eve had
it been needed? Women were so wholly the instruments of Satan
that they had to surrender, if only the man would persist. What
else could they do?
"You think that is true of all women? Are there no exceptions?"
"None. How can it be otherwise, since all sexual passion is in
Satan, and women since Eve have been his instruments?"
"There is a saint here determined to seduce Helene. Can she
resist him?"
"No. She can't even wish to."
"Well, come with me to Agios, to tell me what manner of man
he is, whether of God or Devil."
"Where is this fool?" asked Hareb, scowling round him.
"Out in the desert, but not far."
Hareb alowed himself to be prevailed on, though he growled
with protests; but he was in fact curious, for Mark had told him
great wonders about Agios, as well as about Anthony and others,
all of whom Hareb was eager to see and to measure by his own
yardstick of holiness. They walked together; and Helene, seething
with bitter emotions, saw them go. If men spoke the truth when
they said that women were born in the power of Satan, what hope
was there for them? They might as well all be whores! Look at
the fool out there, stark naked and convinced that in nakedness
he was holier, because Adam was naked in the Garden! What
would Mother Ida think when she saw him?
As the two men approached Agios Hareb saw that it was indeed.
true, that this man was standing bent over, with a great burden of
118stones suspended from his neck. He was not supporting himself
with hands propped on knees but was bearing the whole weight
with his neck and back. Hareb walked round and round the man.
"How long has he stood thus?"
"All night at least,” said Mark, “and today as you see him. He
is a mighty soldier of our Lord. Taking only a little water daily
and a little stale bread he has been here at least two years, des-
troying his body with these torments."
"Is he a eunuch?"
Startled, Mark glanced at Hareb's eyes and then downward and
away. "I don't think so. For if he were would his passions still dis-
turb him?"
"Is this the one called Agios?"
"This is Agios," said Mark, with some pride. "His fame has
spread over all the desert, for no man can endure what he endures.
He is the one the wasps stung until those who had known him
could recognize him only by his voice. He is the one who has
buried himself in sands to his neck, and will again. He never lies
down to sleep but stands, propped against a wall. No saint has yet
matched this saint."
"Of what use is it?" asked Hareb scornfully. "He still has his
passions. If the passions are in his body when he dies his soul
won't be able to leave it. He'll be doomed. Tell him to hang the
stones to his scrotum."
"Agios, do you hear?"
There had been no movement in Agios: like a plant of the
desert with his feet in the earth he stood there, immobile, patiently
enduring the torture. Hareb thought the man a dreadful fool
but he had to admire him for all that: one who could stand day
and night in this position certainly abashed and disconcerted the
Devil.
"I will tell him later," Mark said and walked with Hareb back
to the cells. Hareb was telling him that Satan was a master of snares
and deceits and stratagems, that some Christians who thought
themselves secure were still in his power. As an instance there was
any man who tormented himself with austerities and ordeals, yet
kept his passions alive and clamorous, as Agios was doing. When
the man saw Helene did his staff rise? Mark said he did not know—
and he looked curiously at Hareb in profile, wondering about him.
Well, the degree of holiness, Hareb said, was exactly in proportion
to the absence of passion.
119“Then I am holier than you," said Mark. "But I'm the sort of
poor
devil who can never be very good or very bad. It takes a lot
of wickedness to make a saint. . . .”
Helene had gone into Zinara's cell to sit, feeling terribly dis-
pirited and hopeless; but she peered out when the men returned, to
be sure that Hareb was naked and to wonder if he imagined that
he could live here naked among nuns. As if to settle this point for
her Hareb told Mark that he would go to see what Mother Ida was
doing. A mother superior needed a man to supervise her and
keep her on the path.
With both Mark and Helene staring after him Hareb went to
the door where the nuns lived and called, and in a few moments
Ida appeared. He took one swift glance at this gaunt flatchested
sallow woman, with cold eyes like those of marshbird, and said,
while dropping his hands to cover his parts, “I'm Saint Hareb”-
for he was already thinking of himself as a saint. He gazed at her
with his deep brooding eyes, and Ida was impressed, not alone by
his eyes and his arrogant speech but by his wild and savage beard
that set off his strange pallor. She had not observed that he was
unclothed. She had not looked down.
"I'm glad you came," she said, her voice brisk and incisive. "I'm
sure one of our nuns has a demon in her.”
"What are the signs?"
"Her belly is swelling."
Hareb was not, like many Christian leaders, a specialist in the
signs of demon-possession. Satan in this, as in all his evil machina-
tions, was wondrously cunning: a woman with a demon in her
might swell or she might shriek and have fits or she might take
long walks in her sleep or she might go down on hands and knees
in the wantonness of beasts or she might simply sit and weep all the
time. Since tears were holy it was no wonder that Satan used them
to deceive.
"Bring her here," said Hareb.
Ida went away and returned with a girl who was little more than
a child. Her name, Ida said, was Julia; she was the daughter of a
monk here named Apollo. The girl was shrinking from Hareb's
stern gaze and naked form. She was terrified.
Ida was right, Hareb said; this girl had a demon in her. “Julia,
when did this demon enter you?"
The child tried to speak but could only gasp and, writhing, try
to slip away.
120"How did the demon enter?" Hareb wondered aloud. Had she
felt a strange tickling in her nose or ears or throat? Had she-?
The subject was very indelicate.
"She has been vomiting,” said Ida, looking at the poor shrinking
creature.
Well, yes, of course she would vomit, Hareb reflected; once a
demon was in a person he agitated and tormented him, making
him sick and enfeebled.
"If you're a holy man," said Ida, "you can drive the demon out."
Hareb had never tried to expel a demon. Jesus, his Lord, had
expelled many; he tried to recall the words Jesus had used. It
seemed to him that Jesus had said simply, Demon, begone! "Look
at my eyes," Hareb said to the girl; and when her timid gaze rose,
trying to meet his, he made the sign of the cross and shouted,
"Demon, begone! In the name of the Father and the Son and the
Holy Spirit, leave this girl at once!"
Ida was staring hard. "Do you feel better?"
Julia gasped and they thought she said yes.
"Did you feel the demon leave you?"
Again, tortured, gasping, she tried to speak.
"She'll be all right now," Hareb said. "Do you nuns sing the
psalms and pray and meditate?"
"Oh yes."
"Do they " But did it matter much what women did? What
hope was there for them? Some, like Ida, might inherit a lesser
glory, such as the first of the seven heavens, the one nearest the
earth. Hareb had turned away, feeling troubled; he was not sure
that he was yet holy enough to expel demons and heal the sick.
Some day he would be. Some day he would be the greatest of all
the holy men and his fame would encompass the earth. No other
would be so humble. No other . . . Suddenly he was appalled: the
vision of Julia, a lovely girl, with a luscious soft feminine delicacy,
had brought his staff erect; and he had a sense of what an absurd
thing it was for a saint to be walking naked with his organ up. He
smote it once with the outside edge of his hand. He stopped and
closed his eyes and prayed. He prayed for strength to subdue and
conquer his passions and expel lustful thoughts from his mind.
He prayed for help to make himself pure, as Adam had been pure—
for who could imagine that Adam had an erection before Eve
showed off her allurements? When the prayer was finished he
121drew a heavy breath and opened his eyes and glanced down.
The staff had sunk. He felt suffused with light.
He then became aware that Mark had come up and was looking
at him. With considerable astonishment Mark had been looking
at him from the moment he left Ida's door.
"Mark," said Hareb, sounding angry, “do you pray?”
"Quite a lot," said Mark, smiling.
"If you ever become a saint your whole life will be a prayer.”
"Yes," said Mark, gladly assenting. "But in the next life I'll
probably be where all the women are."
"The greatest saints will be with God."
"And no women there?"
"None."
"I'll be with the women," Mark said, studying Hareb's eyes.
"One of the sisters," said Hareb, "had a demon in her. Her
belly was swollen."
Mark's smile became sly and knowing. “A year ago a sister was
thought to have a demon in her but it turned out to be a baby.”
"Baby?" said Hareb, lowering his brows at him. "How could
that be when there are only saints here?”
Mark's smile broadened. "You'd better ask the saints and you
might begin with Apollo.”
122XIV
Hareb said he would build his own cell but first he spent a few days
exploring the desert roundabout, looking for the plant that had
the odor of a woman in heat. He found not the one he looked for
but one like it and he hid a few sprigs of it in the stone ruins and
now and then sniffed them while thinking of Helene or Julia. A
part of the old wall which still stood, with stones scattered at its
base, was two hundred yards from the building that housed the
nuns and a little more than that distance from the nearest cell.
Hareb decided to erect his own prison not far from the wall.
Most of the stones were too heavy for a man to lift but by going
to his knees Hareb could roll a stone over and over; and like a
huge tumblebug he toiled alone in the heat. With his hands he
scooped out a trench on three sides of a square and rolled stones
into it to serve as a foundation. He then piled sand against the
stones inside and out to make them firm. He made a mortar of
sand and water and powdered desert plants and used his hands as
a trowel.
At some distance Mark would sit with Takuda to watch him.
It was quite a job, said Mark, to put a roof on that would not fall,
for it had to be arched and the walls had to be firm enough to
support an arch. Would Hareb be able to do it? One monk had
built a cell and the roof had fallen in and almost killed him.
Now Agios was a mason and he knew how to do these things but
Hareb had said to hell with Agios. Look at the thing, Mark said.
Hareb was so clumsy and fumbling at this kind of work that the
walls he was erecting were out of plumb and ungainly, and as
likely as not they would all fall down.
123"If they do," said Takuda, "he'll put them up again. He has
nothing else to do."
"He should build a doorway but he's going to leave a whole
wall out. The two walls at that end will spread when the roof
goes on."
"He's having fun," Takuda said.
Sometimes Helene came out to sit with them. "Do you think
the roof will fall down and kill him?" Mark asked her.
"I don't care," she said. "Let's go to Antioch."
"What for? To be burned alive?”
"Maybe the persecution has stopped."
"It will never stop."
"You mean we'll be persecuted forever? Why?"
"Because other people don't like us. Besides," he said, giving
her a sly look, "the martyrs inherit the greatest glory. We should
all be martyrs."
"Men want that. Women don't."
"Some women do," said Takuda, thinking of Soulai.
Looking round her Helene said: "What is this but martyrdom?
-dying out here in the heat and sand!"
"It's better than being burned alive," Mark said.
"Oh, I don't know. That's terrible but brief. This goes on and
on until you can't stand your stink or anything and you go crazy."
"A person can get used to about anything," Takuda said.
"All last night," said Helene, speaking with passion, “I heard
birds singing. But only the nuns sing here and they sound sad and
mournful."
"All the psalms are sad," Mark said.
"Why are Jews such a sad people?”
"Because God is angry at them."
"He must be angry at us for he allows the whole world to per-
secute us."
She arose and went back to Zinara's cell. She sank by a wall and
looked at Zinara, at this woman who did nothing but sew and
weep; and when she could stand it no longer she cried, "Zinara!"
There was no response. She then screamed, “Zinara, you fool,
you idiot! Speak to me!"
Zinara looked up and choked back a sob.
"Great God, why don't you say anything?"
"I've nothing to say."
"Do you intend to sit there and weep until you die? . . .Answer
124me: do you? How do you stand this God-forsaken loneliness, this
silence, this heat? Where is your child? Don't you ever want
to see your child again? Don't you—”
Helene began to weep. Weeping was very unusual with her.
Now she wept because she felt that she was going mad and she
could not endure the silence or the nuns whose dismal chantings
filled the evenings or Mark's eternal and cheerful smile or Bion
shaking with sentimental woe and sucking wine through his reed
or the sad pathetic lonely terrified face of his son or the black
scowling face of Hareb or Agios out in the desert. Lord, O Lord!
Mark had told her that some of the monks and nuns lost their
senses and went babbling off into the wilderness to get lost and
die and be eaten by jackals. Now and then one killed himself.
She could understand that. You had to go mad or kill yourself
or make a pact with Satan, for this was Satan's dominion, this
desert of demons, and you were in his power and your will dis-
solved in this heat and silence and after a while you would be
like these other persons and you would never have the strength to
go away. Lord, O Lord!
"Zinara," she said, sobbing, "let's go now! Let's go to the city!"
Zinara's hands were busy, her head bowed. “Zinara, will you?
O by the living God!" Helene gasped and struggled up. "Look!”
she said, dashing the back of a hand across her cheeks. “I weep
but there are no tears! You weep and there are no tears! Only
Bion has tears, because he drinks wine! The rest of us have no
water in us and we weep dry like a corpse! God! We eat so little
we have no need to go to a privy, even if we had a privy! No, we
squat in the sand like beasts and call it holiness, this loneliness and
filth and stink and eternal sobbing with no tears! O God, how I
hate it!" She rushed over and seized Zinara by her hair and spilled
her forward. "Get up!" she shouted. "If you're a human being
get up!" Zinara struggled to her feet and stood, head bowed, hands
silent.
Then Helene felt an awful revulsion of shame and horror. She
was trying to get control of her seething emotions when the door-
way darkened and Mark looked in.
"Is there trouble?" he asked anxiously. "I heard shouting."
Helene laughed, a wild bitter mirthless laugh. "Go away!" she
hissed at him. "I hate men and I hate you with your damned face
always smiling!"
Then, suddenly, there was the face of Mother Ida. "What is
125it?" she asked, looking first at Helene and then at Mark. Zinara
was still standing, head bowed, her lips moving in prayer.
"Men!" Helene shrieked, spitting the word at Ida. "Men did
this to you! They would do it to me! Men, you gateway to hell,
you deep trench!" She advanced on Ida, bending forward, her
eyes hating her and her voice sneering. "Man-woman love is
Satan's business! Had you heard? An innocent baby is born out of
vice, isn't it? Did you know? The Fathers say we are daughters
of wickedness, don't they?—and you, you sanctimonious idiot,
stand there and believe it!" She was still forcing her back. She
was hissing into her face and with all her strength she was re-
straining her impulse to attack this woman and rend her. "We
stained their chastity, didn't we?—their God damned precious
chastity! And you believe it! They say because of us the son of
God had to die and you believe it! You awful long-nosed virgin!
You unspeakable fraud! You won't let Zinara and me associate
with your nuns, will you? No, we have to sit out in this damned
foul lonely hellhole of a place!" She was still backing her, her
own body thrust forward, fire flashing from her eyes and venom
spitting from her tongue.
"This woman," Ida gasped to Mark, "has a demon in her."
"Ohhh!" Helene breathed, appalled and furious. "A demon
in me? In me!" Aroused to dreadful wrath she moved close to
Ida, breathing into her face, her eyes burning upon the woman's
eyes, her voice between clenched teeth saying, “You have a nun
with a demon in her, haven't you? It's a baby, you unspeakable
fool!"
"Stay back!" Ida cried, alarmed. "Don't you touch me!”
"I wouldn't touch you for the kingdom of heaven! I wouldn't
step across the threshold of that damned whorehouse of virgins you
live in-" She broke off, exhausted by her passions.
Ida went over to Mark. "There's a demon in her," she said.
"Bring Hareb."
Helene cried: "Don't you bring that naked baboon over to me!"
Again she rushed on Ida. "He goes to see you and the nuns, doesn't
he? Yes, with his thing standing up!"
"Ohh!" Ida gasped, and backed away, appalled.
Mark had lost his smile. He was looking at Helene.
She now swung to him. “Well, don't stand there gawking like
a halfwit! You heard her say there's a demon in me. Give me
money and I'll go away."
126"No," said Mark, shaking his head sadly, looking at her with
wide troubled blue eyes. He was fond of Helene but he had not
known that she could be so violent. He supposed there must be
a demon in her. "You must stay with us, Helene, and purify your
soul."
“If I must stay here I promise you one thing—and may God
strike me if I lie. I'll make a pact with Satan."
"May God forgive you such words!"
"I'll make a pact with Satan and I'll become a vampire-the
night demoness. Ahh! I'll torment you men till you all go mad.
By God, if you men say we are wicked and the daughters of Satan
then we'll be the daughters of Satan and destroy you." She had
drawn close and was looking deep into his eyes. She was sneering
contemptuously at the alarm in his face. "I'm telling you, Mark,
that if you Christian men can think no better of us the time will
come when we'll have you in our power. And do you know what
we'll do?"
"What?" he asked, fascinated by the passion in her face.
"Someday in the future, I mean."
"You'll do what?"
"We'll make you our slaves."
"You've already done it," said Mark solemnly. "By your sin-"
"We'll make you our physical slaves. We'll hitch you to chari-
ots like horses. We'll put you on a leash like dogs and accept your
embrace only when we want it—and we'll never want it. We won't
go on forever being degraded by these images of God who cut
their damned balls off!"
"But it's not our fault that Eve seduced Adam."
"Oh, to hell with Adam! What was he? A simpleton, a beetle-
head, a popeyed boy. Did he have to be seduced? Didn't he have
any sense? All other males go sniffing around as if the world is
chiefly vaginas.”
"Look," said Mark, "what set you off this way?"
"Ohhh!" she said, putting a hand to her forehead. "Why, all
the beautiful trees around me and the running water and the
birds singing and the sound of children playing and the woman
sitting by the window waiting for her husband. The smell of
roses in the air and of olive trees and hawthorn in bloom, lilies
in the field, cyclamen in the hedges, the sound of people laughing,
gay people, people having fun, and a goblet of wine with bubbles
bursting and fresh bread with butter on it and fig preserves and
127a soft bed under the night sounds and the music of flowing water
and a man with his arm under me, saying he loves me!"
She went on breathlessly and Mark waited. Then he said: "I
love you."
"Then you are sinful."
“I am,” he said. "I suppose I'll always be."
"Love! You men don't know what it is. You want to be in-
timate with me but that isn't love. If you loved me you'd take me
away from here."
"If I took you away you'd leave me. You love David."
"Then do you say, I will give you love if you'll give me equal
measure."
"Let's go over there," said Mark, "and talk."
They went off a little distance and sat in the sand. Helene
pulled a foot up and slipped the sandal off and probed between her
toes to cleanse them. She brushed sand off her foot and ankle
and pulled her garment up to expose part of one leg. “Look, I'm
filthy. No man could love a filthy woman."
"A bath would make you as good as new."
"Christians." she said, curling her lips, “don't believe in bath-
ing."
"That's because the body is vile." He was looking at her face.
Passion and fury had exhausted her and now she was pale and
quiet.
She leaned back and thrust a leg out and then exposed it almost
to its full length. "If the body is vile why did God make it so
beautiful?"
"It became vile after he made it," said Mark, looking at the leg.
She flung her garment across the leg and said: "Mark, let's go
away from here."
"If we go away will you marry me?”
"No."
"If we lived together—”
"We wouldn't."
"You only want me to help you find David. If we found him you
would then leave me."
"But I'd always remember you for having been so kind to me."
"A man wants more than gratitude."
She turned and looked at him. "Do you expect to live all your
life in this heat and loneliness and filth? If so, then why are you
not as other monks? You share your food with me. It is good food
128but
you
should not eat that kind." Her eyes were now mocking
him a little. "Unless you're going to be a real monk and destroy
your body why stay here?"
"I'm only half a monk," he said, grinning. "I don't expect to
inherit any high order of glory."
"You're too human to be a good Christian."
"That's true," he said. "Let me see a part of your leg.'
After turning on him a perplexed look she flung the garment
back. "Is that enough?"
"You have a lovely knee,” he said.
"Mark, would you lend me money so that I can go? David and
I would pay you back.”
"That isn't what I want."
"You mean you want me, I suppose. Well, that can never be."
"Not even if David-"
"Don't say it! I know he isn't dead."
"He may forget you."
"Not David."
"Anyway, he'll not be what he was when you saw him. He—”
"Please!" she said and covered her leg. "Mark, you're just de-
termined to keep me here hoping I'll surrender. Is that it?”
"Well, no-"
"Oh, don't lie about it! If David doesn't come before long I
won't stay here. I'll go find him."
"Would you be like Sarah of Eygpt? She was a harlot who be-
came a Christian. She decided to make a pilgrimage to Rome to
see the pope and she paid her passage in the old way."
"Are you implying that I've been a harlot?-or would be?"
"But you have no money. It takes money to travel."
"I could walk and beg."
"Across water, you mean.”
"Anything would be better than to stay here."
"But you say David will come here if he escapes. You would
see him sooner if you stay here, for if you set out to walk over the
world he would never find you."
"I've thought of that. Mark, do you think there's a demon in
me?"
"You acted like a woman with a demon in her."
Demons, Mark said, were the souls of criminals and other
wicked people, now dead, that were compelled to wander on the
earth, disconsolate and malicious and vengeful. They had powers
129superior to man's but inferior to God's. After the Day of Judg-
ment it would be their task, somewhere in the bottomless pit in
the earth, to inflict eternal torture on the damned. Most of them
were in hell now but wizards had power to summon them from the
depths to torment the living. Christians who were holy in their
way of living could protect themselves from demons with prayer
and supplication, with tears, with holy water, and with the sign of
the cross. The appetite of demons for the human soul was simply
ravenous, particularly for the souls of good people; and this was
so because they were condemned to an eternity of disembodied
wandering.
In Rome the Christians had a prayer that paralyzed demons
with fright. "Blood of him who for us was made flesh, born of the
holy virgin, Jesus Christ. Blood of him who was born from the
holy mother of God, Jesus Christ. Blood of him who appeared
among men on earth, Jesus Christ. Blood of him who was bap-
tized in the Jordan by the forerunner John, Jesus Christ. Blood of
him who offered himself a sacrifice for our sins, Jesus Christ,
amen."
"Well," she said, with a selfconscious laugh, "if there was one
in me he's paralyzed now. Do the jackals cry because demons are
in them?"
A lot of beasts, said Mark, had demons in them.
"If a person has a demon in him doesn't he have a peculiar
odor?"
"He stinks," Mark said.
Helene sniffed at her arms. "We all stink here."
"It's a different odor," Mark said. "The demon stink is like
burning sulphur."
"Why?" she asked, still sniffing.
"Because burning sulphur is the odor of the fires in hell.”
"Then if one of the nuns has a demon in her instead of a baby
why couldn't Hareb smell her?"
"Maybe he did."
"You said Apollo has the stink of a goat. I can't smell it."
"His passions weren't aroused," said Mark. "When his first lust
is fired you can smell him a mile away."
"And he has froth on his lips?"
"Just like a male goat."
Helene laughed. "What things you men are! Mark?"
"Yes?"
130"Move over and put your arm around me. I'll pretend it's
David's arm." Mark did not like that but he moved over and

"What things you women are!" said Mark.
"You men," she murmured, "don't understand love. All you
know is passion."
"Do you know what happened to a man when a demon left

"Of course not."
"He shrieked and howled and almost knocked himself out.
Then Anthony prayed over him. The demon came out and shot

She took his hand that was around her and moved it down.

"Anthony told about a woman who had a demon in her."

"The demon threw the woman down and she rolled and shriek-

"It is said that he can."

She smote his hand, when, moving away from him, she got to

131Mark was looking a little sheepish. "If Eve was as lovely as
Helene," he said, "and I had been Adam, then what you say is

132XV
Hareb completed the walls of his cell and in vain tried to put a
roof on. Takuda, who sat as immobile as stone day after day,
watching him, went forward to help but Hareb ignored her
presence. He pretended that she was nowhere in sight except when
she got in his way; and then he snarled and barked at her and
poured upon her helpless gray head the words of the Fathers.
"Have you forgotten that married people ought to blush at the
state they are living in? Go away!" Pained, but amused, and
gentle with pity, she would go away and sit to watch and wait.
Once in a while she would bring water to him but usually he
would dash it from her hands and spill it. Or she would offer to
share her food, which Mark gave to her, and Hareb would scowl at
it as though looking at sin itself and strike it from her hand. But
after he was alone he would retrieve it from the sand and eat it.
Unwilling to spend more time in fruitless labor he resolved to
live in a roofless cell and to make himself the holiest of all the
saints. He knew pretty well what he would have to do but he
wanted to order his life in a series of dramatic events of accelerat-
ing power. Though he had tried he found it impossible to weep,
and so gave up all thought of tears. When he heard that one of
the saints uttered three hundred prayers daily he developed a
distaste for prayer. Praying like weeping was for weaklings.
Having the strength of ten and the fortitude and will of goodness
itself he would wage war in the very citadel of evil.
Sitting one day in his cell, thinking of his problem, he wondered
what Agios was doing. It was Mark who told him.
Agios-though none was ever to know how he managed it--
had shaved his head. He then had dug a deep hole in the sands, so
133deep that when he stood in it only his head was above the sur-
face. He summoned Mark to assist him. Agios descended into
the pit and put his arms down at his side and said to Mark: "Bury
me.'
""
"All of you?" asked Mark, astonished.
"Up to my chin.”
While with his hands Mark scooped the sand in, Agios muttered
certain words that appealed to him, his favorite of all being, In-
spexit et despexit. Mark paused to look at the sign, In hoc signo
vincas, which was suspended from the man's neck. "Do you want.
to take the cross off?"
"No."
Wondering what Hareb would think of this, Mark piled sand
around the man, from time to time tamping it with his feet, until
only the head of Agios was visible, a strange head, huge and bald.
What the sun would do to the skull made Mark shiver to think of.
On hands and knees he piled the sands round the man's neck and
leveled and piled and leveled, until Agio's chin rested on the
sand. Then Mark looked gravely at the skull.
"What did Tertullian say?—credo quia absurdum!”
"Inspexit et despexit," said Agios.
"How long will you be there?"
"Fourteen days and nights."
Mark walked round the head, looking at it. He gazed out over
the area roundabout for insects and small earth creatures, think-

the sun had scalded and cooked the flesh it was hard to tell what
might come here to feast, including jackals. He supposed he

reward there would be for him, for assisting holy men to become
holier.
"Shall I bring you food and water daily?"
"No."
"You're not going to fast for two weeks?"
"In two weeks dig me out."
"I'll tell Hareb. Lord, will he be in a fury!"
"He's a weakling," said Agios. "His lower lip quavers like a
baby's."
Mark went to Hareb, sitting in moody reflection back in his

baby's. He means you're a weakling."
134"The curse!" muttered Hareb. "The fool who stands on one
leg."
"Not now. He's buried up to his neck." When Hareb seemed

head is hairless. It looks like some kind of strange desert fruit.
Will you come see it before it is eaten?”
"Go away," said Hareb. "You interrupt my meditations."
Mark told Helene what Agios had done. She did not care to
see the man but she went with Mark because her ennui was so
deadly. A hundred yards away she saw the grayish skull shining
in the sun like a metal globe; and as she slowly approached she
became more and more horrified, for she had only half-believed
what Mark had said. But there the man was. Or there at least
the head was, looking like a decapitated skull set on the sands.
"O my God!" she said.
He would be a wonderful saint, Mark told her cheerfully. He
took her arm. "Come up and see.'
She pulled his hand off. "I can walk without fainting and
falling." She went close to the head and looked down at the hide,
mottled and gray with filth, stretched tight over the frame of bone.
On one side like a small hedge were the bushy brows. She won-

She made a sound of nausea.
"He'll be a greater saint than Anthony."
Helene at the moment was looking at the pale lips when, star-

"What's he saying?"
"What he says most of the time. He looked at the world and
despised it."
Again she walked round and round the skull. It was difficult
to realize that this was not merely a human head set on the sands,
that there was a tall muscular body down under it, set in the earth.
She was feeling sick. "Let's go," she said.
News of Agios spread, and even Bion, sucking and gurgling and
135weeping, went out to see him. On the third day most of the nuns
went. But not Julia. Mother Ida went and a score of the sisters

nuns were like. Their heads were covered and most of their faces.
In the eyes she saw womanliness. Sliding along noiselessly they

They spent a few moments out with Agios, and Helene, who stood
apart, watching them, thought they were praying. In grave pro-
cession with Mother Ida at their head they went back to the house.
Monks came from their cells roundabout or from the colony a

went away to make their own austerities more severe. Hareb
grumbled and muttered but could not be prevailed on to go out
and see this saint who wrestled so valiantly with Satan. Takuda
went once and returned with a strange gentle look of pity on her
face.
It was Mark who set himself as guardian over the skull. He
spent every night out there, sitting by a pile of small stones which
he had gathered to hurl at jackals; sleeping and waking; dreaming
on the image of Helene; thinking about the queer thing that man
was. He went out now and then during the day.
Though the saint's scalp was packed with the filth of years the
sun was terrific in its ardor. By the end of the second day Mark
thought the scalp looked all right. By the fourth day he saw that
it was puffed and swelling. By the end of the week the whole top
of the head looked like a great vegetable or fruit that was overripe
and beginning to rot. He told Helene he was worried about it.
"Then why in hell don't you cover it?" she asked angrily.
"He doesn't want me to."
"Do you realize his skull might pop open?"
"That's what I'm afraid of. Still," said Mark cheerfully, "I
guess that's what he wants. Maybe he'll die there. He'll be known
as Saint Agios."
"Saint Agios! Did Anthony treat himself this way?"
"He fasted forty days and nights in the wilderness. He had him-
self sealed up in a cell for seven years. He did a lot of wonderful
things to humiliate Satan."
Helene laughed. She could never be sure if Mark was serious
or drily facetious. "You think Anthony really upset the Devil?”
"Satan laughs when we sin. He's humiliated when we overthrow
136him." Mark put a finger and thumb to his nose, squeezing it, and
looking down said, "That's what the Fathers say."
"If David is like the men here I don't want ever to see him. I'll
be a nun."
All men were alike, Mark said. Some were weaker, some
stronger.
"If he dies out there his soul will rise like a light to heaven?”
"He won't die," said Mark. "He's tougher than hornbeam. In

Again Helene stared at him, trying to determine if he was

On the tenth day one of the great yellow sandstorms came up
with the sunlight flashing in it like sheaves of gold; and the whole
desert for a few hours was luminously, brilliantly blind as though
the heavens had been opened above a howling wind. Mark was

after the storm had passed Mark could see no difference in the
head, even though the sands for hours had whipped across it. The
flesh on the skull had puffed up in huge blisters and burst open,
and insects, both of the earth and the air, were feeding on the sores.
Mark tried to pick them off one by one but Agios growled at him.
"Let them be! What did the holy Saint Simon say after the
chain round his waist had sunk into his flesh? To those who would
have picked the worms he said, Let them be. Let them eat vile
flesh that God has given to worms."
"The worms that fell he picked up and put back in the wounds."
“If you've taken any worms off put them back.”
A gentle soul, Mark thought that he might come when Agios

them be."
“All right," said Mark, amazed by the man's endurance. "Are

"Neither."
“Do you ever sleep?"
"No."
Mark went over to his pile of stones and dozed until morning.
He felt exhausted, for in these ten days he had slept little. Still,
he had drunk water and eaten nourishing food. What a weakling
he was that he should be dead tired, while this man, who neither
137ate nor slept, still spoke with a strong voice! Of such was the
kingdom. Of such would be the angels in the highest glory. He
thought Agios must be suffering horribly; his beardless face was
swelling and erupting, with the short hair standing like black

man? If Jesus, the son of God, could descend to the earth and
humble himself in the agony of the cross, those worthy to be saints
would have to endure punishments almost as great..
...
He dozed off and awoke and heard the jackals crying, and

On the fourteenth day Mark went to Agios to dig him out. He
had no spade or shovel but his hands. The odors from the skull
sickened him; the whole head looked like an enormous ripe wound
filled with pus. But the mind was clear, the voice was firm. Mark
139knelt and scooped sand away, exposing the neck and then the
shoulders; and he asked Agios if he could draw his arms forth and
free his hands. Agios made a feeble effort. He said his arms felt
like lead.
"I'll have to dig you out clear to your feet." said Mark, and set
to work. Lying on his belly he clasped his hands and used his
arms as a drag to bring the sand up. Floundering and breathing
hard and turning away now and then from the odor he moved
round and round the hole. When he was face to face with Agios
he ventured to look at the man's eyes and was shocked to see that
they were swollen shut. He looked then at the nose: it was like
an overripe vegetable almost bursting with its own juices. The
tatooed cross on the forehead looked as though it had been incised
by the stings of wasps.
For more than an hour he labored and Agios never spoke or
moved. At last Mark had scooped out so much sand that he had a
depression all the way around Agios, and now, standing behind the
man, he braced himself and slipped his arms under Agio's arms
and tried to pull him out. He might as well have pulled at a
ship's anchor. Kneeling, he scooped again, cupping his hands and
filling them and hurling the sand away. After another hour he
had the man freed to his loins. The arms were now free but when
Mark asked him to move them Agios was not able to. Mark took
an arm gently to move it up and down, all the while watching
Agios for signs of pain. Perhaps there was pain but there was no
sign of it. This saint was blind and silent and imperturbable.
Mark softly massaged the arms. Both arms and hands looked
bloodless and dead. Scooping sand out and sweating and praying
Mark toiled until Agios stood only kneedeep; and still he could
not stir.
"Perhaps," said Agios at last, in his clear firm voice, “you'd
better bury me over and I'll die here."
Was the man serious or jesting? Mark sat back to rest and to
look at him and consider the matter. There Agios stood, stark
naked above his knees, the heavy hair on his body matted with
sand, his arms hanging useless, his eyes blind behind the swollen

"You're already a great saint," Mark said. "You'll be a greater
one."
"Dig me out."
140"I'm resting a moment. Agios, what are you going to do next
to purify yourself?”
Agios said he had not chosen a feat. What was Hareb doing?
"Nothing yet," Mark said.
Mark went over and dropped to his knees. He felt ashamed

"Will you take water and food."
141"A little," he said.
Because of the stench Helene had gone away and now, turned,

142XVI
In this area a great and curious eagerness had developed: which
of the two, Agios or Hareb, would be the mightiest athlete of
Christ? Mark held with Agios but Takuda said to wait; she knew
her husband; after he had made up his mind his ordeals would
astound the world.
While Hareb was making up his mind there came in from the
desert a withered little man, gray and old and almost naked.
This was Kepha, whose fame also had spread far. The one thing
about him that filled eyes with wonder was his utter inability to
stand the odor of the human body. Believing all matter to be
corrupt and the human body the most corrupt of all matter, Kepha
had insisted to all persons who would listen that the human odor
was the worst under the heavens. Did not ladies and fops and
libertines and all the wretches of sin and damnation drench them-
selves with scents to destroy their smell? He went almost naked
because the odor of the human body clung to its garments.
When he could find a razor he shaved the hair off his body and
head, because the stink clung to hair also.
He was quite hairy on this day when he suddenly appeared,
leading a small scrawny mare so feeble it could barely walk. This,
he told everyone, was his wife, transformed into a beast. Gravely,
in his thin old voice, he would say: "That she looks like a mare is
only an error of sight in those who see her."
He came in, leading the beast and crying in his little old voice,
"Greetings in our Lord!"
"May the Lord bless you out of Zion," Mark said.
He had heard about this saintly man. He went to find Helene.
Mark knew, all people in the world knew, that the Devil with his
143evil powers could transform human beings into the shape of
beasts or birds or hideous monsters. He had no doubt at all that
this scrawny enfeebled beast was Kepha's wife but he had never
understood why Kepha kept his wife with him, for she must have
been a very wicked woman or Satan would not have changed her.
He looked into the cell and saw Helene sitting in a corner,
brooding, as had become her way. "Helene," he said softly,
"one of the holiest of the saints has come.'
""
Helene looked up, her eyes heavy with frustration and boredom.
Her lips curled in a sneer that was becoming habitual. "You

He has his wife with him. She's a horse."

"Well, not a horse. She's a woman but she's in the form of a
horse."
"Are you crazy?”
"Come and see for yourself."
Helene arose and went with him. She looked first at Kepha
and saw that he was almost naked but there was nothing offensive

shrunken that they looked like vestiges only, half-hidden in the
hair. His face was small and yellowed and ghastly, with no flesh
on it, with only the skin drawn over the bones. His eyes were
small and bright and full of the glory of his saintliness, full of
happy memories of his many years of triumphant wrestling.
"That's his wife," said Mark, indicating the nag.
"Quite an attractive woman, isn't she?”
"Kepha, was your wife attractive?”
"No," he said. "Look at her. You can see."

"What's his wife's name?"
"What's your wife's name?"
"Amma," he said.
"Looks to me as if Amma needs something to eat."

"Not much," Kepha said.
"Where you going?"
144Kepha gestured with a skinny little hand toward Alexandria.
"There."
Mark said he would bring Hareb and hastened over to his cell.

woman.
""

Helene looked at him. No, the man was not jesting. If she had

or a dragon, a unicorn or phoenix or mermaid; and there was the

And Helene was looking at him. She thought him the most

145"There's some truth in that," Mark said. "You can tell that's
