"We're looking at Eve," Hareb said.
Abruptly she turned and ran.
crying, "Zinara, say something to me! Anything! Speak!" Shud-
dering, loathing, tormented, hating men with murderous hate,
she stared wildly at Zinara, and hated her also. Flinging herself
down by the wall she began to weep but after a few moments she
cried bitterly, speaking to no one at all, nor to God but to silence
and her own soul, "Men say their wives are mares! Men look at
women and say, Stop lusting after me! Men. Men look at a
beautiful woman as they would look at her pubic hair if they
saw her naked. Men! Men will never be satisfied until they have
driven us mad or turned us into beasts! Why, O God, did I ever
come here? ." She struggled up and looked down at Zinara's
covered head and at her hands, always busy; and she thought she
would go off somewhere alone and kill herself. These torments,
this loneliness, these creatures who were not human she could not
endure much longer. "Zinara?" She spoke the name over and over
until it had no meaning. "David. . . . David. . . ." She went out-
side. The three men were still standing there; she did not know
if they were apparitions or real men, or whether men or beasts; for
in this burning desert where nothing grew and nothing sang you
could not know after a while, you could not believe, you could
not feel. Afraid that she was about to lose control of her senses,
that even her soul was slipping away, she ran across the sands to
the cell where Bion sat and where his son sat, the deaf-mute, day
after day, month after month, sitting, waiting, his troubled eyes
gentle and friendless. She looked at Bion and then at the son.
"Bion!"
"May God forgive me!" he muttered. "I'm a son of wickedness."
He made a gurgling throaty sound of lamentation, then drew a
deep sigh and gurgled again.
"Maryana!" The boy was looking at her and she felt that he
had heard but he only looked at her with those gentle wondering
eyes. "Maryana?" He looked at her but that was all. "Bion!"
"May God forgive a sinner!" Bion cried, and gurgled again.
146"Bion, you idiot, why do you sit there sucking wine?"
"I've been a wicked man," he said. He burst with silly tearful
lamentations and clapped himself with his hands, crying, "Woe
is me!"
“Maryana?”
"Don't speak to her," said Bion. “She is deaf.”
"She? Why are you speaking of your son as a girl?"
"My son cannot hear," he said. "Woe is me, for I have been
wicked."
Helene looked into the boy's eyes. The boy looked at her as
any friendless and gentle thing might have looked but there was
no change in his face.
She turned away. Where was Apollo, the son of a bitch? He
might be an evil man but he talked like a human being; in this
loneliness there was a blessing in the mere sound of words that
were like words you used to know. Apollo had a cell at a little
distance from Bion's but Mark said he was not often in it. Mark
said that under cover of darkness Apollo crawled stealthily up to
the house where the nuns lived. He said there were nuns in a
colony over near the river and that Apollo went there and tried
to lure the nuns away and seduce them. No doubt it was all true.
No doubt it was true that he took special delight in seducing nuns.
He was a wicked thing but at least there was humanity in his
wickedness. She supposed that Satan might be a jolly fine person
to talk to, that only a scowling and unhuman man like Hareb
could ever talk to God. Apollo had slipped into Zinara's cell
when Helene was asleep and she had been awakened when she
felt his arms creeping round her. She had struck him with all the
force she had and had run away to find Mark. She had not seen
Apollo since then; now she wanted to talk to him, to hear the
sounds of ordinary human passion and coveting and yearning in
words that were familiar, like the scenes of childhood. But where
was he?
He was in his cell, sitting bowed over, his face invisible to her.
She wondered if he slept. "Apollo?" she said softly.
At once he raised his face and for a moment it was a drawn and
haggard face, like the face of one in deep woe. Then, like a lamp
lit in darkness, it awoke with joy and he arose with his eyes burn
ing upon her and his full lips making a queer pucker under their
hair, like lips preparing to suck juices from a fruit. "Helene!"
he said, reaching toward her.
147"Keep your hands off," she said. "Don't get any silly notions.
I just wanted to talk to someone. Anyone.
99
"Come in," he said and made a gesture, as though he were
inviting her into a fine house. "Will you sit?"
She sank down by the doorway. Apollo sat across from her, with
the doorway light falling on his face.
"A monk has just come in," she said, brushing sand from her
hands. "His wife is with him. She's a horse."
"Oh, Kepha," he said. "He has been here before.”
"With his wife who is a mare?"
"Oh yes."
"You believe that thing is his wife?"
Well now, was that so strange? If Balaam's ass spoke; if lions
talked to men and doves sang Christian hymns; if dogs could
speak with the human voice as a dog had spoken to the apostle
Mark; if a witch could summon a devil from hell and make it take
the form of a wolf and recite the Lord's prayer; if demons could
swell a woman's womb until she seemed to be far along in preg-
nancy, yet was not; if women could be impregnated by a serpent
or by the wind or other forces of nature; if Jonah could live three
days in a whale's belly and a man with faith equal to a grain of
mustard seed could move mountains-if these things were true,
why doubt that Satan transformed a nagging wife into a mare?
"Well, but why did he?"
"That I don't know," Apollo said. "Both God and Satan do
things beyond my understanding."
"You men believe just about anything that suits you. We
women can't do that."
"My dear, men believe only what is true."
"That's what you tell us."
"Balaam's ass spoke. Do you doubt it? Jesus walked on water.
Do you doubt it? Why, these things are recorded in our holy
books."
"Isn't there a colony of monks and nuns just south of here?"
About ten miles south, he said. She would not like it there. The
nuns there, and the monks also, were under a disciplinarian far
more stern than Mother Ida. When not praying or chanting
psalms they spent their time plaiting palm leaves; and if they cut
their hands while working, as they often did, they were allowed
to take no notice of the wounds. One monk oiled his hands be-
cause of blood running on to the mat he was making and he was
148severely punished; and another because of his open wounds de-
veloped terrible sores and parts of him had to be cut off, yet all
the while he went on with his labors. The monks were flogged
for the most trivial offenses.
"Here," Apollo said, his gaze roving over her, "we have much
more freedom in working out our salvation. Down there they
have cells so small that they can't lie down or even sit, but sleep
half-standing. In such colonies the rule is silence. They're not
allowed to talk as we are talking now."
Were any famous monks there? None very famous, he said,
but there was a famous nun. Her name was Silvia. It was her
boast that since she was baptized she had never touched water to
any part of her but the tips of her fingers, and to them only rarely.
"You go down there, don't you?"
"Oh, no," he said, his eyes reproaching her.
She was looking at him with curious interest and wondering
if he had made his pact with Satan. Perhaps she could lead him
out. "What were you doing when I came to the door?"
“Oh, let me see. In memory I was hearing a song I once heard
in taverns. White waxen cheeks, soft scented breast, deep eyes
wherein the Muses nest, sweet lips that perfect pleasures bring,
sing me your song, pale Xantho, sing!"
"Sing me your song, pale Xantho, sing!"
"Do you like it?”
"Yes." She might as well come out with it. "Have you made
your pact with the Devil?"
His smile was like a soft light struggling deep in his beard. “Not
yet. When I find a Jew I'll make it."
"Why a Jew?'
He lifted his brows. "You don't know? Because Jews are ac-
cursed people and act as Satan's agents."
"I don't believe it. I've known Jews. Christians ought to be
ashamed for the way they treat them."
"But the Jews are accursed. They rejected the messiah."
"They did not. Jesus was not their kind of messiah."
"You don't know how wicked they are. Did not Justin, the
blessed martyr, say that no other people anywhere, or of any time,
have been so guilty of wrong?"
"Does the fact that he said it make it so?"
"Justin was a very holy man. Didn't you know it?"
With a smile that was almost a sneer she said, "I just didn't
149care. And did you know that Christians generation after genera-
tion rewrote their gospels to make them more and more anti-
Jewish? Do you know that some Christians three times every day
in their prayers curse the Jews?"
"Who told you these abominable things?"
"I heard a Jewish rabbi say them."
“You should not listen—”
"But are those the things Jesus taught?"
"Let's not talk about Jews. They're an odious people."
"But I'd like to understand you," she persisted. "You say you
are determined to seduce me. Is that true?"
"That is true."
"To do that you will find a Jew and make a pact with Satan.
Yet you call yourself a Christian. No Jew in the world could ever
stoop that low."
"Do you know who Tacitus was?”
"I don't care."
"He was a Roman historian. He said that though Jews lust
after women they refuse intercourse with all women but their
own. Why?" asked Apollo, opening his eyes wide and staring at
her.
"Because they're more virtuous than Christians."
"O God, no!" he cried, horrified. "They refuse-"
"I don't want to hear it," she said sharply. "I like the Jews
I've known but such Christians as you are an abomination. I'll
never realize how absolutely wicked you are. If a man can be a
Christian and as wicked as you, then what is a Christian?"
"You're the wicked one," he breathed at her, pretending to be
shocked. "I admit that my flesh is sinful. That's no fault of mine.
My soul-"
"You have a daughter named Julia," she said. She thought she
saw him start but he was a sly rascal who dissembled well. “Is
there a demon in her?" asked Helene with open-eyed innocence.
Then her eyes narrowed. "Mark says she's pregnant, so who is
the father."
"I know the father," he said, shaking his head gloomily. "He'll
be punished."
"You mean he's one of the monks here?”
Apollo nodded, and still nodding said, "He'll be punished.
He'll be stripped naked and given one hundred lashes."
"Who is he?"
150"You'll learn in good time."
She considered a few moments. She waited until he was looking
into her eyes, and said, "You're the father."
His response amazed her. He leapt to his feet and he seemed to
be a very furious man. Thinking that he intended to strangle her
she swung and on hands and knees rushed out of the cell. She got
to her feet and while running turned to face him. His face was
convulsed and hideous.
"You demon out of hell!" he shrieked, shaking a clenched
hand at her. "Your soul will be damned to eternal damnation if
you don't let me save you! Do you know how it was with Eve?”
"To hell with it!" she cried, disgusted and appalled. "You
wicked ugly monstrous thing! So you are the father—”
Abruptly his mood changed and again she was astonished. The
anger and outrage fled from his face and he was sly and quiet and
designing and he was looking at her with calculating eyes, with
cold eyes, in which she saw, as he approached her, the Devil's own
image.
"I guess we're both wicked," he said gently. "We can save our
souls together, but alone we are doomed."
She tried to laugh and failed and choked and put a hand to her
mouth.
"Helene, my precious darling—”
"Don't you touch me!" She backed away and he came after
her. "Stop there!" she cried. "If you don't I'll call Hareb.”
He stopped. He was smiling, an evil man at ease, a cunning
man looking at his prey. "You will come to me," he said, his
eyes so full of strange unhuman things that she felt a little faint.
"Satan will send you to me. Then I will sanctify you. Then—”
"How God damned sweet of you!"
"I'll be your priest, your thurifer, your pastor, your shepherd,
your perpetual curate. I'll be—” she was backing away from him
“—your monk and brother, your father and confessor, your lover
and high priest—”
She turned and ran. She ran until she came to Bion's cell and
there stopped, panting, terrified, and looked back. He was stand-
ing tall and straight against the yellow loneliness and pale mists,
and she thought he was smiling with that evil mocking self-
assured smile, the smile of one created in the image of God, whose
appointed mission in life was to save the souls of the-of the-why,
of course, of the weaker sex!--for that was the way they thought of
151it. He was the kind of thing that fascinated while he repelled, that
lured her even while she despised him; and she despised herself
too while wondering if she could hold out against him, if he
would have his way. In God's name what was it in woman that
made her so weak, that made her feel so insecure and helpless, in
the face of a man's bold and arrogant ardor?
She put her arms against the cell wall and buried her face in
her arms. "David," she whispered, "come, please come! ..."

Two or three weeks later she was aroused from sleep by what she
thought had been a dreadful scream. She sat up, her body ting-
ling all over, her ears straining, her eyes peering over at Zinara.
"Zinara?" she said.
"What?" Zinara whispered.
"Did you hear it? What was it?"
Then the sound rang again in the night, the awful piercing
scream of a woman. Helene sprang up and ran outside, and the
first thing she saw in the brilliant desert light was Mark, midway
between her and the nuns' house. She hastened over to him and
while they both stood tense and listening the sound came again,
the wild and heartrending sound of a woman in terror or pain.

They then heard a cry of pain and a babble of tongues and
sounds like muffled blows or of heavy objects falling to the stone
floor. Mark put his ear to the door and said he could hear moans.
Hareb banged on the door and shouted, "What's the trouble
in there?" When after waiting there was no response he smote

There was the sound of wooden bars being removed. The
door was opened a little and Ida's cold eyes looked out.

With tight lips Ida said, "Julia has had a child." She was look-
153ing accusingly at Hareb. "You told me it was a demon.”

"The Devil," Hareb said. Did she not know that Satan often
lay with women in the guise of man?

in the story of Jesus? A child was born and the stars sang in their

had been born here in this desert, where nothing grew and

"Zinara," she said, "a child has been born! Do you hear me?
There is a baby over there now!"
She did not sleep but listened and waited for morning. What

Before daylight she went out and found Mark standing alone,

In the brilliant night light she saw Mother Ida coming with a

"I don't know. She went to Apollo.'

Takuda came over to look in.

159XVIII
Mark came late in the afternoon, riding an ass, with provisions
hanging from his shoulders all around him. He was saturated
with dust and his eyes were red and he was choked with thirst
and half-dead with fatigue but he was smiling and cheerful. He
had everything, he said, that a city could provide for a baby: milk,
which at once they would hang in the well and try to keep cool;
a very fine flour for gruel; honey and fruits and many other things,
for he was not sure what a baby might be able to eat. Tomorrow
a man would come with fresh food and to take this beast back.
Helene took the food to Takuda, who knew far more about
babies than any other woman here; and Elpha and Helene and
Mark hovered near while Takuda fed the child. It was such a
pitiful scrawny little thing, Takuda said, that she dared to give
it nothing but a little warm milk. After a week or so they might
give it a little gruel, but only a little, for a baby was a delicate
thing. If it lived-
It would live, said Helene, if love could make it live. It would
have three mothers, the three of them, who would take turns with
it, so that never for a moment would it be alone. Mark had also
brought a small bale of cloths, and choosing from these Takuda
diapered the child and wrapped it in a soft garment. Elpha then
held it a little while. A few days later when Ida went to the city.
to buy provisions for the nuns, as was her habit every two or three
months, all the nuns but Julia came to the cell to see the babe.
In turn they held it, these women who had never been mothers
and would never be mothers, these brides of Christ, whose great-
est yearning, as their eyes said so plainly, was not to be a virgin
in the Lord but a wife and mother.
160Poor Julia, where was she? She had been imprisoned, a nun
said, in a small cell, where she now lived alone and wept and
milked her breasts. "I'm going to get her," said Helene, and with
cries of protest upon her head she ran to the house and entered.
Some of the nuns came after her, for it was a desecration for a
sinful woman like Helene to enter the holy place of the sisters;
but Helene was already running through the big gloomy build-
ing, shouting, "Julia! Julia, where are you?" "Here," said a voice.
Helene rushed over and with the strength of a man tore down the
stones that had been piled up to imprison her. She seized Julia's
arm and dragged her out and was aware in this moment of how
fleshless the girl was. "You're going to live with us!" Helene was
whispering. "You're going to be with your child!"
"But Mother Ida!" cried Julia, appalled.
"To hell with Mother Ida. Come!" She almost dragged the
terrified girl across the stone floor and through the doorway; and
while the nuns gasped at such audacity and wickedness or ran
lamenting to their prayers Helene took the girl-child to the cell
and thrust her inside.
"There!" she said, panting, triumphant. "She'll nurse the babe
as a mother should and if—if Ida comes to take her away-" She
turned to Mark. "What will we do?"
"I don't know," Mark said.
"We'll fight her. I'll fight anyone who tries to take this mother
from her child. I'll call on God to help me and if he doesn't help
me I'll never be a Christian again. I'll call on the Devil-”
"For shame!" said Mark.
Then, her fury melting to tenderness, she forced Julia to sit
and take the babe and she laid back Julia's garment to expose the
breasts. "There," she said, “nurse your baby." She looked up at
Mark. "There's some kind of lesson here," she said. "You men
make us ashamed to show our breasts but we feel no shame when
we feed the child, even though men look on." She turned to Bion.
"You'll have to live somewhere else. Four wicked women and a
child born in sin will live here." She looked at the babe, weakly
nursing. "Now it has four mothers. It cannot die."
Bion obediently left the cell and the four women possessed it.
They would make it more homelike, Helene said; they would
have Mark buy mats for sleeping and an image of Mary to place
on a wall; and—yes, they would bring stones to make a table to
eat on and they would hang up two or three pieces of gay cloth,
161as a kind of drapery; and Jesus would consecrate and hallow this
place over four sinful women and a sinful child.
"Only women understand Jesus," she said. "And of women,
only mothers."
Two days later, at a moment when the four women were cooing
over the babe, Ida's gaunt form filled the dorway. Helene leapt
up, trembling, and faced her.
"What do you want?" Ida looked at her but did not speak.
"Listen, if Julia is wicked you should be glad to get rid of her. . .
Well, what is it you want?"
Ida's cold eyes, the eyes of an owl staring in daylight, looked
past her to the other women and the child.
"Don't come in," Helene said. "We're four wicked women
and we have a bastard child. We don't want you here. Now will
you please go away?" When Ida tried to push past her and enter
Helene struck at her, crying, "No! Don't you try to take her or
I'll kill you!"
"You ought to be ashamed," said Mother Ida gently, and made
the sign of the cross. "I only came to look at the babe."
"You-?" said Helene, looking into her eyes.
"Yes."
Helene stepped aside. Mother Ida went in and bent forward
and looked at the child. When she came out after a moment
Helene saw her eyes and saw something in her eyes that she had
never thought she would see there. "Mother, forgive me," she
said.
"Our Lord will forgive you," said Ida and went away.
Choked and hurt and astonished, Helene stood by the wall a
few moments and then burst into tears. She ran sobbing to find
Mark and went up to him crying, "Oh, Mark, I have done a ter-
rible thing!"
Mark put an arm round her. “Now what is it?”
"I was mean to Ida! She came—I thought—but she just wanted
to see the child!"
"Of course," said Mark soothingly.
She stepped back and looked at him, her eyes swimming. "O
my God, I don't know what to say! I'm so horribly ashamed!"
"You're making too much of it," Mark said.
"Oh, maybe. But it's what I saw in her eyes!"
"Yes, I know."
"Not a nun but a mother. Mark, dear?"
162"Yes?"
"Will you help us to fix our little place up so it will be nicer?"
"Why, of course," he said, looking at her eyes and thinking
them twice as lovely for their tears.
"You're a good man. You're the only good man out here.”
"I'm a very weak man," he said.
Hareb meanwhile was sitting alone in his cell, bowed to his
knees and groaning. A man in whom passions ran deep and strong
he was unable to drive out of memory the image of Elpha as she
had stood before him, her breasts and flanks bared, the shame rosy
in her cheeks, her eyes so defenseless when they met his gaze; and
to make his torments worse there swarmed in his mind the sensu-
ous words of his hellhound youth. He had once said as a young
man-God forgive him!—that nothing under heaven could shake
a man like the look of surrender in a woman's eyes when she was
burning toward a lover. He had once said that the heavens melted
into earth when at the same moment a man's mouth sank into the
ripeness of a woman's lips and his staff into her door. Ah, but he
had been a wicked one in those years! Takuda was not the only
woman he had lain with, nor the loveliest among a hundred.
There had been a girl much like Elpha, very young and virginally
tender; and now her name came up the long corridor of memory
and danced in his thoughts. He shuddered, knowing that the de-
mons of lust, merry and mocking, were all around him. They had
seen the way he had looked at Elpha and had thought, Here is one
on whom we can work our evil fascinations, for hear how his heart
pounds, see how his senses swim and dim and darken, how, O Sa-
tan, his staff rises under his piece of rag! See how the blood throbs
in his temples! Here is a man whom we can claim for hell. . . .
He could hear their mocking whispered consultations as they
danced round him, their voices and eyes gloating over him, while
they summoned to his mind the thoughts and images that would
most fatally torment him. Out of his youth and memory they
called up these things, and though he strove to push them down,
to obliterate and destroy them, they came trooping in like the
fragrant sensuous votaries of the heathen goddess, dancing in
flower-strewn lanes. They brought to memory the words of a
Greek doctor: Sensuality in persons can be measured by the length
of their nose! With a groan he put a hand up to his huge beak
and moved back and forth along the bridge, measuring with dull
horror its dreadful length. Next, as though with one torment to
163overthrow and defeat him, they brought to his mind a vision of
all the people who in this moment, everywhere over the earth,
were panting in the raptures of sexual embrace; and the numbers
multiplied until there were a thousand, ten thousand, a million;
until the sum of them was beyond calculation; until the whole
world was alive with sinners mating in their wicked flesh: in
Rome, in Antioch, in Alexandria and Jerusalem and Athens and
Philadelphia—over all the lands of Phrygia and Galatia and Bith-
ynia, of Italy and Spain, of Syria and Egypt and Africa—all these
and all the lands beyond. If there were a hundred million men
and women in the world and they embraced seven times a week,
as Hareb had done in his youth, and spent a full hour each time
dallying and mating, as he, the wicked monster, had loved to do,
then how many in any hour were lying in sin? Millions! He tried
to put the horror away, but the demons dancing and rejoicing
round him served as his mathematicians, and while Hareb, goaded
and groaning, strove not to think or understand, the devils mock-
ingly multiplied and divided, whispering out of darkness the car-
nal knowledge. A hundred million, did he say? Well, then, in
this hour or in any other hour, four million men and women were
gasping and panting with rapture. The total of the sinners was
so appalling—and had he thought of all the hundreds of millions
of beasts and other creatures?-that Hareb struggled up and stood
shaken and outraged. Never before, never until the devils figured
the whole thing out for him, had he imagined that there was so
much damnation on earth. The total of it numbed his faculties,
almost blotted out his senses; and while he stood, furious and hat-
ing, the rejoicing imps multiplied it still further, calling to his
mind the multitude of intimate details; until he bolted outsde
and glowered round him at all the points of the compass, thinking
of all this that no man could see but that God looked upon day
and night. No wonder the prophets had thundered and threat-
ened and cursed; that Jesus had been a celibate; that only a virgin
could bring forth the Son of the Father; or that the Church Fa-
thers had said that people ought to blush and burn at the state in
which they were living! He looked round him, trying to sense the
immensity of wickedness everywhere and at last his gaze rested on
the emptiness before the nuns' house, which for a few moments
the loveliness of Elpha had filled; and then on Bion's cell, where
she now was. God had made women beautiful to test the souls of
men. What had Paul said?-Every man's work shall be made
164manifest; for the day shall declare it, because it shall be revealed
by fire; and the fire shall try every man's work of what sort it is.
Over and over he murmured the words until the devils, abashed,
slunk away. Hareb now felt himself standing in an area free of
evil, an area of light, in which the apostle's holy words were the
sun and the strength. If any man's work abide which he has built
he shall receive a reward. If any man's work shall be burned, he
shall suffer loss but he himself shall be saved, as by fire.
As he stood there, scowling but for a moment free of torments,
he felt that there had been revealed to him a great truth. For had
Paul not said that the quality of a man shall be revealed by fire,
that in fire he shall suffer loss, yet be saved? The martyr, burned
at the stake, fulfilled the prophecy; so also might a man who with
flame destroyed his passion, yet lived. How had Origen done it?—
with fire or with a knife? With fire, it now seemed to Hareb. But
how could he build a fire here, without flint or tinder, to cauterize
and destroy his parts? He could go to the city and find the mate-
rials there. Yes, by the living God of the sun and the waters, he
could test himself to see of what sort he was, he could attack the
Devil in his own citadel. He could inquire who the loveliest har-
lot was, the woman above all women most irresistible to men; the
woman most perfect of body and face and with the deepest and
most unappeased hungers and the most devilish wiles: he could
seek her out and stand triumphant and unyielding before her.
Then how the slinking imps would whimper and whine! Then
how the goodness of the first Adam would fill him with peace!
He would do it. He returned to his cell to sit and at once, like
jackals that had been frightened from a corpse and had been
watching and waiting, all the demons came in and he could hear
their whispering, he could smell their sulphur smell and feel the
air move as they moved around him. When his dry lips formu-
lated the words, Woman is the instrument which Satan employs
to possess our souls, he could hear deep subterranean laughter.
When he muttered the words, Laughter and gaiety come not from
God but from the Devil, he could hear a jubilant and infernal
chorus mocking him. He shut his eyes and felt evil pressing in.
With his arms he made the form of the cross, then with his legs;
but when he tried to recall holy words that had power to abash
demons there came only memory of soft sensuous delights, the gay
musical laughter of women, the fragrance of wines and inns and
arbors. By striking out with his arms or savagely shaking his head
165he tried to drive the images away but they came, one upon another
and masses upon masses, naked and shameless, lying in embrace.
He could smell the odors of passion aroused. "Devils out of hell!”
he muttered, wagging his head, his eyes closed tight. “I'm an
athlete in Christ our Lord! Try with all your allurements to
tempt me, that it may be revealed what man I am! Use soft music.
and sweet scents and the odor of women in heat-with all your
powers test me, use all the powers of hell to overthrow me.
Tormented with the torments of hell he sat until past midnight,
challenging, taunting, groaning, muttering; and then he dozed
and dreamed a dream. He was lying on his back in a nameless area
of holy land and his erect staff, towering mightily above him,
reached to heaven; and up this, as up a gigantic tree, female angels,
clad in silken diaphanous gowns of white, were frolicing and ex-
changing gay words and singing verses from the psalms. His sen-
sations were enormous and pleasant and just under floodtide.
When at last he was awakened by raptures too intense to endure
he dug and rubbed at his eyes and took a few moments to realize
where he was. Then, slowly, he became aware of what had hap-
pened and was rigid with horror.
This dream, or vision-in Hareb's time all dreams were vis-
ions-was repeated a few nights later when again, too exhausted
by his inner furies to stay awake, he sank into slumber. He had
other visions. Late one night he recalled the words of a Christian
saint: "So that I should arrive at a complete cleansing from all
tares and earthiness I gave over my will entirely to wisdom's fiery
furnace as a fire of purification, till all my vain and chaff-like de-
sires and the tares of earthly lust had been burnt away and all my
dross had been melted in this furnace, so that I appeared in spirit
as pure as gold and could see a new heaven and a new earth
formed and created within me." Hareb had pondered these words
before falling asleep. Then he dreamed this dream.
He was somewhere above the earth and had got his head tangled
in the stars and was then flinging off from his body the sperm
astrale, a heavy white and somewhat sticky fluid with which he
had somehow got smeared all over. He clutched at his body, fill-
ing his hands with this stuff and flinging it out into space, until
the sky was filled with it, drifting down like oceans of colostrum;
until the earth was covered with it, as with snow. And then, des-
cending, he used an enormous hose, which seemed to be attached
to his body and to draw on the fluids of evil in his own being; and
166with this prodigious stream he washed all the white stuff into the
rivers and seas, until these were white with it; and almost at once
the seas abounded with a variety of the most horrible dragons and
other monsters that rushed shoreward, beaching themselves, and
lashing with their tails and fins until they fell back and were sub-
merged in the deeps. When the earth had been cleansed and all
its life had been driven into the seas, where innumerable creatures
swarmed and fought and struggled, as in vast wombs, Hareb set
a great fire on the earth and the fire swept over everything and
purified it. All the dross was melted and burnt away, and Hareb
then stood alone on the earth, in the white pure radiance of the
first Adam; and he began to grow in both height and breadth
until he covered the earth and filled all space between earth and
heaven; and his substance was of pure gold and shone like gold;
and around his head was the blinding white light of the Throne.
He grew until he filled the earth and the heavens and was supreme
in his majesty.
There was another vision which he had, though its details varied
from dream to dream. The first time this vision illumined him he
was swimming in what seemed to be a river, with spiced odorous
banks; but presently the river had no visible banks but was like
the sea when viewed from far out; and Hareb was swimming
round and round in the sea, with no purpose, but like an aimless
wandering thing, lost and seeking the shore. Then a strange thing
happened. While he was swimming there appeared to him on all
sides lush borders that were of earth and yet not of earth; and
these borders were dense with the growth of only one plant, the
bloodred rose. There were countless millions of these roses; Hareb
swam up and gathered them by the armful and drew them into.
the depth where he swam, until all around him was water-depth
and rose-depth and he was the only living thing. In one variant
of this vision the sea in which he swam became the belly of a
woman and he was swimming around in this belly and reaching
out of it, through the door of life, and bringing into it the thous-
ands of roses, trying desperately but vainly to fill it with roses,
until he would have a great mattress of soft petals on which he
could curl up and lie, unafraid in primordial innocence. In an-
other variant he was ejected from the belly, in a mighty contrac-
tion of muscular power, as Jonah was thrown from the whale;
whereupon, like a swimmer deeply submerged, he shot up and
up until he reached the surface, gasping and spent and entangled
167in a slimy red sheath that seemed to be strangling him. He fought
to tear this sheath off him, to rend it like weak fabric; and when
at last freed from it he stood up, stood on the water, and walked
forth to the land. His only sensation then was that of a furnace
burning in his belly.
When he awoke from a vision Hareb knew that his soul had
been away from his body and had experienced these things. He
did not know if his soul left his body because it could no longer
endure its corruption and evil, or as a sign that Hareb was win-
ning in his struggle to overwhelm his flesh. He thought it meant
the second of these. He was pleased with the strength he was
showing in his war against hell and its demons, so pleased indeed
that one day, feeling a little indulgent toward weaker men, he
went out to see what Agios was doing.
Agios had survived his ordeal in the earth and his wounds and
soon looked, it seemed to Mark, stronger than before. His hair,
now about two inches long, covered and hid the many scars on
his skull. His swollen lips had healed and his eyes were again
clear and bright.
Hareb found him standing in the dreadful heat, his arms folded,
his face as impassive as usual and his whole frame looking as if
it would endure forever. To a stout leather cord tied round his
scrotum a stone was suspended. Hareb stared at it a moment and
then walked twice around the man, looking him up and down.
"How long you going to stand in this form?" he asked. There was
no reply. Agios had not looked at him. Agios had his gaze fixed
on the far-away sky where a soft piece of cloud was resting above
a mountain. Hareb wondered if the man intended to stand here
until his testicles dropped off or if he did these things only to im-
press the other monks and the nuns.
"If you would conquer the demons of lust," said Hareb, "you
must attack them where they are strongest. Where is that? In
a beautiful woman. If you will come with me to the city we'll
wrestle for our Lord and see who is the mightiest athlete. Will
you come?"
When again there was no reply Hareb began to taunt him.
"You'll not stand here till your stones drop off, will you? When
nobody can see you you kneel on the earth and rest; and when
somebody approaches you rise and stand this way. Is that not
true? Man, why don't you answer me? Do you imagine that some
day you'll be known as Saint Agios? Will Anthony be known as
168Saint Anthony? No, never. But I will be known as Saint Hareb.”
Hareb now faced him and looked at the eyes. "Have you descend-
ed to hell? I have."
His voice as unemotional as his face, Agios spoke. "You weak-
ling," he said, “go away.”
"Hah! You call me a weakling? I challenge you to come with
me to the city. We'll find the most beautiful whore and see which
of us can resist her. Will you come?"
"No."
"You dare not wrestle with Satan!"
"I've overthrown him," Agios said.
“Then why haven't you ascended? Why haven't the apostles
and prophets come down to take you to heaven? You've been
here for years," Hareb went on, his voice rasping with disgust,
"and your lusts are stronger than ever. Have you performed any
wonders? Not one that man has heard of." Hareb glanced down
at the private parts and at the stone, wondering if it was hollow
inside or was as heavy as it looked. He turned to gaze for a moment
at the hole where Agios had been buried to his chin. He searched
the desert roundabout for signs of fresh digging, for he suspected
that this man ate and drank more than Mark knew about.
"Will you go with me to the most beautiful whore?"
"In hoc signo vincas," Agios said.
After again walking round him, slowly, looking him up and
down, Hareb turned away, resolved with some great feat to humili-
ate and put to ignominious flight this dull pretender who had
never dared to do what a man must do to become a saint. He ex-
plored roundabout in the waste, searching for things with which
to make a fire. There was almost nothing but he did find a few
tiny sere plants and he took these over and stored them inside his
cell. All the while there had been growing in him, just under
consciousness, a hot and ferocious yearning to go over and look at
Elpha. He wanted to look, not at her womanly loveliness but at
eyes, for it was his belief that in the eyes of a beautiful woman
the Devil was to be seen stark and plain. When the impulse be-
came conscious he went over to the cell where the women were
and looked in. They were all gathered round the babe and were
discussing a name for it.
her
Alarmed, Helene looked up at Hareb and asked: "What do
you want?"
169"Where in all the desert," he said, "is there more wickedness
than here?" He was looking at Elpha.
Helene stood up. “On the contrary," she said sharply, "this is
the only spot here where God is present. Now go away."
"The gateway to hell," he muttered, still looking at the girl.
"Go away!" Helene cried.
"You women should all be filled with shame—”
She sprang at him, her hands smiting. "Shut up! Go off, you
fool, and wrestle with your demons! Women have work to do.”
Takuda arose hastily. "I think he needs me," she said, and went
over to Hareb's cell to see if he had water and food.
170XIX
While the monks struggled to save their souls four women strug-
gled to save a baby's life. In its first week it became sick and
Takuda said it would languish and die; but Helene, devoting her-
self to it day and night and praying to God for help, said no child
could ever die with four mothers. Between Helene and Julia
there developed a jealousy and a rivalry over it but when they
disputed one another's claims Takuda would rebuke them by
saying, "You're just like the two who came before King Solomon."
Then, feeling shame, Helene would say, "Well, it is her child.
All right, Julia, you hold it.”
At the end of two weeks the child seemed to be out of danger;
and Helene, who had now and then to chasten herself with the
thought that she was not its mother, and did not even have the
pleasure of nursing it or of finding little resemblances between
it and herself, cast about for ways by which she could establish
more claim to it, or at least feel herself to be a part of its life.
The child was a boy. One day Helene said: "I have a name
for it."
Julia, a quiet and gentle girl but a fiercely possessive mother,
looked at her and said, "What?"
"David."
"That's a Jewish name," said Takuda.
"What does the name mean?" asked Julia, for at this time all
names were symbols.
"The one who is loved.”
"But you have a David," said Julia, looking at her reproach-
fully.
171Turning to the mother Takuda said: "What would you like to
call it?"
Julia hesitated, looking down at the child. At last in a voice
barely audible she whispered: "Julius."
"That's only the man form of Julia," Helene said.
"You want the name David," said Julia with spirit, "because
you love a David."
"Julius is a Roman name," said Helene, as though that made
any difference.
"David is a Jewish name," said Takuda.
"A lot of Christians have Jewish names. Then how about Hil-
lel? That means the one who is praised."
"I like Julius," Julia said.
"As usual," said Helene spitefully, "you want everything your
way."
"It's my child," Julia said.
"It belongs to all of us," said Helene.
"For shame!” cried Takuda. “Remember the women and King
Solomon."
"Hyam is a nice name," said Helene. "It means life, and this
is life that God sent to us." She waited, looking at Julia. “Do you
like it?"
"No."
"You mean it has to be Julius or nothing?"
Takuda said: "Why not give all the names to Mark and ask him
to choose?"
"Will you do that?" Helene asked Julia. Julia was silent, her
head stubbornly bowed. "After all," Helene went on, "it's Mark
who buys our food. Without him the child would have died."
Julia looked up. "Then let Mark choose."
So they called Mark and told him the names and Mark said:
"They're fine names but why not call him for one of the apostles?"
"They were Jews," said Takuda.
"John is a fine name," Mark said.
"I don't like it," said Helene. "I knew a John who was wicked."
"Then," said Mark, "why not choose a name that means joy?—
for he seems to be the joy of four women." They thought of
names that meant sadness or toil or worry but none could think
of a name that meant joy. “Santo," said Mark. "In the Roman
language that means saint.”
172"We've seen enough of saints," said Helene. "We want a son
who will be human and who will love life."
"Theon," said Mark, "means godlike. How about that?"
"No, we just want a nice human name meaning something
nice."
"Urien," said Mark. "It means the heavenly one."
"No. Mark, please be serious.”
"Well," he said, trying to be tactful, "why not make up a name?
Take the first letter of Julia, the second of Helene, the third of
Takuda, the fourth of Elpha."
"What is that?" asked Helene.
"Jekh."
"Jekh," said Helene, listening to the sound. "It's kind of nice.
Julia, do you like it?”
"It's all right," Julia said.
And so the child was named Jekh, but Julia thought of it as
Julius, and Helene as David.
They had moved from Bion's cell to Zinara's. One day Helene
said: "We're not going to live in this stone hut, not five of us,
not on a floor of sand with no beds and never a bath in a hundred
years. We'll build a house."
"With what?" asked Takuda.
"Mark will buy things for us. He never gave up his property,
as Jesus told him to, and when I think of babies I'm not sure that
Jesus was right. For as long as men seduce women and babies are
born, mothers if not the fathers must care for them. If we all
went without purse or scrip over the world what would babies
do?"
Takuda shuddered, knowing that the words were profane; but
deep in her mother's heart she warmed to Helene and said, “We'll
build a house."
"Zinara," said Helene, "will you live with us?" Never once had
Zinara spoken since the baby came into her cell, or revealed by
even a glance that she was aware of it. She kept her hands busy
and prayed. "Zinara,” Helene cried, “you weeping fool! Babies
are still born.”
"Don't abuse her," Takuda said gently. "Let's go look at the
stones."
They went to the ruins of the old wall and Helene looked round
her and said: "We'll build a house right here."
And so it was that three of the women, while the fourth, turn
173by turn, held the child, labored day after day with stone and mor-
tar to put up the walls of a house. When convinced that they
really meant to do it Mark came to assist and at last offered to
bring masons out from the city.
"Fine!" Helene cried, dashing sweat from her brow. "Why
didn't you say so before?”
"Helene," he said, chiding her, "do you realize that you are be-
coming more and more shrewish? You're not the same girl who
came here."
“Oh?” she said, looking at him. "I suppose I'm not. Maybe
I'll become a witch.”
"I want you to be what you used to be."
"Never mind that. We want beds and utensils to cook with and
some kind of a vat to bathe in; and a few nice things to make it
homelike, such as curtains or draperies and cushions to sit on, and
a few pictures or images-"
"You can't bring such things to this place. The monks-”
"To hell with the monks!"
"Helene, what is happening to you anyway?”
“I said never mind that. A child was born and that has made
all the difference.”
"But you can't bring luxuries out here."
"Why not?" she asked, raising her brows.
"The monks and nuns-"
"But I don't care what they say. Mark, look, the more I see of
this saintly Christian life the more I despise it. Give us money
and we'll go to the city. But no, you won't do that! You insist
that we all stay in this God-forsaken loneliness, grinding sand be-
tween our teeth, smelling more and more like a privy, without
hope, light, life, love; living worse than the beasts, for even hogs
bathe themselves! And if we have a few little things to make our
house nice will that be wicked? Look up," she said, looking up
at the sky. "Do you think God will mind? I don't. God is not
what you men think he is."
"But luxuries in the desert-"
"We don't ask for luxuries, only for a table to eat on, a few
things to cook our food with, something to sleep on besides sand,
a way to keep ourselves from stinking. But if you don't want to
do this for us we'll do what we can."
"I haven't said I wouldn't. I only want you to know—”
"Yes, yes, you've said it. Mark, look. They all think we are four

They think of us as whores.
evil misbegotten creatures anyway.
worse of us than they already do?
Hareb over there is groaning and moaning because he thinks

She shrugged. Then she laughed, a bitter hard laugh. "I sup-

"I'm not worried." She looked round her. "Well, I must get to

While the women toiled Mark went to the city, and returned
with two masons and a caravan of supplies. He set up a tent for
the masons and put them to work. Then he went again to the
city and returned with an animal-train of building materials for
the roof, for nobody here could build an arched roof of stone on
a building so wide. The women assisted the masons, mixing and
carrying the mortar, rolling stones over to them, preparing their
meals. Two months after Helene moved the first stone the house
175was ready. It looked ugly and cruel like a fortress or a prison but
it had a stone floor and it shut out the terrible sun and it had two
windows, over which Helene would hang cloth, and a door that
could be closed and barred.
The winter season was a little cooler in the desert but Helene
would not have known that winter had come if Mark had not told
her. But, later, she felt the return of spring, though there were
no signs of it around her, no signs at all but the warmer emotion
in her body. In Antioch, she said to Takuda, the flowers were
blooming now and the trees, and the grass was green, and the or-
chards would soon be filled with their fruits. It was a beautiful
world that God had made but men would never know it.
"I've never understood men," Takuda said.
"You surely understood David. He loved life. But when he
comes where can we go so that he will not be found?—a slave
branded a slave all his life! Oh, but we'll find happiness some-
where. Takuda, don't you feel that he is coming?"
"I don't know."
"God has let me know that he will come."
One day she said to Mark that he ought to take his meals with
the women and be the man of the house.
"Some time we might have Hareb and Agios and Bion in to
dinner. We might have a great feast-roast goose and sweetcakes
and wine!"
Mark said gravely: "I'm afraid you don't have much reverence
for holy men."
"I don't. All right, then, when David comes we'll have the
feast."
"You still think he will come?"
"I have to think it if I go on living. He's handsome and head-
strong and impassioned and I love him. I love him and I know
he'll come, for I feel him in my heart and all through me and in
my dreams."
"But he'll have a slave's brand and he'll be-"
"I don't care. A man wouldn't marry such a woman but women
are not so vain. He will still be David.”
The women had made a stone table; the top of it was rough and
ugly, for they had no trowel to smooth the mortar. Helene cov-
ered it with a cloth and on this table they laid their meals and
sat round the table on the stone floor. They built a stone bath-
tub in one corner and its surface like the table was jagged and it
176leaked in many places but it served their purpose. Helene would
never forget her first bath in it. She carried warm water from the
well and filled the tub and after washing all the accumulated filth
off her body she lay in the tub with only her face out until most
of the water had leaked away. When she arose she felt wonderfully
refreshed. The other women bathed and daily they bathed the
child; and they washed the inside walls of their house and its
stone floor. They had straw mats on which they slept. They had
curtains that hung over the two open windows. The food, which
Mark had sent out to them once a week from the city, soon be-
came stale in the dry heat, but they softened it with water or by
wrapping it in wet cloths, and Helene pretended that every meal
was a feast. She wanted a privy for them and she had hoped that
Hareb would abandon his cell, so that they could convert it, pos-
sibly by contriving a stone seat; but Hareb in his perverse way
seemed delighted to find himself living close to four wicked
women. He was only about a hundred feet distant; when he
heard their laughter or gay voices, or Helene's really, for she was
the only one who laughed and tried to be gay, he groaned mightily
and recalled statements by the Church Fathers that all laughter
and gaiety came from hell. He groaned again when he sniffed the
warm sensuous odors of cooking or heard the sounds of eating; but
if Takuda went to him with a delicacy, such as a piece of meat
or a biscuit soaked in meat or fruit juices, he would turn on her
in dreadful wrath and try to strike it from her hand.
"You brought me to the door of hell!" he would roar at her.
"You'll never let me rest till you've pushed me in!”
If food was taken to Mark he smiled and ate it and said again
that he was weak and would never be more than a minor saint, or
perhaps no saint at all. He would not take meals with them in
the house or even enter the house. As it was, he said, he was
wicked enough and they shouldn't tempt him beyond his strength.
"If I save my soul," he said, "it will be by the skin of my teeth.”
"You buy the food, you should share it."
"Once in a while," said Mark.
The monks roundabout and the nuns looked on the house of
the four women as a house of wickedness. For there was laughter
in it, feasting and bathing, and a whore and a mother with a bas-
tard child.
"Let's bring Zinara over," Helene said one day.
She and Takuda went to the cell and when they were unable to
177get Zinara to speak or even to look at them Helene grasped the
woman's arm and dragged her to her feet. Takuda then seized
the other arm and together they took her sobbing and lamenting
over to the house. They gave her a mat in one corner and told
her that she could sit there. When she dragged the woman to her
feet Helene had been appalled, for Zinara was little more than
skin and bones. Helene now prepared a plate of food and took it
to her but Zinara would not touch it; and when they awoke the
next morning Zinara was gone. Some time during the night she
had slipped away, and Helene found her in the cell, her hands
busy and her lips praying. So far as Helene knew, Zinara never
left her cell: what the woman did with her excrement was a mys-
tery that Helene was never to solve.
Early one morning Helene, lying awake on her mat, was startled

only a commonplace Christian greeting but Helene was angered.
"None of your business," she said. Then, curiously, she turned
to look at Julia but Julia pretended to have eyes only for her

"May I come in?"
"No."

say."
He sat. Smiling, urbane, sinister, a man of the world, he usually
had his way with women. The crafty rogue knew that she was
frightened. He knew there was not a monk here who would raise
a hand to protect her, save possibly Mark and he was not afraid
of Mark. Why should any monk try to protect this woman, on
whom they looked as one of Satan's agents? He was gentle, know-
ing that against gentleness in men women found it difficult to
raise their temper.
"What have I to say? That more and more I love you. I have
179waited for you
the better part of a year and that is a long time for

While his soft insinuating voice told of his woes she looked at

He made a statement that angered her. He said, reaching for

"You lie!"
"Now don't be angry, for anger makes a woman witless and
dull. The poet Catullus wooed one as reluctant as you. What did

She drew a sharp breath. "I know better."
"He's dead. I ask you what her sister asked Dido: how can you

He seemed a little dashed. "My name is Apollo but if you'd

Helene, you must take a stand. You know that I will have you

“I've taken my stand." She moved to go.

180like me, whose passion burns all the hotter for being spurned,
there comes a time when love mixes with hate; and then the man,

The poet says the flame ate at her very bones. Seeing the passion
growing in her lover she felt the coming change and feared safety
itself. So the poet says."
"Are you the father of Julia's child?"
"One of the monks,” he said blandly. "Or possibly the Devil.
Who but God will ever know?"
Staring at him with fascination and horror she said: "You're

"But do women love virtuous men? I've never known one.

who is the cause of sin, is glorified by the passion of man,
who was
created in the image of God. In the fires and raptures of love all

or with fire? With fire, and what fire burns as hot as love?"
"Is this all you had to tell me?—that a man more wicked than
Satan himself can purify a woman?”
"That's the story the poets have written. If you had read Catul-

heaven itself when two lovers catch and clasp each other, tongue

Plautus said."
"He must have been an awful bore."
"He was a great poet who knew what passions burn in the
human body. Most of the Christians, Helene, are all wrong. They
would deny these passions, as Hareb is trying to do; but passion
is the furnace in which we purify our souls. It is through passion,
not through fasting and prayers, that we will be saved.” His glow-

men do with base ore? They put it in a furnace and with heat
they separate the gold from the dross. The pure gold that emerges
is the soul. The rest is washed back to the earth. Our bodies are
like that. There is gold in them and we have to free it with fire,
not with the cold and chill of chastity. . . ."
181Did he believe these things? She thought he did. That was
what made the man so terrifying. In his way he was a dedicated
person-dedicated, she was convinced, to the Devil but without
knowing that he was Satan's man. After all, Peta and many other
Christian men had the same belief. She glanced round her, hop-
ing that Mark would come in sight.
In the same gentle insistent voice Apollo was saying, "You will
surrender to me at last, so why delay it? Think of the raptures that
we are missing. If you delay too long, until the fires in me burst
beyond my will-"
Abruptly she got up, saying, "I must go now."
"You're afraid," he said, rising to stand and face her. "That's
a good sign. A woman feels fear first, then helplessness. All right,
I'll let you go; but don't forget that I'm not a man to be put off
forever. Love is better when both lovers acquiesce. When one
struggles the other must redouble his will and strength, until
both completely surrender to the fire."
"Why don't you leave me alone?" she asked, her voice tense.
"The world is full of women."
"But not as beautiful as you and not with your passion. Yes,
the world is full of cold women, whom it takes more than a man's
heat to melt. Very well, go back to your house."
She walked away from him, feeling weak and afraid, yes, great
God, feeling almost helpless, like females among the beasts she
had seen, whom males with furious passion forced to surrender—
males like the ram, the bull, the cock. Trembling she entered the
house and went over to sit on her bedmat. She hated herself
because his words had stirred her-had, may God forgive her,
stirred the womanly hungers in her body, the wonder in her mind.
Was it true then that women were wicked, so wicked that a man
had only to persist to compel their surrender? Sitting on the mat
she tried to examine her emotions and her attitute toward this
scoundrel; and when she felt an impulse to pray she denied it,
thinking, I will not be so weak! That was one trouble with
women: when men, like the barnyard cock, chased them into a
corner they clasped their stupid hands in prayer and supplication
and asked God to be their decency and their will. She would
manage this lecher some way and she would do it herself without
God's help; she would do it because of her belief that she was vir-
tuous and that women, no matter what the story of Eve, had more
character and honor than men; because she despised him with all
182her soul; because she loved David and would wait for him; and
above all because she was not a silly hen to be run down and cov-
ered, nor a cow to be frightened by a pair of flashing horns.
But later when night approached she went outside to look for
Mark. She did not dare now to go very far from the house: Apollo,
she supposed, was concealed somewhere to spy on her, and no
matter how virtuous a woman might be she did not have a man's
strength and in a struggle had to yield. After a little she saw
Mark and called to him and he came over.
"I must talk to you," she said. She then became aware that
Mark was looking at her shrewdly, that Mark knew what her prob-
lem was. She felt furious. For what was the world that God had
made but men who preyed on women while other men, less strong
of will or of passion, stood back and watched and smirked! Will
he succeed?-will she yield?
"No," she said. "I guess I don't want to talk to you after all.
I don't like the look on your face. You're wondering if Apollo is
going to seduce me. Well, let me put your unclean curiosity to
sleep. He won't. He may drive me to kill myself. For if a woman
is weaker in strength, yet has no father or husband or brother to
protect her, what can she do? Rape is common enough, isn't it?
But I want to tell you this, that never in all my life have I had
such contempt for men and the contempt is growing. I was going
to ask you for a dagger but when I saw the look on your face, the
look a man has for women, I couldn't ask you. We are neither
your weaklings, Mark, to protect when you will, nor your whores.
God gave us much the worst of it. Very well. Out of our physically
inferior position we'll develop character greater than men can
ever have. We'll solve our problem somehow, some day. The
time will come when men named Mark will not smirk at us, nor
men named Apollo arrogantly predict our surrender." She was
weeping. When the tears fell to her cheeks she burned with shame
for her weakness. She dashed the tears away and looked upon him
with the wide misty beauty of her eyes.
"I'm sorry," Mark said gravely. "I see now that I have mis-
judged you."
"How could you help it? How could any man help it? For it
is true, I suppose, that most women yield if men persist long
enough. You men know that about us, and so you can predict-
and wait. But if women yield it's not because they want to. It's
183because in the kind of world God made they have to. Well-"
She caught her breath and then smiled.
"Do you want the dagger?"
"Oh, I guess not. I'll take care of myself somehow."
"You know this man won't stop at anything?"
"I know it. Neither will I. He has more strength, I'll have
more guile. He has Satan on his side, I have God on mine. So let
Mother Ida and the nuns shudder with anticipation. This much
I know: David will come to me some day and David will teach
this monster a lesson that I haven't the strength to teach him.”
"If he makes a pact with Satan—”
"I'm not afraid of Satan."
"But if he has all of Satan's power on his side-"
"I'll have God's on mine.”
Mark shook his head sadly. He said she refused to understand
that since Eve's sin God did not protect women.
"Then I'll protect myself."
"But this man is going to sell his soul to Satan."
"Let him sell it."
"It would be better to go away with me."
"No, I'll stay here. The look on the faces of two men has
brought me to my senses. Thanks a lot but I'll stay here.”

Hareb put off day by day going to the city to wrestle with Satan
but when Mark told him that Agios was submitting to another
incredible ordeal Hareb aroused himself. He asked Mark for
enough money to pay for a night with the most beautiful harlot
in the city and Mark gladly gave it to him. He gave him two gold
coins and after a moment of hesitation gave him a third, saying:
"You may need three. Be sure you find the most beautiful one,
the one who is the favorite with wealthy men."
"What is her name?"
"Which is the great favorite I do not know. It may be Thais.
You'll inquire among the fops and they'll tell you but first come
and see Agios."
"I've no time for fools."
Agios was no fool, Mark said, but a most extraordinary saint
who had again thrown the devil. Again he had buried himself in
the sands but this time he had lain down in a trench and Mark
had covered him, leaving only his face and his private parts ex-
posed to the sun. He was fasting but for dry salt, which he ate
daily to increase the torments of his thirst.
But Hareb said no, that a man must wrestle with the Devil in
his stronghold and not out in the sands. He set off for the city.
On arriving there, dusty and fatigued, he learned the names of
several beautiful and popular harlots; and one, called Pyralis with
the Curly Mound, was said to be the most irresistible woman since
Helen or Aspasia. She had thick curly hair and eyes as black as
a smithy's pot; and the hair on her mound was silken curls which
she scented with the odors of the Lost Isles. Another fop thought
Helice more seductive; and still another, Kiki; but Hareb at last
185settled on Thais because, like many of the monks, he had heard of
her as one whom no man had ever resisted. It was told of her that
as a child she had been so beautiful that her mother, an evil
woman, had forced Thais to take up a position in the market,
whence her fame spread; that of all the men who had come to
look at her none had been content merely to gaze on the perfec-
tion of her face, for the reason that almost at once she burned
like a fire in their hearts; that some, physically unpleasant them-
selves yet consumed with ardor, had sold everything they owned
and offered it to this woman for one night of love. She was now
a woman in the full ripeness of her powers; and because she was
so famous and had found no man in all the world who could re-
sist her allurements, Hareb thought that Satan would be more
complacently enthroned in her chambers than in those of Pyralis
or Helice or any other.
She lived in one of the handsomest buildings in the city and had
an elderly and exhausted libertine who served as her manager.
To this man Hareb presented himself and stated his wish and
offered a gold dinar.
"What is this?" asked the old man, sneering. "Do you think
with this pitiful sum to spend a night with Thais?"
The crafty Hareb replied: "Go tell her that if I resist her she
will pay me three dinar and if I do not resist her I will pay her
three."
"You'll pay three," said the man and went to find his mistress.
He returned to say, "My glorious lady, the famous Thais, most
beautiful of all women, accepts your proposal, and begs to tell
you that if you resist her she will pay you twice the sum that you
have offered."
Slowly, deeply, Hareb drew his breath, as though to confine and
shackle his baser passions. "Take me to her," he said.
He was ushered into a sumptuous apartment, more lavishly fur-
nished by far than any he had ever seen; and at once his nostrils
were assailed by sweet spices and incense and what he took to be
the aroma of hot wines. The odor of women in the rut he also
thought he detected. The door behind him was closed. There
he stood, alone, this monk from the desert, in his filthy and tat-
tered piece of rag, his hair matted and gray with dust, his blue-
veined hands clenched, his fierce eyes spying out the details of the
large chamber. On a far table he could see vapors rising, as though
from a bowl of wine over a brazier. He stood waiting, breathing
186deeply, sniffing, thinking that this was indeed the very citadel of
Satan, that here he would overthrow him.
When at last Thais entered from another room she came lightly
with a birdlike tread, a silken robe swishing round her, jeweled
sandals flashing on her feet, her great dark eyes looking across at
Hareb as she came. She faced him, a wondrously beautiful woman,
so clean, so exquisitely robed, so delicately scented, her full white
breasts half-exposed, her lips smiling across white teeth.
"You are Hareb?" she said. Her voice was that of one who had
spent many hours cultivating it, until it was as pure as music.
"I'm Hareb," he said, his voice rather hoarse.
"You are the one who offered me the wager." Her eyes for a
moment were colder than ice but Hareb did not know it. "Now
let us be sure that we understand. If you can resist my love I will
give you six gold coins, each of the value of a dinar; and if you
cannot resist me you will give me three. The period is to be from
now until daylight. Is that our bargain?”
His ferocious eyes had been studying her face, his ferocious
emotions seeing in this woman Satan's chief agent in the whole
world. "Yes," he said.
"Are you a Christian?"
"I am."
Her whole face softened in a girlish smile. "You're very hand-
some," she said. "You have the kind of beard I'd expect to find
on an emperor, and the mustaches I'd expect to find on an assassin.
You have the kind of eyes I'd expect to find in the Christian god.'
Her gaze roved from his brows to his chin. "Well, Hareb," she
said, glancing down at his filth, "before we see whether love or
your Christ is stronger you'll have to bathe."
"I never touch water," he said.
"Oh, but that is no part of our bargain. I am as you see me.
If you are cleansed will you then be weaker? Do you Christians
cover your passions with filth, so that none would wish to chal-
lenge them? Is that the way you protect yourselves?”
"As I am you see me," he said, looking into her amused and
mocking eyes. "I will resist you.'
"My dear handsome brigand, that remains to be seen. In any
case you must bathe and put on other garments."
He was suspicious. "If I do that you'll work some charm on

"Oh no, good heavens! I have no charm but what you see."

195stiffened and thrust out, as his breathing came like stifled sobs and
then as anguished gaspings, and spasms like hot pain ran over his
sweating flesh and the tides of the sea gathered to engulf him. .
An hour later he lay prone on a couch, his horror too deep for
groans, his suffering too deep for thought. In his numbed and
spent body, in his raped mind, there was only the dull horrible
aching sensation of a tortured being who had lost his soul. And
what was Thais thinking? She also was subdued and strangely
chastened. Slowly, with amazement that accumulated piece by
piece until the pattern of incredulity was complete, she realized
that the man either was not conscious or only dimly conscious at
best of what had happened. His stupor was so deep that he was
like one who had been-she groped for the word—who had been
-castrated! That was it. Neither hands on his body nor words
spoken loud and shrill in his ear now communicated with his mind.
His breathing was a little frightening, like that of one in deep
sleep. When she drew a lid back from one eye the eye looked
blind. When she raised a hand and released it it fell like a dead
hand. What had she done?
With incredulity and amazement occupying the whole house of
her senses she stood by the couch looking down at him and hear-
ing only his slow deep breathing. She put a hand to her brow and
drew it across and felt moisture. She stared round her with a
strangely guilty self-conscious look on her face, almost a look of
shame. She then went softly to a chair and sank. She wanted to
laugh, she felt that she ought to laugh, but only stared dully at
the carpet and at last recognized its familiar pattern, as she might
have after long absence; at the curtains over a window and saw
that they were faintly stirred by a breeze; at a censer, and noted
its wisp of pale blue smoke; and then at Hareb on the couch, her
gaze coming back to him as a gaze might that had looked away
from a dead thing. The silence was like the haunting depths of
night just before morning. Well, she would have to get him out
of here. She would have to arouse him somehow and get his old
rag on him and send him back to the desert and his prayers. Why
had the fool come here? What had happened to him? She had
seen men drunk but this man had drunk no wine. She had seen
men drugged with aphrodisiacs but this man was drugged with
something more dreadful than that. She thought of the story he
had told, of Adam and Eve, of Eve's sin and Adam's fall and ex-
pulsion; and she wondered if this thing that had happened to
196Hareb, leaving him witless and senseless, was somehow re-
lated....? But no! It was all too silly to think of.
After another hour she went to the bathroom and returned
with his piece of garment. At the couch she said, "Hareb?" When
she clasped his arms to shake him he astonished and frightened
her by suddenly leaping like a wild man from the bed and seizing
his rag and hastening to the bathroom. After only a moment he
came out, with the rag hanging from his waist, and headed for
the door. She ran over to put herself in his way, for she wanted
him to speak, to tell what terrible thing had happened to him; but
he moved around her, yet without looking at her, and in that
moment she saw his eyes. They were such bleak and desolate
eyes, eyes filled with such wordless suffering, eyes so empty of every-
thing but pain, and his whole face was fixed in such unutterable
despair that she recoiled from him; and afterward she was to for-
mulate it this way and always to think of it this way: He looked
as a man might look who had just been castrated. Was that all of
it? There had been more, oh, there was more in that dreadfully
sad countenance, in those eyes that had caught and mirrored all
of human suffering, as he stumbled across the room and opened
the door and let himself out. She followed him and saw him enter
the street and vanish.
She returned slowly and closed the door, as she might have
closed it on one who had died. In the middle of the chamber she
stood looking round her, wondering if the thing had been real or
if she had dreamed it; and without looking down she put a hand
down to examine herself. She went to the bed and climbed up and
then flung herself, burying her face; and when she tried to laugh,
feeling that she ought to laugh, she was amazed, for she found
herself sobbing, found herself weeping as she had not wept since
she was a small child. . .
