Hareb went down the darkened street, not with the free aggressive
stride that had always been the sign of his will, but with the weary
shuffle of an old man. When daylight came he was out in the
desert, going back to his cell. He knew, but only with the vague-
ness of one numbed by nightmare, that he had wrestled with Satan
and had been thrown. He had met Eve in the garden, and now,
like Adam, with the hot anger of God upon him, driven forth as
a lost wanderer to find his salvation, he was face to face with all
the loosed powers of hell. Some days would pass before he would
become aware of the full enormity of his defeat-before he could
fully accept the fact that he, Hareb, who had dedicated himself
to be holiest of the saints, had walked boldly into Satan's citadel
only to succumb, like any ordinary man, to blandishments and
lust. The awful shock of the thing, the ghastly realization of his
human weakness, almost unseated his mind; and Hareb from the
moment he left the harlot's chambers would never be the same
man again.
It was early forenoon when he came to his cell and shuffled in-
side. He sank against a wall, numbed with shock, sick with shame
and horror; and when, making the sign of the cross, he tried to
pray, he was aghast, for he could not recall a single word of the
Lord's or of any other prayer. He could remember none of the
psalms. No, but the wicked words came, the words, The heavens
and the earth melt and coalesce and the earth becomes heaven
when a man's lips sink into the ripeness of a woman's mouth. . . .
What had Paul said? He could not remember, but there came
words, What rapture when a man buries his face in the valley of
a woman's breasts and presses the breasts to his cheeks! . . . What
198had the Fathers said? He could not remember, but these words
were upon him: With lips clasped round the serpent's head. . . .
For days and nights he was to be tormented by the phantom of a
beautiful woman, the delicate texture of her skin, her womanly
odors, her warm lips seeking over him. For him it was as it had
been with the saintly man Theodore, who walking through a mon-
astery by night, saw unexpectedly the phantom full of deepest de-
ceits, and beauty of so indescribable a nature that it was not of
this world; until the saint's face turned color and his limbs trem-
bled. What had he said to dispel the evil? Hareb could not re-
member. It was with him as it had been with that other saint, in
the desert nigh unto Shaina, who with more than human strenuous-
ness gave himself to the holy life; when the Tempter, having asked
God for him as he had asked God for Job, showed him the form of
a woman surpassingly beautiful, who wandered as a phantom in
the desert or came into his cell, to beseech him to let her rest
there; but then with soft hands and softer words played over his
flesh and his soul, whispering in his ears sweet incitements to lust;
until at last she took hold of his hand and next of his beard and
then of his neck and made him her captive. And what was then
told of him?-that he became like the man who had lost his mind,
like a stallion mad with desire; and above in the atmosphere the
devils cried loud with triumph and laughter, saying, Whosoever
shall exalt himself shall be humbled! The saint then smote his
breast and cried, and gave all his thoughts to redemption, yet
knew that one brought to a senseless state of despair would never
stand again.
His will had been enfeebled and he had no power to dispel the
women who came to tempt him. Their hands were there, playing
over him; their tongues softly entering his ears; their womanly
odors, suffusing his breath until in his fitful sleep he burst with
strangled gaspings; their bewitching murmurings, the sinuous
softness of their white limbs, the passion on their mouth. . . . He
fasted, he tried to pray; but all day and all night they were with
him, whether his eyes were shut or open; for he saw them when
his eyes were wide open, swarming at his cell door, naked and
smiling and showing themselves off most wantonly; and he saw
them no less clearly when his eyes were closed. Awake or asleep,
it made no difference; the whole earth around him hummed with
their music and their voices and like the desert plant was strong
with the smell of their hungers and like the heavens was luminous
199with the lights of their eyes; and he trembled and shuddered and
knew himself doomed. Alone he was and friendless and doomed
and there was no power to save him. For the power of women
was so softly insistent, so constant, that he felt he must inevitably
be drawn back to the city, to hurl himself into all the wickedness.
he could find and the hot baths of pleasure, until his body would
be filled with it like a dead thing with rot. . . .
The next day after his return Takuda came to the doorway and
looked in. She sensed that there was a change in him but the
nature of it eluded her dull sad mind. She spoke his name but
there was no response. He did not see her or hear her. If all the
monks and nuns here had gathered round him he would not have
seen or heard. He was sunk too deep in torments and horrors,
deep below the level of all his senses, deep in his mystical intui-
tions that in these days were the only life he had. When Takuda
put food or water to his lips he was not aware. Even on that day,
almost a month later, when Thais came out he did not see her as
a woman but only as a phantom.
She came with two servents, a man and a woman, and all three
rode handsome horses with glittering livery. She saw Mark first
and said: "Where is the monk called Hareb?"
"In his cell," said Mark, indicating Hareb's cell. He drew close
and looked up at her face. "Are you the one?"
"What are you saying?"
"Something terrible happened to him," said Mark, studying
her beautiful face. "Yes, you must be the one." Helene had ap
proached them and now heard Mark ask: "Are you Thais?"
"How would you know who I am?"
"Then you are Thais," Mark said. "You are the one he went
to."
"Did he tell you?"
"No. He has not spoken."
"You speak in riddles," said Thais impatiently. "I came to see
the man called Hareb."
"What happened to him?" asked Mark.
She turned to Helene. "Where is he?”
Helene indicated Hareb's cell. Thais slipped lightly down from
the horse and then, trying to pick her way, and grimacing because
sand filled her sandals, went over to the cell, with Helene and
Mark following her. At the door Thais peered in and saw Hareb
bowed over his knees, his head drooping.
200"Hareb," she said. She turned to Mark. "Is he sick?"
"Something happened to him," Mark said.
Thais went inside and knelt in the sand by Hareb and said
softly, "Hareb, won't you speak to me?”
"She's the one," Mark said to Helene.
Thais was whispering to Hareb but they could not hear what
she said. There was no movement in him. If he heard her voice
it was only one among the many seductive voices he had been
hearing for days. She tried to put a hand under his chin and
raise his head but his whole body seemed to be rigid. She aban-
doned the effort at last and came outside.
"What is wrong with him?" she asked, looking queerly at Mark.
"Something happened to him," Mark said.
"Where?”
"In the city. Did Satan throw him?"
"Do you have to speak in riddles?"
Icily Helene said, "Why have you come to see him?"
Thais fixed Helene with her full dark eyes and her eyes turned
cold. "Is this man your lover?"
"We are Christians. We don't have lovers."
"No?" said Thais, sneering. "Well, a moment ago I heard a
baby cry over in that building. Where did it come from?”
Helene caught her breath. Mark said quickly, "Did Satan
throw him?"
“Oh, by the gods!" she cried. "What are you talking about?"
“You. Hareb went to see you, didn't he? He wrestled with
Satan and Satan threw him and now you have come to gloat over
him."
"To gloat?" she said, looking into Mark's eyes. "What queer
things you Christians are!" With a gesture she summoned her
servants, and the man came over with her horse. He helped her
to mount. Looking down at Mark she said, “Tell Hareb I came.”
"What shall I tell him you came for?"
"Tell him I'll come again."
Then she rode away, a beautiful woman in the most costly
raiment Helene had ever seen. Mark and Helene stared after her,
and Takuda also, who had come up, until the riders disappeared
into the haze.
"What did happen?" asked Helene.
Mark looked at her curiously. "What do you think?"
"I've no idea."
201"That was Thais," said Mark, "the most beautiful whore in
the city and perhaps in all the world."
"She is very beautiful," Helene said.
"Hareb went to her to wrestle with Satan. But what happened?
I gave him three gold coins and he did not return them."
"Satan threw him," said Takuda. "That's why he acts as he
does."
Mark looked at Helene. "Do you think so?"
"Well, if he had thrown Satan he would have come back tri-
umphant, wouldn't he?"
“But if she had her way with him why does she come now?"
"Does a man know these things!"
"Not this man," said Mark, his grin foolish.
"She's attracted to him, I think."
"The most beautiful woman in all of Egypt attracted to him?"
"You men don't know much about women. You men look for
a beautiful face and body. Women look for something else."
"You mean she's in love with him?”
"Perhaps. Her voice was very tender when she spoke to him.
There was something in her face when she came out.”
"I don't understand," Mark said.
"Don't try to. You men never will."
"But she's a whore," Mark said.
"She is a woman first. Whores can love, can't they? This woman
has known hundreds of men and they have all been alike. Then
she finds one who is different."
"How?"
"Oh, let's not talk about it.
think maybe I know what it is.
She entered the cell and knelt and for a few moments she lis-
tened. His breathing was labored as though he were asleep but
she was sure he was not asleep. She rubbed a hand softly back

She looked at him wonderingly and thought, She bathed him!
Then, putting her face close to Hareb, she sniffed and thought

alarmed. There was no response at all.
Hareb is a lot like David and I
I'll see if he'll talk to me."
202She went outside and found Mark alone. Anxiously Mark
said, “Did he speak?”
"She bathed him."
"She bathed him!"
"Her scents still cling to him."
"I don't understand," said Mark, shaking his head gloomily.
"If Satan threw him he should have come back to fight with a
lion's strength."
"Do you remember the story of Delilah?"
"Samson and Delilah," he said. "But she cut his hair."
"Yes," said Helene, amused, "she cut his hair."
"His hair grew out again," Mark said.
Helene laughed. "Maybe Hareb's will grow again."
"He's doomed," Mark said.
"Mark, in heaven's name, don't be so solemn! Is it such a

"I know what you mean," said Mark with dignity. "Yes, it is
dreadful. He will lose his soul."
"And maybe she'll find hers."
Another week passed and there was no change in Hareb that
anybody could see. All the monks and nuns of the area now knew
that Hareb had gone to the city to wrestle with Satan and had
been thrown. Most of them thought that he was still in the clutch
of the Devil, and anxiously, as though all of human life depended
on the outcome, they waited, chastened and fearful, to see if God
or the Devil would win. The dreadful struggle of this man, sit-
ting alone in his cell, became a symbol of the whole mighty strug-
gle everywhere between good and evil on earth. Will God win?
they asked; or Satan?
“He will starve to death," said the more practical Takuda; and
one day Mark went in to see if he could arouse Hareb. He tried
to relax the rigid posture and failed; and while he was struggling
to bring the hands into view he discovered that they were fixed
with a savage grip on the private parts. This so shocked Mark
that he went outside and stared wildly round him, dashing sweat
from his brow. After a few moments he went back in and he
thrust a hand down to feel over Hareb's hands; and when he
drew his hand forth he saw the blood on it. My God! he thought.
Was this another Christian who had made himself a eunuch for
the kingdom of heaven's sake? He tried to examine Hareb to de-
termine the state of things but the man was rigid, as rigid as he
203would have been if he were dead. Mark thought to roll or push
him over but he felt shame and again he went outside.
Great God, what was to be done! Were they to let him sit here
until he died? If they did that his soul would be lost, for it was
clear to Mark that this man was in the clutch of the Devil and
had no power to free himself. He was a prisoner as completely as
though chained to the floor of hell.
Helene wanted to summon a physician from the city but of
what use were physicians, Mark asked, against the Devil? Did she
not know that all disease and sickness was punishment for sins?
What they needed was a powerful exorcist who would loosen the
Devil's grasp on this monk, and send him away, abashed and crest-
fallen. If only the blessed Anthony would come! As it was, Hareb
would lose his soul just as sure as sunrise.
"He'll starve to death," Takuda said.
It was impossible to get food and water down Hareb's throat.
He was like a man whose chest had grown to his knees, and if
they were to roll him over and try to relax him they would only
expose his shame. The horror of the situation deeply affected
them all. Mark thought he should try to find Anthony, but where
was Anthony? He might be over in the Nitre valley or at Tabena
or at Scete; or he might be with the monks somewhere in the holy
land, or as far away as the Syrian deserts. For weeks or months.
a man could ride and never see so much as Anthony's shadow.
Helene asked if there were no other monks holy enough to cast
out devils. Well yes, Mark said, there were; but like Hilarion
they were immured in cells or they were far away. Agios for years
had wrestled triumphantly and might now be holy enough but
he would not come.
Helene went out to see him. After his second ordeal in the sands
Agios had decided to build another cell, and Helene found him
toiling and sweating. She told him that Hareb would die if some-
thing was not done for him, and there was clearly revealed to her
what she had seen before in the eyes of monks but had never be-
lieved. In the eyes of Agios she saw the wicked delight of a man
who had triumphed over a competitor. She thought there could
be no doubt of it: though the eyes never once looked at her they
flashed and gleamed and over his whole face was the expression
of a man hugely pleased with himself.
"Mark says you're a holy man and a saint. He thinks there's a
devil in Hareb and that you can drive it out. Will you come?"
204From the first moment of looking at him she had known that he
would not come. The fierce flashing of his eyes spoke of his pride
and his scorn and his triumph. "Why won't you come?" she asked
sharply. “If you can cast the devil out— But then, perhaps you
can't!"
Agios never paused in his labor. He had gathered a pile of
stones and was laying the foundation for a cell; and his gaze
flashed past her and around her like an evil light but never rested
on her; and though it was plain from his face that he heard and
understood he said not a word. He was a holy man, busy with his
own salvation; he had no interest in anything but that.
She returned to Mark and said, "He won't come. He won't
even speak. I don't think he's a holy man at all, but a selfish mon-
ster."
He was a very holy man, Mark said. For years he had wrestled
with Satan and won. Where in all the world did she know of a
man as saintly as Agios?
Then unexpectedly there came a holy monk from the area of
Tabena, one Piterius by name, who had heard that in this colony
lived a woman who had found God. Piterius was a small elderly
man, very gentle and soft-spoken and with kindly eyes. He
knocked first on the door where the women lived and when
Helene opened the door he said:
"Good morning. Are you one of the sisters in our Lord?" He
waited a moment. "There is one here, I am told, who is holy
above all women roundabout. Her name is Zinara. Where is
she?"
“Zinara?”
"It is written, Whoso wishes to become a wise man let him
first become a fool, in order that he may become wise. Whoso
would become a saint, let him first humble himself. Where is
this humble woman who eats the scraps thrown from the table
and drinks the water in which nuns wash their hands?"
"Wait," she said. She came outside and called to Mark, and
Mark came over from Hareb's cell. "Here is one who wants to
see Zinara."
"Who is this man?"
The monk said he was Piterius, a companion of the blessed
Anthony. He had come a long distance to see the sister called
Zinara, for the fame of her had spread and Anthony himself had
blessed her. Mark and Helene took him to Zinara's cell. She was
205weeping and praying and mending a garment, her thin pale hands
trembling.
Piterius stepped inside and looked down at her. "Is this the one
who eats the leavings and drinks the wash water and labors for
her sisters?"
Mark said: "This is the one."
"Then I say to you that she is blessed above her sisters, for while
they serve themselves she serves them, and she prays for them who
have cast her out. Like our Lord she loves those who treat her
with contempt and returns goodness for evil." He knelt before
Zinara and bowed his head to the earth, saying: "Sister, put your
hand on my head and bless me." Mark ran over to fetch Mother
Ida. When Zinara did not move, Helene took one of her hands
and put it on the gray head of Piterius and said, "Bless him."
In a whisper Zinara said, “May God bless you."
Piterius was still kneeling when Mother Ida came to the door.
"What does this mean?" she asked. "Does a saint seek the blessing
of the most wicked woman among us?"
Piterius arose and turned to Ida. "What did you say?"
"That you have disgraced yourself, for this is a woman of sin.”
"This woman is your mother and mine and I ask God to give
me a portion with her in the day of judgment. I would bless her
but I am not worthy. What did our Lord say to such as Zinara?—
Blessed are you when men shall hate you, and when they shall
separate you from their company, and shall reproach you, and cast
out your name as evil, for the son of man's sake. Rejoice in that
day, Zinara, and leap for joy; for, behold, your reward is great in
heaven; for in the like manner did their fathers unto the prophets.
And woe unto you," he said to Ida, "when all men shall speak
well of you; for so did their fathers to the false prophets. Bless
them who curse you, and pray for them who despitefully use you,
as this woman has done. It was our Lord who said, Judge not and
you shall not be judged; condemn not and you shall not be con-
demned. Great will be the glory of this woman in heaven, for
she sinned, yet is forgiven, having forgiven her enemics. Why
haven't you taken her into your house?"
“In our house," said Ida with dignity, "we are all virgins."
"But this woman is greater than the virgin. Do the blind out
here try to lead the blind?" He turned to Mark. "How long has
this sister sat alone, praying for those who wickedly used her?"
"For years," Mark said.
206"She can live with us," said Helene, "but we are not holy
women."
"Who is the holiest woman?" Piterius asked, looking at her.
"The one who offers shelter and companionship or the one who
casts her sister out?" He reached down to take Zinara's hand.
"Rise now, for your sins are forgiven." Helene was astonished at
the alacrity with which Zinara got to her feet. She turned, tremb-

said and he led her to the house where Helene lived. At the door

neither thought I myself worthy to come unto you; and the sol-
dier said, Go, and he goes; come, and he comes; do this, and he
does it; but our Lord said, Suffer all the homeless and the outcast
to come unto me."
Mark said to her in a whisper, "This is a saint of great holiness,
he can cast the devil out of Hareb." When Zinara had gone into

him. Can you cast it out?"
"Where is this sinner?" asked Piterius, and they took him to
Hareb's cell. He went inside and looked at the bowed figure.
"How long has he sat thus?"
"For many days."
Mark told the story. Hareb had come here resolved to be the
holiest of all the saints, but seeing that Agios did one thing he
determined on another, and went to the city to wrestle with Satan
in his own citadel. He sought out the most beautiful of all the
harlots, the one called Thais, and what happened then Mark said
he did not know but he suspected that Satan had thrown him.
For when Hareb returned he was like another man, bowed down
with grief and shame; and he had gone into his cell and had sat
ever since as he was now. He would not take food or water or
speak or move and they were afraid that he would die.
Piterius said there must be a very powerful demon in him.
"Can you cast it out?"
"I myself am too unworthy to cast out demons of such power.”
"Where is Anthony? Have you seen him?"
"Not for a long time. Tell me, has this demon spoken?-for
you will recall that when our Lord came down to Capernaum he
found there a man with an unclean spirit, which cried out with
a loud voice, saying, Let me alone, what have I to do with you,
Jesus of Nazareth? Our Lord rebuked him and said, Hold your
207peace and come out of him. But I do not have that power of
God." Piterius now began to examine Hareb and almost at once
he came out with horror plain in his face. He made the sign of
the cross and looked up at the sky and in a low voice he said to
Mark, "This man is dead."
208XXII
And so Hareb died, with the devil in him to the last, and they
all knew that his soul was lost. When the last feeble breath was
drawn the soul had fled the body and the demon had taken it
straight to hell, as any conquering soldier took his captive as the
booty of war. Mark was so shocked that he became ill. The body
would have to be buried quickly, Helene said, because in the
desert heat it would soon decompose; but Mark said he was afraid
to touch it. "Why?" she asked impatiently. "Satan isn't interested
in bodies, is he? The soul has gone."
Prevailed on at last to bring the body out for burial Mark felt
such horror and loathing that he could not look at Hareb, but
shut his eyes and fumbled with his hands, trying to relax the
rigid form and straighten it. He was shocked to learn that Hareb
was little more than skin and bones and almost as light as a child.
Putting a hand in and against Hareb's chest he tried to draw the
torso back, while Helene stood in the doorway watching him; but
after a moment Mark cried aloud and came staggering toward
her. He said that he had felt over Hareb's ribs and had discovered
stigmata in the left side. Did she know that those saints who
achieved the most superlative holiness assumed on their bodies
the wounds of their Lord, the hole in the side where the lance
pierced, the wounds from spikes in hands and feet? His teeth
chattering, Mark told her this, and then said that Hareb had not
lost his soul at all, but had died in the most ineffable saintliness.
"If he has a wound," said Helene, "he made it himself."
"Oh no!" Mark went again to examine Hareb, and when he
came out his face was a wonder to behold. He told her in a hoarse
whisper that Hareb had the wounds in both hands and feet.
209"Let's bring him outside," she said.
They picked the form up and carried it out and Helene looked
at the hands and feet and saw the wounds. She entered the cell
and saw that the sands where Hareb had sat had been drenched
with blood. "He made them himself," she said.
"No."
"He castrated himself, didn't he?"
"My God!" said Mark, staring at her.
"Don't gawk at me that way," she said. "Let's bury him."
They were unable to straighten Hareb's body but they did not
really try to, for they did not want to expose what the man had
done. They decided to bury him in a sitting position and they
put white linen around him and carried him out into the desert,
where Mark dug a grave. They piled stones over the grave to
keep the jackals from digging and when at last they turned away
Helene was exhausted and sick.
She entered the house and stretched out on her bedmat and
closed her eyes. Would David never come! Would she spend all
her life here and grow old and die and be buried in the sands?
She was so sick of it and she hated it with such bitterness that she
wanted to vomit; the earth was swimming under her, her senses
were swimming, and there was no sound in all the dreadful silence
but that of Jekh at his mother's breast. I must do something! she
thought; we'll all go mad here, we'll be only a group of mad peo-
ple, dying and burying one another! She tried to force her mind.
to dwell on pleasant things, on cool water flowing, on flowers, on
people laughing—for who, great God, ever laughed out here, but
in grief or terror?
The remainder of the day and all night she lay without moving
but the next morning she aroused herself, sensing that the despair
which had seized her was a wish for death. More to occupy her
thoughts and hands than to care what she did she brought water
from the well and hurled it against the hot stones of the house.
She took water inside and poured it over the floor to cool it and
she wondered if she could make some kind of cooler for their
food. She decided to try.
In the north wall of the house was a small window, and another
like it in the east wall. Sometimes from the north came a refresh-
ing sea breeze. Over the north window she hung several pieces
of blanket cloth, and above the window, suspended from a ceiling
beam, she hung a large water pouch, after she had punched small
210holes in its bottom. She filled the pouch with water and water
dripped upon the cloths and evaporated. She then fetched stones
in and made a stone table by the window and put their food on it,
where evaporating water could cool it.
Zinara assisted her. There had been quite a change in this
woman since Piterius told her she was forgiven, that she was
greater than a bride of Christ. In spite of all her prayers and tears
and fasting Zinara was rather plump and she had about her a
seductive womanliness. Her eyes were greenish, her skin fair, her
hair a pale and dusty brown. There was something a little sly in
her face and eyes and Helene did not trust her, but she took her
as a companion, for there was nobody else. Takuda sat more and
more like a graven image; Julia was always busy with her child;
and Elpha, shy and withdrawn, was still only a girl.
When the cooler was completed and the food set under the wet
cloths Helene looked at it and cried, "What a clumsy mess!"
When she turned to Zinara the woman gave her a quick warm
smile that made her face look sweet and womanly; but if Helene
looked at her a moment too long or if her stare was searching then
Zinara's smile would vanish quickly and completely and into
her eyes would come the sly look of a friendless creature.
"What else can we do?" Helene said. "We're two women, we
should be able to think of something. For a woman a house is to
live in but a man just comes in to eat and sleep and then go out
and preach or kill; and he does about as much of one as the other,
I guess."
When Helene spoke with irony or bitterness Zinara did not
know what to make of her and she was afraid. She would with-
draw and stand by a wall, swaying her body gently from side to
side, her gaze darting and unhappy; but if Helene spoke to her,
then her face would open in that warm spontaneous smile, show-
ing fine white teeth.
"We could ask Mark to bring food out for a feast. A funeral
feast, that's what we could have. We could eat and drink to the
death of all of us. Or let's go to the city." Now the fright in Zi-
nara's face was so plain that Helene was astonished. "Would you
be afraid to?"
"Yes."
"But why? We could see persons there who don't pray and
weep all the time." Thinking that was a cruel thing to say she
added quickly, “We might hear a bird sing. We might hear a
211child laugh. We might look into a tavern and see a woman danc-
ing with her lover." She sighed. She shrugged. "Well, let's pre-
pare our supper."
Their meals were chiefly of water and hard thin bread on which
they spread a paste of fruit. Helene would cut the bread in pieces
and spread the paste and lay them on a cloth on the stone table.
In the center of the table was a stone crock filled with water. They
drank from the crock or soaked their bread in it. Sometimes Mark
brought them a little meat and a few carrots or cabbages; and now
and then Helene managed a stew. Mark had his own food cache
somewhere in the desert, and smelling on his breath wine or spices
or honey Helene supposed that he had delicacies that he never
brought to them.
A few days after Hareb's burial Helene and Zinara were sitting
on a stone ledge not far from their house. They had been talking,
or Helene had been talking really, for Zinara said little, being, as
Helene had learned, a very secretive person who kept her own
counsel. Zinara was sitting in a careless manner so that her dusty
garment was drawn tight across her belly and when Helene first
observed that the belly was rounded out she thought that the
woman was paunchy from childbirth. After a little while she be-
came suspicious. My God, she thought, she is pregnant! She won-
dered about it a few moments and said at last: "Have you seen
Apollo lately?"
Zinara made a movement as though to flee.
Shrewdly studying Zinara's face Helene said, “He says he will
have me, even if he has to make a pact with Satan. He's a very
wicked man. Don't you think so?" Zinara was silent and miser-
able. “Has he ever molested you? But he must have, for there
were nights when you were alone and you are attractive. But he
never has?"
Zinara fixed her lips to speak but no words came.
"There were several nights when I was not with you. Was that
six or seven months ago?"
Abruptly Zinara swung and looked at Helene, and Helene re-
coiled from what she saw in the woman's eyes. They were the
eyes of a desperate trapped creature that was ready to flee or ready
to kill.
"What in the world is it?" asked Helene.
Almost shrieking, Zinara cried: “I hate you!”
"You hate me? Good heavens!"
212Then Zinara's defenses crumpled, her poise fell. She stood up,
breathing deep and full like a person in pain, her greenish eyes
fixed on Helene, her hands clenched.
"Sit down," Helene said. "Acting won't get you anything.”
Zinara was looking at her with eyes murderous and baffled and
terrified. She asked hoarsely: "What do you intend to do?"
"Do about what?"
"Oh, God damn you, don't sit there and pretend! I hate hypo-
crites!"
Helene stood up. "Now listen, Zinara: who is the hypocrite?
Why ask what I intend to do? What do you intend to do?”
"Kill myself, I guess!"
"Let's sit." Helene sat and took one of Zinara's hands and drew
her down. "I'm not going to tell on you, if that's what you mean.
But I don't want to be the fool either. Was it Apollo?"
"Yes."
"God, what a son of a bitch!" After a few moments of thought
Helene said: “I'm no Mother Ida. I don't think the chief fault is
woman's at all. Men are after us day and night, night and day;
they wear us out, eat us down, flatter us, rape us, make pacts with
the Devil-anything, anything to get on top of us!
“Look," she said, indicating the house where they lived. “That's
what a woman wants, a home, children, husband. We'll love a
man, as I love David, marry, be happy. But what do men do?
With the beautiful slave-girls they capture? Sell them into whore-
houses. With their friends' wives? Seduce them if they can."
Turning to Zinara and speaking more quietly she said: "What
did they do to me? Flung me into a brothel. And once they got
me there they practiced on me every vice from Sodom to Rome.
If I'd had to stay there what would they have done? Thrown me
out as soon as my beauty faded, to be a beggar, an old slut on the
waterfronts.
"I've thought about a lot of things since I came here-for what
is there to do but think. Maybe I'm not much of a Christian any
more. We women are weak sometimes, because we want a baby.
Lord, I've said to myself, If David is dead, if he will never come,
am I to live all my life without a child of my own? I'd rather die.
In those moments of weakness I've been tempted to let some man
have me, so that I might be a mother. If that is what men mean
by the sin of Eve, let them make the most of it.
"You know," Helene went on, staring at the wall of the house,
213“a woman's values are different from a man's in some ways. Shame
for bastards, for instance, never came from us. There are no bas-
tard children, only bastard fathers. I think I'd love my child as
much even if I loathed its father; for look at Julia, she loves her
child, even though the father is her own father.
"I think it will be good for you to have another child. Perhaps
that is what Piterius meant when he called you the mother, for
you are more the mother than I. I am too mean and vain and
ambitious and wicked. So don't be ashamed. Jesus understood
these things and he was a man but I suppose there has been only
one man like Jesus. He understood that it is not wickedness in
woman but hunger for children that makes her sin."
She looked for a moment into Zinara's eyes and Zinara gave her
a quick fleeting smile. "There's an old story I like that Mark told
me. A wife's husband was slain and she was weeping on the grave
when a soldier came along, and with no trouble he took her. I
was angry when I first heard the story but then I thought about
it a lot and I understood that the woman was weak and yielding
because of loss. It is strange but I think it is true that the times
when I'd most nearly surrender to a man are the times when I'm
most heartbroken over David.
“But men, Zinara, finding us weak with mother-longing seduce
us; and then what do the sons of bitches say? That we seduced
them, that we destroyed their godliness! Adam, stark naked and
saintly and in the image of God, never thought of such things.
Oh, no. Well, he hasn't thought about much else since then.
Maybe Piterius knew what Jesus knew, and what Jesus knew
about women is the sweetest thing any man has ever known."
She gave a short bitter laugh. "Do I seem to argue that we are
perfect? O God, we can be the most sly and calculating and treach-
erous things—but in a world made by men don't we have to be?
Don't we have to let them think that they're the image of God,
struggling to save their precious souls? What would happen to
us if men knew how clearly we see them? Even if David ever
comes to me shall I dare to be honest with him in all things? No
woman but a fool ever lets a man know how well she knows him.
And so in a way I guess we are pretenders and hypocrites, but
only because men have made us so." She turned again to Zinara.
"Do you understand what I am saying?"
"Yes," said Zinara softly.
"Do you know what Hareb did to himself? Can anyone imagine
214a woman so stupid that she'd try to pull her womb out and destroy
it? Can you?"
"No."
"Do you feel any shame for the child in your body?"
"No."
"Did Apollo say he would sanctify you?"
"Yes."
"Well, I've been here over a year and I'm twenty years older
than when I came. I still believe in Jesus, the gentle Jesus who
understood women; but I don't believe a lot of stuff these Chris-
tians believe. Neither did Jesus. He never believed that the love
that produces a child is from Satan. He never believed that a
woman should be filled with shame at the thought that she's a
woman. David used to tell me a lot of things. He told me about
a poet named Juvenal. A man is mad, Juvenal said, who marries;
for why should he put up with a tyrant when there are ropes to
hang himself with and bridges to jump off. If a man is fool enough
to marry, why, the wife weeps to get her way, her tears are always
ready to fall. What makes them think we want to marry them?
All we want is children and a way to support them. But the Chris-
tian Fathers, the long-whiskered frauds, say that marriage is a
dreadful sin and that it would be better for the race to perish than
for men to foul their saintly hands by touching a woman. O God,
they make me sick to think of!"
After another silence Helene said, "You will have your child
and maybe our house some day will be filled with children."
Zinara slipped to her knees and put her face in Helene's lap, her
hands patting Helene's thighs. "Bless you!" she said.
"How long has it been?"
In a whisper: "I don't know.”
Helping her to rise, Helene put first a hand, then an ear, to
Zinara's belly. "I can hear its heart beating. How wonderful that
we can have a child in our own body! Maybe that's what's wrong
with men. They're envious. All they can do is to go ranting
around, trying to create out of their mouths."
Zinara gave her that warm spontaneous smile but almost at
once her face turned frightened and suspicious. Never had she
heard such strange words as Helene was saying.
"Maybe God is female. Did you ever wonder about it?"
"No."
215"If you live in this God-forsaken place long you wonder about
a lot of things."
“Look!" Zinara whispered.
Helene looked and saw Apollo standing by Bion's cell, looking
over at them. Zinara made a movement toward flight but Helene
grasped her, saying, "Why run away? He can't hurt you. Lord,
Zinara, he's not interested in you any more. He's after me.”
"He's coming over!" Zinara gasped.
Apollo crossed the sands and came up to them, looking at
Helene, his strange depthless eyes touched with yellow. "Sunning
yourselves?" he asked.
"Like a lizard," said Helene. "In this cold northern climate our
house is like ice."
He glanced at Zinara, whose gaze was downcast, and looked
again at Helene, whose eyes were so full of contempt for him that
they held little else. "I've been talking with Bion," he said, ur-
bane, bland, at ease. "Now that Hareb has gone, don't you think
that Bion should compete with Agios?"
"How delightful! One stands with his head in the sands and
the other cuts off—what do Jews call it?"
"The secrets," he said, smiling at her. "Have you seen the
secrets that Roman women carry in holy processions?"
"Men will never be happy until they are all staff and stones."
She laughed inwardly, for she knew that she had startled him.
He gave her a searching glance and said, "And women? Do
they want to be all womb?"
Zinara gave a stricken gasp and fled. Apollo turned, graceful
and poised, to watch her run across the sands and dash into the
house.
"I observe," said Helene, trying to wither him, "that you've
sanctified another woman. I understand now that man's ambition
is to sanctify all women and then without dying to ascend bodily
to heaven to sit with God."
"Augustus almost did that," said Apollo, and easily, gracefully
he sank to the stone ledge at her side. He had been to the city
and he had on a garment of fine fabric and he smelled clean.
"Even his own daughter."
"I suppose a lot of men besides you and Augustus seduce their
daughters and their sisters and mothers."
"Or one another. The more we degrade the flesh the more
brilliantly our souls will shine."
216"Only Nero's can ever shine as brightly as yours."
"I've been to the city-"
"I can smell it."
He raised a sleeve to his nose and sniffed. "With all my friends
in the city I left word to send out a Jew to help me make the pact
with Satan. Then, Helene darling, you'll be mine."
"You will sell your soul to Satan for me? Does that mean that
you will be tormented forever?”
"It means that Satan will do with me as he pleases."
"He could tell you to take different forms and go around se-
ducing women?"
"He could."
"Then you're not running much risk after all, are you?”
"I've no way of knowing what Satan will do with my soul."
"When is this Jew coming?"
"As soon as one wicked enough shows up."
She was staring at him curiously. "You really believe this thing
can be done?"
"My dear girl, I know it can be done. I've seen instances.”
"But why give your soul to Satan forever?"
"Because," he said, vainly reaching for one of her hands, "with-
out his help I'll never have you unless I rape you. I don't want it
that way. I want you to come-and don't doubt that you will
come-begging me to take you. Oh, I know it. I had a vision the
other night in which you came and hurled yourself upon me.
What raptures we shall know! What delights when I hear your
sweet moans and know—"
"Oh yes! What moans you'll make before life is done with
you!"
“Well, that may be," he said, raising his shoulders in a bland
shrug. “But after I've shucked off the flesh my soul will leave as
a pure white light. I can see it ascending—”
"But not if Satan has it. And all these women you have fouled,
where will their souls go?"
"To heaven.”
Again she looked into his strange eyes, now kindling with erotic
fires. He wearied her. He made her feel unclean and stupid
and—yes, afraid. She arose and swung to face him. "Very well,
you loathsome monster, when this Jew comes make your pact with
Satan! And when I come moaning and panting and choked with
passion the stars will no longer be shining and the earth will be
217dark! When I come to you-" She broke off, unable to say what
she wanted to say. She felt sudden sick fury, and stepping close
she struck his face with all the power she had. Then, shamed by
what she had done, confused and alarmed by its meaning, she
ran to the house and entered and barred the door behind her.
Sitting on the stone ledge Apollo laughed softly. He felt that
he had this woman as surely as if the pact with Satan had already
been made.
218XXIII
Though a woman of courage Helene was badly frightened; she
did not doubt the existence of Satan nor his powers, nor did she
doubt that a man could make a pact with him by pledging his
soul. She had been hoping that Apollo was only trying to intimi-
date her but now she was convinced that he meant to go ahead
with it. She did not know if she would have strength enough to
resist the infinite cunning and guile that Satan could bring against
her.
While she was considering her problem she was amazed by an
unexpected event. There was a famous monk named Bessarion,
of whom she had heard Mark speak. It seemed that Bessarion had
gone to the city and had learned what Thais had done to Hareb;
and being a bold man of invincible saintliness he had gone to her,
and either with his own eloquent tongue or with miracles and won-
ders had converted her to the Christian faith. How he achieved
such a transformation in a harlot, whose fondness for luxurious
living was known far and wide, Helene was never to learn; but
that he actually did it became apparent a few days after she slapped
Apollo.
For Thais came out to the colony, not on a glittering horse, as
formerly, but afoot and alone, trudging across the sands and ar-
riving gray with sweat and fatigue. Mark saw her first and he
stood gaping with astonishment, because Thais came in costly
raiment and jewels, staggering along and falling and rising to
stagger again. She was half-dead from heat and thirst and barely
able to breathe. Mark went to meet her, and she faced him, dusty,
stricken and gasping, her beautiful hair wildly disordered, her
219whole appearance that of a woman who had wandered for hours
in heat and sandstorms.
"I'm Thais!" she gasped. For a few moments she breathed with
difficulty and swayed and seemed about to fall. Then, still sucking
for breath, she cried: "For our Lord's sake I have done it!"
"Done what?" he asked, his wonder growing.
"Repented! I-I'm now a sister out of Zion!"
"A Christian?" he asked, doubting his senses.
Her eyes opened wide and she looked at him not as a Christian
but as a woman, as a woman who knew all that was to be known
about men. Her lids fell and she whispered, "I'm choking!"
"I'll bring water," Mark said.
"But first help me to walk."
He took her arm and at once she put her arm around him. A
Christian? Well, possibly, but still a woman, still a harlot. Or
was she lying? Was this some evil snare or some hoax? He helped
her to the house where the women lived and she went feebly in-
side and sank to the floor. Helene came over; and Mark, standing
in the doorway, said:
"This is Thais. She says she has become a Christian."
Helene looked at Mark to see if he was jesting. Then she looked
at the prostrate Thais and saw first her jewels and then her gar-
ments, which were of the costliest fabrics, even her chemise or in-
tima, a part of which was exposed, being of heavy silk. Her foot-
wear, boots of soft leather that came above her ankles, seemed to
be trimmed with gold and embroidered with pearls. And there
were jewels where her flowing crimson veil was knotted on her
forehead, and gold ornaments at her breast.
"What has she come here for?"
"I don't know. She wants a drink."
"Please!" Thais said, making a supplicating gesture toward
Helene.
Helene brought water and Thais gulped it so eagerly that she
spilled a part of it down over her breast. Then she thrust the
stone cup at Helene and said, "More!"
"She walked out," Mark said.
Takuda came over, and Julia with her child, and Elpha at last;
and the four women stood around Thais, sprawled on the floor,
and looked at her. Thais drank again and looked up at the as-
tonished faces and smiled and said, "I came out to live in a cell."
"In a cell!" said Helene. "In garments like those!"
220Thais struggled to her feet and stood in her jewels and silks
looking round her. She saw Mark in the doorway and said: “Is
there a cell for me? I want to be sealed in like Sister Alexandra."
She would not be put off. She was told that she could live with
Ida and the nuns or in this house with the women; but she said
no, she would be alone, where she could spend all her time in pen-
itence and prayer. Mark said, jesting cruelly, that she could have
Hareb's cell, and on hearing the name, Thais said eagerly, “Yes!
I will live there."
Still convinced that this was a hoax or some cunning trick of
the Devil Mark said he would show it to her; and she went with
him followed by Helene to Hareb's cell. Thais looked into it and
said simply, "I will live here but you must seal me in. I shall be
here until I die."
Mark and Helene exchanged incredulous glances; and Helene
said: "In the clothes you have on?"
"Yes."
"Do you realize that if you are sealed in there'll be only a small
hole, through which to receive food and water? Do you know
what it means to live in a place like that, alone, day after day and
year after year?"
"Yes."
"She can try it," Mark said. "If she wants to get out we'll let
her out."
"What will you eat?" Helene asked, gazing at her with bound-
less curiosity.
"Bread and water. Saint Bessarion told me that those sealed
in live on bread and water."
"So you have seen Bessarion?" said Mark.
"He prayed with me all night," Thais said. "He explained to
me that I've been wicked and will lose my soul, but can save my
soul if I repent and give all my thoughts to my Lord." She spoke
like a child. Then she stripped off her jewels and ornaments, say-
ing to Mark, "Take these. I've given all my wealth to the poor,
as Sister Candida did. And for years she has lived on dry bread
dipped in vinegar." She went into the cell and looked round
her and said in the strange childlike way, "I'll live here." She
came out and said to Helene, "You can teach me some prayers.
I have never prayed."
Mark looked round him at the sky and the earth, still doubting
his senses, for though he had seen marvels in his life he had never
221seen anything like this: here was a woman, famous for her beauty,
who had all the luxuries and pleasures of life, yet had abandoned
everything, riches, friends, people, to live alone in a small dungeon
on water and bread. Where under heaven was there a greater
wonder than this?
"Will you teach me prayers?" Thais asked Helene.
"If you wish. But I'm a wicked woman. You should have a
monk or a nun teach you."
"Will you?" she asked Mark.
"I also am wicked," he said.
She looked at them in turn, her eyes shrewd. "Are you lovers?"
"No."
"I want the hole on the north side," said Thais, rubbing a palm
over the stone. "Which is the north side?"
"Over here," said Mark, and went to the north wall.
"You have to put a roof on it," Thais said. "Then seal up the
door."
"Yes," said Mark, as though speaking to a child delirious with
fever.
"Can you do it now?" asked Thais, her voice a little impatient.
"I'll have to fetch Agios. He's the mason."
Mark went out to the desert to see if Agios would come; and
Thais said to Helene, “Let us sit and you teach me." She sank
with her back to the cell wall, and Helene sat, still looking at her
incredulously. Was the woman mad? Was this only a whim of
the moment? "Bessarion taught me some words," Thais went on,
her gaze on the sky. "Blessed are the poor for theirs is the king-
dom of God. Blessed are those that hunger for they shall be
filled. Blessed are those that weep, for- For what?"
"For they shall laugh."
"For they shall laugh. Woe unto you who are rich, for you have
received your consolation. Woe unto you who are full, for you
shall hunger. Jesus said these things."
"Yes," said Helene, feeling both mirth and pity.
"He said-Bessarion told me-except that you be converted,
and become as little children, you shall not enter the kindom of
heaven. He said, whoever shall humble himself as the little child,
the same will be the greatest in his kingdom."
So that, Helene thought, explained her strange childlike
quality!
222"Jesus said he came to save those who are lost. Are you lost?"
she asked, glancing at Helene.
Helene gave a little laugh. “I suppose I am."
"Then you should repent and pray and wait. Didn't Jesus tell
us to?"
"Yes."
"He said he would come again, but of that day and hour no
man knows, not even the angels. He said that those who are
greatest on this earth will be servants, and those who are servants
here will be exalted." She was silent, her eyes squinting a little
at the sky; and Helene supposed that she was searching her memory
to recall other things which Bessarion had told her. "Jesus said
the rich will not enter into the kingdom. He said he said the
saints in his kingdom will be like little children. This life is so
short," Thais said, speaking haltingly, like one recalling only parts
of a lesson, "but the next life is forever. What we are there de-
pends on what we are here." Again glancing at Helene she said:
"Can you teach me a prayer?"
"Our Father, who art in heaven-
""
"I know that one. Bessarion taught me that."
Helene tried again. "The Lord is my shepherd, I shall not
want . . . ." She went through the twenty-third psalm and said,
“Repeat it after me." Together they recited it several times and
Thais said, speaking to herself:
"I will fear no evil... Goodness and mercy shall follow me..
I will dwell in the house of our Lord forever... Tell me another."
"May God bless you and guard you. May God make bright his
face toward you and be gracious to you. May God lift up his face
toward you and appoint you for well-being."
"Is that one Jesus gave us?"
"It's a Jewish prayer."
"But aren't the Jews wicked?”
"Goodness and mercy shall follow me all the days of my life.
That is Jewish. And a man shall be as a hiding-place from the
wind, and a covert from the tempest; as rivers of water in a dry
place, as the shadow of a great rock in a weary land. That is
Jewish. The wilderness and the solitary place shall be glad for
them; and the desert shall rejoice and blossom as the rose. A
Jew said that. But they that wait upon the Lord shall renew
their strength; they shall mount up with wings as eagles; they
shall run and not be weary; and they shall walk and not faint.
223And the Lord will come to open the blind eyes, to bring out the
prisoners from the prison, and them that sit in darkness out of
the prison house."
"Are you a Jew?" asked Thais.
"Jesus was, so why do you ask?”
"Jesus was? Bessarion never told me that."
"Some Christians don't like to admit it. They paint him with
light hair and blue eyes.”
"They do?" she said, wondering about it. "You seem to like
Jews."
"Some of them I have known."
"But don't most Christians hate Jews?"
"I suppose. But isn't it wicked to hate them when Jesus was
a Jew?"
“Our Lord was a Jew," said Thais, thinking about it. Then
she said: "I like you. When I'm sealed in I guess I'll be very
lonely. I want you to come every day for a few days and pray
with me. Will you?"
"If you really want me to."
"Bessarion says I've been so wicked I'll have to pray a lot. He
says I must weep a lot. Do you ever weep?"
"Once in a while."
Mark now came in sight, with Agios. Though little more than
skin and bones Agios walked erect like a man with abundant
strength. He came up and fixed his fierce eyes on Thais.
Mark said: "This is one of the holiest of the saints."
Thais looked up at him. "As holy as Bessarion?"
"Almost."
"Then he can seal me in.”
Sealing her in took several days. The men had first to put a
roof on the cell and this they did by laying timbers across the
walls and piling stones on the timbers, which were then sealed
with mortar. They made a small hole in the north wall. They
brought stones with which to close the doorway and when they
were ready they called Thais from the house, and she came, with
Helene at her side. Without looking round at the world, as
Helene would have done, as a gesture of farewell to all that she
loved, Thais entered the cell, and Mark and Agios laid the stones,
filling the interstices with mortar, block by block. Helene had
been fighting her grief and her suspicions, telling herself in one
moment that this woman was only a harlot, who did not mean this
224at all, and in the next that she did mean it and would die there.
Compassion won at last and before the last stones were laid Helene
stepped up and looked inside and said:
"I want to kiss you farewell."
Thais came forward and Helene kissed her cheeks and then
impulsively her mouth. When she had turned away, shaken by
emotions that were a little strange to her, Agios and Mark laid
the last stones and sealed the hole. Now she is in there, Helene
thought, trying to understand this darkest of all riddles; it is very
gloomy and silent in there; and the only ray of light comes from
the one small hole; and there she will live alone and die alone!
Her silks will fade and rot and fall off her and she will be naked;
and she will be a scrawny thing living in her filth; and she will
pray and weep year after year until some day she is found dead!
O Lord, O Lord, it was too much for the human soul to bear!
Feeling ill and frightened she went to the house and flung
herself on her mat to weep. Did it matter that the woman was
so beautiful? Helene told herself that it did not but in her heart
she knew that it did: to immure one so lovely, one with such
charm, with such affinity to flowers and gardens and sunrise.
David, she whispered, in God's name come before I go mad, be-
fore I go shrieking out into the desert, as so many have, to be lost,
to fall and die, to be eaten by the jackals!....
After a little while she could not endure her torments but
went over to the cell and put her mouth to the hole. "Thais!" she
whispered.
"Who is it?" asked a voice in the muffled gloom.
"Helene. Are you all right?"
"I'm praying," said Thais, standing up to the hole. "I will fear
no evil, I will fear no evil. Though I walk through the valley of
the shadow of death I will fear no evil, for our Lord is with me.
He suffered agonies on the cross for me," she said, speaking into
the hole, "so how small will be my sufferings, compared to his."
"May I bring you water or bread?"
"Not now. I will pray. I know many prayers now and I will
say them over and over and then I'll think about him on the
cross."
Why, Helene asked herself furiously, had she not given the
woman her bedmat? She told her that she would bring it or
anything else that she owned, and the men could remove a stone
to make entrance for the things; but Thais said no, she did not
225want them. Bessarion had told her that she should enter the
cell with only the garments on her back. Still, there was one
thing she would like, a small cross, an image of the tree on which
Jesus had died.
"I'll have Mark get you one. Do you want me to pray with you
now?"
"Just teach me a new prayer."
Helene tried to recall a prayer or noble verses from the holy
books but she could think only of the gentlest of all stories of
compassion. She said: “A woman was caught in adultery and
they took her to Jesus, saying, under the laws of Moses a woman
is stoned for this sin. What do you say we should do with her?
And Jesus said, let the one among you without sin cast the first
stone. Then he pretended not to see or hear the men, and a few
moments later when he looked round him he was alone with the
woman, for all the men had slunk away. Jesus then said to her,
where are your accusers? Does no man accuse you now? And the
woman said, no man. Jesus said, neither do I, go, and sin no
more." Helene had spoken with her lips to the hole, at the far
end of which she could see the eyes of Thais, staring out at her.
"Tell me another story about Jesus."
Every day Helene went to the cell and talked with Thais, or
told her stories about their Lord or prayed with her. The woman's
cheerfulness amazed her. She had thought that after a few days
in that dark ugly little place, with nothing but the clothes on her
back, Thais would asked to be released, or would soon go mad,
as some had done who had been immured; but Thais said she was
happy. She had her prayers and her thoughts and she was con-
vinced that her Lord was with her. It was Helene who daily
became more distracted; she was troubled by thoughts of Thais
when awake, she dreamed about her when asleep. She tried to
imagine what Thais did all day and all night when nobody was
there to talk to her. Did she merely sit and stare at the dark walls
that entombed her? Did she weep? Did she know that she might
live for thirty or forty years? Was she aware of what an eternity
of time that would be, alone, alone!-and after a year or two,
naked, unclean, half-blind!
Mark said she might ask to be released. Some of them did, even
men.
"Why do
you say even men? It is women, not men, who endure
226suffering without complaint. Have you ever known of a woman
who asked to be released?"
Mark thought of all the immured monks and nuns he had
known and said at last that he had not. He said they were usually
cheerful for a while, for a few weeks or months, and then they
began to weep.
"But not all."
"No, not all."
"She may become famous like Alexandra or Sara."
"Perhaps."
"She has never once mentioned Hareb. Does she think she
killed him?"
"Does she know he is dead?"
"I'm sure she does. Otherwise she would have asked to see him.
Mark, I will tell you a secret if you'll keep it."
"All right," Mark said, and Helene knew by his eyes that he
had guessed it.
"Then you know?"
"Yes, if you mean Zinara.”
"Are you men going to let that son of a bitch stay around here
forever, making life a hell on earth for women? There are whips
on Ida's wall. Why don't you use them?"
Mark said that was not the way God wanted it to be. Satan
had won in Eden and he had his rights; mankind was now a kind
of battleground, a free-for-all, between Satan and God. Man could
save his soul if he would. He had free will and free choice. God
had said to man, After your sin and fall the choice is now yours,
the choice between heaven and hell. If they were to flog Apollo
would they not be trying to give advantages to God? Was man's
redemption to be made easier by removing temptation? Women
also had free will and free choice: they could resist Apollo or
yield, and God stood aloof from the struggle, for he had given his
laws and commandments, to be obeyed or spurned. God had
made it clear what man must do to save himself, and had even
sacrificed his only son out of his great pity and compassion. Jesus
had served as an example and had pointed the way; he had said,
Take up your cross and follow me. Wasn't that plain enough?
Mark was looking at her with faintly humorous interest. "Are
you afraid of this man? Would you have us kill him, so that you
will no longer be tempted?"
"I'm tempted!" cried Helene, her eyes blazing.
227"Then why ask us to remove the temptation? Do we men ask
women to remove temptations? Did any man ask this beautiful
Thais to seal herself away?"
"It's not for me," said Helene, wearied by his catechism. "It's
for the weaker ones. He now has his eyes on Elpha."
Well, said Mark, fixing her with his gentle blue eyes and his
gentle smile, she would have to understand that some were weak
and some were strong. Satan's harvest of souls would no doubt be
a large one. "For more than a year I've been waiting to see—”
"Oh, you thought I would!"
"A man never knows."
"You still think I might?"
"I think," said Mark shrewdly, “that in wanting us to beat him
you had more in mind than these other women. Besides, Helene,
you say it is good for a woman to have a child even if she has no
husband. Julia has a child. Zinara will have one. Maybe Elpha—'
"That innocent girl!"
""
"Just what do you mean by innocence? She has free will to
choose between good and evil.”
"O God, it's not as simple as that. Won't you men ever realize
the horrible advantage you have? Women want children, yes, and
you men keep after us, preying on our longings, our motherhood?”
Of course it was not simple, on either side, Mark said patiently.
That longing in women Satan took advantage of. But man had his
weakness to, his infernal and sleepless lusting. If men did not
understand a women's problem, did women understand a man's?
Did Helene understand how he had to struggle day and night
against his wish to lie with her?
Gravely Helene looked at him. She looked into his eyes and
saw there something that was like pain and reproach. She looked
at his mouth and wondered for no reason plain to her why she
had never wanted to kiss it. "Has it been so hard for you to
struggle against me?”
“Harder than you'll ever know. But of course you have made
it easier, for you don't care for a man like me. Not that way. You
women are always most attracted to the scoundrels."
"You really think that's true?"
"Look into your heart and answer."
"But why is it?-if it is."
"Because women—"
228"Oh don't say that! Say instead that you men are most attrac-
ted to wanton women."
"Well, perhaps, if they are beautiful; but a man really prefers
a good woman, with character and spirit."
"To marry!"
"Women seem to prefer men who are most successful with
women. I have never known why."
They were silent a moment. Then, touching his arm, she said:
"I understand you better now. I see more clearly now that men
have a problem too. But why did God create men with this terrible
lust in them, and women this weakness for babies?"
"It is Satan."
"No. The longing for a child is not Satan."
"Would you say a woman's yearning is from God but a man's
is from the Devil?"
"Maybe both are from God. Mark, do you really love me?"
"Very much," he said.
"What does such love mean to you? Is it good or evil?"
"Well," said Mark, looking away at the sky, "I don't know. I
feel tenderness for you; that seems to me to be good. I lust after
you and I know that is evil. I know it is sinful to want to see you
naked; but when you come out in the morning and I see your
face and the whole world lights up for me I know that is not sin-
ful. When you touched me a moment ago it was as if heaven
touched me; but almost at once I thought of your body and that
was wicked."
She tried to force a laugh. "What a world!" she said. "Well,
I feel that David will come. But if I wait another five years and
he does not come, will you want me then?"
"I don't think so," he said. "For I would be-well, this is what
I mean. When I embraced you you would be saying to yourself,
this is David.”
"What on earth are you saying?"
"Aren't women like that? Men are. I'll confess something. In
the time you've known me I've gone twice-But no, I won't lie
to you. I've gone several times to a whore. Every time I have
shut my eyes and told myself, this is Helene in my arms. Then
the whole experience became so vivid that I was almost sure
it was." She was looking at him increduously. He shrugged. "Is
it never that way with women?”
"I don't think so."
229"It is with men. If they can't get the woman they want, when
they are intimate they pretend they have that woman. Men have
told me so. I imagine that when Apollo was with Zinara he pre-
tended it was you.'
"How strange you men are!"
"And women too."
“Mark, I am fond of you. Nobody else has ever been so good
to me. If we wait—”
"Oh, I know," he said stiffly. "I know what you would say.
Well, I must go to the city now and get food and a cross for Thais.”
230XXIV
Helene was so deeply troubled by Thais and Mark and her own
confused emotions and hungers that she resolved to go away for
a few days. She asked Mark for money and he gave her several
gold coins.
"Oh no," she protested. "Not so much."
"What you don't need you do not have to spend. You'd better
get some pretty clothes. You know what I'd like when you re-
turn?—or do you intend to return?"
"I'll come back. Elpha is going with me."
"I'd like you to come beautifully robed, as Thais was. And
don't walk. Hire a litter and come like a queen."
"Do you really mean it?"
"I want to see you once as beautiful as you can be.”
Did he think she was going to search for David? He did not
say so but he had that look in his eyes.
She had thought to take Zinara with her but Zinara was too far
in pregnancy and she was awkward and she was a poor companion.
And so one morning Helene set out across the sands with Elpha,
not on the route leading to the city, for Helene was afraid she
would be drawn to the mad monk in the cell, but east toward the
river. She had heard that Christians were there who lived like
beasts and she told Elpha that they might as well see them; for
what was a Christian anyway? Elpha responded with her gentle
childlike smile. She was not a girl who said much, and indeed
for days at a time she said nothing at all. Helene loved her be-
cause she was so kind and considerate, never complaining, always
submissive and humble, and always like Mark so cheerful, yes, and
so human. They took with them bread and dates and two pouches
231of water, and for two or three miles they trudged along without
speaking. Elpha's feet were bare, and Helene's sandals were so worn
and patched that they were little better than none. After a while
she took them off and threw them away.
When they came to the river Helene did not know if the monks
were upstream or downstream but because the city was north she
decided not to go farther south. There was a road parallel with
the river and in the grasses and vegetation there were many paths.
They were following a path when they came to a clearing, pro-
tected on all sides by heavy thickets; and there before them, taken
by surprise, were three of the strange creatures that had once been
men. They did not live long, Mark had said, for they ate only
grass and roots or such wild fruits and nuts as they might find.
When the women surprised them the three monks were sitting to-
gether and seemed to be sunning themselves, but at once they
moved off, going on hands and feet, with their rumps thrust up,
like ungainly things whose hind legs had grown too long. They
hopped away and entered a thicket, but then turned, like beasts,
to look out.
The girls approached them, but slowly, softly, alert to dangers,
as they might have approached any wild thing. Two of the monks
now disappeared; but the third, like a beast that served as sen-
tinel for a flock, stood there, on hands and knees, in the edge of
the thicket, staring out, his long matted hair falling down his
cheeks and reaching to the earth. Helene advanced until she was
about fifty feet from the man and then stopped to look at him. She
met the eyes, black and intent and watchful, like the eyes of any
suspicious wild thing. Mark had told her that these monks after
a few years lost their power of speech and only grunted, or some-
times squealed like pigs. This monk like the other two was com-
pletely naked but for his covering of heavy black hair.
Helene was afraid of him. He was staring at her so intently
and he was crouched like a beast that might spring. Her voice
queer and tremulous she asked, "Are you a Christian?" There was
no reply; she had expected none. She looked into the strange
black eyes, thinking about him; and asked: "Can't you speak?
Are you a brother in our Lord?" Then suddenly she felt shame
and she backed away. She returned to Elpha and with a little
troubled laugh said: "What things men are!"
"Why are they here?" Elpha whispered.
Because, Helene said, they had taken literally some biblical
232verse. She could not recall the verse but it was something that
God had said, that wicked men should be as the beasts of the field,
or something like that.
"Are they really men?" asked Elpha, her eyes wide with wonder.
"Mark says they feed like horses and cows; they bite the grass
off and don't use their hands. After they lose their teeth they
starve to death." Again her distress broke through in a troubled
laugh, so dry of mirth and so full of pain. "What a Christian is,
Elpha, I no longer know. O God.”
"Do they save their souls living this way?"
"They think they do," she said, looking up at the sky, "but I'd
like to know what God thinks."
"Can't they talk?”
"Mark says they talk as beasts talk. He says that when they
scratch one another or dig bugs out of their hair they grunt the
way pigs do when their dirty hides are rubbed. When they are
mad they hop around and squeal or bark like dogs.”
Elpha's quiet gray eyes, so filled with wonder that they seemed
to be listening, looked at Helene and at the thicket and at Helene.
"Are they holier than the other monks?"
"O Lord, don't ask me. As Takuda says, a woman knows
nothing about such things. I don't think Jesus ever told men to
live like pigs."
Helene wanted to see more of these strange creatures but she
was afraid that if she went to the thicket they might rush at her.
They might bite and rend, like beasts; for Mark had said that
when frightened they could be very savage. "We'd better go,"
she said.
A mile or two down the river they paused for the night, choosing
shelter among trees away from the road and the paths. They ate
dates, and bread which they softened in water; and after they had
eaten they stole down to the river and bathed and washed one
another's hair. Then under the trees they lay down and Helene
clasped one of Elpha's hands, which returned her pressure; and
they looked through leaves at the sky and talked until the stars
came out. Helene wanted to ask Elpha about her mother, and
her life before she came to the desert with her father; but she
felt she had no right to look into the girl's unhappiness. Poor
thing, she had suffered enough, and with such infinite patience!
She told her the story of Ruth, and of the gentle Hosea, whose
wife had been a whore; and of Job, whom God had tested in the
233furnace of agonies worse than death. She told of the many wonder-
ful things that Jesus had done; how he had cast out demons, healed
the sick, raised the dead, and multiplied fish and a loaf to feed
five thousand; until with a start she realized that Elpha was asleep.
Helene then rose to an elbow to look at the girl's face. It was not
a perfect face but the skin was very fair and soft, and the mouth
was as tender as only a girl's mouth can be. Helene pressed lips
to the girl's cheek and lay back and looked at the stars. In this
country the night was so wide open and close and brilliant that
you felt you could touch the nearest stars and put your hand on
the first heaven. The stars were spirits, someone had said. Mark
said they were envelopes of light within which angels lived. The
first heaven was only a few hundred feet above the earth; and be-
yond it was the second, the third, fourth, fifth, sixth; and above all
these the seventh, where God reigned supreme in a realm of light.
Mark said he sat on a throne of gold and around it the holiest
angels sang day and night. Helene thought heaven would be
nicer if there was marriage in it and children and homes, and
husbands coming in to the evening meal and firelight.
The next morning at daybreak they ate and set off down the
road, hands clasped, to the city. Would she find David there?
But no, of course not; for David would go to Antioch and friends
there would tell him where to find her. For almost two years
now she had waited, and Mark, gentle and generous Mark, had
waited too. Well, she would buy beautiful garments, as he had
asked her to, and a little of the finest perfume, and she would make
herself as lovely as she could. She would go with Elpha to a tav-
ern, to hear laughter again, to see men and women dancing, to see
them drinking, to see them human; but first she would hire a
room and she would buy things for Elpha and herself; and in the
room they would put on costly scents and their raiment, and they
would be gay, God forgive them, and they would pretend for two
or three days to be rich and fragrant and happy and loved; and
then would go back to the desert, Helene to wait, Elpha, poor
child, to discover that Apollo had her marked for seduction.
Late the next day they were in their room and they were laugh-
ing hysterically as one by one they felt and caressed the lovely
things that Helene had bought. "How wicked we are!" she said,
and, sobbing, held white silk to her cheeks; held the wonderful
soft leather boots in her arms like a child; breathed with all her
depth from the jar of scent; and then, with abandon, clasped
234Elpha in her arms and held her close, pretending, while she re-
called Mark's words, that she was holding David. "I love you!”
she whispered; and Elpha, clinging close to her, whispered, "And
I love you!"
"Now we'll dress-you'll be David for me, and I for you will be
the man you'll love some day. We will dine-oh, heavens!-we
will drink fine wines, and, may God forgive us, we'll not return
till daylight, both of us quite drunk with wine and wickedness!
Come, we'll dress you first."
For both of them Helene had bought garments of the same lux-
urious quality but differing in color. For both there was a chemise-
gown of white silk. Over this Helene's tunic would be white with
a red girdle and a red veil, though this was less a veil than a head-
piece attached at the front and flowing back over her hair and
down her shoulders. Elpha's outer garment was a wonderful shade
of blue that gave shadows and depths to her gray eyes. Her
girdle and veil were white. Each had a pair of soft leather
boots that came above the ankles. Of accessories each had a
small bag of the same material and color as the boots, in which
to carry scents, probes and color-pastes for cheeks and lips and
eyes, though the loveliness of both was so colorful that it needed
no enhancement. She had bought small silver ornaments to fix
the girdle and the garment at their breasts.
When they were gowned and ready they looked at one another
and exclaimed with delight. "How beautiful you are!" Helene
said; and Elpha said shyly, "You are more beautiful, for you are
taller and your eyes are dark."
"Did God intend women to look this way? Is it wicked to be
beautiful? Yes, the Christians say, for we tempt men, the poor
sad darlings, who have souls to save! . . . Well, now we'll find a
nice tavern with gay music and we'll dine like queens. For see,
I have much money left." She looked hard at Elpha and said,
"Have you ever been drunk?'
"No."
"Have you ever tasted wine?"
"No."
"Tonight you'll taste the finest there is, the kind fat Christian
bishops drink. And food! Shall we have roast duck or goose?
Heavenly grace, I'm drooling!”
They went to the street and hand in hand walked around the
city, pausing now and then if they heard music to peer in, or to
235sniff odors coming from doorways, or to glance back at a fop
now and then who trailed them. Helene supposed that here, as
in Antioch, some taverns were low dives, filled with harlots and
dangerous men; and though some were more respectable, all such
places, the Christians said, were houses of Satan.
When at last they entered a place that did not look too expen-
sive, nor too vulgar, and sat at a table, there came to them a fat
oily little man, rubbing his hands and beaming.
"Gracious and beautful ladies," he said, bowing low, his shrewd

evening?"
"Food and wine," Helene said.
"Ah! The gods must have directed you! Wine, yes, yes. To-
morrow's cares for them that are dumb. Death tweaks mine ear
and whispers low, live while you can, I come. Wait till I bring
you a hamper of flowers."
He hastened away and Helene felt alarm. After all, she told

“Don't let him," Elpha whispered.
The man returned with a basket of white lilies and red roses;

"No," said Helene, protesting. "We can't afford all these things.

Such a dinner Helene had never eaten before and was never to
eat again. She had not known that such delicious things were con-
cocted by cooks anywhere in the world, though she had heard that
Roman banquets cost fortunes. Nor was she able to identify most
of the dishes set before them, some of them cool and lovely and
amazingly colorful, some hot and spiced and odorous. There
were five different meats and as many gravies; some kind of small
bird roasted with honey and cinnamon; oysters stewed in garum;

recognize; preserves and sweetcakes-the table was so laden with

from a pale amber to a ruby red and each seemed more exquisite
than the others. Khamhait stood back at a respectful distance,
beaming, watching every move that Helene made; and if she
seemed to hesitate he would rush forward and overwhelm her

her? That was one of Khamhait's favorites, the breast of dove
saturated with the fumes of wine and basted with butter and
then slowly steeped--but no, he could not reveal his secrets!
Only Khamhait could cook dove like that.
237"It is wonderful," Helene said, looking round her.
