While Helene and Elpha were away there came to the colony a
strange man. Mark saw him approaching out in the desert and
his heart leapt, for he thought it was Helene. After a moment of
thought he knew that Helene would not be alone, and he won-
dered then if it was Anthony, whom he had not seen in a long
while. He went out to meet the person and observed as he drew
near that it was a man who seemed to drag one leg and to have a
large turbanlike headpiece. The man came up and Mark then saw
that the headpiece covered a part of the face, including one eye.
The other eye, black and savage, looked at Mark, and with the
chill of fear Mark thought this creature must be a desert brigand.
Staring with the one fierce eye the man said: “Is this the place
called Mother Ida's nuns?"
"This is the place."
"You have one here named Apollo?"
"Yes."
"Where is this man?"
"I don't know. Perhaps in his cell."
"Who else lives here?"
Mark gave the names of several nuns and monks. Helene and
Elpha, he said, as though the matter were of importance to this
stranger, were not here now.
"Where are they?"
"They went to the city."
"Take me to the man called Apollo.”
Mark saw that the man wore only one filthy rag, suspended from
his shoulders and falling a little way down his thighs. As they
walked together Mark asked: "Are you a Christian?"
242"I'm a Jew," the man said. "My name is Isaac."
Ah, good Lord, so that was it! A Jew had come to make Apollo's
pact with Satan!
Trying to hide his emotion Mark asked: “Do you live in the
city?"
"I was born in Jerusalem."
They passed the house where the women lived and Mark said,
"Some of the women live here. Ida and the nuns live over there."
The man said coldly: "I'm not interested in women."
Then they came to Bion's cell and Mark looked in to see if by
chance Apollo was there. They went to Apollo's cell and Mark
was startled to find Apollo sitting inside, bowed low as though
in contemplation or prayer.
"Here is a man to see you,” Mark said.
Apollo rose and came outside. He looked at the Jew's dirty
face or at the part of it exposed and at the rag and the bare feet.
Isaac turned to Mark. "Leave us," he said.
Reluctantly Mark went away; and when he was out of hearing

told in the city-"
"Yes, yes," said Apollo. The man, Apollo reflected, was a Jew
all right, and looked evil enough to be one of Satan's captains.
"I want to make a pact with Satan. Can you do it for me?"
"It depends on what you want the pact for."
“I must ask first, do you speak for Satan?”
Isaac growled: "Do I look like one who speaks for God?”
"Has Satan ever used you before?"
"Come on," Isaac snarled, "get on with it. What do you want?"
"A certain woman," Apollo said, wondering how he could ex-
press matter so delicate. "I love this woman."
"What's her name?"
"Do you have to know that?"
"Does Satan?" asked Isaac, sneering. "Would you hide from

"Her name is Helene."
"You want to copulate with this woman?"
"Well-" said Apollo, offended by the indelicacy.
"Haven't you?”
"No."
"Have you tried?"
"Oh yes, for more than a year."
243"Does she like you?"
"Well, I think so," Apollo said, looking into the lone fierce

"What time?"
"When I thought she was close to surrender. But tell me,"

"Aren't all Jews accursed?" asked Isaac with a bitter sneer.
"Speak, man: are they?"
"But not all Jews-"
"I know what you would say. Does Satan hate the Christians?"
"He does."
"Because they're the new chosen people of God?"
"Yes."
"I hate them," said Isaac, his lone eye glowing with unspeakable

fit agent of Satan than a crippled Jew?"
"Of course," said Apollo, fascinated.
"I killed my own father," Isaac went on, his eye glowing. “I

last year and I'm the man who kindled the fire. Now let's be

with Satan what you want?"
Apollo swallowed hard. "Yes."
"You're ready to give your soul for his help?"
"Yes."
"Do you understand, you fool, that if you pledge your soul
it will be forever?"
"I understand that."
"You're willing to lose your soul for this woman?”
Apollo looked away at the sky and when his eyes returned to
Isaac's face there was in them something frightened and lost, some
sad nameless suffering, the boy of long ago who stood in the man.
In a plaintive, abject, almost whining voice he said he had thought
long and earnestly about these things, the loss of his soul, eternal
damnation, an eternity of torment; but the torment in him now
was more than he could bear. He had fixed his whole being on
possessing this woman, on possessing her utterly, with her will
and spirit and passion all abandoned to him. If he made this
pact, if he paid such a dreadful price, she was to come to him in
244complete surrender, begging him to take her, to take her as his
woman, his slave, to do with as he pleased. Unless Isaac could
give that kind of power he was not willing to pay the price.
"Do you, a Christian, doubt the power of Satan?”
"No."
"Women are his agents, aren't they? Is that what you Christians
believe?"
"Yes."
"Do you know it?"
"The holy Fathers have said so."
"I think you're a coward," said Isaac. "You want this woman
but you haven't the guts to pay the price."
"I'll pay it," said Apollo. "I want to know if she will come-”
"She will come crawling to you. She will hurl herself upon you.
She will seize you and suck your manhood down into her throat.
She will exhaust you and leave you lying helpless like a dead thing
uncovered. She will be your slave, here and hereafter. But for

ever. Are you ready?'
Apollo hesitated a moment and said, "Yes."
"Come inside,” said Isaac and moved to enter the cell.
Apollo said: "Not there. Satan will not listen in there. I'm a
holy man, I've spent many hours there praying. I've sanctified
this place."
"Then we'll go,” said Isaac, "where the demons are."
They walked away together across the sands. At last Isaac

In a voice barely audible Apollo said, "Yes, I am ready."

Mark meanwhile had spied on the two men, and when he saw
them go away into the desert he knew that the pact with Satan
would be made. This so unnerved and frightened him that he ran
to the house where the women lived to tell the dreadful news.
Zinara began to cry. Julia drew her child close to her bosom to
shelter it. Only Takuda seemed able to speak.
"Why does he commit this awful wickedness?”
"He wants Helene."
"You mean-?" asked Takuda, feeling as much anger as she
had
power to feel.
"She'll now be in his power," Mark said. He did not doubt it
at all. Few Christians would have doubted it. The powers of
Satan were enormous, even when no pledge was given; and when
a soul was pledged there was nothing that God could do about it.
The soul was doomed, damned and lost, and in its bitterness, after
realizing what it had done, it was infinitely more cunning and
wicked and ruthless than any ordinary soul, even of murderer or
pimp.
"Go find her," Takuda said. "Tell her not to come back."
"He'll find her no matter where she goes. He has pledged to
247Satan and Satan will guide him. Satan looks everywhere. No soul
can hide from him, any more than from God."
"She were better off dead," said Takuda.
Mark, the jealous rogue, did not want Helene to hide. He
wanted her to return. He was eager to see the struggle between
a good woman and a man of evil. Would Helene triumph?-or
Apollo? Apollo, he supposed, for God did not interfere in these
dreadful struggles between evil and good; having given to man
free will and choice he left him to his fate.
Helene and Elpha returned the next afternoon, riding on sleek
horses and bringing with them a servant to take the horses back
to the city. They were dressed in their lovely garments and they
were fragrant with costly scents; and Helene was gay and giddy,
for on the way over she had been drinking wine. She had brought
food and wine with her. The moment Mark saw how surpassingly
beautiful she was he knew that Satan was already busy assisting
Apollo; for who but the Devil at such a moment would return
her from the city, dressed like a queen and giddy with wine? He
was so entranced that he did not observe Isaac the Jew, standing
off at a distance, coldly watching. Exclaiming with joy and greet-
ings Helene slid down from the horse and threw herself into
Mark's arms and kised him.
“Mark, what a time we had! Feasting and fun, wine, food-will
you have a drink?”
"No," he said, dismayed by her frivolous gaiety.
"Aren't you glad to see me?"
"Yes," he said.
"Then why do you look so gloomy and downcast?" With a
bright gay movement she framed his face with her two scented
hands and kissed his forehead. "Do you like my clothes?"
"They're all right," he said.
"Mark, what is the matter? You told us to have fun. You gave
us money. You said to spend it, didn't you?”
"Yes," he said, looking at her.
"Am I beautiful."
"Very beautiful.”
"Mark, tell me what's wrong." Elpha now came over to stand

Elpha. "Isn't she lovely and sweet?"
248"Yes," Mark said.
"She didn't get drunk the way I did. I sat all night and drank

"Yes," he said, still looking at her.
"I spent all your money. Did you expect me to?"
"Yes."
"You gave us a lot and we bought so many things. Please, won't

"No, thanks."
"Mark, why in hell do you act this way? Have I been so sinful?"
She asked him to go to the house with her and when he protested

"All right," Mark said.
“Mark, I've never seen you this way! What is it?" When Mark

"I did," he said.
"Why don't you tell her what has happened?" asked Takuda.
Helene looked at Takuda, then at Mark, and realized in her

"No," Takuda said.
“Uh-Agios?"
"No."

"No."

"Oh. He's dead, I hope."
"A Jew came out from the city," Mark said.
All the brightness left Helene's face. "A Jew. You mean—?"
"Yes."
249"For me?"
"Of course for you."

But then she felt suddenly weak, though whether from fear or

250"Mark, did you think I went to the city to be-with some man?"
"No," he said.

She sank to the stone bench, trembling with hurt and outrage.

She turned at last to Mark and asked in a tired voice, "Well,
how is it to be? Am I to go meekly to him or will he come here?”
"I don't know," Mark said.
"You don't know. I'm helpless, you mean, so it doesn't matter.

man comes with the Devil at his elbow; and then-well, I sup-

Apollo was content to wait. He had cursed his God and
pledged his soul to Satan and he had no doubt at all that Helene
would soon be completely in his power. It might take a day or
a few days for Satan to destroy her will; but she would come. He
wished he had a bed or at least a fiber mat for this conquest; he
had looked forward to it so long, he so wished to consummate
it in style. If Mark would give him money he would go with her
to the city.
Helene carefully laid away her fine garments and put on her
cotton. When night came she slept soundly and arose at day-
break. She then fetched water from the well for a bath and while
preparing it said to the women: "I intend to give the Devil
every possible advantage, for he will need it. I'm going to put on
my fine clothes and my choicest scent. Give to Caesar, Jesus said,
the things that are his—and these things, men say, belong to him—
silk, linen, perfume, cleanliness." She shook desert dust from her
hair and then washed it, then bathed and dressed, taking her
time, arranging her masses of lovely hair, her clothes, with the
most meticulous care, and then applying the scent to the tips of
her ears, her breasts, her throat. When she was almost ready she
asked Elpha to call Mark; and when Mark appeared at the door
Helene said:
"I wanted you to see that I'm doing everything I can to help
Satan along. How do I look?"
"Very beautiful," he said.
She went over to him. "Look me up and down. Do I smell
sweet? I have cleansed my teeth and even scrubbed my tongue. Do
252I look like one of Satan's women? And by the way, where is this
Jew? Is he still around?
Mark knew where Isaac was; he was indeed still around, hiding
out in one of the empty cells. But Mark lied. He said he didn't
know.
"I'd think he would have stayed around to observe his master's
power. Is Apollo waiting for me?"
"I suppose he is."
"Well, I'm ready to go to him now-and Mark, you darling,
you'll never know who is going with me." She turned to look
at the women but none of them met her gaze. Julia pretended
to be absorbed in her child. Zinara seemed to be praying. Takuda,
gaunt and sad, sat with her eyes closed, her veined hands in her
lap. Elpha was standing, hands piously clasped, gaze downcast.
"A drink of wine," said Helene, and poured the cup full. "Mark,
shall we drink a toast to the power of Satan?"
"No, thanks," Mark said. He was frightened and miserably
unhappy. He was thinking that this woman whom he loved as he
loved his own soul would soon walk with the most bland and
incredible innocence right into Satan's arms.
Helene drained her cup and with a piece of linen she had bought
in the city wiped her lips. She looked down over her garments
to see if a drop of wine had stained them. Then she turned to
Mark and smiled. "Well, here I go."
Mark moved aside and she left the house and stood for a few
moments looking south. Then, walking carefully in her soft

Mother Ida in a doorway, and on her right the sly Bion peering
round a cell wall. Yes, God yes, they were all watching her, she
supposed; they were all pitying her and possibly they were pray-
ing for her; and not one of them but thought she was doomed.
Would their ears strain for the sounds of carnal raptures? Would
some of them tiptoe up to Apollo's cell to swallow their Adam's
apple up and down and listen? She turned to wave at Mark and

again until she came to Apollo's door.
She glanced in and was startled to find him standing there and

253seen eyes so wickedly triumphant. Then, trying to be cool and
self-possessed, she looked him up and down.
At last she said: "My lord, is that the best garment you could

He was looking at her, his eyes crafty with the evil power which

He drew a deep breath but he did not speak.
"My lord," she said, “am I beautiful enough to please you? Is

"Helene," he said, his voice thick.
"So you can speak? I was afraid you had lost your voice."
"Helene, I don't want it to be here. I'll ask Mark for money

"To the city?" she said, turning her face sidewise in query.
"I'll get a fine room with a bed and linen sheets-crimson sheets;

She was amused. He was so intense, so rigid almost; and in his

See if he'll give you money."
Like a man suddenly released Apollo came out and hastened

what I have to say. Stand over there, against the wall."

"What do you mean?"

It's not rituals or sacraments or a dismal self-righteous face; and

there, thinking with outlandish antics to win God's approval.
It's not in Bion who deserted his only child. It's not in Hareb,

255You men have corrupted it, and now, to save your stinking little

256just leave you to your shame and to your devil." She entered the

Without looking back or quickening her stride she walked

garments and appearance for signs that she had surrendered. And
the Jew Isaac, peering out from a cell, was also watching her, his
lone black eye filled with contempt and hate and unutterable pain.
Helene went to the house and entered and stood in the big
room, looking in turn at the women. As before, none of them met
her gaze. Poor pitiable creatures! she thought. Poor things, so
robbed of their womanliness, their courage, their spirit, that they
were like things in jail, with men as their wardens! Men called,
women surrendered: that is what life had been for them, and their
minds and wills were so paralyzed that they thought it must be
this way for all women. Should she tell them that Jesus had won?
No, for they would not believe her.
When she vanished into the house Mark came out of hiding and
stood in view. Had Apollo taken her? There had been nothing in
her appearance or in the way she had walked to suggest it. If she
had not surrendered, what, then, had happened? Consumed by
impatience and jealousy he moved toward Apollo's cell, going
more boldly the farther he advanced, until at last he was trotting;
and he came to the doorway and looked in. Apollo was sitting
bowed over, his face hidden.
"Apollo?" he said softly. "It is Mark." There was no reply.
The man did not look up or give any sign that he heard. Then
Mark turned to the sound of footsteps and saw Isaac. The lone
eye looked at him and asked a question, and as though the question
had been spoken Mark said: “I don't know. Did nothing happen
here?"
Isaac came up and looked inside. He looked hard at Apollo
and then went in and seized the man's shoulders and rocked him.
"You fool!" he cried. “Did you go back on your pledge, you piece
of carrion?...Speak, God damn you, or I'll choke your heart out!"
"What?" mumbled Apollo, looking up.
"Did the woman come to you?”
257"Yes."
"Did you have her?... Answer, you dog!"
"Uh-yes."
"Then why do you sit here like a fool spurned?”
What happened next astonished Mark beyond words. Isaac took
Apollo by the throat with both hands and choked him, flinging
his head back and forth, smiting the wall with it until the blows
shook the entire cell; and then, with what seemed to Mark to be
superhuman strength, Isaac lifted the man to his feet and smote
him in the mouth and bashed his head against the stones. Apollo
choked and whined and then squealed like a beast.
“Come with me!" Isaac breathed, and he dragged the half-sense-
less man out of the cell. Again he smote Apollo a dreadful blow
in his mouth and then grasped the reeling man and breathed with
hatred that was deeper than hate, "Come with me!" And Isaac
went off into the desert, taking Apollo with him.
Speechless, Mark looked after them and noted the direction
they took. He stared until they passed out of sight. Going to a
stone ledge at a little distance from the house of the women he
sat and wondered dully what in the world had happened to Isaac
and what he intended to do with Apollo. Where was the power
of Satan? After a few minutes he could no longer endure the sus-
pense but went back to Apollo's cell.
He stepped inside and stood looking round him for some sign
that Helene had surrendered. He could smell her scent; the small
place seemed to be filled with it. He knelt and moved a palm
carefully over the sands, thinking that if she had lain down a long
hair or two from her head might be there. But he could find
nothing at all. Then it occurred to him that they might have
been intimate while kneeling, or even while standing, for he had
heard tell of such things. Still, if Apollo had triumphed why had
he been sitting there like a defeated simpleton, and why, above all,
had Isaac taken him into the desert?
Mark came out and looked into the desert but there was no sign
of the two men. He went to the cell where Isaac had hidden.
Earlier he had fetched food and water to the man but it had not
been touched. Mark returned to the stone ledge and sat and stared
at the closed doorway through which Helene had passed. What
was she doing now? he wondered. What had passed between her
and Apollo? He sat there until darkness came, and so far as he
knew, neither Apollo nor Isaac had returned from the desert.
258Drawn by a will not his own Mark went again to Apollo's cell
and looked in, and to the other cell. The food and water were
still untouched. He turned south, moving slowly, uncertain of
what he intended to do, but drawn in spite of himself out into the
desert to learn what had happened there. After he had gone a
half-mile or more he heard the dismal crying of jackals. He walked
toward the crying and after a while he could see the forms, only a
little darker than the earth or the sky; the forms of the beasts like
shadows against the sky; forms that were trotting with heads down.
He stood and looked at them and became conscious at last that
the beasts were racing around, as they always did when they were
on the scent of something, or had found something buried in the
sands. He knew that they were craven things that fled on the
approach of man and he moved toward them at last, his nerves
tingling, his emotions gathering to his throat. The whole night-
scene was so eerie and lonely and terrible that he began to shout
as though he were attacked, and his voice rose in loud unhuman
wailing until he was almost screaming, but unaware that he was
making a sound; and wailing he went toward the beasts, his gaze
on them, and saw them slink away like shadows and disappear one
by one.
The first object he came to was a foot and the lower part of a
leg, and he stared at it a moment without understanding what it
was. He rubbed at his eyes and looked and rubbed again. Less
amazed than confused he stared round him and saw only the
brown sands, the horizon, misty and lonely, and the stars. He
stopped to look more closely at the object before him. "What is
it?" he asked in a whisper. He stared and whispered, "What is it?”
Was it a man's foot and leg? He left it and walked again, going
round and round, back and forth over the sands; and fetched up
with a shudder of horror before most of the torso. It was head-
less. It had been disemboweled and its guts were scattered all
around it. Wondering if he saw these things or if the Devil was
confusing his wits he bent forward to touch the object and then re-
coiled and looked at his hand. The ends of two fingers were wet
with blood and with a thumb he rubbed gently across the wet-
ness. Then he walked again, round and round, and stopped with
a choked cry, for before him was a human head. It was lying with
the face to the stars, the eyes open. "I do not know this man!"
Mark muttered, but he did know the man. The features were
· horribly distorted but he knew them. And he knew now that
259Satan, using as his agents the jackals, had destroyed Apollo. It
never occurred to him that Isaac had done this. He knew, as all
Christians knew, that when a man revoked his pledge to the Devil
he was destroyed, and destroyed in the most dreadful way, by rend-
ing him in pieces and scattering him over the earth. Mark had
heard of men whom Satan had destroyed in the desert but until
this moment he had never seen one; and he stared fascinated at
the dead face, thinking, This is how he does it! He walked again
and saw other pieces, and pieces of the garment; and when his
senses were numbed with horror he turned away, back to the
colony, now and then turning to look behind him, and turning
again, until he could see nothing but the desert, and a lone jackal
appearing, as if out of the sky.
At Apollo's cell he paused and was again shocked: two human
testicles, still in their pouch, had been attached to the doorway.
Mark looked at the ghastly object, doubting his senses, and while
he looked he smelled the lingering scent of Helene. Then he
thought he heard voices. Turning away, he went to the other cell
and stepped inside and bent over to look at the food and water
which a Christian had fetched for a Jew. They had not been
touched. He went to Bion's cell and slipped inside and sank
down by a wall. He now felt terribly sick and terribly afraid. He
tried to formulate in his mind the experience but he could think
of only one thing, that Apollo had been faithless to his pledge
and the devil had destroyed him. All night he sat, numbed and
wretched, trying to think of the matter but only feeling it; and
when daylight came he arose like a man leaving a sick-chamber and
went to the stone ledge.
He could feel the coming of morning with its greater warmth
and over in the east he could see the immense soft unfolding of
daybreak. He thought for a moment that he heard birds singing.
He thought he heard the voices of children. I must go to the
city, he thought, and get food, for the food is almost gone; and I
must get a cross for Thais, because I forgot it last time. I must
bring some wine for Helene, for she likes wine. I must bring
some toys for Julia's child. I must bring some things for Zinara,
some clothes for swaddling and a goose-feather pillow for her
babe, for she will be a mother soon. I must bring all these
things. . . .
The door opened and Helene stood there, looking across at him.
“Good morning, Mark!"
260I should bring some nice things for Takuda, for she is a sad
lonely old woman and would like some nice things. I will bring
her a soft pillow for her weary wrinkled old cheek. I will bring
her some boots. Why have I been so stingy with my money, I
wonder? Why . . . .
"Mark, good morning!" Helene left the doorway and came over.
"Mark, can't you speak?”
"I'm rich but selfish," Mark said, without looking up. "I must
bring a lot of things."
Helene sat by him on the stone and put an arm round him.
"Mark dear, what are you saying?"
Approaching so softly that there was no sound of footfall, Isaac
the Jew suddenly came out of the morning and stood before them.
261XXVII
If Satan himself had appeared Helene could have been no more

She did not see the mouth hidden in the beard. Then she looked

"Mark! Mark, what does he want?"

262Her lover! "My lover?" she said, her confusion growing. "He's

"I know all about him."

"He-"
"That David is dead."
"I don't believe you!"
"That David is dead."

263"How would you know? Did he tell you?"

"What?"
"I said this is David."

"Yes," Mark said.
"Does she love you?"
"No."
"Whom does she love?"
"David."
"I tell you that David is dead."

"David died in the mines."
"I know what you mean."
Two hands came up and put the headpiece on and drew it over

264der violently all over; and then she shook like one deeply chilled.

"David?"
"What?" he said.
"I've waited, I've kept my promise, I am yours."
"Oh no, you're not," he said.
"David, I love you.”
"You lie."
"No, David, I don't lie. Take me in your arms, please."

"Put your arms around me."
"No."
"You mean you don't love me?”
"The David you knew is dead."
"The David I love is here."
She heard a sound and turned and saw that Mark was weeping.

"Oh no."

"Oh, David!"
Mark now slipped quietly away, unable to endure the scene.

"Yes."
"You look it." The most terrible thing about him was that he

"I wanted to keep myself beautiful for you."
265"I wanted to keep myself handsome for you."
“David darling, that doesn't matter. It will never matter to me.'
"You lie but do you know that you lie? Perhaps you loved a

She sat and looked up at him.
"The David you loved is dead. I want to tell you how they

"It does matter."
"Not to a woman, not to me.”

"David, please!"

David laughed and his laugh was hard, bitter, mirthless. "There

"Look at my eyes," he said. She looked at his good eye. “At the

266“David darling!" she said, weeping.

Mark was gone several days and there was strange deep quiet in

"Where?"

"I'll go with you,” she said.
"No."

271"We'll be brother and sister," he said.

I hear her scream. Nobody here would ever take her out."
"Let's go ask her."
They went to the cell and Helene put her face to the hole in
the north wall. "Thais? It is Helene."
"I'm praying," said the voice in the gloom.
"Come over here, for I want to talk to you." After a few mo-
ments her eyes appeared and looked out. “Mark and Elpha and I
are going away. Will you come with us?"
"No."
"Thais, now listen. I know why you're in there. You think
you killed Hareb but you didn't. You think he was a holy man
but he wasn't. He was a very foolish and selfish man. Are you
listening?"
"Yes."
"Hareb had a son, named David. I loved David. They took
him away to the mines and I waited for him. He came the other
day" She broke off and turned to Mark. "I can't go on!"
"I'll talk to her," Mark said and stepped up to the hole. "What
Helene means is that you're not as sinful as you think you are.
She wants you to come with us. I have money."
"No," said Thais. "God is with me here and I will stay with
God. He is my shepherd and I shall not want. Though I walk
through the valley of the shadow of death I will fear no evil; for
he is with me and he comforts me . . .”
"She says she won't go," Mark said, turning to Helene.
Helene stepped up. "Thais?"
"Surely goodness and mercy shall follow me all the days of my
life and I will dwell-"
"Thais, now you listen! When we get a home we'll send our
address to the bank in the city. If you ever want us-"
272"My Lord is with me," Thais said. "I will dwell in the house
of our Lord—”
"But you'll remember the bank?"
Thais was again reciting the psalm and they turned away.
"When we get a home, I said! Will we have a cottage with
flowers?"
"If you want it."
"Will I be a good wife, do you think?”
"I think so."
“Will I— O Mark!" she cried suddenly, her heart breaking,
and flung herself upon his shoulder to weep.
Mark went to the city and brought three horses out but at the
last moment Elpha decided not to go. Helene had heard her
weeping during the night. Her friends were here, Elpha said—
Takuda, Julia, the child. Perhaps some day... And so Mark and
Helene said their farewells, though it was not farewell, Helene
said, for they would return some day for a visit. They said good-
bye to the women and the child and then, both of them weeping,
went over to Thais.
"Thais," Helene called in, "this is goodbye!" She listened and
heard the word, "The Lord is my shepherd..." "Thais!" She
turned to Mark. "We may as well go." The four women had
come outside to see them off; a hundred yards out Helene turned
to wave to them and to cast her gaze for a last time over the ugly
scene where she had known two years of nightmare. She would
always see in memory the grim Mother Ida, arms folded, standing
as guardian over her nuns; and the sly Bion weeping and gurgling;
and the head of Agios above the sands; and the dead wasted body
of Hareb, hands clutching the parts he had torn away—these and
the sad faces of four women and the happy face of a child. She
would remember how close the heavens were and the brilliance
of the stars and the night—and the crying of jackals. She would
remember, Thais. . .
Looking over at Mark she said, "I feel awfully sad."
"So do I," he said.
"I feel that I'm leaving a part of me here."
"Yes."
"I've never been able to pray, not really. Did Jesus pray much?”
"I don't know. I think he said long prayers are not necessary."
"Where will we live?"
273"I don't know. Some place where they don't persecute Chris-
tians."
"But you said they have stopped that."
"So they say in the city.”
"Which one of all the different sects will we belong to?"
"I don't know."
"Mark, do I weary you with my chatter? I have to talk. What
would Jesus think if he knew there are a hundred sects today, all
pretending to follow him, yet all hating one another?"
"I suppose Jesus knows all about it."
They rode in silence until Helene saw the lone cell in which
the mad monk lived. She asked if he was still there but Mark
said no, he had died.
"You never told me."
"I never told you anything that would make you unhappy."
They spent the night in the city, in separate rooms, and the
next day proceeded by boat to Caesarea. Helene said she would
like to live in Bethlehem where Jesus was born but Mark said
the country there was almost as desolate as the desert. They would
have a home some day, he said, a lovely cottage, with a flower and
vegetable garden and fruit trees and running water; for after years
in the desert water came to be for any man, as it was for Jews,
almost the greatest of God's many wonders. Down the Orontes
from Antioch there was a beautiful spot. Or perhaps they would
live in Galilee or in Greece or in some part of Italy or on some
island.
"If we lived in Alexandria we could go out to the colony once
in a while. What kind of Christians are there?"
Mark said most of the Christians in Alexandria believed that
Jesus was a phantom and not a man. Arius was there, a very
famous Christian, a gaunt man like Agios with a wild look in his
eyes and convulsive movements that shook his whole body. He was
an old man now and for many years had been disputing with an-
other famous Christian named Alexander. Arius said the Lord
was not co-eternal with the Father, and therefore not his equal.
Only recently a synod of a hundred bishops had met and excom-
municated Arius, whereupon his doctrines had spread all the more
readily and were now chanted in the streets and the market-places.
If you went into a shop to ask the price of bread the baker would
sing to you, "The Son is subordinate to the Father!" Or if the
baker happened to belong to the other faction he would gravely
274announce to you, "The Son and the Father are equal.” If you asked
to have money changed you were affronted by the question,
"Did the Son arise out of nothing?" or the Arians would accost
women on the street and ask, “Tell me, did you have a son before
you were a mother?" The whole city was convulsed and in vio-
lent uproar because of the contending doctrines.
"How funny!" Helene said.
The emperor Constantine, Mark said, had become a Christian.
Some said he had seen a vision in the sky, and some that the only
vision he saw was the reproachful faces of his relatives whom he
had murdered. Anyway he was disturbed by the furious bickering
among the multitude of Christian sects and had decided to con-
voke the Christian leaders from all over the world into a great
council, there to settle their differences and decide, if they could,
what Christianity was. As they proceeded northward they found
Christians everywhere talking about this forthcoming meeting.
Why should there be a hundred different sects? people asked,
and gave always the same answer, My church alone has the truth.
If Constantine called all the leaders together, said one, he would
learn that this sect was right; but no, said another, he would
learn that this one was right. And so the hot arguments ran from
tongue to tongue, while Christians wildly abused one another and
even came to blows. Mark thought they should go to this great
convocation.
"To hear them quarrel, you mean? If the truth is in the gospels
then can't men read and see what the truth is?"
There were so many gospels, Mark said, and they disagreed on
so many matters.
"But if Jesus taught love they all agree on that, don't they?"
"It's the doctrines," Mark said. "Are the Son and Father equal?
That's the big quarrel."
"Good heavens! You mean it's as silly as that?"
275XXIX
Though Christians everywhere, when not persecuted and sup-
pressed, behaved as though they constituted a majority of the popu-
lation in the Roman world, they comprised in fact only minority
groups. Of a million people in Rome there were only fifty thou-
sand Christians, and in rural areas there were almost none at all,
for the pagani or pagans, as Christians contemptuously called
them, were too conservative to embrace the new faith. But the
Christians were widely scattered, being found from Spain to
Armenia, from the whole of Asia Minor to southern Gaul.
For the site of his great council, which would endeavor to
harmonize the conflicting beliefs, Constantine did not choose
Rome. He chose Nicea, a small town at the end of a sea loch,
where the Bithynian mountains came down to the shore close to
Nicomedia. It was chosen, some said, because it was so accessible;
and others, because its name meant the City of Victory.
One principal matter to be settled, Mark and Helene learned,
was that of the trinity. This doctrine was stressed in Rome, but in
Antioch Christians stressed the humanity of Jesus, in Alexandria
what they called his unity. Another matter was the date of the
Last Supper; a third, the power of bishops; and a fourth was what
the Roman church called the heresies of Arius and Miletius.
There was also the matter of celibacy, for it had become a habit
with many priests to travel with unmarried women as companions.
Constantine, though a shrewd politician, had on his hands,
Mark said, a problem too tremendous for any man to solve. On
arriving with Mark in Nicea Helene realized that the bitterness
was more intense than she had imagined. Everywhere, before the
council opened, Christians were in angry and often violent dispute.
276They were pouring in from all over the world, their emotions
hot and eager; more than three hundred bishops came, though
there were only seven from the entire Latin area, together with two
thousand presbyters, deacons and acolytes. They came from Eygpt
and Lybia and Nubia, from Spain and Italy and Gaul, from
Pannonia and Arabia and Syria and Cappadocia, and even from
Persia and Scythia. They came, most of them, like princes of
state, resplendent in costly fabrics and glittering with ornaments
and jewels; and all but a very few were illiterate, for not one in
ten could read. Athanasius, one of the most famous, came with a
flock of loyal monks from Africa, wild-eyed, almost naked, as
hairy as beasts, their skins tanned like leather. The city over-
flowed with them and everywhere Mark and Helene went they
heard the angry disputes.
They heard a furious man saying to a group that Christians
were nothing but practicers of black magic; for at the Council of
Arles was not every one of them an exorcist? They were worshipers
of relics, these impostors who called themselves the Third Race. . . .
They heard a Christian crying. "What trivial punishments! If a
Christian laspes without having been tortured he's given only a
year's penance; if neither tortured nor imprisoned but only a base
coward he is given only three, yet a child who lies with a beast is
given fifteen, a married person twenty-five and five years in prayer!
Is denial of our Lord so much less a sin. . . ." They heard another:
"You see Christians everywhere bowed before images, including
these black statues of Mary, that are not Mary at all, but Isis!
We're becoming a people of images and relics and counterfeits and
even Jews know better! And there are dupes and dullards who tell
us that Jesus was born December 25th, which is the birthday of the
Invincible Sun, as everybody knows! In Phrygia we know that he
was born January sixth. .
And another: "From all the lands west of Greece only seven
bishops have come, and they have the gall to say that upon them
our Lord built his church! He built it upon the people, not the
bishops! Who gave these men the power to forgive sins, that can
be forgiven only by God?..." They heard one from Egypt: "How
long will certain Christians persist in the childish belief in resur-
rection of the body? Where does this come from? From a verse in
Job that has been maliciously or stupidly mistranslated. . . .'
99
His countenance very sober and his blue eyes kindled with
wonder, Mark went from group to group, listening intently; hear-
277ing a burning-eyed Valesian, naked but for loin cloth, cry "Why
condemn us because we take proselytes by force to save their
souls, and sometimes castrate them? For Jesus commanded us. . . ."
To a Paternian saying that Christians must worship both creators,
both God and Satan. . . .to a deacon declaiming about Mary-who
would not officially be declared a virgin for another hundred and
twenty-six years: he was angry because certain Christians, he said,
were offering to her cakes baked in the form of male genitals, in
the way of heathen people; while still others called her the Mother
of God, an abominable wickedness, for she had been only a
woman, a Jewess of mortal flesh. . . .
"A Nestorian," Mark said.
...
"Do some Christians think Mary was a goddess?"
"I suppose they do, or he wouldn't be so outraged."
They heard a bishop, speaking in Greek with an accent, dis-
cussing the matter of Easter. In Rome, he said, it was argued that
it should be on Sunday after the first full moon after the spring
equinox; but it should be the fourteenth of the Jewish month
Nisan, for on this day the last supper was held. Why were Chris-
tians in Rome adopting this heathen date? "Our Lord was a Jew,"
he said. "If Roman Christians are going to reject the Jewish festi-
val of Passover, let them be consistent and reject the holy books
of the Jews also. And why are they saying our Lord's birthday is
the same as that of the sun-gods? . . .”
"Are they?" Helene asked, looking at Mark.
"Some of them."
"Wasn't the resurrection at the time of the Jewish Passover?”
"That's what the gospels say."
"The first Sunday after the first full moon after the equinox
wouldn't be the same day every year, would it?"
"No."
"Do you think this council will settle all these matters?"
It would not, she was to learn with amusement and astonish-
ment. How, she wondered later, could anyone have expected it
to?-for word had gone round that Sabinus, Bishop of Heraclea,
had said that with the exception of the Emperor and one other
person, whom he did not name, the assembled bishops were “a set
of illiterate simple creatures, who understood nothing."
The Council formally opened June first, in the large main hall
of the imperial residence, which was jammed to the doors, while
hundreds stood outside unable to gain entrance. Helene and
278Mark had gone early and had managed to get seats inside. The
bishops, with their assistants, deacons and presbyters, were seated
on benches ranged up and down either side of the long hall; and
at the far end was a low chair wrought of gold, in which Constan-
tine would sit. First to enter, after the assembled host was seated,
were a score of the emperor's family and friends. When Constan-
tine appeared the assembly arose at a signal, and the emperor,
arrayed in purple and gold and precious stones, proceeded to his
throne. Forty-nine years old, he was a tall and powerfully built
man, with broad shoulders, a short thick neck, ruddy complexion,
a hooked nose, and brilliant flashing dark eyes. He was partly bald,
and his beard, slightly curly, was thin. He looked fat and bloated.
Now as always the spear of the soldier was in his hand, and on his
head was a helmet studded with jewels, which his enemies said he
wore even when he slept. Mark was thinking that this man had
slain some of his relatives. Helene was thinking, The first emperor
of Rome to be a Christian!
The bishop who sat first on Constantine's right now arose and
delivered an oration in the emperor's honor and praise, which
Helene thought most effusive and flattering; and concluded with
a hymn, which he himself had written, to the glory of God. Then
Constantine spoke to the assembled Christians.
"Dearest friends," he said, "it was once my chief desire to enjoy
the spectacle of your united presence; and now that this desire is
fulfilled, I feel myself bound to render thanks to God, the uni-
versal King, because, in addition to all his other benefits, he has
granted me a blessing higher than all the rest, in permitting me to
see you not only all assembled together, but all united in a com-
mon harmony of sentiment."
Mark and Helene exchanged glances.
Constantine was speaking in Latin and had to pause from time
to time for the interpreter to translate into Greek, which was the
language of most of those assembled.
"I pray therefore that no maligant adversary may henceforth in-
terfere to mar our happy state; I pray that, now the impious hos-
tility of the tyrants has been forever removed by the power of God
our savior, that spirit who delights in evil may devise no other
means for exposing the divine records to blasphemous calumny;
for, in my judgment, intestine strife within the church of God is
far more evil and dangerous than any kind of war or conflict; and
these our differences appear to me more grievous than any outward
279trouble....as soon as I had heard that intelligence which I had
least expected to receive, I mean the news of your dissensions. .
I immediately sent to require your presence.
•
"And now I rejoice in beholding your assembly; for I feel that
my desires will be most completely fulfilled when I can see you
all united in one judgment, and that common spirit of peace and
concord prevailing among you all . . . .Delay not, then, dear
friends, delay not. . begin from this moment to discard the
causes of that disunion which has existed among you. . . . For by
such conduct you will at the same time be acting in a manner most
pleasant to the supreme God, and you will confer an exceeding
favor on me, who am your fellow servant."
•
That was the principal part of his address. He now served as
chairman and opened the assembly to discussion and debate, and
that harmony of sentiment which he had hoped for was nowhere
present. For days and weeks the great hall was to ring with out-
cry and protest and denunciation, as three hundred Christian
leaders strove in vain to agree on a few articles of their faith.
Some expounded the relation of the Father to the Son, and of
both to the Holy Ghost, and of all three to Mary. Some demanded
that the descent of Jesus into hell be made an official dogma. Some
disputed about the date of the birth or the resurrection. But the
argument that led to the most bitterness and violence was over
the relationship of the Father to the Son.
Bishop Alexander, but even more, his presbyter Athanasius,
carried the burden of the argument against Arius and his follow-
ers. Athanasius was a young man, very short of stature, almost
dwarfed, and quite stooped. He had a nose so hooked that it was
like the beak of a bird. But in this assembly, where so few could
read or write, and even fewer had power of eloquence, he stood
out brilliantly. The hot and sometimes shrieking dispute between
the church in Rome and the Arians continued for days. Athanas-
ius said:
"They conceal their pernicious doctrines by means of their
plausible and persuasive mode of conversation; thus they deceive
the unwary, and calumniate our religion on all occasions. They say
there was a time when the Son of God had no existence! They say
he must have had a beginning! What blasphemy is this? They
say they are also able, like him, to become sons of God; and they
quote the holy Scriptures. I have nourished and brought up chil-
dren, failing to understand what Isaiah meant! That the Son of
280God was not created, that there never was a time when he did not
have existence, is stated by John the Evangelist . . . .The Word
who created, that is, our Lord and Christ, cannot be of the same
nature as things created . . . .'
On and on this eloquent dwarf thundered at the great assembly.
"There is no separation between the Father and the Son; that idea
cannot even be conceived by the human mind. The Son of God
is not begotten, for the Father alone is unbegotten, but the manner
in which the Son was begotten of God is inexplicable and beyond
the comprehension of the Evangelist, and perhaps even of the
angels. How, then, can we call these men pious, who seek to
investigate this subject, contrary to the injunction, Seek not what
is too difficult for you, neither inquire into what is too high for
you? Knowledge of these things cannot be attained by the human
mind, but must be accepted on faith.
"Did not Paul say, Eye hath not seen, nor ear heard, the things
that God hath prepared for them that love him? God said to
Abraham, the stars could not be numbered by him. How can
anyone, unless his mind be deranged, presume to inquire into the
nature of the Word of God? Our Lord said, No man knows the
Son but the Father, and no man knows the Father but the Son.
The Father said, My secret is for me and mine. The Father is a
Father because he has a Son, hence is it that he is called a Father.
But he did not beget his Son in time. . . . These bishops reject the
passages in Scripture which say, My Father and I are one. They
say that they alone are wise, but even the devils are not guilty of
such impiety...."
Helene whispered to Mark: "Is all this what men mean by
God?"
When Arius arose to reply he was hissed and booed and now
and then shouted down. A tall emaciated man, he spoke quietly,
almost sadly. How could anyone believe, he asked, that the Son
had always been, that neither by thought nor interval did the
Father precede the Son?. . . .
"Silence, you wicked man!"
"Excommunicate him!"
Constantine pounded for order. "Let him speak," he said.
"God must have had an existence prior to that of the Son. We
say
and believe, and have taught and do teach, and shall continue
to teach, that the Son is not unbegotten... that he existed not be-
281fore he was begotten, or created, or purposed, or established. We
are persecuted and voices cry for our excommunication—”
"Excommunicate this wicked man!"
"Throw him out!"
Looking round her, Helene saw that some of the bishops had
shut their eyes and stopped their ears with their fingers; and when
Arius had completed his simple argument, Nicholas, the plump
and red-faced bishop of Myra, rushed upon him and struck him on
his jaw. And again voices were wildly angry against him.
"Cut him off and all the devils who believe with him!"
"They are sons of wickedness!"
"They are worse than Jews!"
"My God!" Helene gasped, and closed her eyes and thought of
Thais.
A few days later the Arian creed, which said the Son was neither
co-equal nor co-eternal with the Father, was brought to the floor,
signed by eighteen bishops; and other bishops in great fury tore
it into shreds and scattered it and called on Constantine to banish
the eighteen. Constantine, whose sympathies were with Arius but
who was a politician above all things, decided to cast them out.
The whole bitter dispute turned on two words: homoousios, which
said that Father and Son were of the same substance, and homo-
iousios, which said that the Son was of like substance. When Con-
stantine said that the first word was to be adopted, all the bishops
who had stood with Arius, but two, deserted him, and Arius and
these two were excommunicated. But the bitter controversy con-
tinued to rage.
When Helene heard a deacon saying that the Son was in fact
the very God himself she tried to interrupt him; and when at last
she had his attention she said: "But I thought the son was Jesus,
and Jesus was a Jew in Israel, wasn't he?"
"A Jew!" the deacon cried and choked. "Do you call the Word
a Jew?-the Word that was in God and was with God and was
God?"
"But Jesus was a Jew, wasn't he?”
"Who is this wicked woman? Does she belong with Arius?"
"No, damn it, I don't belong with Arius! I asked if Jesus was a
Jew. Was he a man?"
The deacon appealed to his audience. "A man, she says! The
Word, the unbegotten Word, a man! A Jew!"
"But what about Jesus?"
282"Come," said Mark, and took her arm to lead her away.
"But what about Jesus?" she said. "Where does he come into
all this?"
"I don't know," Mark said.
The Holy Synod at last agreed on a definition of the Father and
the Son and it was written in several languages and posted up in
many places. Helene and Mark stood before a poster to read and
she saw the words, "Who for us men, and for our salvation, came
down from heaven, and took our naturé, and became man. . . .'
"Who for us men!" she said.
"It means women too," said Mark gently.
"Oh no it doesn't.'
""
It became known that Constantine, who hated Jews, wished all
Christians to abandon Saturday, the Jewish Sabbath, as holy day
and adopt Sunday, the day of the sun. Some Christians had been
observing both, and some, the one or the other. Mark and Helene
were sitting in a tavern drinking wine when Helene said: "You
think Chirstians will take the sun's day?"
"They might.'
"And the sun's birthday, December 25th?”
"I'd not be surprised."
"Are we still Christian?" she asked, looking at him curiously.
"I no longer know," said Mark, shaking his head sadly, "what
I am."
"What a Christian is, you mean. Jesus taught love and forgive-
ness. Are these men Christians who hate and curse one another
and want to kill one another? If you don't judge a religion by the
kind of people who believe it what do you judge it by?”
"It's principles," Mark said.
"You mean homoousios or homoiousios?"
"The gospels," he said.
"No, Mark dear, you can't get around it that way. Either
Christians love all men, including Jews, or they should forget
about Jesus. You men take homoousios and bishops in their scarlet
and jewels. We women will take the one in Galilee who forgave
the harlot."
The Council would continue with its furious disputes until
August 25th but Mark and Helene left before that day. When
they were leaving the city Helene stopped to look back; and she
said, “Mark, there was one person who did not come."
283Mark thought of persons who might have been there but who
were not. "You mean Anthony?"
"No. I mean the one who was not there."
"The bishop of Rome? He was too old to make such a long
journey."
"No."
He looked at her suddenly, with pain in his eyes. "You mean
David?"
"No. Jesus," she said.
284NOTES AND COMMENTARY
Materials in the Notes appear in order under the headings below. Be-
cause Roman Church apologists have in times past tried to discredit
certain scholars it may be relevant to point out here that the Catholic
Encyclopedia admits the scholarship and fairness of Lecky; and a
Catholic, J. M. Gillis, has admitted in the Brooklyn Tablet (Feb. 17,
1945) the fairness and competence of Lea. On Lea's scholarship more
will be said in the Notes to my next novel, in which he will be quoted
frequently and sometimes at length. I perhaps should also say that
incestuous relationships among Christians were not uncommon. Work-
man tells of a brother and sister who "had completed the renunciation
of all their wealth by silently enduring the reproach of living in
shameful immorality—for a virtuous maiden an unnatural self-abnega-
tion, to this age inconceivable." Inspexit et despexit was the inscrip-
tion on the tower where St. Benedict dwelt.

AGAPE Robertson: "In the 4th century the irrepressible disorders of
those assemblages led to their being officially discounte-
nanced..." It is enough to take the Catholic Encyc. admission that “the
agape gave rise to flagrant and intolerable abuses" or Cyprian's ques-
tion, "Why was this pest introduced into the Church?" The Agape was
a Christian 'lovefeast.'
ANTHONY Duchesne (a Catholic) says: "From his earliest child-
hood he showed a great aversion to intercourse with his
fellows; he could never be persuaded to go to school; and hence he re-
285mained all his life an unlettered man, not understanding Greek, and
not knowing how to read even in Coptic." A. Wallis Budge: "When we
remember that Anthony was . . . an uneducated Eygptian, we need feel
no surprise at the stories of his conflicts with devils and phantoms
he could neither read nor write. . ." He was a Coptic. Harnack says,
"The peculiar affinity between Coptic Christianity and monasticism
has not yet been adequately explained."
APOSTLES' E. Carpenter: "One has only, instead of the word Jesus,
CREED to read Dionysus or Krishna or Hercules or Osiris or
Attis, and instead of Mary to insert Semele or Devaki or
Alcmene or Neith or Nana, and for Pontius Pilate to use the name of
any terrestrial tyrant who comes into the corresponding story, and lo!
the creed fits in all particulars into the rites and worship of a pagan
god." See PAGAN INFLUENCE.
ASCETICISM Harnack: "no age, perhaps, was ever more deeply
penetrated with the idea that the fashion of this world
passeth away, that life is not worth living." Lietzmann: "Only the
ascetics were regarded as Christian in the full sense. At bottom this
was the view of the hermits and monks of the whole world in ancient
times." Without consideration of this monastic spirit, says Jackson,
it is impossible to understand Christianity in the 4th century, though
as Edward Robertson says, C had never been without its monasteries
and anchorites. Says Moxom: "The ascetical tendency, which appeared
very early in the Church, developed rapidly in the 2nd century. .
growing disposition to attach special value to virginity and celibacy."
The powerful ascetic impulse, says J. E. Carpenter, “is reflected in the
vision of one hundred and forty-four thousand virgins who had not
defiled themselves with women, surrounding the Lamb upon Mount
Zion." Still, says Whittaker, "The ascetic side of C was hardly at all
distinctive till it degenerated into repulsive monkery such as that of
the Thebaid," when, says M. Smith, "monasticism began to assume a
corporate character." "Like a mighty tide it swept onwards," says
Angus. “Every great teacher from Plato to John the Baptist, from
Paul to Plotinus, axiomatically accepted asceticism as an essential of
and qualification for religious life." When, says E. Carpenter, “one
thinks of the enormous power of this passion, and its age-long hold
upon the human race, one realizes that once liberated from the in-
stinctive bonds of nature, and backed by a self-conscious and self-
seeking intelligence it was on the way to become a fearful curse." And
286it is significant, says Glover, "that Christian monasticism and the
cenobite life began in Eygpt, where great monasteries of Serapis exis-
ted long before our era."
Asceticism in C, says Workman, coincided “with the loss of the
Church in the world. . . .No true Christian could view without dismay
the growing secularity of the Church"-see CHRISTIANITY. The
widespread revolt, says Allen, "seemed like a veritable stampede from
the Catholic church. . . .thousands of men and women. . . .then taking
their flight." The hermit, says Duchesne, a Catholic, “was a living criti-
cism of ecclesiastical society. The mere fact of his retirement proved
that in his estimation the Church had become an impossible dwelling-
place for anyone who wished to lead a really Christian life." It is
significant to note that the ascetic temperament was nourished not by
Judaism but by pagan elements. M. Lazarus says, “As for ‘a young
disciple who fasts while he pursues his studies, a dog may eat his meal',
is the sarcastic way in which the Talmud disposes of the ascetic." M.
Joseph: "Judaism does not approve of the ascetic temper." Guigne-
bert: "Asceticism does not correspond to any natural tendency in
Judaism. . . . Its basis is essentially individualistic. . .personal and
anti-social." Enslin reminds us of the Jewish saying: "He who sub-
jects himself to needless self-castigation and fastings or even denies him-
self the enjoyment of wine is a sinner."
Asceticism, says Taylor, is "purification and detachment from things
of sense," and is, says Fisher, "a natural product of the oriental re-
ligions." The life, says Selbie, "of poverty, celibacy, and retirement
from the world came to represent the true Christian ideal . . .” It was
an attempt, says Latourette, "to be freed from the trammels of the body
and this present evil world." Hatch: "To wear the blanket and 10 let
the hair grow was to profess divine philosophy . . .” Adeney: “These
men forsook the cities and fled into the desert, living in solitary huts or
caves... thinly clad, feeding meagerly on a vegetation diet, casigating
themselves. . . vying with one another in an eager rivalry of self-mortifi-
cation. . . .wrestling with evil impulses. . . .developing amazing extra-
vagence of self-torture, sometimes going mad. . ." Goodenough: "the
more rigorous an ascetic, the more violently he abuses the bodily part
of his existence in the hopes of destroying it. . . .Christians climbed to
the top of tall pillars where they spent their entire lives, as had the
saints of Astarte before them." Michelet: "these unhappy hypochon-
driacs are to be seen roaming the desert, full of self-loathing and self-
horror." Macarius told of two monks who lived naked with the beasts
for forty years. Draper: "If they were not recorded by many truthful
287authors, the extravagances of some of these enthusiasts would pass
belief. Men and women ran naked upon all fours. . .” Lea: “celibacy,
fasting, solitude, are direct incentives to insanity, as is shown by the
epidemics of diabolical possession and suicide which were so frequent
in the stricter monastic establishments."
The solitary life, Peter Damiani was to say centuries later, "is the
school of celestial doctrine and the divine arts. For there God is whole
that is learned. Tears, which are from God, approach the tribunal of
divine hearing, and quickly obtaining what they ask, assure us of the
remission of our sins." Leuba: "voluntary suffering was regarded in
addition as expiation for sin and as visible token of utter devotion to
God." They felt themselves spiritually pure, Angus says, in proportion
to the price the self-torture cost. Abbott: "When the Oriental Chris-
tian had convinced himself of the vanity of the world, he said: 'It is
the weakness of the flesh and the enticements of the wicked which
tempt me to sin. Therefore I will withdraw from the world and mor-
tify the flesh.'" CE: “Mortification is one of the methods which Chris-
tian asceticism employs in training the soul to virtuous and holy
living." Lecky: "A hideous, sordid, and emaciated maniac, without
knowledge, without patriotism, without natural affection, passing his
life in a long routine of useless and atrocious self-torture, and quailing
before the ghastly phantoms of his delirious brain, had become the
ideal of the nations which had known the writings of Plato and Cicero
and the lives of Socrates or Cato. For about two centuries, the hideous
maceration of the body was regarded as the highest proof of excel-
lence." A famous ascetic of the time was St. Simeon Stylites: "He had
bound a rope around him, so that it became imbedded in his flesh,
which putrefied around it. . . .For a whole year, we are told, St. Simeon
stood upon one leg, the other being covered with hideous ulcers, while
his biographer was commissioned to stand by his side, to pick up the
worms that fell from his body, and to replace them in the sores, the
saint saying to the worm, 'Eat what God has given you'." Simeon was
worshiped by multitudes in Syria; an admiring disciple said, “When
he walks vermin drops from his body." In Phrygia, says Cumont, "a
sinner would write his sin and the punishment he suffered upon a
stela for every one to see." Budge: "The greatest monk was he who
could fast the longest, rest and sleep the least, pray the greatest
number of prayers, keep vigil the longest, work the hardest, endure
the blazing heat of the day and the bitter cold of the night, and who
could reduce his body to the most complete state of impassability."
Some castrated themselves; and May tells us that "Sextus Philosophus,
288popularly known as Pope Sextus II, openly advocated mutilation."
Believing with Athenagoras that demons "lusted after virgins and
succumbed to flesh" they hated women. May: "hatred of women and
denial of pleasure are the two pillars of asceticism." Havelock Ellis:
"the ascetics, those erratic and abnormal examples of the variational
tendency, have hated women with hatred so bitter and intense that no
language could be found strong enough to express their horror. They
knew that every natural impulse of a woman is the condemnation of
asceticism. All true lovers of the artificial and perverse find woman
repulsive; 'Woman is natural,' it is written among the sayings of
Baudelaire, 'that is to say abominable”.”
Filth was regarded as a virtue, not only then but for centuries
afterward in the Church. St. Jerome: "Does your skin roughen without
baths? Who is once washed in the blood of Christ needs not wash
again." Lea: "Jerome tells us, the filthier a penitent is the more beauti-
ful he is." Briffault: "Saints and ascetics are as a rule very dirty. St.
Jerome declares that for an adult person to wash is a practice to be
condemned, and he expresses his admiration for women who abstain
altogether from ablutions." Duchesne: "The outskirts of the town
became covered with hermitages, veritable dens, which gave shelter
to two or three monks, sometimes only to one: in these they lived the
life of savages, emaciated, unclean, in rags." Dr. Howard W. Haggard:
"St. Anthony was never guilty of washing his feet. These hideous, sor-
did, and emaciated egotists... became saints of the Christian religion.”
Reginald Reynolds: "The blessed St. Catherine of Siena... withheld
from the stool ad majorem dei gloriam. .In its first embraces with
Roman civilization, the Christian Church would appear to have done
little to preserve, and a great deal to undermine, the standards of
cleanliness among the people. Thus we read of the Blessed St. Jerome
rebuking the dames who followed him for too much washing. And it
may be doubted if cleanliness had any part in Christianity until the
return of Paganism, at the end of what we term the Middle Ages, to-
gether with the return of Judaism. . . .corrected the laxity of Christen-
dom with a strong mixture from the Ancients of Rome, Greece and
Palestine. Was not St. Agnes canonized primarily for her refusal to
bathe?. .the Blessed St. Benedict himself had said: To those who
are well, and especially the young, baths shall seldom be permitted."
As for women, M. Smith says, “Asceticism and the monastic life found
almost as many adherents among the women of the early Church as
among the men."
..
•
A great deal of nonsense has been written about asceticism by per-
289sons who failed to understand it—that, for example, it is humble un-
selfish surrender to God. Dark powers, says Ennemoser, "may very
easily seize the roots of feeling and reveal moral abysses, which the man
fixed in the limits of time hardly suspects and from which human
nature recoils. Such an illicit ecstasy and evil inspiration is at least
recognized in the religious teachings of the Jews and Christians. . ."
Havelock Ellis has demonstrated, says Bloch, "that little waves of
erotic sensation accompany all religious emotions." Bevan: "Asceti-
cism and lubricity are often plants springing from the same soil.” He
says of Augustine, "He suffers agonies of shame and contrition because
he cannot eat after fasting without some gust of sensual pleasure."
But many have recognized, with Reinach, that "Monasticism was
essentially the egotistic effort of the individual to ensure his own sal-
vation while repudiating the duties and responsibilities of life.”
Asceticism, say Lundberg and Farnham in Modern Woman “has no
more inherent virtue in it than priapism, which it really is in inverted
form." The ascetic goal, says G. F. Moore, "is purely individualistic”,
a judgment given by Enslin, E. C. Butler and others. Abraham Myer-
son: "We know of no other creature that could consider itself vile, its
body obscene, and its vital functions offensive. If we ask ourselves
how such fantastic notions could arise. . ." But, psychiatrist as he was,
he did little to elucidate the origin of the notions. Of St. Romualdus
Taylor says the egotism "rose with him to spiritual megalomania. . . .
as he made his ways displeasing to men, his self-approval was en-
hanced." Harnack perceived that the "self-sufficient Wise Men is almost
diametrically opposed to the humble soul." Nothing, says D. E.
Schneider, a psychoanalyst, "is so obscene as self-torture which almost
always flows from the sick distrust that underlies pathologic self-love."
Pathologic self-love is the root of asceticism and it is a great irony that
this, more than any other single thing, has been canonized by the
Church. I asked an eminent psychologist to define asceticism. He said:
"It is a defensive diversion of interest toward non-responsive goals."
I suggested that the last words were meaningless and he amplified:
"In asceticism egoism is so intense that the subconscious mind will ac-
cept no fulfillment of the yearning.”
AUGUSTINE He had, says Humphrey, "a profound contempt for
all educational traditions." Workman: "His famous
epigram, ‘Inter faeces et urinas nascimur', could only have come from
one who had developed an exaggerated doctrine of total depravity."
290Of the Gospels Augustine said, “I would not believe the Gospels to be
true, unless the authority of the Catholic Church constrained me."
BAPTISM A little later two monks, Pelagius and Coelestius, argued
against the doctrine of human depravity and the damna-
tion of unbaptized infants, but both monks were condemned. Robert-
son: "the new faith ultimately introduced the frightful dogma of the
damnation of all unbaptized infants, a teaching before undreamt of,
and capable only of searing the heart." St. Fulgentius: "Hold most
firmly, and by no means doubt, that little children, whether they die
before or after birth, pass, without the holy sacrament of baptism, from
this world, to be punished with the everlasting punishment of eternal
fire." The dogma rests, as Tennant says, on belief in a “taint inheri-
ted by children from the fallen Adam."
.
CANON, THE The Roman Church had a difficult time arriving at
a Canon. Klausner: "Up to the second half of the
second Christian century the Acts of the Apostles had not been ac-
cepted. ." Wellhausen says the books included or rejected, from
the great many Christian writings, were determined by "the taste of
the period." Pfleiderer: "For a long time, the Church was undecided
as to the place of certain writings in the Canon; as late as the 4th
century, seven pieces (Epistles to the Hebrews, Apocalypse of John,
and five Catholic Epistles) were not acknowledged as canonical by
some of the congregations. . . ." Loisy: "At the time when the Church
was canonizing her collection of writings she energetically went to
work in assigning them to writers whose names would confer authen-
ticity on the selected books... those were selected for canonization
which had been adopted for liturgical use by the most important
(churches) . . . .The first gospel. . . .seems to have gained authority
in the Syrian congregations; the third held the field in Greece proper
and in Rome, where Mark also was in use; the fourth had taken root
in the congregations of Asia. . . ."
CATHOLIC Allen: "the term 'Catholic' . . .first began to be em-
ployed toward the close of the second century." Walker:
"By the latter third of the second century it was calling itself the
'Catholic' Church"-meaning the universal church. S. E. Johnson:
"the Catholic church was founded in the second century. It received
its permanent form in the course of the conflict with gnosticism and
Montanism"-see SECTS.
291CELIBACY Donaldson: "In the Christian Church arose an inordi-
nate estimate of the virtue of celibacy. A large family
came to be regarded almost as a disgrace, as a proof of lasciviousness
. . . .thus. . . .Constantine abolished most of the pains and penalties
of celibacy and childlessness, and Justinian abolished all the clauses
that dealt with inheritance." Workman: "Celibacy and the preserva-
tion of virginity were increasingly regarded, if not as the expiation of
sin, yet as the greatest gift that could be laid at the crucified feet of
Christ." Lea: "The fierce declamations of St. Jerome afford a terrible
picture of the disorders prevalent among those vowed to celibacy, and
of the hideous crimes resorted to in order to conceal or remove the
consequences of guilt. . ."
CHRIST After the Edict of Milan, says Jackson, "Christ became a
god of battles." Case: "not the Jesus of history but rather
the risen and heavenly Christ of faith has held the central position in
believers' thought from the earliest times down to the present."
CHRISTIAN Emperor Julian: "There is no wild beast like an angry
CRUELTY theologian." Gibbon: "From the time that C was in-
vested with the supreme power, the governors of the
church have been no less diligently employed in displaying the cruelty,
than in imitating the conduct, of the Pagan adversaries. . . .the primi-
tive church. . . .delivered over, without hesitation, to eternal torture,
the far greater part of the human species." Whittaker: "The official
recognition of C by the State meant that the rulers were to second the
propaganda of an intolerant theocracy." Reinach: "As soon as C felt
itself master of the Empire, it began to persecute not only the pagans,
but dissident Christians." Legge: "Constantine's accession proved to
be ... a turning-point in the history of the world. His so-called con-
version put into the hands of the Catholic Church a weapon for the
suppression of all rivalry, of which she was not slow to make use."
Hatch: "When the associated Christian communities won at length
their hard-fought battle, they burned the enemy's camp." A little
after they came into power, says Humphrey, "full legal rights belonged
only to the orthodox." Trattner: "So it came about (irony of ironies!)
that the name of Jesus 'meek and mild', a symbol of joy on earth and
good will among men, was transformed into a living terror." Victor
Robinson: "Even the crimes of imperial Rome grow pale in compari-
son with the amount of human blood that was now to be shed over
myths." Klausner: "this hate-filled doctrine. . . .A part of humanity
292was created from the beginning for damnation." Troeltsch says Chris-
tianity disintegrated the ancient world; Ferrero, that it destroyed the
Empire by abstention. As for the Jews, Macalister says the Christians
“made the Jews suffer in the day of their power." Bentwich: "Justinian
went on to menace with exile and the extreme penalties of the law all
Jews who dare to maintain that there is neither resurrection nor last
judgment, or that the angels are not divine beings." Christian cruelty
will be more fully noted in Notes to my next novel.
..
CHRISTIAN vs. JEW Derwacter: "Jewish proselytism was no crime
until Christianity came into political power
and made it so. .It was a crime for a Christian to become a Jew, and
it was a crime to lead any Christian into Judaism. . . .The Cohortatio
ad Graecos, of the 3rd century A. D., argues that the presence in the
synagogue of the same books that the Christians appeal to is a divine
dispensation." Christians, say Baron, “hated above all the Jews" and
points out that Christian leaders were still busy rewriting the gospels
to make them more anti-Jewish. Cheyne: "The OT Scriptures, being
at first the only religious authority, besides the words of Christ. . . .has
to be Christianized. . .” Lazarus says "certain of the Rabbis sought to
justify an interpretation which accords with the view of Augustine,
that the virtues of the heathen are but splendid vices."
CHRISTIANITY My novel A Goat for Azazel revealed what Chris-
tianity was like in its beginnings. What had it
become two centuries later? Box: "When C emerged into the wider
world it came face to face with an educated world." Tertullian asked,
"What resemblance is there between a philosopher and a Christian?”
implying that, fortunately for the Christian, there was none. May:
"Within a period of four centuries C had changed its attitude of emo-
tional expression to an attitude of emotional suppression." The
theories which came to prevail by the 4th century were, says Hatch,
"the result of at least two centuries of conflict;" and Duchesne of the
5th century says: "In the field of doctrine rival schools dash them-
selves into collision, parties wax hot and engage in strife. The lesson
of the century before and of its deplorable dissensions goes absolutely
for nothing." Most Christians held "Nature herself accursed," says
Michelet. "They condemn her as a whole and in every part, going so
far as to see Evil incarnate, the Demon himself, in a flower."
By the 4th cen. C had lost Jesus, a process which began, as Schweit-
zer says, with Paul who "did not desire to know Christ after the
293flesh..." Loisy: "the idea of an absolute and unchangeable orthodoxy
is in flat contradiction to the nature and history of the human mind, of
human knowledge and even of Christianity itself. That which was
now canonized as the 'apostolic faith' was far from being the simple
faith of the first believers in Jesus, nor was it that of the first apostles
to the Gentile world." Guignebert: "Nothing of him has survived
save the memory of his existence and the influence of his work on his
intimate associates. We see his figure transformed into the vindication
of events which he could not have foreseen, and of institutions which,
to say the least, would have seemed strange to him. . . . The divine
legend. . . of identifying the Nazarene with God, very soon oblitera-
ted and submerged the few fragments of human reality preserved in
the memory of Galilean disciples. .Nothing, or very little, of his
work remained, nor could it be expected to survive the unparalleled
transformation of his person." Dean Dudley: "C, as an institution of
government, was little better than the old religion. It soon became
transformed, so that Christ would have been ashamed of its name.”
(This confusion of Christ with Jesus is common in Christian scholar-
ship). Gilbert: "When the Jesus of the Gospel was transformed into
the Alexandrian Christ, what was achieved was not the gathering of the
Gentile world into the Kingdom of Heaven, not an acceptance by that
world of the religion of Jesus, but an overthrow of the very basis of
that religion." Trattner: "The church lost sight of Jesus the man. He
was plainly choked by the mephitic vapors of dogma. . There is the
Christ of the Apostles' Creed, 'the only Son of God the Father Al-
mighty'. There is the Christ of the Nicene Creed, 'Very God of Very
God, being of one substance with the Father'. There is the Christ of
the so-called Athanasian Creed, who is at once 'Perfect God and
Perfect Man'." Lake: "The Church conquered the world by offering
salvation through a redeeming Lord. Jesus made no such offer. . .
the personality of Jesus was quite eclipsed by the supernatural value
attached to him. . . .Catholic Christianity conquered because it was
popular, not because it was true, and failed for the same reason.”
Wernle: "A new Church-the Christian-took the place of the Jewish,
and its claims are mostly the same: external, ceremonial, legal, and
theological. Jesus' words condemn his own Church down to the
present day."
...
Christianity lost Jesus, and, says McGiffert, "the subjection of
spirit to law and of the individual to the institution. . . foreshadowed
the rise of Catholicism." The first period of C, says Sismondi, was
morals; the second was orthodoxy. Moxom: "The sacerdotal idea
294is entirely absent from the NT, and also from the writings of the
Apostolic Fathers." Ritschl: "The distinction between the active and
the passive members of the congregation-in other words, the Catholic
conception of the priesthood-is foreign to the first two centuries."
Case: "astonishing that a religious movement, which began with the
conviction that 'all kingdoms of the world and the glory of them' were
the proper possession of Satan, should ultimately hail with approval
so close a union with Roman imperialism. . . .Creative mental effort
had been virtually eliminated. . . .The dominance of faith over know-
ledge remained unchallenged. . . intellectualism was completely sub-
ordinated to the dogmas and institutions of religion." Allard: "there
had arisen a regular Christian aristocracy." Baron: "the division be-
tween clergy and laity in the Catholic Church surpassed in sharpness
anything known in Judaism." (This is saying a great deal; see under
Poor in Notes to Jesus Came Again). Draper; "Religion had disap-
peared, theology had come in its stead." Schmidt: "Had the Church
possessed a tithe of the spirit of him who substituted a foot-washing
for the eucharist. . .understood that 'the letter killeth' . . .stagnation of
doctrinal development, a rigid fixity of institutional character and a
deadening imposition of external authority on the consciousness of
men would have been impossible."
Christianity had borrowed widely from the whole Roman World
(see PAGAN INFLUENCE); Augustine, wiser than modern theolo
gians, said that C "existed among the ancients. . ." On C as a syncre-
tistic religion the works of Burkitt, Hatch, Troeltsch, Angus, Munro,
Dodd, Nock, McGiffert are among the best. Even Mosheim admits
that in the matter of rites and ceremonials there was “little difference
in those times between the public worship of the Christians and that
of Greeks and Romans." Robertson: "What survived as C was really
an idolatrous polytheism." C proceeded, says Wernle, toward helleni-
zation, "to its transformation into Greek philosophy and mysticism;
