but in so doing it clearly shows us that in disassociating itself from
Judaism, it has disassociated itself from the Gospel. . ." J. E. Carpen-
ter: "At the opening of the 3rd century C stands at the parting of the
ways....The presuppositions of the higher Greek theology combined
with the Jewish view of 'One God'. . .to produce a fresh construction
of the Godhead, which culminated in the doctrine of the three Per-
sons in one Substance. . . . The two great sacraments, Baptism and the
Eucharist, were soon set in the light of the mystery-cults of Greece,
Eygpt, and the East, and the sanctity of an exclusive priesthood gradu-
ally enevoloped them." Lake: "The Catholic C which emerged from
295·
""
the struggle between Adoptionism and the Logos Christology was a
curious combination of both. . Jesus was regarded as the eternal
Logos who became man, not as the inspired and perfect man who be-
came God." Riddle: "The correspondence between C and Graeco-
Roman cults is well known. . . ." Bevan: "Harnack has explained in
detail how Christian belief, as it appears by the end of the second
century, represents a 'Hellenization' of the original Gospel.
Coulton: "C, absorbing so much as it did from every strand of
thought in the Roman Empire, took over from Judaism not only
its trust in one good and almighty God but also its combination of
exclusiveness and prosletytism." Cheyne: "The Christian religion is
a synthesis, and only those who have dim eyes can assert that the
intellectual empires of Babylonia and Persia have fallen."
Depravity of the Christian priesthood had become commonplace.
Haslehurst: "Cyprian draws a rather sad picture of the decay of
Christian life. . . ." Riddle: "In their common worship the behavior
of Christians was highly emotional, and this fact led to other serious
charges against them. Whatever may or may not have been the justice
of the charge, there was a widespread belief that they practiced sexual
immorality; and in the most extreme form of propaganda urged against
them it was said that they were guilty of incest and of a form of can-
nibalism. . .” Gibbon: "By some of these unfaithful wards the riches
of the Church were lavished in sensual pleasures; by others they were
perverted to the purposes of private gain, of fradudulent purchases,
and of rapacious usury." Lecky: "The Agapae [which see], or love
feasts... had become scenes of drunkenness and of riot. . . .it had been
common to find clergy professing celibacy, but keeping, under various
pretexts, their mistresses in their houses. . . .Virgins and monks often.
lived together in the same house, and with a curious audacity of hypo-
crisy, which is very frequently noticed, they professed to have so over-
come the passions of their nature that they shared in chastity the same
bed. Rich widows were surrounded by swarms of clerical syco-
phants..."
CHRISTMAS For centuries there was no generally accepted day as
the birthday of Jesus. At last, as Frazer says, “the
Nativity of Christ [he means Jesus] was assigned to the winter sols-
tice in December because that was deemed the Nativity of the sun."
Cumont: "Before religion had reached the point of proclaiming that
God must be sought in the realm of the ideal and the absolute, out-
side the world of sense, the one rational and scientific cult was that
296of the Sun." Weigall: "The festival of the Annuncation of the Blessed
Virgin is celebrated in the Roman and Anglican Churches on March
25th, a date fixed by the fact that it was exactly nine months of embryo-
geny before December 25th, which was the date adopted by the Church
as the anniversary of the birth of Jesus. But . . . December 25th was
really the date, not of the birth of Jesus, but of the sun-god, Mithra
[and all other sun-gods]. Horus, son of Isis, however, was in very
early times identified with Ra, the Eygptian sun-god, and hence with
Mithra; and thus the Christian festival really commemorates the
Annunciation of Isis, not of Mary." Halliday: "the 25th of December
...birthday of the unconquered sun, a date which the Church found
it politic to adopt." Robertson: "which the early Christian appropri-
ated to their Christ in total ignorance of the real time of his birth, is
no longer denied by competent Christian scholars." Allen: “it is a
circumstance difficult to explain that Christmas Day should not have
been introduced until after the middle of the 4th century." It was
not till the 6th century that a Scythian monk, Dionysius Exiguus, was
commissioned to fix the day and year of Jesus' birth. He chose the
birthday of the sun, which had already been accepted by many Chris-
tians.
CHURCH, THE See CHRISTIANITY. Loisy: "the Church before
becoming an institution was an idea. We make
bold to say it was also a myth. . ." Hatch: "Prophesying died when the
Catholic Church was formed. . . .The voice of the prophet had ceased,
and the voice of the preacher had begun. . . .when one generation was
bound to accept the symbolical interpretations of its predecessors, it
became at once the slave of dogmatism and the tyrant of souls."
CHURCH HISTORY We should beware, says Lecky, of the "glaring
mendacity that characterizes the lives of the
Catholic saints"-and the early historians, he might have added.
Eusebius was the first historian. Will Durant says that for the history
written by Eusebius, "sources were critically and conscientiously
examined, the statements are as accurate as in any ancient work of his-
tory" (!) Bunsen: "he perverted chronology for the sake of making
synchronisms." Baynes: "the first historian of antiquity who was durch
und durch dishonest." But we should bear in mind with Streeter
that "it was far more difficult than we are apt to suppose for them to
draw a clear distinction between history and legend."
297CLERGY Easton: "by the end of the century [3rd] the ministers
had become a 'clergy', a divinely 'chosen' group." Ignatius
said: "Plainly we ought to regard the bishop as the Lord himself."
Lea: "ecclesiastics, who passed their lives in female companionship,
surrounded by young female slaves. . ." Breasted: "The old men who
cared for the smaller country congregations were finally called merely
presbyters, a Greek word meaning 'old men', and our word 'priest' is
derived from this Greek term."
CONSTANTINE Eusebius, whom Will Durant found accurate,
called him a man "adorned with every virtue of
religion." John Stuart Mill: "It is one of the most tragical facts of
all history that Constantine, rather than Marcus Aurelius, was the
first Christian emperor. It is a bitter thought how different the C of
the world might have been, had it been adopted as the religion of
the empire under the auspices of Aurelius. . ." Dudley: "He was
willful, voluptuous, and self-conceited." Rostovtzeff: "realized that
religion is the only conceivable sanction of despotic power." Enslin:
"who lacked no quailty more conspicuously than piety." Humphrey:
"Constantine long delayed his baptism" because, says Latourette, “since
the sacrament washed away all sins and could not be repeated, it had
best be deferred as long as possible." Draper: "never conformed to
the ceremonial requirements of the Church till the close of his evil
life." Uhlhorn: “Rarely is a man so differently judged as Constan-
tine. . ." Baynes: "The representations attempted by modern scholars
of the convictions and aims of Constantine have been so diverse that
at times it is hard to believe that it is one and the same emperor. . .
The more closely Constantine's life and achievement are studied, the
more inevitably is one driven to see in them an erratic block which
has diverted the stream of human history." Of his cold-blooded
murder of his relatives Duchesne says "we have very little information
with regard to these horrible affairs. . . .We might pass over the death
of Maximian and of Licinius, who were restless and inconvenient
rivals; but his son Crispus, and the son of Licinius, and his wife
Fausta!" That is a Catholic speaking.
CROSS, THE B. Z. Goldberg: "it was enough to draw a vertical line
to suggest the lingam and a horizontal one to sig-
nify the yoni, while the union of the two was represented by the cross.”
Hannay: "the most intensely masculine cult, that of the Moham-
medans, has the most intensely feminine symbol for their faith, the
298Crescent, Diana, the ark in the sky, the universal womb, while the
Christians, who introduced the Virgin idea into the Godhead-for the
Holy Ghost is the mother of the gods-have the Cross as a symbol, a
purely male phallic symbol." Iamblichus to Porphyry: "Because the
Phalli and the crosses are signs of the productive energy, and urge to
the continuance of the world." Seymour: "this mystical symbol . . .
was reverenced as the 'hidden wisdom' among the Chaldeans, Phoeni-
tians, Mexicans, and every ancient people in both hemispheres." Col-
enso: "It appears to have been the aboriginal possession of every
people of antiquity. Delineated on temples, palaces, natural rocks,
sepulchral galleries, on the heaviest monoliths and the rudest statuary,
on coins, medals, and vases of every description, and preserved in the
architectural proportions of subterranean as well as superterranean
structures of tumuli and temples. . . .Of the several varieties of Cross,
St. George, St. Andrew, Maltese, Greek, Latin, etc., etc., there is not
one amongst them the existence of which may not be traced to the
remotest antiquity." Budge: "To the Cross were transformed the
powers and attributes of the old Eygptian amulet ankh, and the
histories of the monks supply many instances of its use as an amulet.”
Draper: "At first the simple cross was used as a substitute for the
amulets and charms of remoter times; it constituted a fetish able to
expel evil spirits, even Satan himself." Moody: "The Christian con-
viction that the sign of the cross must be stamped upon all nature and
all art." Cumont: "During the first five centuries Christians felt an
unconquerable repugnance to the representation of the Savior of the
world nailed to an instrument of punishment more infamous than
the guillotine of today." (For quaint and fantastic beliefs about the
cross, see Lecky, Rationalism, I, 205 and passim.)
DECIUS As Christian apologists have tried to transform Constantine
into a saint, so have they represented other emperors as the
blackest scoundrels; but of Decius Gregg says: “one of the noblest rep-
resentatives of later heathenism, animated by the same virtuous prin-
ciples in private as in public life." It is true, as Riddle says, that he
tried "to uproot the Christian movement altogether."
DIOCLETIAN
See the first coment under DECIUS. Lecky: “a mod-
erate, placable, and conspicuously humane na-
." Duchesne speaks of his "good sense" until prevailed upon
by the vengeful Galerius.
ture.
299EASTER Socrates, church historian of the 5th century, wrote that
the Easter festival "has been introduced into the Church
from some old usage, just as many other customs have been estab-
lished." He was right. Clemen: "It was the Christians of Cappadocia
who first made the vernal equinox the date of Easter." Streeter: “A
divergency between the churches of Rome and Asia in regard to the
day on which Easter should be observed was a matter of acute contro-
versy throughout the second century, leading to the excommunication
of Asia by Rome about A. D. 195." Loisy: "The so-called quartodeci-
man usage, maintained by the congregations in Asia at the end of the
second century and condemned by Pope Victor, was the primitive usage
of all the Christian congregations and is indeed presupposed in the
gospel tradition. But the glory of the Sun's day proved irresistible,
and this attraction, coupled with the growth against purely Jewish
observances, soon shifted the Christian Passover to the Sunday follow-
ing the Jewish celebration." Today's Easter is, of course, the pagan
resurrection of the sun.
FAITH Celsus complained: "Some Christians will not so much as
give or accept any account of what they believe. They adhere
to the watchwords, 'Prove not, only believe', and 'Thy faith shall save
thee'. Wisdom is an evil thing. . ." Tertullian: "When we believe,
we have no desire to sally beyond our faith. For our belief is the
primary fact. There is nothing further . . . to be ignorant of every-
thing outside the rule of faith is to possess all knowledge." Augustine:
"We believe in order that we may know, we do not know in order
that we may believe. Faith, then, is to believe what you do not see,
while truth is to see what you have already believed." Anselm: “I
do not seek to undestand in order that I may believe, but I believe in
order that I may understand. For this, too, I believe, that unless I
first believe, I shall not understand." Harnack: “At bottom the faith
required was blind faith."
FORMALISM There came over Christianity, says Hatch, in the last
half of the second and first part of the third centuries
"an enormous change. .the party which endeavored to preserve the
ancient ideal was treated as schismatical. . . .Morality became subor-
dinated to belief. . . .The Pauline ethics vanished from the Christian
world." Again: "no sooner is any new impulse given either to philo-
sophy or to religion than there arises a class of men who copy the
form without the substance, and try to make the echo of the past
300sound like the voice of the present. So it has been with Christianity.”
And again: “the word Faith came to be transferred from simple trust in
God to.. .the acceptance of a series of propositions. . .in abstract.
metaphysics....the transference of Christianity from a Semitic to a
Greek soil. . . .the Nicene Creed rather than the Sermon on the Mount
has formed the dominant element in Aryan Christianity."
FORGERY On Christian forgery see Notes to my novel A Goat for
Azazel. Deliberate forgery continued for centuries. Mos-
heim: "The greatest and most learned doctors of the 4th century were
without exception disposed to deceive and lie, whenever the interest
of religion required it.”
GOD Maximus of Madaura wrote to Augustine: "Who is that God
of yours, of whom you Christians claim, as it were, the ex-
clusive possession and the first discovery?" Celsus, another pagan:
"It does not make any difference whether one calls the highest God
Zeus or Adonai or Sabaoth or like the Eygptians Amon, or like the
Scythians Papaios." Maximus Tyrius: "God is the Father and Creator
of things that are, older than the sun, older than the heaven, master
of time and eternity and of all changing Nature.”
HELL Minucius Felix: "The fire burns limbs and restores them,
feeds on them, nourishes them." Cyprian: "nor will there
be any source whence at any time they may have other respite or end
their torments." Farrer: "It is almost enough to make one despair
of human sanity to think that some seven centuries after Epicurus such
a book should have been written as the 21st of Augustine's City of
God." Reinach: "The descent into hell has been one of the articles of
Christian dogma since the Council of Nicaea.”
HERESY Bousset: "A heretic is a man with an opinion." Enslin:
"what a debt every religion-and Christianity perhaps
most of all-owes to its heretics, for notions dubbed heresy at bedtime
often arise the next morning with a halo of orthodoxy firm upon their
brows." Donaldson: "the Church, in combatting these sects, succeeded
in defeating them, but always carried off a large portion of their
heretical opinions for its own permanent use." Bentwich: "the decay
of national life, the atrophy of human reason, and the incursion into
the Graeco-Roman world of 'Orientalism' produced such a welter of
superstition and heresies as has never been before or since." At the
end of the 4th century, Humphrey points out, a new law defined a
301heretic as "all those who even in a minor matter deviate from the
opinion and path of the Catholic religion." Of the multitude of
sects that alone is now deemed not to have been heretical which with
State aid was able to destroy its competitors!
HOLY GHOST We have seen the statement that the Holy Ghost
was the mother of the gods, for in its early stages, as
Robertson says, it was feminine; and we know from Origen that in
the lost gospel according to the Hebrews Jesus was made to speak of
"my mother, the Holy Spirit." On what is sin against the Holy Ghost
modern Christian churches differ. Whittaker: "Criticism seems to
have justified the audacious suggestion of Hobbes, that what was
originally meant by the sin against the Holy Ghost, which could never
be forgiven, was resistance to the ecclesiastical power."
MARRIAGE
Rudwin: "The affection of one sex for another was
believed, from the earliest period of the Christian era,
to be under the special control of the powers of hell." Chrysostom:
"Marriage is good, but virginity is better. . . .I consider virginity to be
as high above marriage as the heavens are above the earth." Ambrose:
"Married people ought to blush. . ." Justin: "sexual activity is un-
necessary to life." Etc. On marriage with outsiders Tertullian had
written: “If heretics are unwilling to come over to the catholic church,
they are not to be allowed to marry catholic girls. Resolved also that
neither Jews nor heretics be allowed to marry such. . .”
MARTYRDOM Diocletian: “As a rule, the Christians are only too
happy to die." Tertullian: "condemnation gives us
more pleasure than acquittal." But not all, for as Watkins says, "the
apostasy in the Decian persecution was simply appalling." Antoninus
had exclaimed to Christians: "Unhappy men! if you are thus weary
of your lives, is it so difficult for you to find ropes and precipices?”
Riddle: "in cases of martyrdom certain symptoms of a psychosis
appear. One finds, for example, a morbid desire for. . .martyrdom.”
Workman: "their indifference to-we might almost call it their en-
thusiasm for-death." Lecky: "The desire for martyrdom became at
times a form of absolute madness." Jackson: "the desire for martyrdom
was almost an obsession with the more ardent believers."
MARY Christian apologists have bitterly resisted the obvious fact
that Mary was assimilated to Isis. Weigall: "Artemis was.
identified with Selene, the Moon-goddess, in which capacity the cres-
302cent moon was her symbol; and Isis, similarly, was identified with the
moon. This accounts for the presence of the crescent moon in so many
paintings of the Virgin Mary. Isis was also identified with Venus or
Aphrodite. . . .Aphrodite was born of the foam of the sea. . .when
the Madonna took the place of Isis she also took over the title Stella
Maris, 'Star of the Sea'. . . .In her aspect as the mother of Horus, Isis
was represented in tens of thousands of statuettes and paintings, hold-
ing the divine child in her arms; and when Christianity triumphed
these paintings, and figures became those of the Madonna and Child
without any break in continuity: no archaeologist, in fact, can now
tell whether some of these objects represent the one or the other."
(Weigall was an eminent archaeologist). Robertson: "As for Mary,
her worship seems from the first to have been associated with that of old
statues of a nursing Goddess-Mother, and the statues followed the cult,
some black statues of Isis and Horus being worshiped to this day as
representing Mary and Jesus." Hannay: “All the early statues of Mary
and Jesus introduced into Europe were actual statues of Isis and her
babe Horus, the Savior." Draper: "the earliest representations are
veiled. The Eygptian sculptors had thus depicted Isis; the first form of
the Virgin and Child was the counterpart of Isis and Horus." Glover:
"Isis, like the Madonna, was painted and sculptured with a child in
." Bouchier: "that most marvellous transformation in
history, which eventually raised the modest Galilean peasant woman
to the vacant throne of Cybele and Isis." Showerman says the transi-
tion of the cult of Isis "was quite as much a process of blending as a
violent displacement." For Karl Pearson she became, in Rudwin's
words, "the vindication of the right of the common folk to a goddess
of their own kind."
her arms.
Mary was also assimilated to the Mother of the Gods. CE says
Nestorious in the beginning of the 5th cen. "had declared against
the new and, as he asserted, idolatrous expression 'Mother of God',
thereby opposing the sentiments and wishers of the humbler people.”
Carus: "the worship of Isis, called 'Mother of God', 'our Lady', 'the
Holy Lady', etc., terms which are literally repeated afterwards in
Christianity with reference to the Virgin Mary." S. A. Hook: “Simi-
larly the Virgin Mary. . .has frequently taken the place formerly held
by the female deities of antiquity." Case: "Dionysus was reputed to
have been the son of Zeus, and already in the time of Euripides his
mother Semele was famed as one who gave birth to a god' (theon
tekein), the prototype of the later Christian title theotokos, applied to
the mother of Jesus." Steinthal: "Mary had a multitude of lovely
303and charming features ascribed to her, which under heathenism were
attributes of Freyja, Holda, and Bertha." Rhys: “Mary inherits the
festival of Cybele." Bevan: "Bousset has given ground for thinking
that in the Mother we have the old Semitic Ishtar (Astarte), who had
been at once in strange fashion the virgin-goddess and the goddess of
lust and prostitution." Glover: "It is interesting to note in passing
that the land (Greece) which introduced the Mother of God (Cybele)
to the Roman world, also gave the name theotokos (Mother of God)
to the church." Latourette: "The Christian emphasis upon the Virgin
Mary as the 'Mother of God' is alleged to owe much to the Magna
Mater....The cult of the Virgin Diana may have contributed to the
worship of the Virgin Mary.". But it is to be noted that the Church
did not declare her a virgin until 451, and then only because of popu-
lar demand.
MASS, THE
Dembitz: "It seems that while now the prophetic lesson
is only the last exercise of the desk, being followed by
Musaf, it was formerly the very last of all the Sabbath morning ser-
vices; hence it obtained the name of Haftara (leave-giving or dismis-
sal), whence many think the Missa (the meeting is dismissed), or
Mass, of the Christians derives its name." Hannay: "The Eygptians
called the cakes 'Mest', then the Hebrews learnt it during their cap-
tivity and called it 'Mass' (or plural Massoth), lastly the Church of
Rome adopts it as 'Mass or Messe'." E. Carpenter: "The word host
which is used in the Catholic Mass. . .is from the Latin Hostia which
the dictionary interprets as ‘an animal slain in sacrifice, a sin-offering'."
Allen: "The last half of the 4th century was the beginning of an age
of ritual activity, in the course of which the Lord's Supper was trans-
formed into the Mass. . ." Goodenough: "As the Mass came more and
more to be regarded as a heathen sacrifice, so it increasingly was equip
ped with external trappings and ceremonials borrowed from pagan
ritual."
MYSTERIES Latourette: "Catholocism is declared to owe to the
mystery cults its ideas of secrecy, symbolism, mystical
brotherhood, sacramental grace, and the three stages of the spiritual
life." It owes more than that; see under MYSTERY in Notes to my
novel A Goat for Azazel.
MYSTICISM Loehr: "mysticism in general is man becoming one
with God." M. Smith: "The mystic is one who seeks
304for an ever closer and more direct relationship with Reality, that is,
God...." Silberer: "We can define mysticism as the religious state
which struggles by the shortest way towards the accomplishment of the
end of religion, the union with the divinity; or as an intensive culti-
vation of oneself in order to experience this union." Picton: "Mysti-
cism consists in the spiritual realization of a grander and a boundless
Unity, that humbles all self-assertion by dissolving it in a wider glory."
Du Prel: "Soul is our spirit within the self-consciousness, spirit is the
soul beyond the self-consciousness." Goethe: "Mysticism is the scho-
lastic of the heart, the dialectic of the feelings." Récéjac: "Mysticism
is the tendency to approach the Absolute morally, and by means of
symbols." Harnack: "Mysticism is rationalism applied to a sphere
above reason... A mystic who is not a Catholic is a dilettante."
Lasson: "The essence of mysticism is the assertion of an intuition
which transcends the temporal categories of the understanding."
Nettleship: "Mysticism is the consciousness that everything we ex-
perience is... symbolic of something more." Bonchitté: "Mysticism
consists in giving to the spontaneity of the intelligence a larger part
than to the other faculties." Von Hartmann: "Mysticism is the filling
of the consciousness with a content... out of the unconscious.”
Michelet: "The complete suppression of individuality and the death
of free will, this is the great principle of mysticism." Noack: "Mys-
ticism is formless speculation." To all of which the reader may say
nonsense and a lot of it certaintly is nonsense. For me, Von Hartmann
and Lasson come closest to the matter. Whatever else it may be,
mysticism is the play of the subconscious content in the conscious
processes and needs, like asceticism, the attention of the psychologists.
NICEA, COUNCIL OF St. Gregory: “I never knew of a Synod that
did any good or prevented any evil.” Mil-
man: “Nowhere is Christianity less attractive than in the Councils of
the Church." Schwartz: "Never has an emperor so triumphed over the
Church as did Constantine in and through the Council of Nicea."
Draper: "So little efficacy was there in the determination of the Coun-
cil of Nicea that for many years afterward creed upon creed appeared."
Gwatkin: “The whole future of Christianity was determined by it;
and we must fairly face the question whether Athanasius was right
or not." E. Carpenter: "The Nicene creed had nothing to propound
except some extremely futile speculations about the relation to each
other of the Father and the Son, and the relation of both to the Holy
Ghost, and of all three to the Virgin Mary-speculations which only
305served for the renewal of shameful strife and animosities..." Rhys
points out: "Sabinus, Bishop of Heraclea, declared that, with the
exception of the emperor himself and one other person, the three
hundred assembled bishops were 'a set of illiterate simple creatures,
who understood nothing"." And Goguel reminds us that the “sub-
ordination of Christ to God is very clearly affirmed by Paul (I Cor.
xv, 27-28)."
PAGAN
Many instances have already appeared in these
INFLUENCE Notes but the notion so widely prevails that Chris-
tianity was a 'new' religion that developed in a
vacuum that still more data must be given. Most Christians of this
time were sun-worshipers. The conservative Cath. Encyc. admits:
"Not only Gnostics and other heretics, but Christians who considered
themselves faithful, held in a measure to the worship of the sun.
Leo the Great in his day [5th cen.] says that it was the custom of many
Christians to stand on the steps of the church of St. Peter and pay
homage to the Sun by obeisance and prayers. It is true that the
believers in Mithras also observed Sunday as well as Christmas. Con-
sequently Constantine speaks not of the day of the Lord, but of the
everlasting day of the Sun."
Frazer: "When we reflect how often the Church has skilfully con-
trived to plant the seeds of the new faith on the old stock of pa-
ganism..." Whittaker: “in the third generation the incipient Catho-
lic Church, with its incorporation of pagan myth." Robertson: “A
dozen ecclesiastical historians have avowed the wholesale adoption of
pagan rites, symbols, and conceptions by the early Christian Church."
Legge: "In the days of her infancy. . . C borrowed much from the
rivals over which she was in the long run to reign supreme
The
form of her sacraments, the periods of her fasts and festivals, and in-
stitutions like monachism, cannot be explained without reference to
those religions from whose rivalry she so long suffered." Lactantinus
said naively: "The teachings of the heathen philosophers do not
differ from those taught by us who are Christians" and Faustus said
to Augustine: "You celebrate the solemnities of the Gentiles, their
Kalends and Solstices, and as to their customs, those you have retained
without any alteration and nothing distinguishes you from the pagans.
except that you hold your assemblies apart from them." When so
accused the Christians said the pagans had plagiarized them. Harnack:
"The charge of plagiarism was not merely levelled against philosophy,
... but also against any rites and methods of worship which furnished
306actual or alleged parallels to those of Christianity." Inge: "The
pagans repeated so persistently the charge that Christ borrowed from
Plato what was true in his teaching, that Ambrose wrote a treatise to
confute them. As a rule, the Christians did not deny the resemblance,
but explained it by saying that Plato had plagiarized Moses.”
Lea: "Many of the observances of Latin C would seem explicable
by derivation from Buddhism, such as monasticism, the tonsure, the
use of beads, confession, penance, and absolution, the sign of the cross,
relic worship, and miracles wrought by relics, the purchase of salvation
by gifts to the Church, pilgrimages to sacred places..." Hatch: “A
large part of what are sometimes called Christian doctrines, and many
usages which have prevailed and continue to prevail ... are in reality
Greek theories and Greek usages changed in form and color... but
in their essence Greek still." Halliday: "In art again the debt of C
to pagan cults is certain. There can be little doubt that representa-
tions of Mithras shooting at the rock or Mithras mounting the chariot
of the Sun directly influenced Christian representations of Moses at
Horeb or the ascent of Elijah... pagan prototype of the Christian
basilica." Robertson: "After it had become a State religion, the adop-
tion of images was as necessary to its popularity as the adoption of
pagan festivals and rites . . . . Purgatory was yet another pagan tenet,
which in the first five centuries was regarded by the Church as hereti-
cal." Weigall: "The festival of Osiris had in ancient times become
identified with the feast of lamps commemorating the dead in general,
held early in November. But the Christian feast of All Souls, in honor
of the dead, likewise falls at the beginning of November
This
festival was first recognized by the Church in 998; but Grazer has
shown by this recognition the clergy were simply regularizing an im-
memorial and widespread pagan custom which they could not sup-
press." Goodenough: “When a heathen temple was taken over it was
frequently dedicated to some saint, who was honored with precisely
the same rites and prerogatives as had been given to the old god....
The most famous instance of this sort of invasion of paganism into C
is the case of the old Roman Pantheon, a temple in Rome dedicated
primarily to Jupiter Vindex, but used as a temple for deities of all
sorts. In 607 this temple was given to the Bishop of Rome, who pure-
fied it and dedicated it to the Virgin and all the Saints, and from this
dedication became fixed one of the most important of the church fes-
tivals, that of All Saints, which still survives as the popular festival
of Hallowe'en among those who little dream they are perpetuating an
institution of Roman paganism." Even CE speaks of "numerous cases
.
307in which Christian saints became the successors of local deities...
This explains the great number of similarities between gods and
saints"; and CE cites instances of "statues of pagan gods baptized
and transformed into Christian saints."
PENANCE Watkins: "Even a slight acquaintance with Christian lit-
erature of the age should preclude the statement that
there was anywhere a general practice of confessing what are now
called venial sins. . . . There was no capital sin with regard to which
the Catholic Church did not claim the privilege and admit the re-
sponsibility of exercising her Divine Master's commission of Recon-
ciliation here in this present life." Some of the 'heretical' sects (which
see) denied this privilege.
PERSECUTION Reinach: "The Church of the second and the
third centuries suffered less from persecution than
from heresies." Moxom: "From the time of Trajan onward, the more
thoughtful and patriotic emperors viewed the growth of C with ever
deepening solicitude." Robertson: "It was the insistence on the immi-
nent end of the world, the preaching of celibacy, the disparagment of
earthly dignitaries, the vehement assault on the standing cults of the
State, no less than the refusal to sacrifice to the emperor's statue, that
had so long made Christism seem the natural enemy of all civil gov-
ernment." Hearnshaw: "One of the main causes why the Roman
Empire departed from its usual policy of religious tolerance, and
persecuted the early Church, was that the members of that Church
refused military service and preached pacificsm." Lecky: "The rela-
tions of C to the sentiment of patriotism were from the first very un-
fortunate." Legge: "The officials of the Roman Empire in time of
persecution sought to force the Christians to sacrifice, not to any of
the heathen gods, but to the Genius of the Emperor and the Fortune
of the City of Rome; and at all times the Christians' refusal was looked
upon not as a religious but as a political offense." Under Diocletian,
says Mason, “terrible was the persecution."
PETER, SEE OF Harnack: "The Church of Rome was founded
by some unknown missionaries at the beginning
of the apostolic age." Moxom: "The claim that St. Peter founded
the church in Rome was not heard until 170 A.D.; it was a baseless
claim..." Lea: "The transmission of the power from the apostles to
those who were assumed to be their successors is the most audacious
308non sequitur in history, and the success of the attempt can scarce be
overestimated as a factor in the development of religion and civiliza-
tion." C. H. Moore: "The first St. Peter's was built hard beside a
shrine to the Great Mother of the Gods; there for three-quarters of a
century the old and the new mysteries strove in conscious rivalry."
Angus: "The Vatican stands where the last sacrament of the Phrygian
taurobolium [blood-bath] was celebrated." Robertson: "The use of
the special title of 'Papa' by him of Rome was probably an imitation
of Mithraism, in the hierarchy of which the chief priest was 'Father
of Fathers', as the God was 'Father Mithra,' and, like Attis, probably
called Papa."
RELICS
masses
•
·
Duchesne: "In the Christian religion the cultus of the
saints, of relics and of images, is a contribution of the
We hear of blood collected on pieces of linen or in
sponges at the time of the martyrs' agony; less frequently of bones of
similar origin, I think, for it was only later that people began to
open the tombs and cut in pieces the bodies of the saints . . ." Rob-
ertson: "To find what might pass for the bones and relics of saints
and martyrs, to frame false tales concerning them; to win illiterate
and poor pagans to the Church by imitating their festivals and cere-
monies-these were, by the grieving admission of many Christian
historians, among the common activities of the Church from the second
or third century onwards; and the priesthood were the natural agents
of the work." Reinach: "Relics, which had become at once a disgrace
to the Church and a source of revenue to the clergy."
RELIGION Jerome: "There is nothing so easy as by sheer volubility
to deceive a common crowd or an uneducated congre-
gation." Polybius said the Romans used religion "as a check upon
the common people. seeing that every multitude is fickle and full
of lawless desires, unreasoning anger and violent passion, the only re-
source is to keep them in check by mysterious terrors and scenic
effects..." Diodorus: "The myths that are told of affairs in Hades,
though pure invention at bottom, contribute to make men pious and
upright." Varro: "It is in the interest of states to be deceived in
religion." And Livy wrote that Numa used "fear of the gods as the
most effective thing for an ignorant and rough multitude.”
SECTS
There were so many Christian sects-there were still almost
a hundred in Augustine's day-that only a few of the most
309•
important ones can be glanced at here. The dissensions among them
were so bitter and scandalous that Eusebius after a few scathing words
refused to record them. Lecky: "There is no wild beast so ferocious.
as Christians who differ concerning their faith. . . . The eighty or
ninety sects into which Christianity speedily divided, hated one another
with an intensity that extorted the wonder of Julian and the ridicule
of the Pagans." Julian said: “The savage beasts are not more formid-
able to men than the Christians are to each other when they are
divided by creed or opinion." Hatch: "I will not pain your ears by
quoting the torrents of abuse which one saint poured upon
another, because the one assented to the speculations of a majority,
and the other had speculations of his own." Donaldson: “Men dis-
puted with the most bitterness and ferocity about minute points of
doctrine which are now incomprehensible almost to every one... and
they pronounced sentence of eternal damnation without the slightest
compunction on all who differed from them." E. Carpenter: “The
horrible rancours and revenges of the clergy and the sects against each
other in the fourth and fifth centuries..." Robertson: "Life-and:
death struggle was to determine what orthodoxy was to be." Cumont:
"Let us suppose that in a modern Europe the faithful had deserted
the Christian churches to worship Allah or Brahma, to follow the
precepts of Confucius or Buddha, or to adopt the maxims of the
Shinto; let us imagine a great confusion of all the races of the world
in which Arabian mullahs, Chinese scholars, Japanese bonzes, Tibetan
lamas and Hindu pundits should all be preaching fatalism and pre-
destination, ancestor-worship and devotion to a deified sovereign,
pessimism and deliverance through annihilation-... would offer a
pretty accurate picture of the religious chaos in which the ancient
world was struggling before the reign of Constantine." Lea: "During
these early centuries there was no central authority and consequently
no uniformity of practice."
Manichaeism This sect, Moxom points out, "late in the 4th cen-
tury numbered among their adherents so able a man as the great
Augustine." Latourette: "Several notable scholars hold Manichaeism
to have been an offshoot of Zoroastrianism, or at least Iranian. . . .
Another scholar esteems it primarily an outgrowth of Christianity.
Another finds in it late Judaism, another bases it upon the Mandaeans,
and still another maintains that the prevailing element is Buddhist.”
It exercised, says Harnack, "a great attraction on the deeper spirits."
Legge: "Manes was indeed, as the discoveries of the last decade have
taught us, an innovator in religion entirely worthy to rank with Zoro-
310aster, Buddha, and Muhammad." His system "aimed at sweeping
into one vast synthesis or eclectic church the three religions of Zoroas-
ter, Buddha, and Christ." A. V. W. Jackson: "Mani endeavored, by
making a synthesis of elements from various existing religions, to form
a new religion, eclectic in character... one that should not be con-
fined by national borders but be universally adopted." St. Bernard
said: "If you interrogate them, nothing can be more Christian; as to
their conversation, nothing can be less reprehensible, and what they
speak they proved by deeds. As for the morals of the heretic, he cheats
no one; his cheeks are pale with fasting, he eats not the bread of idle-
ness, his hands labor for his livelihood." Lea: "Of all the heresies
with which the early Church had to contend none excited such mingled
fear and loathing as Manichaeism... no religion can show a more un-
broken roll of those who unshrinkingly and joyfully sought death in
its most abhorrent form in preference to apostasy. If the blood of the
martyrs were really the seed of the Church, Manichaeism would now
be the dominant religion of Europe." Of its founder Strzygowski says
he was "a painter, and therefore the only religious Founder from the
outset aware of the importance of the formative arts as a means of
influence." Manichaeism was not completely destroyed until the
Middle Ages.
Montanism Allen: "The Montanist, the Novatian, the Donatist,
were all alike in this respect, that they did not believe that salvation
depended on adherence to the Catholic church..." Adeney: “The
Montanists perceived that with growth in numbers, wealth, and gen-
eral prosperity, the Church was losing its early purity and the fine,
heroic enthusiasm of simpler times." Hatch: "Montanism was a re-
bellion-not only against the officialism of C, but also against its
worldliness." Wand: "It is often said of the Montanists, those second-
century enthusiasts who wished to set what appeared to be the present
guidance of the Holy Spirit against the rigid forms of tradition, that
they were in point of fact merely reproducing primitive Christianity.”
Jackson: "Distinguished for the unwordliness of their lives... for
their protest against the growing worldliness of the Church." Hasle-
hurst: "A protest against laxly administered discipline, as were Nova-
tianism and Donatism." Loisy: "The historical significance of Mon-
tanism lies in the fact that here we have a rekindling of the inner fire
whose light illuminated the first believers." Bigg: "One of those
outbreaks of mysticism which from time to time have visited the
Church, giving it new life yet threatening it with destruction."
Marcionism Harnack calls him the first reformer of the Catholic
311Church: Marcion said the Church had been corrupted by additions
made by men to the teachinsg of Jesus. Streeter: "The genius of
Marcion confronted the loosely jointed system and the heterogeneous
undefined theology of the 'great Church' with an opposition Church-
well organized, with a clear-cut theology and a definite selection of
sacred books in a New Testament." Hatch: "Marcion made so deep
a chasm between the Law and the Gospel... that the two were re-
garded as inherently hostile." Duchesne: "His fellow-schismatics called
him 'most holy Master', and regarded him with the utmost veneration.
They believed him to be with Christ and St. Paul in heaven; the Sa-
vior having Paul on His right hand, and Marcion on His left." At
the end of the 4th century, says J. E. Carpenter, his followers were
still "active in Rome and Italy, Egypt, Palestine, Syria, Arabia, and
even Persia.”
Arianism Jackson: "For us today it is almost as difficult to under-
stand why the controversy caused so much commotion in the world
as to comprehend its nature." Gwatkin: "a grave and blameless pres-
byter." Duchesne: "His manner of speaking was gentle; his addresses
were persuasive. The consecrated virgins, who were very numerous
in Alexandria, held him in great esteem; among the higher clergy he
counted many staunch supporters." Jerome: "The whole world
groaned in wonderment to find itself Arian." Adeney: "Arianism
caused the most serious division in the Church that has occurred dur-
ing the whole course of the history of Christendom... an extremely
simple system... professed to be free from the obscurities of the popu-
lar theology. It banished mystery from religion. Its appeal was to
logic." The struggle, says Gwatkin, "went on for a full three hundred
years in all, and on a scale of vastness never seen again in history."
Schmidt: "Whether Arianism, if unchecked, would have led to a mono-
theism like the Jewish or Muhammadan, with an Ebionitish Chris-
tology, or developed into a polytheism more marked than the practical
tritheism of the Church, is a question not easily answered." Reinach:
"In these days there are no professed Arians, perhaps because all Chris-
tians are Arians at heart."
Another prominent heresy was that of Donatus, of whom Duchesne
says: "Donatus was not an ordinary man. He was intelligent and well
educated, and of ascetic morality.... His followers, who were very
proud of him, treated him as a being of a higher order than them-
selves." Reinach: "Donatus died in exile and his followers were
massacred."
Mithraism This was a pagan religion from which C borrowed a
312great deal. Though Harnack and Roese think it never was a serious
threat to C, Cumont, Dieterich and many other scholars take the op-
posite view. S. H. Hooke: "In the third century Mithraism was the
most serious rival to C." Finkelstein: "More dangerous to infant C
than either [Stoicism or Epicureanism], was the dualistic religion of
Mithras, which as late as the 3rd century challenged the advance of
the Church in almost every province of the Empire." E. Carpenter:
"Christianity narrowly escaped being called Mithraism—so nearly re-
lated and closely allied were these cults with each other." Halliday:
"Similarities between Chrisitan and Mithraic ritual led the Christian
Fathers to attack the Persian cult with special venom... the sacra-
mental meal of bread and water (or wine), the use of baptism, the
sealing of initiates, the promise of resurrection, for such features as
these they could account only by supposing that the devil had in-
spired a deliberate parody of divine ordinances." Dill: "It is little
wonder that the Fathers, from the 2nd century, saw in Mithra the
most formidable foe of C. Indeed, the resemblances between the two
religions... were very striking." Duchesne: "Both religions observed
the Sunday, the Day of the Sun.... Mithraism had its ascetics, of both
sexes, like the Christian Church."
Nearly all of the dissident sects were eventually exterminated by the
Roman Church, sometimes (as in the case of the Albigensians: see my
next novel) with the most cold-blooded massacre.
SUNDAY We have already seen that the Church took over this
pagan holy day. Wand: "There was no suggestion at all
that it should be kept in the same way as the Jewish Sabbath, by a
cessation from work. It was not indeed regarded as being the same
kind of day as the Sabbath." Latourette: "Up to the middle of the
5th century Christians were still observing the Jewish sabbath, al-
though even in the 2nd century this was not universal or generally
binding."
SUPERSTITION Irenaeus: "Some surely and certainly drive out
demons.... Others possess a fore-knowledge of the
future... it is impossible to enumerate the spiritual gifts and bless-
ings which, all over the world, the church has received from God in
the name of Jesus." Halliday: "To an average individual of the 3rd
century after Christ it seemed more probable that the cause of any
given phenomenon was supernatural or miraculous, than that it should
be the inevitable result of some natural cause. ... This lamentable at-
313titude of mind... cut away from the foundation of true scientific
speculation." Harnack: "Monica, Augustine's mother, like many a
Christian widow, saw visions frequently; she could even detect, from
a certain taste in her mouth, whether it was a real revelation." For
two centuries after Justin, says Lecky, there isn't a "single Christian
writer who does not solemnly and explicitly assert the reality" of exor-
cism. Every large church, says Harnack, had its exorcists, who were
"honored as persons endowed with special grace. . .'
""
TRINITY, THE In the early church there were two views: the
Adoptionist, that Jesus was mortal man who be-
came a god, the other, that he was a pre-existent god who became a
man. The second won but left, says Lake, "its permanent mark on
Christian theology in the 'threeness' of the doctrine of God." Draper:
"For thirty centuries the Egyptians had been familiar with the concep-
tion of a triune god." Carus: "A doctrine which has become very dear
to Christians and is generally regarded as typical of the Christian faith,
but which is nevertheless common to all Gentile religions, being
glaringly absent in Judaism only." Murray: "These ideas were too
widespread and too deeply rooted to be suppressed, and soon began
to lead away from strict monotheism to something more emotional
and more familiar; to the immemorial worship of the Mother Goddess,
to the old Trinity of Father, Mother and Child...” Among Chris-
tians, says Conybeare, the idea of trinity in unity did not emerge
until the end of the 3rd century. Clemen: "The doctrine of the Trin-
ity is not to be found in the New Testament-for the words of I John
v-7 f. are notoriously spurious." Pfleiderer: "After having accepted
the divinity of the Saviour from the Gnostics, the Church could not
avoid following them so far as to place the God-Father alongside the
Son and the Ghost as other divine beings; thus, the Church 'trinity'
resulted..." Goodenough: "The Church was compelled to offer vir-
tually a polytheistic religion." Moxom says that around the Nicene
doctrine of the trinity "the Church organized itself for a struggle that
continued with varying fortunes for three centuries, and . . . has
been renewed at different times... even down to our own time. [After
262] the view that the Son was of the same essence as the Father, and
equal with him, rapidly gained adherents." Allen: "The doctrine of
the Trinity did not finally triumph until the new city of Constan-
tinople rose to power..." The doctrine was officially proclaimed in
380.
314VIRGIN, THE It has doubtless been clear to the reader long before
this that the Christian Virgin also had a pagan
origin. Allen: "The sudden rise of the worship of the Virgin Mother,
regarding which the creeds are silent, but whose rapid spread no
creeds could hinder..." Robertson: "There was finally forced on the
Church a cult of the Mother as Virgin Goddess, without which it could
never have held its own against the great and well-managed worships
of Isis and Rhea-Cybele and Demeter..." Legge: "The worship of
the Virgin as the Theotokos or Mother of God which was introduced
into the Catholic Church about the time of the destruction of the
Serapeum, enabled the devotees of Isis to continue unchecked their
worship of the mother goddess by merely changing the name of the
object of their adoration, and Prof. Drexler gives a long list of the
statues of Isis which thereafter were used, sometimes with unaltered
attributes, as those of the Virgin Mary." G. S. Hall tells us that for
Ruskin she appeared as the mater dolorosa, in which "loveliness and
patheticism were chiefly striven for.... The second type was the ex-
alted crowned and enthroned queen of heaven and of virtue ... a third
type, which is the ... ideal mother." The elevation of Mary continued
century after century and still goes on in the Roman Church. As for
the gospel virgin-birth story it is now admitted by all scholars to have
been a later addition: see Cath. Encyc. xv, 451. Angus: "In the early
Church virgins were encouraged to dedicate their members and their
flesh to Christ" (on girls who would become nobody's bride but
Christ's see such sources as Eileen Power, Medieval Nunneries). It was
believed that a nuptial relationship existed between Christ and virgins.
Cyprian said: "If a husband come and see his wife lying with another
man, is he not indignant and maddened?... How indignant and an-
gered then must Christ our Lord and Judge be, when He sees a Virgin,
dedicated to Himself, and consecrated to His holiness, lying with a
man!... She who has been guilty of this crime is an adulteress, not
against a husband, but Christ." Among the Saxons later Queen Ethel-
dred refused to surrender to her husband. We are told that sixteen
years after burial her body was exhumed and it was learned that her
clothes and flesh had not disintegrated at all, and the horrible wound
of which she had died was entirely healed. For her chastity she was
canonized. Augustine said virginity was superior to marriage. For say-
ing that it was not, the monk Jovinian was excommunicated. Jerome
said marriage was to be tolerated only because it produced virgins.
It was widely believed for ages by Christians that virginity could work
miracles. Ambrose said that because of her chastity Mary sister of
315Moses led the women through the Red Sea. Thecla for her chastity
was reverenced by lions. Catherine of Alexandria refusing all mar-
riage was taken to heaven and was then by Mary herself betrothed to
Christ.
WOMEN With the Bible in mind Elizabeth Cady Stanton wrote:
"I know of no other books that so fully teach the subjection
and degradation of women." Helen Gardener: "Every injustice that
has ever been fastened upon women in a Christian country has been
'authorized by the Bible' and riveted and perpetuated by the pulpit."
Though elsewhere he called women "fellow-servants and sisters" Ter-
tullian expressed the view of most of the early Fathers when he wrote:
"God's verdict on the sex still holds good, and the sex's guilt must
still hold also. You are the devil's gateway.. For your deceit . . .
the very Son of God had to perish." Moncure D. Conway sums it up:
“There is not a more cruel chapter in history than that which records
the arrest by Christianity of the natural growth of European civiliza-
tion regarding woman... the tremendous wrong that Christianity did"
--and a wrong that continued almost up to our own day, and the re-
sults of which are still with us everywhere. An exploration of some of
those results will be attempted in the concluding novel of this series.
